# 2 Kings

# ANNOTATIONS ON THE SECOND BOOK OF THE KINGS

**COMMONLY CALLED THE FOURTH BOOK OF THE KINGS.**

## THE ARGUMENT OF THE SECOND BOOK OF KINGS

The second book of the Kings continues the history of the kings of Israel and Judah to the captivity of them both. The history of Israel is from the rebellion of Moab in Ahaziah’s time ch. 1. 1. to the captivity of Hoshea, ch. 18. 10. which was the space of CLIX. years, wherein twelve kings reigned one after another. The history of Judah is from the end of Jehoshaphat’s reign to the captivity of Zedekiah, which was the space of CCXCIV. years (besides the XXVI. years mentioned ch. 25. 27.) wherein fifteen kings, besides Athalia the usurper, ch. 11. 1, &c. reigned one after another. The two books of the Kings are distinguished, not as if they contained histories of different matters, but because if the whole history had been compiled in one book, it would have seemed too great a volume. See on ch. 1. 1.

## CHAPTER 1

**Ver. 1. ***{Then}* Heb. *And*. This copulative particle knits the two books of Kings together, and shows that this second book goes on with the histories begun in the former.

*{Moab}* i.e. the people of Moab, whom David had subdued and made tributary, 2 Sam. 8. 2. See chap. 3. 4.

*{rebelled}* Or, *fell off*. They refused any longer to be subject to Israel. See 1 King. 12. 19.

*{against Israel}* When the tribes were divided into two kingdoms, one kingdom kept under some common enemies, the other others; Israel kept Moab under, and Judah kept Edom under, 1 King. 22. 47.

*{after the death of Ahab}* Ahab, as all his predecessors before from David, had kept Moab in subjection: But by his death the kingdom of Israel received a great blow; and his son Ahaziah being wicked and weak could not recover the strength which was lost; neither did he long continue, by reason of his dangerous fall. Moab therefore quickly takes that opportunity to rebel. This is noted as a part of God’s revenge on Ahaziah, whose impiety was very great, 1 King. 22. 52, 53. The loss of a dominion which one’s predecessors for many generations have held, is a great judgment. Witness Q. Mary’s loss of Callis.

**V. 2. ***{And Ahaziah fell down through a lattice in his upper chamber}* It seems there were cross bars or lattices, to let light into the king’s chamber, either weak or rotten or not well fastened, whereon he leaned or trod, and they failing he fell through, and mortally hurt himself. Some take the word translated *upper chamber* for the flat roof of his house; and *lattesses* for battlements, as Deut. 22. 8.

*{that was in Samaria}* Namely in his royal palace in that city. See 1 King. 16. 24.

*{and was sick}* The Hebrew styles every hurt a *sickness*, 1 King. 22. 34. 2 Chr. 35. 23.

*{and he sent messengers, and said unto them, Go, enquire}* It seems he feared death, but earnestly desired life, which made him so inquisitive of the issue of his sickness.

*{of Baalzebub}* The meaning of this word is *a fly-lord*. The Ekronites so called that idol whom they worshipped, because being infested with flies they thought their Baal freed them from that annoyance; or because their priests told them that in the shape of a fly their Baal buzzed oracles in their ears. See 1 King. 16. 31.

*{the god of Ekron}* Ekron was a great city within the lot of Judah, Josh. 15. 45. Judg. 1. 18. But the Philistines recovered it, and kept it, 1 Sam. 5. 10.

*{whether I shall recover}* Heb. *if I shall live*. The king’s bruise was so dangerous as his surgeons and physicians could give him little hope of recovery; and therefore he sends to Baal-zebub. As he slighted Jehovah the true Lord and God of Judah, so also the calves and Baal the idols of Israel, and sends to a strange god. Idolaters know not where to rest.

**V. 3. ***{But the Angel of the Lord said}* God makes known his mind to his prophets sometimes immediately by his Spirit, and sometimes immediately by the ministry of angels, and both ways does much honor the sons of men, and withal gives occasion to others of having the ministry of man in high account, Acts 10. 3, 5, 6.

*{to Elijah the Tishbite}* See 1 King. 17. 1.

*{Arise}* See 1 King. 14. 2.

*{go up to meet the messengers of the king of Samaria}* God prevented the messengers going to that abominable idol.

*{and say unto them}* God puts an express answer into his prophet’s mouth, that he might be the more faithful in delivering it, and the king give the more heed to it.

*{Is it not because there is not a God in Israel, that ye go to enquire of Baalzebub the god of Ekron?}* This is a sharp rebuke of Ahaziah’s folly. By sending to the idol of Ekron he showed that he thought there was no God in Israel, or that the God of Israel could not, or would not, tell what should be the issue of his hurt.

**V. 4. ***{Now therefore thus saith the Lord}* See 1 King. 12. 24.

*{Thou shalt not come down from that bed on which thou art gone up}* Heb. *the bed whither thou art gone up, thou shalt not come down from it*. The judgment is denounced against Ahaziah as spoken to his face, for the surer evidence thereof. This phrase, *Thou shalt not come down*, implies he should not recover: For when men recover, they use to rise and come down from their beds.

*{but shalt surely die}* Heb. *dying thou shalt dye*. See 1 King. 8. 13.

*{And Elijah departed}* Having done his message he went to his accustomed habitation; or to mount Carmel. See v. 9. & ch. 4. 25. & 1 King. 18. 19.

**V. 5. ***{And when the messengers turned back unto him}* So soon as the messengers had received their answer from Elijah, they returned to the king, and went not on to Ekron: For they believed that he was a prophet that spoke to them, and that in the name of the Lord.

*{he said unto them, Why are ye now turned back?}* He knew they could not have been so soon at Ekron, and therefore asks the reason of their speedy return.

**V. 6. ***{And they said unto him, There came a man up to meet us}* One whom they knew not so met them in the way as they discerned that he purposely came to deliver his message, and that he was a prophet.

*{and said unto us, Go, turn again unto the King that sent you}* It was Elijah’s charge that made them so soon return.

*{and say unto him, Thus saith the Lord, Is it not, &c.}* They faithfully deliver their message. Compare v. 3, 4. with this.

**V. 7. ***{And he said unto them}* The king’s reply shows that he was affrighted at the message.

*{What manner of man was he}* Heb. *what was the manner of the man?* Of what stature and countenance was he? What kind of apparel did he wear? How did he go?

*{which came up to meet you, and told you these words}* The matter so nearly concerned the king that he must needs be informed about him that sent the message.

**V. 8. ***{And they answered him}* They describe him as well as they can.

*{He was an hairy man}* Heb. *a lord of hairs*. See on Gen. 14. 13. This has reference to his apparel rather than to his person. His upper coat that he had on was a rough hairy coat. Prophets did use to wear such, Zach. 13. 4. whereby they made themselves a pattern for penitents, because a main point of their doctrine was repentance. In the like habit the witch describes Samuel, 1 Sam. 28. 14.

*{and girt with a girdle of leather about his loins}* It was an ancient custom to have fair costly girdles; but such a girdle as Elijah wore showed a contempt of all bravery. John Baptist was thus attired, Matth. 3. 4. to show that he was that other Elijah which was to come, Mal. 4. 5. Mat. 17. 11.

*{And he said, It is Elijah the Tishbite}* Certainly this king had seen Elijah before in his father’s time, or at least heard before of him, and of his manner of going.

**V. 9. ***{Then the king sent unto him}* The king was so enraged at this hard message as he resolved to take away his life that sent it: And questionless, he was put on thereto by his mother Jezebel, who was as much incensed against Elijah, 1 King. 19. 1. as Herodias was against the Baptist, Mat. 14. 8.

*{a captain of fifty}* Captains were distinguished by the number of soldiers under them. Some were over the whole host, 1 Chr. 11. 6. some over a thousand, 1 Sam. 18. 13. some over an hundred, Acts 23. 23. some over fifty, as here.

*{with his fifty}* He sent so many, not to honor the prophet, (for then he would have sent courtiers) but to affright him the more, and force him to come if he made any resistance. But herein he showed his folly: For if he took Elijah to have been but as other men, what need of so many well armed to fetch one man? but if he conceived that God would in special manner protect him, he might well know that multitudes would avail nothing, seeing *with God it is nothing to help, whether with many, or with them that have no power*, 2 Chron. 14. 11. Nor fifty, nor five hundred, nor five thousand are able to fetch those whom God will hold back.

*{and he went up to him}* He supposed he was able to fetch him down.

*{and behold he sat on the top of a hill}* So did Samson in a like case, Judg. 15. 11. This hill is supposed to be mount Carmel. See v. 4.

*{and he spake unto him, Thou man of God}* Of this title see 1 King. 13. 1. Elijah was accounted a man of God and an extraordinary prophet by the king and by all sorts of people; and this captain could not be ignorant thereof, yet either too too scornfully or too too imperiously he here gives him this title, *q.d.* Though you be a man of God, yet I will have you down; herein manifesting an arrogant and atheistical disposition, as if he would dare God to do his worst.

*{the king hath said}* By alleging the king’s command he seems to prefer a mortal man before the eternal God.

*{Come down}* For he was on a mount. He expresses the king’s command in a most imperious manner, and so manifests both his own and also the king’s stoutness against God.

**V. 10. ***{And Elijah answered and said to the captain of fifty, If I be a man of God}* The prophet takes the word out of the captain’s mouth, and thereby convinces him of his impiety.

*{then let fire come down}* Fire is one of the terriblest creatures: With it Sodom and Gomorrah were destroyed, Gen. 19. 24. With it the whole world shall be consumed, 2 Pet. 3. 7. To it the torment of hell is resembled, Mark 9. 43. It was by an extraordinary spirit that a man of God should by prayer draw such a judgment upon men. See Luk. 9. 44. Rev. 11. 5. Lev. 10. 2. Num. 16. 35.

*{from heaven}* The lowest heaven is here meant, namely, the air. Coming from the air it came from above, and so from God, Job 1. 16. 1 King. 18. 38.

*{and consume thee and thy fifty}* So Numb. 11. 1. It much aggravated the judgment that none of the company were spared.

*{And there came down fire, &c.}* The judgment was every way answerable to the prayer of the prophet. This shows the efficacy of prayer, Jam. 5. 17. And this was Elijah’s ninth miracle. See 1 King. 17. 1.

**V. 11. ***{Again also he sent unto him another captain of fifty, with his fifty}* Ahaziah was nothing moved with the former judgment, though it were a very terrible one. What can work upon an obdurate heart? He lay on his deathbed, yet he neither feared God, nor cared for the life of his subjects, but exposed one company after another to fearful judgments.

*{and he answered and said unto him, &c.}* The second captain shows himself as impudent and obstinate as the former. Yea, his fault is herein the greater, in that he would not be warned by the former judgment.

*{O man of God, thus hath the king said}* See v. 9.

*{Come down quickly}* This word *quickly* is added over and above the former, implying that he would admit no delay. His obstinacy herein appears to be the greater.

**V. 12. ***{And Elijah answered, &c.}* See v. 10. The judgment on this captain with his fifty was as great as that on the other. Like sins pull down like judgments. This was Elijah’s tenth miracle. See 1 King. 17. 1.

**V. 13. ***{And he sent again, &c.}* This he did as it were in despite of God. Increase of judgments make some men’s hearts harder.

*{and the third captain of fifty went up, and came and fell on his knees}* Heb. *bowed*. In a most humble manner, but with civil reverence, he implores pity.

*{before Elijah}* Had this been divine worship, surely so holy a prophet as Elijah would no more have suffered it than Peter did, Acts 10. 25, 26. Or the angel, Rev. 19. 10. & 22. 9.

*{and besought him}* Herein this third captain dealt prudently and piously; yet he failed in that he did not press the former judgments upon his master the king, so as to have restrained him from sending a third time. But he thought he might by humble supplication prevail more with the prophet than by any admonition or advice with the king.

*{and said unto him, O man of God}* He otherwise uses this title than the other captains did, humbly acknowledging him to be sent of God, and in reverence styling him *man of God*.

*{I pray thee}* Here he shows the humility of his mind and the earnestness of his desire.

*{let my life, and the life of these fifty thy servants}* In respect to the prophet and his holy calling he accounts himself and his soldiers to be his servants.

*{be precious in thy sight}* That you suffer them not as vile things to be cast away, but rather let them be preserved.

**V. 14. ***{Behold, there came fire, &c.}* See v. 10, 12. He expresses the judgments as matters remarkable, acknowledging that the prophet had power so to deal with him and his.

*{therefore let my life, &c.}* See v. 13.

**V. 15. ***{And the Angel of the Lord said unto Elijah}* See v. 3.

*{Go down with him, be not afraid of him}* God encourages his prophet by his wonders and word against all fear. So Jer. 1. 17. Ezek. 2. 6.

*{And he arose}* Being assured of God’s protection he makes no delay.

*{and went down with him unto the king}* They that trust in God fear not the face of an angry king.

**V. 16. ***{And he said unto him}* The prophet said to the king.

*{Thus saith the Lord}* See 1 King. 12. 24. The prophet premises as for his own warrant, so to daunt the king the more.

*{For as much as thou hast sent messengers, &c.}* The very same message that he delivered to the king’s messengers v. 3, 4. he faithfully and boldly delivers to the king’s face, though it were to the king a most terrible message.

*{shalt surely die}* Heb. *dying thou shalt dye*. Though the king enraged by this message at the first thought to slay the prophet, yet now the prophet is before him he neither speaks nor does anything against him. God can turn the stoutest stomachs as he pleases, Prov. 16. 1.

**V. 17. ***{So he died}* Doubtless he was so affrighted with the prophet’s words as it hastened his death.

*{according to the word of the Lord which Elijah had spoken}* v. 6. See 1 King. 13. 5.

*{and Jehoram reigned in his stead}* Jehoram was the son of Ahab, ch. 3. 1. and brother to Ahaziah, who had no son.

*{in the second year of Jehoram the son of Jehoshaphat king of Judah}* The parentage of this latter Jehoram is set down to distinguish him from the former Jehoram, son of Ahab. For both the kings, of Israel, and of Judah, reigning together, were of the same name. Jehoram son of Jehoshaphat was made viceroy over Judah in his father’s stead, when he went out to war with Ahab, 1 King. 22. 2. This was the eighteenth year of Jehoshaphat’s reign. And though Jehoshaphat lived some years after Ahaziah’s death, yet Jehoram successor to Ahaziah is said to begin his reign in the second year of Jehoshaphat’s son. See ch. 3. 1. & 1 King. 22. 42, 51.

**V. 18. ***{Now the rest of the acts of Ahaziah, &c.}* See 1 King. 22. 39.

## CHAPTER 2

**Ver. 1. ***{And it came to pass when the Lord would take up Elijah}* When the time was come that the Lord had appointed for that purpose, and which he had revealed to Elijah, and other prophets, v. 3. Of taking up Elijah see v. 11.

*{into heaven}* Namely, into the highest heavens, where glorious angels and glorified saints are.

*{by a whirlwind}* See on v. 11.

*{that Elijah went with Elisha}* For Elisha followed him as an attendant, 1 King. 19. 21.

*{from Gilgal}* *Gilgal* signifies *a rolling* or *a removing*. The reason of the name is rendered Josh. 5. because there, by circumcising those who were not circumcised in the wilderness their reproach was rolled or taken away. Gilgal was a famous place. There the Israelites first camped when they came out of the wilderness through Jordan; There they were circumcised, and there celebrated their first Passover in Canaan, & manna there first ceased; There was the ark first settled; There the camp of the Israelites continued so long as the wars of Canaan lasted, Josh. 4. 9. & 5. 9, 10. & 9. 6. & 14. 6. There Saul was made king; There sacrifices were offered up; There Agag was hewed in pieces, 1 Sam. 11. 14, 15. & 10. 8. & 15. 21, 33. There David was received again to his kingdom, 2 Sam. 19. 15, 40. There was a college of prophets, about providing for whom two great miracles were wrought, ch. 4. 38, &c. It became afterwards a place of great wickedness, Hos. 9. 15. Amos 4. 4. Elijah now went thither to visit that college of prophets, as Samuel had done before, 1 Sam. 7. 16. See on Hos. 4. 15.

**V. 2. ***{And Elijah said unto Elisha, Tarry here, I pray thee}* Elijah might move Elisha to tarry behind, lest with the sight of his departing from him he should be overmuch grieved, or lest he should divulge abroad what he should see; Mat. 17. 9. Or to try whether he would leave him or no, or to stir up in him a more earnest desire and diligent care of following him, as Luk. 24. 28. that he might be a witness of what was done.

*{for the Lord hath sent me to Bethel}* Of Bethel see 1 King. 12. 29. In Bethel also was a college of prophets, v. 3. Indeed at the division of the tribes in this city one of the golden calves was set up to be worshipped: but it had been won by Abijam before this, 2 Chron. 13. 19. and might thereupon now belong to Judah. Or if the king of Israel held it; yet though he and his people were idolaters, they might suffer prophets of the Lord to have a society there. This society Elijah went to visit before his departure, that he might establish them in the faith; His mind being herein like to Peter’s, 2 Pet. 1. 14, 15.

*{And Elisha said unto him, As the Lord liveth}* See 1 King. 1. 29. He uses this oath the more to manifest his settled resolution to cleave to his master so long as he was on earth.

*{and as thy soul liveth, I will not leave thee}* This is added as a further asseveration, the rather because it was sensibly evident that his soul lived. See 1 Sam. 20. 3. & 25. 26. Herein Elisha showed his grateful faithfulness to his master.

*{So they went down to Bethel}* See more of Bethel on Hos. 10. 15.

**V. 3. ***{And the sons of the Prophets that were at Bethel}* See 1 King. 18. 4.

*{came forth to Elisha, and said unto him}* This they might do in Elijah’s absence.

*{Knowest thou that the Lord will take away thy master}* The Lord had beforehand made known how he meant to take away Elijah.

*{from thy head}* From being over you as an instructor and governor. The chiefer and elder prophets in their colleges sat as doctors, in high seats, over the heads of their disciples, Neh. 8. 5. who sat at their feet, Acts 22. 3.

*{to day}* The very time of Elijah’s assumption was known.

*{And he said, Yea, I know it}* Elisha also was beforehand informed of his master’s departure.

*{hold you your peace}* This is a secret check, *q.d.* I need not be instructed by you herein; therefore trouble me not with any discourse thereabout. He feared lest he should be too long detained by them. Some apply this to the sons of the prophets, and thus translate it, *they held their peace*.

**V. 4. ***{And Elijah said unto Elisha, Tarry here, &c.}* Elijah being to go unto another place, yet makes a further trial of Elisha, whether he might be moved to leave him, or no.

*{for the Lord hath sent me to Jericho}* Jericho was in the tribe of Benjamin, Josh. 18. 21. See on Josh. 6. 26. 1 King. 16. 34. Here also were prophets trained up, as in Gilgal, and Bethel: All these societies Elijah visited before his death.

*{And he said, As the Lord liveth, &c.}* See v. 2.

**V. 5. ***{And the sons of the Prophets, &c.}* See v. 3.

**V. 6. ***{And Elijah said unto him, Tarry, &c.}* Elijah makes the same motion a third time, yet further to try his servant’s constancy. Thus Christ thrice tried Peter’s love, Joh. 21. 15, 16, 17.

*{for the Lord hath sent me to Jordan}* This fourth place was near about that place where Moses was showed all the land of Canaan, Deut. 34. 1.

*{And he said, As the Lord liveth, &c.}* See v. 2.

*{And they two went on}* Elijah perceiving Elisha’s unalterable resolution, tries him no further, but suffers him to go along with him.

**V. 7. ***{And fifty men of the sons of the Prophets}* The number of the young prophets was not small; for there being fifty at the least in one college, how many were there in all the societies!

*{went and stood to view}* Heb. *in sight, or over against*. Namely, in sight of that place where Elijah was to be taken up.

*{afar off}* For they dared not approach too near. This was thus ordered by the divine providence, that there might be many witnesses of the truth of the assumption of Elijah. Thus was Christ taken up while many beheld it. Acts 1. 9.

*{and they two stood by Jordan}* i.e. Elijah and Elisha stood ready to pass over Jordan so soon as way should be made.

**V. 8. ***{And Elijah took his mantle}* This was that upper garment which he used to wear, and whereby he was known, ch. 1. 8. and with which he covered his face, 1 King. 19. 13.

*{and wrapped it together}* That he might the more freely use it.

*{and smote the waters}* A means was used, to show that that great work was done not casually, but by the prophet’s ministry: And so unlikely a means was used, to show that a divine power accompanied the means. So Exod. 14. 21.

*{and they were divided hither and thither}* Some one way, some another. As Exod. 14. 21, 22. & Josh. 3. 16.

*{so that they two went over on dry ground}* This miracle was as great as the dividing of Jordan, Josh. 3. 17. And it was Elijah’s eleventh miracle. See 1 King. 17. 1.

**V. 9. ***{And it came to pass when they were gone over}* They had now no other place to go to, but were near the place where Elijah should be taken up: hereupon the motion following is made.

*{that Elijah said unto Elisha, Ask what}* This offer Elijah made by divine instinct. It is somewhat like that which God made to Solomon, 1 King. 3. 5. Yea, and like that which Christ made to his disciples, Joh. 16. 23.

*{I shall do for thee}* This he says not as having power in himself to do whatsoever should be required of him, but only as God’s instrument and minister, by whom God wrought what he wrought. So Acts 8. 17. Or he may intend no more under that phrase than *what he should crave of God for him*.

*{before I be taken away from thee}* The help which God’s servants do us by prayer, or otherwise, must be sought of them while they live.

*{And Elisha said, I pray thee}* He could not but thankfully accept so gracious a motion, and therefore readily and humbly asked as follows.

*{let a double portion of thy spirit}* Heb. *a mouth (or a portion) of two in thy spirit*. This phrase is oft used for *a double portion* or *two parts*, Deut. 21. 17. Zech. 13. 8. Elisha, being to succeed his master and be head of the prophets, desired that, as the firstborn had a double portion above his brethren, he might have a double portion above the other prophets. If we read it thus, *the double portion of thy spirit*, it will imply no more than such a portion of the spirit as Elijah had. And if the phrase be taken for the doubling of Elijah’s gifts on him, then it implies a holy, earnest, unsatisfied desire of promoting God’s glory, and doing good to God’s people, even above his master that excelled therein. Such a desire is warranted 1 Cor. 12. 31. & 14. 12. Such was the desire of David’s servants in relation to David and Solomon, 1 Kings 1. 37, 47. They who apply it to the miracles of the one or the other, and affirm that Elisha wrought double the miracles that Elijah did, mistake the mark. For if there had been registered but eight miracles of Elijah and sixteen of Elisha, it would not follow that Elisha doubled Elijah’s miracles, seeing Elijah might do many more than are registered, as John 20. 30. But there are at least twelve miracles of Elijah’s recorded, and but twenty of Elisha’s. See 1 King. 17. 1. & v. 14.

*{be upon me}* Be by God conferred on me, and abide in me, to enable me to do what I shall be called unto.

**V. 10. ***{And he said, Thou hast asked an hard thing}* Heb. *Thou hast done hard in asking*. He means it was a very rare and difficult thing which Elisha had required, and such a one as none but God can do. See Dan. 2. 11.

*{nevertheless if thou see me when I am taken from thee}* This Elijah adds as a sign to assure Elisha his desire should be granted. Hereby therefore Elisha’s desire is justified: for if it had not been lawful his master would have reproved him for it, as Christ reproved two of his disciples and their mother for making a request above that which was meet for them to have, Luk. 9. 46, 47. Mar. 10. 37, 38.

*{it shall be so unto thee}* This he spoke by a prophetical spirit, whereby he was assured that God would grant to Elisha such a spirit as he desired.

*{but if not, it shall not be so}* This is added to make Elisha more heedful in observing the manner of Elijah’s departure, that he might be an eyewitness thereof, as the men of Galilee were of Christ’s ascension. Acts 1. 10, 11.

**V. 11. ***{And it came to pass, as they still went on and talked}* Heb. *went going and talking*] This phrase implies continuance in what they did. It is probable that Elijah informed Elisha of such matters as should fall out in Israel after his departure, as Christ a little before his ascension spoke to his apostles, *of the things pertaining to the kingdom of God*, Acts 1. 3.

*{that behold}* A most remarkable matter follows.

*{there appeared a chariot of fire, and horses of fire}* This was a fiery apparition in shape like horses drawing a chariot; as ch. 6. 17. A chariot is a fit means to carry a man from place to place, and horses use to draw chariots; therefore God’s angels that carried up Elijah appeared in such a form. They appeared in fire, for the greater brightness and glory; but it was such a fire as consumed him not, like that which appeared to Moses in the bush, Exod. 3. 2. Fire fitly answered the heroic spirit of Elijah, who was inflamed with a holy zeal. Chariots and horses set forth the ready and speedy ministry of angels, Psal. 68. 17. and fire sets forth their zeal, Psal. 104. 4.

*{and parted them both asunder}* The nearest and dearest in this world cannot always enjoy a mutual communion, but must be parted asunder, as Elijah and Elisha here were.

*{and Elijah went up}* Though there were a chariot and horses, together with a whirlwind to carry him up, yet most willingly did he go with them; *He went up*.

*{by a whirlwind}* A whirlwind is a strong turning wind, which with violence gathers things into it, and carries them up. This carried up the horses and chariot with Elijah in it, and that with some seeming violence, at least with speed.

*{into heaven}* By *heaven* is here meant that which is called the *third heaven*, 2 Cor. 12. 2. that heavenly paradise where the souls of saints departed are, where Christ in his human nature is, Acts 1. 11. and where all believers after the general resurrection shall be with Christ their head in everlasting glory. To this glorious heaven was Elijah now carried in his soul and body both, so as that, like Enoch, Heb. 11. 5. he never died. *He was changed in a moment; His corruptible putting on incorruption, and his mortal putting on immortality*, 1 Cor. 15. 51, 52, 53. This rapture of Elijah was a type of Christ’s ascension, Mar. 16. 19. It also gives evidence, as of the resurrection of our bodies in general, so more particularly of the last rapture of such as shall be living on earth when Christ comes to judgment, 1 Thes. 4. 17. This rapture was an effect of Elijah’s faith, and in that respect may be accounted his twelfth miracle. See 1 King. 17. 1. See Rev. 11. 12.

**V. 12. ***{And Elisha saw it}* He was careful in observing that sign which his master had hinted, v. 10.

*{and he cried}* He earnestly prayed to have the promise of his master accomplished. In this sense is the word *crying* oft used, as Exod. 14. 15. 1 King. 22. 32.

*{My father, my father}* This word *father* is a title of excellency and superiority, yea also of entire affection and special respect. See ch. 5. 13. Elisha uses it here to testify his high esteem of Elijah, and his confidence to obtain what he asked. He doubles the phrase in testimony of his true and hearty acknowledgment. So ch. 13. 14. Of doubling words see 1 King. 8. 13.

*{the chariot of Israel and the horsemen thereof}* He so styles Elijah, both in allusion to the present manner of his triumphant rapture by chariot and horses into heaven, and also in relation to his former course of life, wherein by his prayer, his care to bring men unto the Lord, and other good endeavors he had been a better defense to Israel than chariots and horsemen could be. In this latter respect a king of Israel so styles Elisha, ch. 13. 14.

*{and he saw him no more}* For he was taken out of his sight, and retained in heaven; as Acts 1. 9. Indeed once after this did Elijah with Moses appear on earth, Matt. 17. 3. but that was after Elisha’s days, so as he saw him not.

*{and he took hold of his own clothes}* This particle *own* is expressed, to show that they were not the clothes of Elijah. All his clothes except his mantle, v. 13. were consumed before he entered into heaven, where there is no need of clothes.

*{and rent them in two pieces}* An ancient and usual expression of grief and astonishment, Gen. 37. 29, 34. See 1 King. 21. 27.

**V. 13. ***{He took up also the mantle of Elijah}* This he took up to keep it in memory of his master. By it also he wrought a miracle, v. 14. See on 1 King. 19. 13.

*{that fell from him}* This was to give a visible evidence that there is no use of clothing in heaven, & to show that he was to be no longer a prophet on earth, but that Elisha was to succeed him, and had this left him as a sign of Elijah’s spirit in him. And it is probable that Elisha wore this mantle.

*{and went back}* Namely to the places whence he with his master had come before.

*{and stood by the bank}* Heb. *lip*. For the banks of a river are as the lips to the mouth, which keep in the moisture.

*{of Jordan}* This was the last place from which Elisha with his master had come: and he must pass over this river before he could go to the other places where the colleges of prophets were.

**V. 14. ***{And he took the mantle of Elijah that fell from him}* This was the instrument his master had used before, v. 8. therefore he again uses the same.

*{and smote the waters}* In imitation of his master; and so to try whether his desire of having Elijah’s spirit were granted or no.

*{and said, Where is the Lord God of Elijah?}* That God which wrought wonders by Elijah. This he says to show his desire that God would now work by him, as he had before wrought by Elijah. These therefore are not words of diffidence, but of fervent prayer, and of earnest desire to have an experimental proof of his master’s promise, yea, and of his own faith therein.

*{And when he also}* As well as his master, v. 8.

*{had smitten the waters}* With his master’s mantle.

*{they parted hither and thither}* See v. 8. This is the first miracle that Elisha wrought: Sundry others are mentioned hereafter, in all about twenty. See v. 22. 24. & 3. 20. & 4. 6, 17, 36, 41, 44. & 5. 14, 27. & 6. 6, 10, 13, 17, 18, 20. & 7. 6, 18. & 13. 21.

*{and Elisha went over}* That evidence which he had of God’s power in dividing the waters made him confident that he might safely go through Jordan on dry land.

**V. 15. ***{And when the sons of the Prophets}* See v. 3.

*{which were to view at Jericho, saw him}* v. 7. Jericho was not far from Jordan, so as they might soon see what was done. Besides, they knew that Elisha had gone over with his master, and therefore seeing him on this side, they must needs know that he came through Jordan.

*{they said, The spirit of Elijah}* Not that Elijah was the author or giver of that spirit; but that such power of God’s Spirit as was manifested in and by the ministry of Elijah, was now in Elisha. Like miracles argue a like spirit.

*{doth rest on Elisha}* Is bestowed on him, and does abide in him.

*{And they came to meet him}* To congratulate the gift bestowed upon him, and to accept him for their master.

*{and bowed themselves to the ground before him}* This they did in a civil manner, to evidence their acknowledging him to be their father and master. See ch. 4. 37. If it were divine, then was this worship performed to God, as 1 Cor. 14. 25.

**V. 16. ***{And they said unto him}* Hereby also they acknowledged his authority over them, in that they would not do what they thought meet to be done without leave from him.

*{Behold now, there be with thy servants}* This title is an express note of subjection to him.

*{fifty strong men}* Heb. *sons of strength*. Not so much for war and to oppose enemies as for labor, travel and endurance.

*{let them go, we pray thee, and seek}* They thought Elijah might be carried from place to place, as 1 King. 18. 12.

*{thy master}* This has reference to that relation that was betwixt Elijah and Elisha. Or they might think he was yet living on earth, and still retained the dignity which formerly he had.

*{lest peradventure the Spirit of the Lord hath taken him up}* See 1 King. 18. 12.

*{and cast him upon some mountain}* Heb. *one of the mountains*. Mountains were least inhabited, and were desolate places; so as without sending to seek him there, there would be none to bring word whether he were there or no.

*{or into some valley}* This also is to be taken of a desolate place.

*{And he said, Ye shall not send}* He knew that Elijah’s body and soul being both in heaven, all their labor in seeking him would be in vain.

**V. 17. ***{And when they urged him}* They were so importunate as they would take no nay.

*{till he was ashamed}* Either that himself should seem inexorable, or that they without cause should be so importunate.

*{he said, Send}* He yielded to them, that they might by experience see their error, and be more fully assured of Elijah’s ascent into heaven. On such grounds did John send his disciples unto Jesus, to know whether he were he that should come. Yea, further he yielded to them, that he might not seem careless of his master’s good, or ambitious of his place.

**V. 18. ***{And when they came again to him}* After they had discerned their error, and found their labor to be in vain, they returned to him. This is spoken of the fifty men sent.

*{for he tarried at Jericho}* Till the fifty men returned, that so finding their labor lost, he might have the more evidence to convince and upbraid their folly, as he does in the words following.

*{he said unto them, Did I not say unto you, Go not}* See the like Luk. 24. 5.

**V. 19. ***{And the men of the city}* The inhabitants of Jericho are here meant.

*{said unto Elisha}* This they did, either to try whether he had such a spirit as Elijah had, or in confidence that Elisha could do what he did.

*{Behold I pray thee}* See v. 11.

*{the situation of this city is pleasant}* The air good, the pastures fair, and the place every way delightsome for habitation.

*{as my lord seeth}* They speak as a body or society, in the singular number, and give him a title of authority over them, *my lord*, and withal make him a witness of the truth of that they said; all, the more to move him to grant their desire.

*{but the water is naught}* Or, *unwholesome*. See Numb. 13. 20. The waters were not so by nature, nor in former times, when Israel came first into those countries; but by a special judgment, and that (as it is probable) upon Hiel’s presumptuous sin in building Jericho, 1 King. 16. 34. For God can, and oft does, *turn a fruitfull land into barrennesse for the wickednesse of them that dwel therein*. Psal. 107. 34. *and make the rain of a land powder and dust*, Deut. 28. 24. See on Josh. 16. 1.

*{and the ground barren}* Heb. *causing to miscarry*. That ground is said to miscarry whose fruit grows not to maturity, or is noxious, so as many perish thereon. That which was before said of the unwholesomeness of the waters may also be applied to the barrenness of the earth.

**V. 20. ***{And he said}* Elisha was ready to exercise the power of that spirit which God had given him.

*{Bring me}* He used them as ministers to prepare the means he intended to use, that so they might the better discern the truth of the miracle, being eyewitnesses thereof. So did Christ, Joh. 2. 7.

*{a new cruse}* He would have it new, that it might be manifest there was not anything before in the cruse that might have any virtue to effect such a work.

*{and put salt therein}* This may seem a most unlikely means. For salt of itself rather causes barrenness than fruitfulness. See Judg. 9. 45.

*{And they brought it unto him}* This shows both their readiness to do what they could for redressing the evil they complained of, and also their faith in that power which God had given to Elisha.

**V. 21. ***{And he went forth unto the spring of the waters}* Herein he showed his wisdom, in cleansing the springhead; for so the streams also would become wholesome.

*{and cast the salt in there}* Being by God’s Spirit directed to use this means, though unlikely, yet in faith he uses it.

*{and said, Thus saith the Lord}* He would not arrogate to himself the power of healing water and land, but ascribes it to the Lord.

*{I have healed these waters}* God makes the means effectual which he directs his servants to use.

*{there shall not be from thence any more death, or barren land}* The waters and land were made useful, not only for the present, but also for the future. This word *death* implies that the waters were not only bitter but poisonous; and the land destructive to the fruits thereof.

**V. 22. ***{So the waters were healed unto this day}* This *day* has reference to the time when this history was written, which was many years after this fact. See 1 King. 9. 13.

*{according to the saying of Elisha which he spake}* This shows this miracle was wrought by the ministry of Elisha. It is his second miracle. See v. 14.

**V. 23. ***{And he went up from thence unto Bethel}* Here was a college of prophets, v. 2. to which he went, 1. to visit it, 2. to assume his government there, 3. to settle orders.

*{and as he was going up by the way}* Not doing aught to provoke any.

*{there came forth little children out of the city}* These questionless were the children of idolaters that lived in Bethel, where one of the calves was set up, 1 King. 12. 29.

*{and mocked him}* This they did in regard not only of his profession, who acknowledged himself a worshipper of the Lord, but also of his function, being a prophet of the Lord, yea further, of that extraordinary spirit wherewith God had endowed him, which was made famous throughout all Israel. The apostle accounts such mocking *persecution*. Compare Gen. 21. 9. with Gal. 4. 29. For such a sin Ishmael was cast out of Abraham’s family, Gen. 21. 18. and Cham cursed by his father, Gen. 9. 25.

*{and said unto him}* The particular wherein they mocked him is here intended.

*{Go up}* This is the same word that is used v. 11. of Elijah’s going up into heaven. It properly signifies to *ascend*. Now as Elijah ascended, they say to him that had the spirit of Elijah, *ascend you*. It is a kind of blasphemous scoff, whereby they make a scorn of that wonderful work of God, as if it had been a mere illusion; and withal scoff at the spirit which Elisha had, as if he looked for the like translation: but they think it an impossibility, *q.d.* Go up you, if you can, into heaven, as your master pretended to do.

*{thou bald head}* The most pious, prudent and every way excellent and profitable men may be baldheaded: but the wicked are ready to take occasion of mocking where there is no just cause.

*{go up thou bald head}* They repeat their scoff, the more to vex the prophet. Repetitions of evils are great aggravations.

**V. 24. ***{And he turned back}* In detestation of such impiety.

*{and looked on them}* As with the eyes of his body, so with a prophetical spirit; to show that what he did he did advisedly, and well considered both the persons and the cause.

*{and cursed them}* He prayed to God to execute a just judgment upon them, and by his prophetical spirit declared that God would do so.

*{in the name of the Lord}* By divine instinct, and by commission from the Lord; not out of a revengeful mind in his own cause, but to the glory of God, and withal, calling upon him. See 2 Chron. 14. 11. The contempt of his sacred ministry thus stirred up his spirit. The sin was great in itself, but greater in regard of the person against whom it was committed, Numb. 12. and in regard of the occasion, which was only a natural infirmity or defect; but most of all as it had relation to that extraordinary work of God, v. 11. Though Elisha herein imitated his master who called for fire upon impious persons, ch. 1. 10. yet is not this for our imitation; for both the one and the other did what they did by an extraordinary spirit. See Luk. 9. 55.

*{and there came forth}* This was directly ordered by divine providence, as that which is noted 1 King. 13. 24.

*{two she bears out of the wood}* As lions abounded in those countries, so likewise did bears, 1 Sam. 17. 37. These used to lie in wait to catch a prey, Lam. 3. 10. And she-bears were the most fierce, 2 Sam. 17. 8. Prov. 17. 12. Hos. 13. 8. God therefore uses such here.

*{and tare forty and two children of them}* Such as had learned to scoff at a prophet, deserved justly to be punished. Their childhood exempted them not from punishment, because it restrained them not from sin. The number of two and forty shows that many were gathered together to practice this impiety; And it may be they were put on thereto by their idolatrous parents; and so the parents were justly punished by the loss of their children. This coming forth of the bears and destroying the children upon Elisha’s cursing them was miraculous, and his third miracle. See v. 14.

**V. 25. ***{And he went from thence}* Neither the parents of those children nor any other of the idolaters there living dared attempt any revenge against Elisha. For this sudden destruction much affrighted them, as Exod. 12. 29, &c. Besides, the hearts of all men are in God’s hand, to restrain them as it pleases him. See ch. 16. 1.

*{to mount Carmel}* See 1 King. 18. 19. This was a place whither Elijah had oft recourse, 1 King. 18. 42. & it may be had had a habitation there, and therefore Elisha went thither. Or being a retired place, he might give notice to the children of the prophets to come to him thither, that there he might more freely and securely confer with them.

*{and from thence he returned to Samaria}* Elijah was at Samaria when he declared to Ahaziah his death, ch. 1. 2. 15. and questionless Elisha was with him, and in that respect he is said to return thither. It being a populous city, Elisha had opportunity of doing more good there.

## CHAPTER 3

**Ver. 1. ***{Now Jehoram the son of Ahab}* Ahaziah was the eldest son of Ahab and succeeded his father immediately; but died without children, and left this Jehoram his brother to succeed him.

*{began to reign}* Heb. *reigned*.

*{over Israel in Samaria}* See 1 King. 16. 29.

*{the eighteenth year of Jehoshaphat King of Judah}* Jehoram the son of Jehoshaphat was then viceroy: whence it is said, ch. 1. 17. that *this Jehoram reigned in the second yeer of the other Jehoram King of Judah*. When Jehoshaphat went with Ahab to fight against the Syrians 1 King. 22. 2, &c. he left Jehoram his son to govern the kingdom; but at his return he resumed the scepter, and so is here styled *king of Judah*. It is certain that Jehoram was a most wicked man, 2 Chron. 21. 6, &c. but whether Jehoshaphat took the power again into his hand upon his son’s ill government, or no, is uncertain. It is most probable that being so able to rule as he was, he substituted him to reign only during his absence.

*{and reigned twelve years}* From the death of his brother, ch. 1. 17. to his own, ch. 8. 24.

**V. 2. ***{And he wrought evil}* See v. 3. & ch. 6. 31. & 9. 22.

*{in the sight of the Lord}* See 1 King. 21. 20.

*{but not like his father}* i.e. Ahab, who was the worst of kings, 1 King. 16. 33. and sold himself to do wickedly, 1 Kings 21. 25.

*{and like his mother}* Jezebel. See ch. 9. 22. 1 King. 16. 31.

*{for he put away the image of Baal}* Heb. *statue*. Either there was some eminent statue of Baal made by Ahab, as Dan. 3. 1. or here the singular number is put synecdochically for the plural; all, whatsoever, statues of Baal Jehoram demolished. Of Ahab’s worshipping Baal see 1 King. 16. 31. Baal was preferred before the Lord, but Elijah brought the people to see their gross error, 1 King. 18. 39. Yet both king and people it seems had returned to that detestable idolatry again, as the dog to his vomit, and sow to the mire, 2 Pet. 2. 22. Jehoram therefore put away the image of Baal.

*{that his father had made}* There was great need of a reformation, seeing those idols which his father had made still continued.

**V. 3. ***{Nevertheless he cleaved unto the sins of Jeroboam, &c.}* See 1 King. 16. 19. They who are zealous in reforming some things, may retain other abominations: which makes them odious in God’s sight, and takes away the glory of their partial reformation. See ch. 10. 28, 29, 31. These two phrases, *cleaved to*, and *departed not from*, imply a firm resolution and an obstinate persisting.

**V. 4. ***{And Mesha King of Moab}* *Mesha* is an ancient Hebrew name, and signifies *salvation*. It was given to a city, Gen. 10. 30. to one of the sons of Caleb, 1 Chr. 2. 42. and Shaharaim of the tribe of Benjamin begat a son in the country of Moab, and called him *Mesha*, 1 Chr. 8. 8, 9; And it may be thence the Moabites had the name.

*{was a sheep-master}* A possessor of many sheep. His country abounded with that kind of cattle, Isa. 16. 1.

*{and rendered}* By way of tribute every year.

*{unto the King of Israel}* After the revolt of the ten tribes the kings of Israel held that jurisdiction over Moab which David had got, 2 Sam. 8. 2. For Moab was on the other side Jordan, and coasted upon the kingdom of Israel, Numb. 21. 13. See ch. 1. 1.

*{an hundred thousand lambs}* Under this word *lambs* are comprised their dams also; so as he sent sheep, young and old.

*{and an hundred thousand rams}* These were also young and old.

*{with the wool}* When their wool was best grown, and before they were shorn. This was the best time, and then they were most worth.

**V. 5. ***{But it came to pass when Ahab was dead}* Ahab was a valiant man, and had great armies, and so kept under those whom he found subdued by his predecessors.

*{that the King of Moab rebelled against the King of Israel}* See ch. 1. 1. Ahaziah having bruised his body with a fall anon after he came to the crown, ch. 1. 2. could not well manage the affairs of his kingdom; and it may be the Moabites took an occasion from thence to rebel. But Ahaziah was worse than his father Ahab: for he did not only worship Baal in Israel, 1 King. 22. 53. but also sent to Baal-zebub the god of Ekron. God therefore left him: And this was the most proper cause of Moab’s rebellion.

**V. 6. ***{And King Jehoram went out of Samaria}* He went out of the city, where his palace was, into the country, hither and thither throughout all his tribes to raise soldiers, that he might repair the damage his brother had suffered.

*{the same time}* Heb. *in that day*. Moab began to rebel in Ahaziah’s time; but so soon as Jehoram came to the crown, which was in that very year, he sought to reduce them.

*{and numbered all Israel}* All that were fit for war. Of these he took a view, as 2 Sam. 24. 1. or he mustered them.

**V. 7. ***{And he went and sent to Jehoshaphat King of Judah}* Jehoshaphat was by marriage Jehoram’s uncle, ch. 8. 18. by reason of which affinity there was the greater friendship betwixt them. This moved the one to send to the other for aid in time of need.

*{saying, the king of Moab hath rebelled against me}* This was a just cause of sending to Jehoshaphat for aid. For Moab was a common enemy both to Israel and Judah, and had before this joined with Ammon and Edom against Jehoshaphat. See 2 Chr. 20. 1. 35.

*{wilt thou go with me against Moab to battle?}* This is the sum of that ambassage which Jehoram sent to Jehoshaphat.

*{and he said, I will go up}* This was the sum of Jehoshaphat’s answer. And it may seem strange that he should so readily yield to the king of Israel, having before been reproved from the Lord for joining with Ahab his father, 2 Chr. 19. 2. and also punished by the Lord for joining with Ahaziah his brother, 2 Chr. 20. 35, 36, 37. But certainly he conceived Jehoram better than either his father or his brother, seeing he had put away the image of Baal, v. 2. and so given some hope that he might proceed to a further reformation.

*{I am as thou art, &c.}* See 1 King. 22. 4.

**V. 8. ***{And he said, Which way shall we go up?}* This question Jehoram propounds to Jehoshaphat after they were met together with their armies, being ready to be ordered by his advice.

*{And he answered, The way through the wilderness of Edom}* It appears v. 6. that Jehoram had procured the king of Edom to take part with him, and in that respect, this advice is the rather given: for they had thereby assurance that they might safely go through the land of Edom, and so unexpectedly set upon the Moabites.

**V. 9. ***{So the king of Israel went, and the king of Judah}* Jehoram having made himself strong with the aid of two other kingdoms besides his own, went on confidently against the enemy.

*{and the king of Edom}* He was only a deputy there, 1 King. 22. 47. but is called *king*, because he was supreme governor in Edom under the king of Judah.

*{and they fetched a compass}* About the wilderness of Edom. They went not the direct way to Moab. See v. 8.

*{of seven days journey}* By that which follows it appears that they were all these days without water.

*{and there was no water for the host}* Some lay the blame on the commanders, that led the army through a wilderness; but it was their sin that brought such a heavy judgment on them. And in particular, God would hereby discover to Jehoshaphat his sin, in taking part with such kings without consulting with the Lord.

*{and for the cattle that followed them}* Heb. *at their feet*. Under the word *cattle* are comprised the horses on which they rode, and all manner of beasts which being provided for their food were driven after the army. This much aggravates the judgment.

**V. 10. ***{And the king of Israel said}* This king having persuaded the other kings to go along with him, he it was especially that brought so great an army into so great a distress.

*{Alas}* A word of much grief, fear and astonishment, Numb. 12. 11. Josh. 7. 7. Judg. 11. 35. Joel 1. 15.

*{that the Lord hath called}* He impiously lays the blame of their distress upon the Lord, as ch. 6. 33. See v. 9.

*{these three kings together}* This he adds as a further aggravation of the judgment; that not he alone was in danger, but two other, and those not private persons, but kings, on whom many depended; *Three kings* with their armies. Hereby he impeaches the divine providence, as if the Lord had purposely sought the ruin of three kingdoms.

*{to deliver them into the hand of Moab}* For through want of water the armies were so enfeebled as they could neither go on in their intended course, nor return home again; so as they must needs be made a prey to the king of Moab.

**V. 11. ***{But Jehoshaphat said}* Though this pious king much failed in joining with these wicked ones, yet by this judgment he is now made wiser.

*{Is there not here a Prophet of the Lord}* This was his prudence in this great strait, to seek advice from a prophet of the Lord: but herein he condemns his former neglect, as 1 King. 22. 7.

*{that we may enquire of the Lord by him}* This was the end of his asking for a prophet. He knew that the Lord made known his mind to his people by his prophets, Amos 3. 7.

*{And one of the king of Israels servants answered and said}* It seems some also of the king’s court were well acquainted with the courses of the prophets, 1 King. 18. 7, 12.

*{Here is Elisha the son of Shaphat}* See 1 King. 19. 16. Elisha either followed the army, or was in some place near it, which this man was privy to: else he could not say, *Here is Elisha*.

*{which poured water on the hands of Elijah}* This being an ordinary service which servants used to perform to them on whom they attended, it implies that Elisha ministered unto Elijah as his household servant. Therefore was he called Elisha’s master, ch. 2. 3.

**V. 12. ***{And Jehoshaphat said, The word of the Lord is with him}* Jehoshaphat supposed that Elijah being taken away, this his servant succeeded him in his prophetical function, and had his spirit, as ch. 1. 16. 1 King. 18. 18. & 21. 29. And it is probable that he also had heard of some of Elisha’s famous acts.

*{So the king of Israel and Jehoshaphat, and the king of Edom}* Kings in their distress can go to the Lord’s prophets. See ch. 5. 9.

*{went down to him}* They did not send for him, but went themselves to him. A note of great reverence.

**V. 13. ***{And Elisha said unto the king of Israel}* The king of Israel was generalissimo, and he who had gathered the rest together, and against whom the Lord had the greatest quarrel, therefore the prophet first directs his speech to him.

*{What have I to do with thee?}* Heb. *what to thee and me?* So Joh. 2. 4. *q.d.* You are an idolater, and I a prophet of the Lord; how is it therefore that you come to me? or how can you think that the Lord should answer you by me? This savors of the spirit of Elijah.

*{Get thee to the prophets of thy father, and to the prophets of thy mother}* Of these see 1 King. 18. 19. It may be those idolatrous prophets also followed the camp. However they were still tolerated in Israel. Hereupon Elisha upbraids unto the king his toleration of such prophets as could give him no safe counsel.

*{And the king of Israel said unto him}* He could not justify himself in that matter, and therefore again pleads his present distress.

*{Nay}* Upbraid not, I pray you, this my fault.

*{for the Lord hath called these three kings together}* Here he pleads favor, if not for himself alone, yet for the two other kings joined with him. And he acknowledges the judgment to be of the Lord, that the prophet might be moved to seek help & mercy from the Lord. Thus this phrase is somewhat otherwise to be taken than it was v. 10.

*{to deliver them into the hand of Moab}* His guilty conscience made him misdoubt the worst. See v. 10.

**V. 14. ***{And Elisha said, As the Lord of hosts liveth before whom I stand}* See 1 King. 17. 1.

*{Surely were it not that I regard the presence of Jehoshaphat the king of Judah}* Great was the piety and integrity of Jehoshaphat, 2 Chr. 17. 3, 4, &c. & 19. 3, &c. and this was it which moved this faithful prophet to regard him. It was not the dignity of kings, nor the number of three, but the goodness of one of the three, which most wrought upon Elisha.

*{I would not look toward thee, nor see thee}* Words of a high strain, if not of contempt, towards a king. But they were the words of a prophet that had the Spirit of the Lord in him, and that in a measure more than ordinary.

**V. 15. ***{But now bring me a minstrel}* The Hebrew word signifies any kind of musician, even one skillful in singing or playing on instruments. Prophets used to have such, 1 Sam. 10. 5. to quicken their spirits and rouse them up from dullness and heaviness. And Elisha’s spirit at this time was somewhat heavy, by reason of the idolatry and other wickednesses of the people among whom he was. The minstrel or musician here intended might be some Levite skillful in singing divine psalms, and playing them on a lute, harp, or other like instrument. When Isaac would bless his son, he desired to cheer up his spirit with savory meat and wine before he went about it, Gen. 27. 4, 25. It may be also that Elisha called for music to cheer those that were present, that they might attend his prophecy the better. Thus the Jews had music at their sacrifices, to quicken the people to prayer and praises, 2 Chron. 7. 6. & 29. 25, 26.

*{And it came to pass when the minstrel played}* By the means he used his spirit was quickened. So was Saul refreshed by the like, 1 Sam. 16. 23.

*{that the hand of the Lord came upon him}* A divine motion of God’s Spirit stirred him up to prophesy as he did. See 1 King. 18. 46. The gift of prophecy is from God. See ch. 4. 27.

**V. 16. ***{And he said, Thus saith the Lord}* Elisha being quickened in spirit readily reveals the mind of the Lord made known to him.

*{Make this valley full of ditches}* Heb. *Ditches, ditches*. Of doubling the word see 1 King. 8. 13. Here it imports very many ditches. See ch. 4. 3. He bids them make these ditches, to try their obedience, and to prepare means to hold water for their use. Besides, this making of new and dry ditches did more manifest the miracle, as 1 King. 18. 34, 35.

**V. 17. ***{For thus saith the Lord}* See ch. 2. 21. & 1 King. 12. 24. & 20. 13.

*{Ye shall not see wind, neither shall ye see rain}* The wind is an ordinary means of gathering clouds together, and causing them to shower down rain; but here is neither any means of rain seen, nor rain itself falling from heaven.

*{yet that valley shall be filled with water}* God can provide in abundance what is needful without means, so there was no ordinary means to cause fire, 1 King. 18. 38.

*{that ye may drink}* God’s provision is for man’s need.

*{both ye, and your cattle, and your beasts}* Providence extends itself to man and beast, Psal. 36. 6.

**V. 18. ***{And this is but a light thing in the sight of the Lord}* See ver. 9. Though the benefit were very great, yet to the Lord it seemed but small, because he intended a far greater blessing. God’s bounty oft extends to more than is desired, 1 King. 3. 13.

*{he will deliver the Moabites also into your hand}* This was the greatest thing that then they desired, and the main end of their gathering together.

**V. 19. ***{And ye shall smite every fenced city}* i.e. cities of war, with thick walls and strong bulwarks.

*{and every choice city}* Such as had fair edifices, and whither people much resorted on sundry occasions.

*{and shall fell every good tree}* This may seem against the law, Deut. 20. 19. But that inhibition was in regard of those countries they should subdue for their own habitation and use. Or, this was an extraordinary charge for this time.

*{and stop all wells of water}* A very great damage Gen. 25. 15.

*{and mar}* Heb. *grieve*. An elegant metaphor, whereby affections of reasonable men are attributed to the senseless earth.

*{every good piece of land with stones}* Hereby the land was made unuseful for tillage or pasture. God would have the Moabites so spoiled every way as they might not dare to rebel again. Whereas it is said, *Ye shall smite, &c.* it is to be taken as a prediction, a promise, and a command too, as ch. 8. 12. 1 Sam. 13. 3. 1 Kin. 20. 28.

**V. 20. ***{And it came to pass in the morning when the meat offering was offered}* At the time for the ordinary morning sacrifice, Ex. 29. 39. Then the true and faithful ministers of the Lord were at their devotion. We read of a like at the evening sacrifice, 1 King. 18. 36. See Dan. 9. 21. Acts 2. 15.

*{that behold}* See ch. 2. 11.

*{there came water by the way of Edom}* They were in the wilderness of Edom, v. 8. and along from the country of Edom they see water running down to this wilderness; but they knew no springhead, nor river, nor any such like means, from whence it should come. This is Elisha’s fourth miracle. See chap. 2. 14.

*{and the country was filled with water}* It did more than fill the ditches; for it lay abroad in the valley thereabouts.

**V. 21. ***{And when all the Moabites heard that the Kings were come up to fight against them}* So famous a matter as the joining together of three armies out of three kingdoms could not but quickly be divulged far and near.

*{they gathered}* Heb. *were cryed together*. By proclamation, sound of trumpets and beat of drums they called together.

*{all that were able to put on armour, and upward}* Heb. *gird himself with a girdle*. They were wont with a warlike belt to gird their armor close together; whence the apostle takes his metaphor; Eph. 6. 14. The meaning here is, that all of all sorts, both younger and elder, such as never before went to war, and old experienced soldiers, were gathered together, as Josh. 8. 3.

*{and stood in the border}* In the utmost coast of their own land; For theirs was a defensive war.

**V. 22. ***{And they rose up early in the morning}* Probably to see whether the enemy were near them, or no.

*{and the Sun shone upon the water}* i.e. at its first rising.

*{And the Moabites saw the water on the other side}* i.e. over against them.

*{as red as blood}* There use to rise thick vapors out of waters which the sun at its rising or setting cannot dispel; but shining weakly through them, it makes the waters appear red as blood.

**V. 23. ***{And they said, This is blood}* They knew not how water could come into that dry wilderness, seeing no clouds had gathered in the sky, nor rain fallen from thence, v. 17.

*{the Kings are surely slain}* Heb. *by destroying are destroyed*. See 1 King. 8. 13. By *kings* are meant themselves and their armies.

*{and they have smitten one another}* Heb. *a man his neighbor*. Their army was so destroyed, 2 Chr. 20. 23. they might therefore think that others might do the like.

*{Now therefore, Moab, to the spoil}* That conceit of the Israelites destroying one another exceedingly deceived them, and made them go beyond their own borders; otherwise haply they might have continued safe, and stood against their enemies. But by this incitation of one another to the spoil, they rush upon the foe to their own great disadvantage.

**V. 24. ***{And when they came to the camp of Israel}* Because the quarrel was Israel’s, and the king of Israel got the men of Judah and Edom to join with him, they all are comprised under this title *Israel*.

*{the Israelites rose up and smote the Moabites}* It is probable the Moabites came in no good rank, but disorderly, and thereupon the Israelites got the greater advantage.

*{so that they fled before them}* The Moabites finding themselves deceived were soon put to the worst.

*{but they went forward smiting}* Or, *they smote in it, even smiting*.

*{the Moabites even in their country}* The Israelites having put the Moabites to flight, pursued the victory, and slew them everywhere, in the fields, and in the cities.

**V. 25. ***{And they beat down the Cities, &c.}* Here is an accomplishment of all that was foretold and enjoined, v. 19.

*{only in Kirharaseth left they the stones thereof}* Heb. *until he left the stones thereof in Kirharaseth*. Kirharaseth was the chiefest city in Moab; so called by reason of the workmanship and strength of the walls. See on Isa. 16. 7. By *the stones thereof* is meant the strong stone wall wherewith it was fenced. This the enemy could not demolish.

*{howbeit the slingers went about it}* Engineers, who with violence forced stones against the wall out of their engines, as now gunners shoot bullets.

*{and smote it}* Or, *battered it*. The king of Moab with so many of his soldiers as were left got into this city, and so defended the same as the Israelites could not surprise it, as they had done other cities in Moab. Yet they endeavored everywhere to batter and beat down the walls thereof.

**V. 26. ***{And when the King of Moab saw that the battle was too sore for him}* He observed the violence of the assailants to be so great as he feared he should not be able long to stand out, but that the enemy would enter his city and seize upon him.

*{he took with him seven hundred men that drew swords}* Valiant and well exercised soldiers.

*{to break through even unto the King of Edom}* His purpose was to have charged through the king of Edom’s troops, supposing that they had been the weakest, and that he might soonest have prevailed against them.

*{but they could not}* He found Edom’s quarters better manned and stronger set than he imagined, so as he was forced back again into his city.

**V. 27. ***{Then he took his eldest son}* This relative *his* may have reference either to the king of Moab or to the king of Edom; both being mentioned in the verse before. If to the former, then the king of Moab offered his own son and heir unto his god, to move him by such an extraordinary sacrifice to help him in that desperate case. A folly worse than that of the Baalites, 1 King. 18. 28. If it have reference to the king of Edom, then the king of Moab having gotten his son into his hands, in a spiteful revenge thus offered him up, Amos chap. 2. vers. 1. Of sacrificing children, see chap. 16. 3.

*{that should have reigned in his stead}* This is added to aggravate the heinousness of the fact.

*{and offered him for a burnt offering upon the wall}* So as the enemies might see it. He might hope the enemy would raise the siege upon the beholding this his resolute act.

*{and there was great indignation against Israel}* Of the king of Edom and his soldiers; which might easily be, if he that was sacrificed was the king of Edom’s son. Or, if he was the king of Moab’s son, both Edom and Judah might be much grieved against Israel, who was the cause thereof. Or there might be great indignation and vexation of spirit among the Israelites themselves by reason of that desperate fact.

*{and they departed from him}* They raised the siege and marched away, much troubled at that lamentable spectacle.

*{and returned to their own land}* Every king with his soldiers returned to his own country; so as they put an end to that battle. The Moabites might hence gather that their god was pleased with that abominable sacrifice, in that the enemies raised their siege thereupon, and went clean away. But they who are acquainted with God’s Word know that his mind is not always manifested by events. A prosperous event does not simply justify, nor a cross one condemn an action.

## CHAPTER 4

**Ver. 1. ***{Now}* Heb. *And*. This copulative knits together the histories of Elisha. Where this was performed, is not set down; but the mention of *neighbors* v. 3. implies that it was in Samaria or some other city or town. Here is recorded the fifth miracle of Elisha. See chap. 2. 14.

*{there cried}* Crying has reference to the extension of heart, or of voice, or of both. In regard of the heart, it has reference to God, who alone knows the inward disposition of the heart. See Exod. 14. 15. In regard of voice, it has reference to man, who only can hear the voice. It may have relation to God in regard of both, Psal. 77. 1. & 88. 1. and to man too as here in this place.

*{a certain woman of the wives}* One that had been a wife. For prophets and other ministers of God had wives, and so had apostles also, 1 Cor. 9. 5. though Papists now deny that liberty to their priests.

*{of the sons of the Prophets}* See 1 King. 20. 22.

*{unto Elisha, saying}* She knew that Elisha was a prophet of the Lord, and was persuaded that he could give her advice what to do in her present distress: Besides she had heard of great miracles done by him, and thereupon hoped the more confidently to receive succor from him.

*{Thy servant my husband is dead}* Elisha was a head over the sons of the prophets, and therefore they were styled his *servants*, ch. 2. 16. Besides, this word servant is a title of reverence and dutiful respect, and so here used.

*{and thou knowest}* It seems her husband was in his lifetime well known to Elisha, and so he could well judge of the truth of what she does here testify concerning his integrity.

*{that thy servant did fear the Lord}* Under this phrase of *fearing God*, due piety and other graces beseeming his profession are comprised.

*{and the creditor}* One in whose debt the foresaid prophet died, so as that he came to exact it of the widow and her children. By this it appears that one who fears God may be in debt, and die in debt. For many occasions may fall out to disable a conscionable man from paying what he ought to pay, and would pay if he could.

*{is come to take unto him my two sons to be bondmen}* When there were no goods to discharge a due debt, the custom was to take the debtor himself, if living, or, in case he were dead, his children, and either to use them, or sell them for bondslaves. See Mat. 18. 25. & Lev. 25. 39. Yet the law expressly forbade the Jews to compel their brethren to bond service. Here are four motives which this woman uses to press Elisha to succor her: 1. Her own condition; she was a widow: 2. Her husband’s function; he was a son of the prophets: 3. The kind of debt; it was contracted by her husband in his lifetime: 4. The danger wherein her children were fallen thereby.

**V. 2. ***{And Elisha said unto her}* His answer shows his readiness to do what he could for her.

*{What shall I do for thee?}* He intimates that he himself had not wherewith to discharge her debt. The speech is somewhat like that of the king of Israel, chap. 6. 26, 27. *Whence shall I help thee, &c.* or that of the apostle, Acts 3. 6. *Silver and gold have I none*.

*{Tell me, what hast thou in the house?}* This question he propounds to be informed whether she had anything by her that was fit to be sold, wherewith to discharge the debt. It is just that debts be paid out of that which the debtor has, if it be not of absolute necessity for preserving life. What can be spared must be let go to pay a just debt, especially to save children from being bondservants. The two interrogations were added one to another, the more to prepare the woman with confidence to expect some relief, as v. 14. See ch. 2. 9. Acts 3. 4.

*{And she said, Thine handmaid}* As she testified reverence in relation to the prophet and her husband, v. 1. so she also does here in her own case. See 1 King. 1. 13.

*{hath not any thing in the house}* She meant such things as might be sold, as jewels, plate, corn, &c. For questionless she had bedding for herself and sons, and such other necessaries. But this shows that she was very poor.

*{save a pot of oil}* Her case in this kind was little better than the poor widow’s 1 King. 17. 12. Oil was among the Jews of much use; as to anoint the head, to make the face shine, Psal. 104. 15. to make cakes.

**V. 3. ***{Then he said, Go borrow thee vessels}* These might be some earthen large pots, or stands, or wooden runlets, or firkins. Such vessels they were as were fit to hold great store of oil, that so there might be means to receive that blessing which the Lord should give, as chap. 3. 16. & Joh. 2. 6, 7.

*{abroad of all thy neighbours}* Thus the miracle might be the further divulged, the power of God more manifested, and more people induced to acknowledge Elisha a true prophet. For such as lent the vessels, such as bought the oil, and the creditor whose debt was discharged with the money, had occasion to inquire after the miracle, and to be instructed therein.

*{even empty vessels}* That the truth of the miracle might be the better discerned. See ch. 2. 20.

*{borrow not a few}* Or, *scant not*. Both in Hebrew and Greek there is but one word wherein that phrase, *borrow a few*, is comprised. The prophet intended she should have enough to discharge the debt to the full, and withal to have means for a future livelihood.

**V. 4. ***{And when thou art come in, thou shalt shut the door upon thee}* He gives this charge, 1. To get evidence of her faith, whether she and her sons being alone, she could expect supply. 2. To put her in mind of calling upon God, when she and her sons had none to seek unto but him only. Christ bids us shut the door when we go to prayer, Mat. 6. 6. 3. That she might not be interrupted nor hindered till the work were fully ended. 4. That it might more evidently appear that the supply she had was from God.

*{and upon thy sons}* That they might be witnesses of the miracle, as Mar. 5. 40. and also helpers to her, in bringing and carrying away the vessels, as v. 6.

*{and shalt pour out into all those vessels}* By the divine providence that oil which was in her own pot so increased as it filled all the vessels which she borrowed.

*{and thou shalt set aside that which is full}* That so every vessel one after another might be filled.

**V. 5. ***{So she went from him}* This is an evidence of her confidence in God. The prophet having given her direction what to do in secret, she is not oversolicitous to have him present with her, but believed his word, that her desire should be accomplished. Naaman by the contrary showed want of faith, 2 King. 5. 11. and Barak weakness of faith, Judg. 4. 8.

*{and shut the door upon her and her sons}* This her obedience is a further evidence of her faith.

*{who brought the vessels to her}* Her sons testify their obedience to the prophet and to their mother, and their faith in God.

*{and she poured out}* See v. 4.

**V. 6. ***{And it came to pass when the vessels were full}* Namely, all the vessels that she had borrowed.

*{that she said unto her son}* Either she spoke to one of her sons, who was not at that moment busied in setting aside a full vessel: for they might by course take the vessels one after another from their mother; or one might be employed in bringing to her empty vessels, and the other in carrying from her full ones. Or synecdochically the singular number for the plural, *son* for *sons*: for she had two sons with her, v. 1, 4, 5.

*{Bring me yet a vessel}* She being busy in pouring out oil did not mark whether all the vessels were filled or no, and therefore calls for another. It appears hereby that the vessel out of which she poured still continued to have oil in it.

*{And he said unto her}* That son to whom she spoke.

*{There is not a vessel more}* An evidence of the continuance of God’s providence so long as there is occasion.

*{And the oil stayed}* If it had still continued to increase, there being no vessels to hold it, much oil might have been lost; and God will not have his blessings lost, John 6. 12. nor use extraordinary power further than needs, 1 King. 17. 14. Josh. 5. 12.

**V. 7. ***{Then she came and told the man of God}* Both to testify her thankfulness, as Luk. 17. 15, 16. and also to receive direction what to do with that great blessing.

*{And he said, Go, sell the oil}* Oil was a very vendible commodity, being useful to many purposes. See v. 2.

*{and pay thy debt}* Or, *creditor*. Here we are taught to be conscionable in discharging just debts.

*{and live thou and thy children of the rest}* Here is an evidence of the bounty of God, who oft gives more than his children ask. See 1 King. 3. 13. She desired only to have her debt discharged, and she has not only enough for that, but an overplus for her own and her children’s future maintenance. This passage further teaches us, that the widows and children of ministers are to be provided for.

**V. 8. ***{And it fell on a day}* After the former history of the poor widow.

*{that Elisha passed}* The prophet went up and down from place to place to do the more good.

*{to Shunem}* See 1 King. 1. 3.

*{where was a great}* This indefinite particle *great* may be applied to anything that makes one to be in high esteem, as fame or reputation, honor, wealth, power, &c. ch. 5. 1. & 7. 6. Exod. 11. 3. Esth. 10. 3. Gen. 24. 35. See ch. 10. 6. So is God’s goodness to great ones, as well as to mean ones.

*{woman}* Certainly this was such a woman as Solomon describes Prov. 31. 10, &c. *The heart of her husband trusted in her*, and he committed the managing of the affairs of the house to her; and thereupon is she here set down to be the entertainer of the prophet. Besides sundry circumstances of the history show she was a very pious and prudent matron.

*{and she constrained him}* Heb. *laid hold on him*. For they that are very desirous and earnest to have a friend abide with them, will lay fast hold on him or some part of his garment, to keep him from departing away. See Luk. 24. 29. Acts 16. 15.

*{to eat bread}* To refresh himself with food, or, as we speak, to take a meal’s meat. See Gen. 31. 54.

*{And so it was that as oft as he passed by}* This further shows that the prophet used to go up and down from place to place, that he might visit the faithful, and seek opportunities of doing the more good.

*{he turned in thither to eat bread}* Because he found good entertainment and hearty welcome, he did the oftener visit that family; that he might do the more good therein. Christ advises his disciples so to do, Luk. 10. 7.

**V. 9. ***{And she said unto her husband}* However she welcomed him, yet she would not set apart a special room for the prophet’s use without her husband’s consent.

*{Behold, now I perceive that this is an holy}* This title *holy* has relation to his inward disposition and outward conversation. It comprises under it all those graces which adorn a professor and teacher of the true religion. Certainly the prophet had behaved himself piously, justly, soberly, charitably, and every way unblamably.

*{man of God}* This title has relation to the prophet’s function. See 1 King. 13. 1.

*{which passeth by us continually}* i.e. very often. As oft as he had occasion to go that way he came to that house.

**V. 10. ***{Let us make a little chamber}* Questionless there was room enough in the house for the prophet to lodge in; but her desire was to have a retiring place for his proper use, and to be reserved only for him.

*{I pray thee}* A phrase testifying reverence and earnest desire.

*{on the wall}* Their house might be by the town wall, as Rahab’s, Josh. 2. 15. and the chamber being on the wall, the prophet might thereby have the freer ingress and egress. Or her meaning might be to make a little chamber with a wall or partition to separate it from other rooms, that the prophet might be the more private to himself.

*{and let us set for him there a bed}* To lie on.

*{and a table}* To lay his books or other things upon.

*{and a stool}* To sit upon.

*{and a candlestick}* To hold a candle for light, as he should have occasion to use it. All these were fit for study and rest. Under them other necessary commodities may be comprised. This is a good pattern of hospitality, much commended in Scripture, Rom. 12. 13. Heb. 13. 1. 1 Pet. 4. 9.

*{and it shall be when he cometh to us, that he shall turn in thither}* By thus accommodating all things for him he will be encouraged upon all occasions to take up his lodging with us.

**V. 11. ***{And it fell on a day that he came thither}* Namely to the Shunamite’s house.
