*{and he turned into the chamber}* The chamber being first showed him, and he being told that it was so fitted and prepared for him, he did the more willingly accept of it.

*{and lay there}* So as he was there day and night. And no question but that both governors and others also of the house received many divine instructions from him.

**V. 12. ***{And he said unto Gehazi his servant}* The prophet was so taken with the woman’s kindness as it made him not only to think with himself but also advise with another how to recompense it. An ingenious spirit and a grateful mind will be inquiring how to requite a benefit.

*{Call this Shunamite}* That the kindness he intended might be the more acceptable, he would know from herself what she most desired: for such a kindness is the best.

*{And when he had called her, she stood before him}* Expecting what the prophet would with her. See v. 15.

**V. 13. ***{And he said unto him, Say now unto her}* The prophet by his servant speaks to the woman, that she might the more freely declare her whole mind: For persons that are in esteem work an awe in them who highly esteem them, so as they dare not express their mind to them so freely as to others.

*{Behold, thou hast been careful for us with all this care}* This phrase implies a very sedulous and diligent care, such as has a fear of failing in some duty mixed with it.

*{What is to be done for thee?}* This further shows that the prophet’s desire was to do an acceptable kindness to her.

*{wouldst thou be spoken for to the king}* Kings have power to do great courtesies: This very woman afterwards had experience thereof, ch. 8. 6. The prophet it seems was gracious with the king because of that great kindness he did for him, chap. 3. 16, &c.

*{or to the captain of the host}* The prophet might fear that this woman being a professor of the true religion might suffer some injury by soldiers; and the victory which this captain had over the Moabites according to his prophecy, ch. 3. 18, 24. might ingratiate him to him.

*{And she answered, I dwell among mine own people}* She means that she had so good content among her kinsfolks and neighbors as she had no need of any extraordinary favor from king or captain. She lived in peace, and had no quarrel with any; she had a competent livelihood, and was content with her estate: and should she have need of king or captain, her own friends might therein be helpful unto her.

**V. 14. ***{And he said, What then is to be done for her?}* By this reply it appears that Gehazi brought the forementioned answer from the woman to his master: and he earnestly desiring some way or other to requite her kindness, would not rest in that answer, but makes this further inquiry. His grateful mind made him as careful for his hostess as she had been for him; v. 10, 13.

*{And Gehazi answered, Verily she hath no child}* Questionless Gehazi had further consulted with the woman about this very thing, whether a child would not be acceptable unto her. Or knowing the disposition of women that wanted children in those days, and withal that she had no child, he might think that of all things a child would be most welcome to her; and he knew that his master could obtain one of the Lord for her.

*{and her husband is old}* This he adds to amplify that kindness. It is probable that the woman was barren; and her husband withal being old, there was little hope of her having a child without an extraordinary blessing from God.

**V. 15. ***{And he said, Call her}* It seems she went away from the prophet after her first coming to him, v. 12. as Bathsheba did from David, 1 King. 1. 28. Or else that first calling her v. 12. must have reference to this: as if Gehazi had been commanded first to declare the mind of Elisha to her; and upon his return to his master was commanded to call her to him.

*{And when he had called her, she stood in the door}* This she did by reason of a modest shamefulness, which kept her from entering into the prophet’s chamber. The like is noted of Sarah, Gen. 18. 10.

**V. 16. ***{And he said}* That which follows, he spoke to the woman herself, face to face. For it is said v. 12. that *she stood before him*.

*{About this season}* Heb. *set time*. i.e. that time of the next year. See Gen. 18. 10.

*{according to the time of life}* i.e. the time of a woman’s going with child. Not that she should presently conceive, but that from her first conception she should go her full time, and then bring forth a son, and all within the space of a year. This was a usual kind of expressing that point, as Gen. 18. 10, 14. Rom. 9. 9.

*{thou shalt embrace a son}* i.e. Conceive, bear, bring forth, and enjoy her child, whether by giving it suck, or otherwise.

*{And she said, Nay}* This negative does not import a rejecting of the promise, but a little doubting of the truth thereof. It has reference to the last clause of the verse.

*{my lord}* This is a title of reverence. For she had the prophet in high account.

*{thou man of God}* See 1 King. 13. 1. She here uses this title to strengthen her faith. *q.d.* Will a man of God promise that which shall not be performed?

*{do not lie}* Deceive me not with vain hope. This shows her earnest desire of obtaining what the prophet promised, and a kind of binding him to the performance thereof.

*{unto thy handmaid}* This is a title of humility. See v. 2.

**V. 17. ***{And the woman conceived}* This verse compared with the former shows that the promise was not in the substance only but also in the very circumstances thereof accomplished. The conception and birth of this child was miraculous. For it is probable she was barren; her husband was said to be old: all was done upon promise. So as this was Elisha’s sixth miracle. See chap. 2. 14.

**V. 18. ***{And when the child was grown}* To some three or four years at least: for he was able to go into the fields.

*{it fell on a day}* It was so ordered by God’s providence, who oft mixes sours with sweets.

*{that he went out to his father}* It seems the father much cockered the child, and the child dearly loved the father, whereby it was moved to go out unto him.

*{to the reapers}* His father went to oversee the reapers, and he followed after him. This circumstance showed that this fell out in harvest time, when the sun shined very hot; which might be some occasion if not the proper cause, of the child’s sickness.

**V. 19. ***{And he said unto his father, My head, my head}* The doubling of the phrase intimates the extremity of his headache. See Jer. 14. 19. & 1 King. 8. 13.

*{And he said to a lad}* To one of his younger servants. For such a one does the Hebrew word translated *lad* oft set forth, v. 22, 24. Judg. 16. 26. 1 Sam. 20. 25, 26.

*{Carry him to his mother}* The father was busied about his harvest abroad. Mothers, as they are most at home, so they are most tenderly affected towards their young children. See 1 Tim. 5. 10, 14.

**V. 20. ***{And when he had taken him, and brought him to his mother}* The lad carried the child to the house, and delivered him to his mother.

*{he sat on her knees till noon}* It appears by this circumstance that the child went into the field in the morning, and the mother observing him to be very sick held him in her bosom, the better to quiet, and refresh him.

*{and then died}* A very doleful accident; a mother to behold a child so much desired, given as an especial favor of God, and that by miracle, a son, an only son, past his infancy, able of himself to go into the fields, suddenly, unexpectedly, within her arms, to die. Here we see how the dearest that we have are subject to be taken from us, and how God in wisdom oft tries his dearest in the dearest things they have, Gen. 22. 2. & 37. 3, &c. Ez. 24. 16, 21. The pain that the child had in his head was some preparative to his departure.

**V. 21. ***{And she went up}* Into the prophet’s chamber, v. 11.

*{and laid him on the bed of the man of God}* It is manifest that she had some hope of receiving him from death again; and it is probable that she did not make known the death of her child to any of her family, lest she should fill the house with lamentation and crying, and lest any of them might hinder her from going to the prophet, as accounting it altogether in vain.

*{and shut the door upon him, and went out}* She tarried not long in the chamber, lest any should come to her in that while, but went out and made fast the door, that none might come in before the prophet should give direction what to do, and lest any finding the child dead might wind him and coffin him up. Besides, by this means she would have the child more ready for the prophet, when he should come. All evidences and signs of her faith, by which *she received her dead raised to life again*, Heb. 11. 35.

**V. 22. ***{And she called unto her husband, and said, Send me}* She would neither go herself abroad, nor use servant nor beast, without her husband’s consent.

*{I pray thee}* A word of reverent respect, and earnest desire.

*{one of the young men}* This is the same word that was translated *lad* v. 19. She means one of the servants.

*{and one of the asses}* See 1 King. 13. 13.

*{that I may run to the man of God}* i.e. make all the speed I can, v. 26. Jer. 51. 31. Joel 2. 4.

*{and come again}* This she adds to move her husband the rather to grant her request, because she would not be long from home.

**V. 23. ***{And he said, Wherefore wilt thou go to him to day?}* It seems he yet knew nothing of the child’s departure.

*{it is neither new-moon, nor Sabbath}* These were the times wherein God’s people used to go to prophets for advice. For then people ceased from the works of their calling, and prophets set themselves apart to instruct and advise such as should come to them. Of new moons see 1 Chron. 23. 31.

*{And she said, It shall be well}* Heb. *Peace*. No offense will hence arise. The prophet will take my coming in good part.

**V. 24. ***{Then she saddled an ass}* i.e. caused it to be saddled.

*{and said to her servant}* This word is the same that v. 19. was translated *lad*, and v. 22. *young man*.

*{Drive, and go forward}* Either she means that the servant should put on her ass apace, and run along himself by her, or that he should ride apace before her.

*{slack not thy riding for me}* Heb. *restrain not for me to ride*. Spare not to ride apace for my ease, but make what haste you can.

*{except I bid thee}* If there be any cause of stay I will tell you.

**V. 25. ***{So she went and came unto the man of God}* By the haste she made she quickly came to the place where the prophet was.

*{to mount Carmel}* See 1 King. 18. 19.

*{And it came to pass, when the man of God saw her afar off}* Or, *over against him*. It seems was sitting at the door, as Abraham, Gen. 18. 1. and so saw her the sooner.

*{that he said to Gehazi his servant}* Gehazi was attending upon him, and he used to communicate his mind to him.

*{Behold, yonder is that Shunamite}* She that has showed so much kindness unto me.

**V. 26. ***{Run now, I pray thee}* See v. 22. The greatest speed that man can make is by running, he has no wings to fly.

*{to meet her}* The prophet would testify his kindness to her before she could come at him.

*{and say unto her, Is it well with thee?}* Heb. *Peace*: Men use first to inquire after the welfare of persons themselves with whom they have to do.

*{Is it well with thy husband?}* Heb. *Peace*. Her husband had joined with her in giving entertainment to the prophet; and withal, husbands are to loving wives as themselves: therefore he inquires after the welfare of her husband also.

*{Is it well with the child?}* Heb. *Peace*. The prophet knew her heart was set upon her child, therefore could not but ask after his welfare. These questions are thus distinctly propounded to manifest the great care the prophet had of her and hers.

*{And she answered, It is well}* Heb. *Peace*. Her answer is directed to the two former questions; the last she reserved till she should come herself to the prophet.

**V. 27. ***{And when she came to the man of God to the hill}* See v. 25. That former phrase is spoken of her approach into the sight of the prophet; this, of his very presence.

*{she caught him by the feet}* Heb. *by his feet*. Hitherto she had concealed her passion, but the prophet’s words afterwards show that she was much perplexed in her soul, which by this gesture she manifested. Hereby also her reverent respect to him is testified, as Mat. 28. 9. She also does this to move the prophet, as if she would not let go his feet till he had granted her desire.

*{but Gehazi came near to thrust her away}* Gehazi herein had respect to his master, (as the disciples had to their Lord, Mat. 15. 23.) for he conceived the woman was too troublesome to him. But this was too great officiousness.

*{And the man of God said, Let her alone}* The prophet had a better opinion of her than his servant, and perceives she had cause to do what she did.

*{for her soul is vexed within her}* Heb. *bitter*. Therefore he would not have her who was so perplexed already to be further vexed.

*{and the Lord hath hid it from me}* Prophets had not always the spirit of prophecy. See ch. 6. 6. 2 Sam. 7. 3.

*{and hath not told me}* It is from God’s making known secret things unto his prophets that they know them, ch. 6. 12.

**V. 28. ***{Then she said, Did I desire a son of my Lord?}* This interrogation imports a strong negation, *q.d.* You know that I never moved you to ask a son of the Lord for me, so as it was not any entreaty of mine that forced you thereunto. I was not of Rachel’s mind, Gen. 30. 1. Therefore I have not pulled upon myself this judgment by my own undue importunity.

*{did I not say, Do not deceive me?}* See v. 16. This she adds to put the prophet in mind that he of himself had promised a son unto her, to move him the rather to restore her son again to life. And by these questions she gave him to understand that now she had no son, but that he was dead; which the prophet soon discerned, v. 29.

**V. 29. ***{Then he said to Gehazi, Gird up thy loins}* See 1 King. 18. 46.

*{and take my staff in thy hand, and go thy way}* God having oft wrought miracles by such external things as belonged to his servants, the prophet commands his servant to use his staff for the child’s recovery. So Elijah used his mantle to divide the waters of Jordan, and Elisha used the same, ch. 2. 8, 14. Thus sundry miracles were wrought by Moses his rod, Exod. 4. 2, 3. Thus diseases were cured by the linen that came from Paul, Acts 19. 12.

*{If thou meet any man, salute him not}* Let no office of courtesy stay you at all. See Luk. 10. 4.

*{and if any salute thee, answer him not again}* Neither offering nor answering courtesies, must any way hinder him.

*{and lay my staff upon the face of the child}* This is the reason why the prophet bid his servant take his staff in his hand, namely, that he might lay it upon the child’s face. Certainly the mother had covered the child all over but the face. Or by a synecdoche the *face* may be put for the whole body.

**V. 30. ***{And the mother of the child said, As the Lord liveth, and as thy soul liveth}* See ch. 2. 2.

*{I will not leave thee}* Though she dared not wholly distrust the means the prophet appointed, yet because she knew he was the man by whom God used to work miracles, she would not leave him till he himself went to her child, lest upon the failing of the former means her child should not be restored.

*{And he arose, and followed her}* The prophet was willing to gratify her, and the rather because himself might somewhat doubt whether the deed might be done by his servant or no.

**V. 31. ***{And Gehazi passed on before them, and laid the staff upon the face of the child}* He obediently observed the charge given him, v. 29. It seems the mother of the child had given him the key to open the door which she had shut, v. 21.

*{but there was neither voice, nor hearing}* Heb. *attention*. i.e. Notwithstanding Gehazi did what the prophet commanded, yet no evidences of life followed thereupon. The child could neither utter anything, nor hear any noise.

*{wherefore he went again}* Heb. *he returned*. He saw that he did no good by his stay, and thereupon returned.

*{to meet him}* i.e. his master. For he supposed his master would come himself to see the issue of that which was done.

*{and told him, saying, The child is not awaked}* Not revived. For death is a kind of sleep. See 1 King. 2. 10.

**V. 32. ***{And when Elisha was come into the house}* For he quickly followed Gehazi, v. 30.

*{Behold the child was dead, and laid upon his bed}* It is remarkable, that the child being laid on the prophet’s bed, and the prophet’s staff by his own appointment laid upon the child’s face, the child should still remain dead. Some attribute this to the unbelief of Gehazi, some to the unbelief of the mother of the child. Note Mat. 13. 58. But certainly the prophet had no warrant from God, either ordinary or extraordinary, to command Gehazi to lay his staff upon the child, but did it of his own head; and therefore God withheld his power, without which all that man can do is nothing. Prophets themselves must have their warrant from God to do what they do. Though the Lord were pleased to use Elisha’s ministry in working miracles, yet did not he give him authority to use his servant’s ministry in that kind.

**V. 33. ***{He went in therefore}* The prophet discerning his error, falls himself to work.

*{and shut the door upon them twain}* Namely, upon himself and the dead child. For he would now be alone, that he might be the more fervent. So did Elijah, 1 Kings 17. 19.

*{and prayed unto the Lord}* See 1 Kings 17. 20. This course of praying was not taken before; which may be one reason why the applying of his staff was fruitless. And he might first pray that his own error might be forgiven, and then that the child might be restored.

**V. 34. ***{And he went up, and lay upon the child, and put his mouth upon his mouth, &c.}* This he might do in imitation of his master, 1 Kings 17. 21. Though the stature of Elisha and his child were so different as the eyes, mouth and hands of the one could not lie just even upon those of the other, yet the prophet laid his as near the child as he could, and that for the reason rendered, 1 Kings 17. 21.

*{And he stretched himself upon the child}* This was an effect of the fervency of his spirit. By way of allusion some infer, That we must apply ourselves to all the necessities of the saints; and other, That we must in such a manner spiritually apply ourselves to Christ, and mystically stretch ourselves upon him.

*{and the flesh of the child waxed warm}* This was a sign that life began to come into him.

**V. 35. ***{Then he returned, and walked in the house to and fro}* Heb. *once hither and once thither*. He might rise up and walk about to revive his spirit, and to thank God, for the first sign of hearing his prayer. Besides, men when they are very earnest in their prayers, are oft moved to change the posture and gesture of their bodies.

*{and went up, and stretched himself upon him}* Because the former sign was but of the beginning of life, he returns again to do as he had done before, v. 34. extending his spirit, to have that good work begun perfected.

*{and the child neesed seven times}* A sign of life increasing. For sneezing is a strong motion which cannot be without life; and its being seven times reiterated implied strength.

*{and the child opened his eyes}* As the eyes by death use to be closed up, so the opening of them shows life apparently. Thus God by degrees does manifest his blessing, that he may quicken our spirits to hold on in prayer, and make us more sensible of the increase, and distinctly observant of the greatness thereof. This was Elisha’s seventh miracle. See ch. 2. 14.

**V. 36. ***{And he called to Gehazi, and said, Call this Shunamite}* This shows that neither Gehazi nor the woman were with Elisha when he stretched himself upon the child. See v. 33.

*{So he called her}* Questionless he that first moved the prophet for a child for the Shunamite, v. 14. was very glad of the restoring her child unto her, and therefore readily and quickly calls her.

*{and when she was come in unto him, he said, Take up thy son}* See 1 Kings 17. 23. His meaning is, that though she had left him a dead child on the bed, yet he being now revived, she might again take him and enjoy him as before.

**V. 37. ***{Then she went in, and fell at his feet}* Grief and passion moved her to take the prophet by the feet, v. 27. but now joy and thankfulness make her to fall at his feet.

*{and bowed her self to the ground}* This she did in testimony of her high esteem, reverent respect and grateful heart; but all in a civil manner. See ch. 2. 15. & 1 King. 1. 23.

*{and took up her son}* This verifies that of the apostle, Heb. 11. 35. The widow of Zarephath, 1 King. 17. 23. and here this Shunamite *received their dead raised to life*. No question but that most joyfully and thankfully she took it up from the prophet’s bed, and with a motherly affection hugged it in her arms.

*{and went out}* From the prophet’s chamber into her house. Howsoever, when the child was dead, she shut it up in that room, that it might not be known, v. 21. yet now with much joy she made known both its death and its restitution.

**V. 38. ***{And Elisha came again into Gilgal}* This prophet, as his master before him, went up and down, like unto Christ, Acts 10. 38. doing good. He was before at Gilgal, and therefore is said to come again to it. See ch. 2. 1.

*{and there was a dearth in the land}* i.e. of Israel. The idolatry of the Israelites, besides sundry other sins, brought this upon them. And in the common judgment the true worshippers of God, as these sons of the prophets, were involved. See Ezek. 21. 3. And hereby the sin of the wicked is exceedingly aggravated, in that it pulls vengeance not only upon themselves and such as are like them, but also upon the righteous: For whom notwithstanding the Lord can provide in the midst of judgments; as here he did for this college of prophets.

*{and the sons of the Prophets were sitting before him}* This phrase shows that as disciples they sat on lower seats to hear the instructions of their master. See Acts 22. 3. & 1 King. 10. 5. Elisha came to the colleges of the prophets to instruct them; and they readily heard his word, as Mary did Christ’s, Luk. 10. 39. It is probable that at this time he did the rather come unto them, to provide for them, and to keep them together, that by reason of the famine they might not be dispersed.

*{and he said unto his servant}* This was one that did attend that college to provide necessaries for them.

*{Set on the great pot}* There were many to be fed, as appears v. 43. and therefore he makes the greater provision.

*{and seethe pottage for the sons of the Prophets}* Questionless there was meat also provided that the pottage might be the more savory and nourishing: but by this means the meat satisfied the more.

**V. 39. ***{And one went out into the field to gather herbs}* They had not such a garden as is mentioned 1 King. 21. 2. but were fain to go out into the field to gather herbs.

*{and found a wild vine}* Heb. *a vine of the field*. A plant growing in the fields and hedgerows, the leaves whereof are broad like the leaves of a vine.

*{and gathered thereof wild gourds his lap full}* This plant is taken to be *Coloquintida*] the leaves or herbs whereof are bitter and poisonous. Of those he gathered good store.

*{and came and shred them into the pot of pottage}* As they use to do with wholesome and savory potherbs.

*{for they knew them not}* He that gathered the wild gourds intended no mischief; nor were they who saw them put in accessory to any evil: All this was done out of ignorance.

**V. 40. ***{So they poured out for the men to eat}* Supposing the broth fit to be eaten.

*{and it came to pass, as they were eating the pottage}* As they were ready to eat thereof, it being set in dishes before them.

*{and they cried out, and said}* It seems, that one that had tasted of the pottage, found the danger thereof, and told the rest of it; and thereupon they cried out.

*{O thou man of God}* Of this title see 1 King. 13. 1. They here use it the rather to move the prophet to help them in this distress, and to show the ground of their faith that he was able to do so.

*{there is death in the pot}* The broth therein is poisoned, and will poison all that eat it. *Death* by a metonymy is put for that which may cause death, as Exod. 10. 17.

*{and they could not eat thereof}* While it remained so poisonous. Afterwards they did eat of it, v. 41.

**V. 41. ***{But he said, Then bring meal}* Not that meal had any virtue to draw out bitterness or poison: but that it might be discerned that the virtue came from God, who by his servant appointed that means to be used, as Exod. 15. 25.

*{and he cast it into the pot}* For being guided by God’s Spirit, he believed that that means, though unlikely in itself, would be effectual.

*{and he said, Pour out for the people, that they may eat}* He knew that all the danger was taken away; and therefore confidently caused the broth to be set before them, that they might eat thereof.

*{and there was no harm in the pot}* Heb. *evil thing*. The venom was clean taken away, and that which was poisonous before was now made wholesome: So as this was Elisha’s eighth miracle. See ch. 2. 14.

**V. 42. ***{And there came a man from Baalshalisha}* This was the place in Ephraim which is called Shalisha, 1 Sam. 9. 4. And after Baal was there shut up, it was called *Baal-shalisha*.

*{and brought the man of God bread of the firstfruits}* This no doubt was a pious man, and one that feared God, seeing in obedience to the law he was willing to dedicate the firstfruits to the Lord: But because he could not carry them to God’s house as the law required, Exod. 23. 19. nor to the ordinary priests, because they were all in Judah, 2 Chron. 11. 13, 14. he brought them to this extraordinary prophet, and to the college of prophets. Thus he honored God with his firstfruits, Prov. 3. 9.

*{twenty loaves of barley}* Besides the firstfruits. Herein he gave a testimony of his charity to the prophets of God in that time of dearth. Though it were but barley bread (such as was brought to Christ, Joh. 6. 9.) Yet in such a dearth it manifested a better respect to the prophets than the finest wheat would have done at another time. See on 1 Sam. 9. 7.

*{and full ears of corn in the husk thereof}* Or, *in his scrip, or garment*. The meaning is, that he brought corn not beaten out of the ears. These may be part of the firstfruits, before mentioned. Such were appointed under the law to be brought to the priests, Deut. 18. 3, 4. and such, people did use to parch and eat.

*{and he said}* i.e. Elisha.

*{Give unto the people}* To the sons of the prophets before mentioned, who were styled the *people* v. 41.

*{that they may eat}* For for this end it was brought.

**V. 43. ***{And his servitor said}* He that attended upon him, namely Gehazi.

*{What, should I set this before an hundred men?}* Though he had seen many miracles wrought by his master, and thereupon had cause readily to have obeyed without any question, yet, like Andrew and other disciples Joh. 6. 9. Luk. 9. 13. being very weak of faith, he makes doubt of that which his master intended.

*{He said again, Give the people that they may eat}* The incredulity of his servant made him the more earnest in pressing his charge. See v. 42.

*{for thus saith the Lord}* This warrant he produced, to strengthen his servant’s faith the more: 1 King. 12. 24.

*{They shall eat, and shall leave thereof}* They shall not only have enough for the present, but also leave for another time.

**V. 44. ***{So he set it before them}* Elisha’s servant upon the forenamed warrant and promise, being somewhat strengthened in faith, now readily obeys his master.

*{and they did eat, and left thereof}* This is an evidence of a true miracle, like that Mat. 14. 20. Joh. 6. 11, 12. For, though this miracle were not altogether so great as that of Christ’s, Mat. 14. 17, 21. yet was it as true a miracle as that, being above the course of nature, and a divine work. So as this was Elisha’s ninth miracle. See ch. 2. 14.

*{according to the word of the Lord}* See 1 King. 13. 5.

## CHAPTER 5

**Ver. 1. ***{Now Naaman}* This history is very memorable concerning the cure both of Naaman’s body and also of his soul; and therefore he is the more distinctly and largely described, 1. By his name, 2. By his office, 3. By his master’s esteem of him, 4. By his dignity, 5. By his victories, 6. By his prowess.

*{Captain of the host}* This phrase oft sets forth the general of an army; a very great office.

*{of the king of Syria}* Benhadad, ch. 8. 7.

*{was a great man with his master}* Heb. *before his master*. See ch. 4. 8. This phrase *with*, or *before his master* implies a high and great esteem wherein the king of Syria had him.

*{and honourable}* Or, *gracious*. Heb. *lifted up, or accepted in countenance*. As he was advanced to high honor and dignity, so his name was great among the people.

*{because by him the Lord had given deliverance}* Or, *victory*. It is probable that the army that went against the kings of Israel and Judah, 1 King. 22. 29, &c. was commanded by this Naaman in chief; and that this place has relation to that affair. Though they were heathen and enemies of God’s people that got the better, yet the victory is attributed to the Lord. For even heathen men are God’s instruments, Isa. 10. 5, 6.

*{unto Syria}* The whole kingdom was in danger when the two kings came against Ramoth-Gilead, 1 King. 22. 29. therefore the deliverance is extended to Syria.

*{He was also a mighty man in valour}* This phrase has respect to the courage and magnanimity of his mind and to his military abilities, as the former had to his good success.

*{but he was a leper}* See Lev. 13. 18, &c. Men of the greatest dignity, fame and parts are subject to the worst of diseases.

**V. 2. ***{And the Syrians had gone out by companies}* This is meant of excursions made by some troops into the land of Israel for spoil. See ch. 13. 20, 21.

*{and had brought away captive out of the land of Israel a little maid}* This maid might be abroad in the country, either alone, or with some few that were not able to withstand the enemy, and so taken. The sequel shows that it was ordered by a special providence of God. See 1 King. 11. 39.

*{and she waited on}* Heb. *was before*. For they that wait on others use to be in their sight to attend their commands, Psal. 123. 2.

*{Naamans wife}* It is probable that this was a fair and comely maiden, and thereupon brought as a present to Naaman the general, who gave her to his wife. See Judg. 5. 30.

**V. 3. ***{And she said unto her mistress}* Naaman’s wife, though a heathen, might use the maid well; and the maid in gratitude declare to her mistress a means of curing her husband.

*{would God my Lord were with the Prophet}* Heb. *before the Prophet*. She means Elisha, whom she believed to be a prophet of the Lord, and of whose great works she had heard.

*{that is in Samaria}* Samaria is here to be taken synecdochically for the *kingdom of Israel*. See 1 King. 18. 2.

*{for he would recover him}* Heb. *gather in*. The leprosy used to spread itself over the body: so that gathering of it in was a taking of it away.

*{of his leprosy}* Though she had not heard of any leper cured by Elisha, (for Christ says, *Many lepers were in Israel in the time of Elizeus the Prophet, and none of them cleansed*, Luk. 4. 27.) yet by other miracles that he had wrought she gathered he could cure this disease; yea she believed that the man of God would willingly and readily do such a cure to such as should seek it of him. Few in Israel believed so much, Luke 4. 27.

**V. 4. ***{And one went in, and told his Lord, saying}* One that wished well to Naaman, being a servant or soldier under him, went and informed him what the maid had said. Or it may be meant of Naaman himself, who having intelligence of the maid’s words, told his master thereof. Then the phrase *his Lord* has reference to the king. By reason of his place Naaman might not go out of the land without the king’s leave.

*{Thus and thus said the maid that is of the land of Israel}* See v. 2, 3. Though she were a servant, a maid, and a captive, yet her wholesome counsel was received.

**V. 5. ***{And the King of Syria said}* The king’s respect to Naaman made him readily yield to what might be good for him. And though Naaman were a leper, and that disease counted incurable, yet he would make trial what might be done.

*{Go to, go}* These words seem to be spoken to Naaman himself, whom the king in his great respect admitted into his presence. Or this conference between the king and him might be by way of message one to the other.

*{and I will send a letter to the King of Israel}* Jehoram was then king of Israel, ch. 3. 1. and an enemy unto this king of Syria: yet for his servant’s sake he writes to him.

*{And he departed, and took with him}* Heb. *in his hand*. He could not himself in his hand carry all that is after mentioned; but because he took care that all should be carried along with him, it is said to be *in his hand*. Or, with his own hand he might deliver it to his servants to carry along with him.

*{ten talents of silver}* Three thousand seven hundred and fifty pound sterling. See 1 King. 16. 24. & 1 Chr. 22. 14.

*{and six thousand pieces of gold}* When the distinct pieces are not expressed, shekels are ordinarily understood: and six thousand shekels of gold is seven thousand five hundred pound sterling, double to the silver before mentioned. See 1 King. 10. 16. If the word *pieces* be understood, the sum will arise according to the quantity of the piece.

*{and ten changes of raiment}* Of upper garments; such as our gowns or cloaks. These in those countries they used oft to shift: therefore changes or shifts of new garments were wont to be given or sent for presents. See Gen. 45. 22. Judg. 14. 12. Naaman carried so much silver and gold, and so many changes of raiment, partly for his own expenses and honor, having a great train, and a long journey to go; and partly to present to the prophet, thinking he might be moved with gifts, as heathenish and idolatrous priests were. See Numb. 22. 7, 17. He also might intend to give presents to the king and his courtiers.

**V. 6. ***{And he brought the letter to the king of Israel}* It was Naaman that brought the letter.

*{saying}* This has relation to the substance of the letter, as if the king of Syria had spoken to the king of Israel, which he did by writing.

*{Now when this letter is come unto thee}* The main sum of the letter, not the whole form thereof, is here set down.

*{behold, I have therewith sent Naaman my servant to thee, that thou mayest recover him of his leprosy}* His meaning is, that the king should procure the prophet to cure his servant. For he took it for granted that the king of Israel well knew there was a prophet in his kingdom could do it, and that he had power over that prophet to command him to do what he would have him. But herein he was deceived; for kings have not power to command their subjects anything above their ability, as miracles are.

**V. 7. ***{And it came to pass when the King of Israel had read the letter, that he rent his clothes}* This was a sign of much passion, Gen. 37. 29. Though he knew that Elisha could work miracles, yet at that time he did not think thereof, or at least would take no notice of it.

*{and said, Am I a God}* He supposed the king of Syria required of him that he should cure Naaman with his own hand.

*{to kill, and to make alive}* This is God’s prerogative, Deut. 32. 39. 1 Sam. 2. 6.

*{that this man doth send unto me to recover a man of his leprosy}* He supposed that to cure the leprosy was as hard as to raise the dead: Which was true. For neither could be done but by a divine power.

*{wherefore consider, I pray you}* This he said to his own subjects and counselors of state, as 1 King. 20. 7.

*{and see how he seeketh a quarrel against me}* The manner of the king of Syria’s expressing his mind in his letter v. 6. might give some occasion to the king of Israel to be jealous, and to fear lest in confidence of his former success, 1 King. 22. 35, 36. he should seek occasion of a new war. This was the true cause of his passion; and that which before he said of making him a god was but a fair pretense.

**V. 8. ***{And it was so, when Elisha the man of God had heard that the King of Israel had rent his clothes}* It seems there were some in the court that well knew Elisha, and bore a respect to him, believing that he was a prophet of the Lord, by whom God had done great works; and these quickly brought him word of Naaman’s coming to the king, of the letter that he brought, and of the king’s manner of receiving it.

*{that he sent to the King, saying}* It was a matter of great consequence, much concerning the honor of God, and therefore he was careful to send a speedy direction to the king.

*{Wherefore hast thou rent thy clothes?}* This is an implicit reproof, whereby he shows there was no cause why the king should manifest such passion, seeing he knew there was a prophet in his land that had done as great a miracle as the curing of a leper, and that before himself, ch. 3. 16, &c. and he might have sent for that prophet, to have known his mind in this case.

*{let him come now to me}* The prophet does thus send for that great man that came out of Syria, not out of any stateliness or pride in regard of himself, but as the servant of the Lord, and as one who in a great case was to make known the mind of the Lord, upon which the greatest that be must attend.

*{and he shall know that there is a Prophet in Israel}* Great works are done by the ministers of the Lord, that his name and power may be known far and near. Because the Israelites took not much notice of the prophet that was among them, God would have him made known to foreign nations, Luk. 4. 27.

**V. 9. ***{So Naaman came with his horses and with his chariot}* With such state as he came to the king he also came to the prophet. The desire he had to be cured made him testify such respect to him from whom he expected cure.

*{and stood at the door of the house of Elisha}* He was willing to know the mind of Elisha before he presented himself to him: but questionless he sent in a messenger to signify he was without.

**V. 10. ***{And Elisha sent a messenger to him, saying}* The prophet did not this in scorn, as is noted, v. 8. but he would give Naaman to understand that it was enough to know the mind of God without a prophet’s speaking to him face to face, or doing any particular act himself upon him. So dealt Christ with the ruler, John 4. 50.

*{Go and wash in Jordan seven times}* Every particle in this charge was for trial of his obedience; as, 1. That he should go away from him. 2. That he should wash himself; which could be no natural means of taking away his leprosy. 3. That he should do it in Jordan, whereas there were many excellent sweet rivers in Syria, v. 12. 4. That he should do it seven times. Thus was the obedience and faith of a king of Israel tried, ch. 13. 19. And so Christ tried the man that was born blind, Joh. 9. 7.

*{and thy flesh shall come again unto thee}* The leprosy much eats up the flesh of a man, and brings upon him a thick scurf.

*{and thou shalt be clean}* This implied a perfect cure of the disease. For the leprosy was a foul disease, and made a man unclean, Levi. 13. 45. He added this promise to encourage him the rather to observe the charge he gave him.

**V. 11. ***{But Naaman was wroth}* Naaman thought himself by this answer scorned and contemned by the prophet; and because his own expectation even in the manner of doing that which he desired was not punctually fulfilled, his choler was moved. Men are hardly satisfied in the things they desire, if their own humor be not fulfilled.

*{and went away, and said}* He manifests his displeasure to move his followers to be of his mind.

*{Behold, I thought}* Heb. *said*. Or, *I said with my self, He will surely come out, &c.* Here he plainly shows the reason of his displeasure.

*{He will surely come out to me}* Men are prone to trust too much to external and visible means.

*{and stand, and call on the name of the Lord his God}* This conceit savored of some piety. For hereby he acknowledged that what the prophet did or could do, was by a gift given of God, yea by power immediately derived from God; for obtaining whereof prayer was an especial means; and, that to stand is a fit gesture for prayer.

*{and strike}* Heb. *move up and down*.

*{his hand over the place}* Namely, upon his flesh that was infected with the leprosy. This further shows his doting on visible means. Thus many are ready to prescribe means unto God, and to tie him to one course.

*{and recover the leper}* He means himself. And hereby he shows that he believed that God was able to cure him, and that by the ministry of his prophet: but his faith was mixed with vain conceit.

**V. 12. ***{Are not Abana}* Or, *Amana*. It is called *Adonius*, and Pharpar is called *Orontes*.

*{and Pharpar rivers of Damascus}* Much is written of the clearness, sweetness and usefulness of these two rivers. The one is said to run through many parts of Damascus; whereby it comes to pass that most of the houses in that city are furnished with store of good water. The other is said to flow along by it, whereby their gardens, orchards, vineyards, meadows and other places are made very fertile.

*{better then all the waters of Israel}* He by experience knowing the benefit of the waters of his own country supposes there could not be better in another nation.

*{may I not wash in them and be clean?}* This has relation to the prophet’s charge v. 10. And hereby the Syrian implied that the prophet might as well have sent him to the rivers of Damascus as to Jordan, and that they might have as great a virtue to cleanse him of his leprosy as the water in Jordan. He supposed he was sent to Jordan for the virtue that was in that water.

*{So he turned, and went away}* From the prophet’s house.

*{in a rage}* See v. 11. He does not only slight the prophet’s charge, but is also much incensed against him.

**V. 13. ***{And his servants came near, and spake unto him, and said}* These were faithful servants, who though he were in a rage, yet would not soothe him up therein; but observing that he was unjustly and unduly moved, and that to his own prejudice, they made an assay to pacify his mind, and that for his good; and give him good advice.

*{My father}* This title uses to be given to all sorts of superiors, Exod. 20. 12. as to kings, 1 Sam. 24. 12. to priests Judg. 18. 19. to prophets, ch. 2. 12. & 6. 21. & 13. 14. and here to a prince; yea also to men of age and dignity above others, Acts 22. 1. Here it was given to Naaman, either in regard of his place, he being a great man, v. 1. or in regard of his disposition, he being mild and gentle towards his servants, and careful of their good, as a father of his children; or in regard of the deliverances that he had wrought for Syria, which made him accounted and styled a father of his country. Whatsoever the respect were, it is a note of reverence mixed with love. And whereas the word *servants* is of the plural number, and yet they said, *My father*, it is because they were all of the same mind, or because one spoke for all.

*{if the Prophet}* There is an emphasis in this title. They imply that he that gave the direction ought to be hearkened unto, seeing he was a prophet.

*{had bid thee do some great thing}* Some difficult thing, which would have required much cost and pains.

*{wouldst thou not have done it?}* This interrogation implies a strong affirmation, *q.d.* Certainly you so desire the taking away of your leprosy as you would not stick at anything you could do for the removing of it.

*{how much rather then when he saith unto thee, Wash and be clean}* When he requires a matter so easy to be done. Hereby they showed they believed the prophet’s word, and that obedience ought to be yielded thereto, without questioning the probability or improbability of the means.

**V. 14. ***{Then went he down, and dipped himself seven times in Jordan}* He went up deep in the river, and drenched himself over head and ears, so oft as the prophet had enjoined him. The leprosy was spread over his whole body, from the crown of his head to the sole of his feet; therefore he so dipped himself.

*{according to the saying of the man of God}* Having well taken his servant’s advice, he followed the prophet’s direction both for the substance and circumstances thereof.

*{and his flesh came again like unto the flesh of a little child}* i.e. fresh, soft, clear, without scar or wrinkle. His disease was thoroughly cured, and his flesh in a better case than if he had had no leprosy at all.

*{and he was clean}* Luk. 4. 27. This shows the accomplishment of the prophet’s promise, v. 10. So as God passed by his former incredulity and distemper, and accepted of that faith which was wrought in him by the counsel of his servants. This was Elisha’s tenth miracle. See ch. 2. 14.

**V. 15. ***{And he returned to the man of God}* His passion being now clean allayed, and he finding to his great comfort and benefit the power and efficacy of the prophet’s direction, his grateful mind makes him return to him again, like the tenth leper, Luk. 17. 15.

*{he and all his company}* That he might now testify as great respect to the prophet after he had received the blessing, as he had done before v. 9. when he expected it.

*{and came and stood before him}* Namely, at the door of his house, as v. 9.

*{and he said}* To Elisha, being come out to him.

*{Behold, now I know that there is no God in all the earth but in Israel}* He believed that his cleansing was by a divine power, and that it was the God of Elisha who had brought it so to pass; and thereupon he acknowledges him not only to be a god, but also the only true God. Thus that which Christ says of the centurion Luk. 7. 9. *I have not found so great faith, no not in Israel*, may be applied to this captain, who it seems, was cured both in body and soul. Neither thought he it enough to believe in his heart, unless he also confessed the Lord with his mouth, Rom. 10. 10.

*{now therefore, I pray thee, take a blessing}* A gift in testimony of gratitude. So Gen. 33. 1. This prophet of the Lord was more honored by this Gentile, than by most in Israel.

*{of thy servant}* See 1 King. 18. 13.

**V. 16. ***{But he said, As the Lord liveth before whom I stand}* See 1 King. 17. 1.

*{I will receive none}* It was God’s honor that Elisha aimed at, not his own profit. It was enough to him that the God of Israel was acknowledged by this Syrian to be the true God. Miracles are the immediate work of God; and that power Elisha having freely received would freely exercise, Mat. 10. 8. Simon Magus was sharply reproved for a conceit clean contrary hereunto, Acts 8. 18, 20. Besides, herein the prophet showed that it was enough for him that the Syrian was healed; and hereby he would allure him the more to hearken to his counsel in other things, that did so much good for no gain.

*{And he urged him to take it}* This further shows the grateful disposition of the Syrian, and his true and earnest desire to have the prophet receive somewhat. See the like Luk. 24. 29. Acts 16. 15.

*{but he refused}* Having good ground to refuse all recompense he would not be forced from his principles.

**V. 17. ***{And Naaman said, Shall there not then, I pray thee, be given to thy servant}* This is a form of an earnest desire, 2 Sam. 13. 26. He would do nothing without the prophet’s direction and approbation, and therefore makes this prayer unto him.

*{two Mules burden of earth}* Of mules see 1 King. 1. 33. Some think Naaman craved this earth out of Elisha’s house, because he asked him leave to carry it away: For if he had taken it otherwhere out of the land of Israel, he should rather have asked the king leave, or at least the owners of that land out of which he would take the earth. Or because he saw that the waters of Jordan were effectual upon the prophet’s charge to cure his leprosy, he might think that the earth also which the prophet gave him leave to carry away, might be as consecrated earth. But the words following show the reason why he desired to carry away that earth.

*{for thy servant will henceforth offer neither burnt offering nor sacrifice}* See 1 King. 3. 15. & 8. 62.

*{unto other gods}* This shows that having been before an idolater, by this miracle he was brought to see that Jehovah the God of Israel was the only true God, and the gods of other nations but false gods, to whom neither burnt offering nor sacrifice was to be offered up.

*{but unto the Lord}* Of this title *Lord* put for *Jehovah* see 1 King. 18. 21. This shows him to be a true convert, in that he turned from false gods to the only true Lord. And herein he was a type of the calling of the Gentiles, 1 Thess. 1. 9. Now this shows that the end of his desire of so much earth as is before mentioned was to make an altar thereof to the true God: for an altar might be made of earth, Exod. 20. 24. Howsoever the Jews were bound to go to the altar that was in the temple, and thereon to offer their sacrifices, yet we cannot say that Gentiles were tied thereunto. The Syrian would testify that he worshiped the God of Israel, by erecting an altar of the earth of Israel: and this altar, with his sacrificing thereon, should be a testimony to the Syrians among whom he lived that the God of Israel was the true Lord. We deny not but there might be much weakness in this new convert, and that his conceit of carrying that earth away, even for the forementioned use, savored of superstition; but his zeal was good, and the ends that he aimed at commendable.

**V. 18. ***{In this thing the Lord pardon}* Pardon supposes a fault. It is therefore a sin which he here acknowledges, and craves pardon for.

*{thy servant}* See v. 15.

*{that when my master}* The king of Syria: Of whom see v. 1.

*{goeth into the house of Rimmon}* Rimmon was the idol of the Syrians. The word signifies *a pomegranate*. It is derived from a root that implies *exaltation*: for they exalted their idol into the room of a god. This also is the name of a man, 2 Sam. 4. 2. of a city, Josh. 15. 32. of a rock, Judg. 20. 45.

*{to worship there}* The house of Rimmon was their idolatrous temple and place of worship.

*{and he leaneth on my hand}* It was Naaman’s place and office to be next the king, so as he used to lean on him on all occasions. The like is noted of a prince in Israel, ch. 7. 2. Whether the king went to the idol temple, or any whither else, Naaman must go with him. It may be for the time of his leprosy he forbore that duty; but now his leprosy is taken away, he conceived he must do it again. Thus was he brought to do outward reverence before the idol.

*{and I bow my self in the house of Rimmon}* In this phrase his worship towards the idol is set out. For the Hebrew word translated *bow down* is frequently used to set out divine worship, oft applied to the true God, as Psal. 5. 7. & 99. 9. So also to idols, Deut. 4. 19. Ezek. 8. 16.

*{when I bow down my self in the house of Rimmon}* He repeats the same phrase again, to show his detestation of the fact, and grief for the occasion of committing it.

*{the Lord pardon thy servant in this thing}* This also is a repetition, testifying an earnest desire of pardon, that his bowing might not be laid to his charge. Many take this as a purpose of the Syrian to join with idolaters in their outward worship: but the words do not necessarily imply any such purpose, but rather a supposition of some frailty; *q.d.* Though I purpose to worship no god but the God of Israel, yet if my master require me to accompany him into the house of Rimmon, and there, he leaning on me, I be caused to bow down, the Lord pardon me. Thus he accounts his bowing a sin. Besides, that which he speaks of was a matter belonging to his office; so as he had no purpose to worship the idol, but to continue his service to the king. But the words may also be taken of the time past, *q.d.* *In this thing the Lord pardon thy servant, that when my master went into the house of Rimmon to worship there, and leaned on my hand, and I bowed my self in the house of Rimmon; that I bowed my self in the house of Rimmon, the Lord pardon, &c.* The very words of this text are in other places translated by the time past, as בבא *when he went*, Psal. 51. 1. & 52. 1. & 54. 1. so, *leaned*, 2 Sam. 1. 6. and, *bowed down*, Exod. 33. 10. And by this translation many scruples and doubts are taken away, and all pretext for outward bowing before an idol removed. And this exposition seems most proper, because in the verse before Naaman protests that he would not offer unto any other god but unto the Lord.

**V. 19. ***{And he said unto him, Go in peace}* This is a usual farewell phrase, as Exod. 4. 18. and does not here imply an approbation of any sinful intention, but only a friendly dismission, without any reference to that which Naaman had said. Or if it have relation thereunto, what can it imply but an absolution, signifying that God had pardoned his former idolatry; or a consolation, intimating that God would bear with his frailty; or a direction, advising him not to trouble himself about bowing before an idol, but to remember his promise of worshiping the Lord.

*{So he departed from him a little way}* Heb. *a little piece of ground*. Some take it to be a mile, or thereabouts, Gen. 35. 16. His departure shows that he was fully satisfied about his cure, and about the prophet’s friendly dismission. The intimation how far he was departed has relation to the history following about Gehazi, who followed Naaman anon after he was out of Elisha’s sight.

**V. 20. ***{But Gehazi the servant of Elisha}* Such as are trained up under good masters do not always prove like their masters; witness Judas and Gehazi here.

*{the man of God}* See 1 King. 13. 1.

*{said}* Within himself, and to himself, as Mat. 9. 3.

*{Behold, my master hath spared Naaman}* Has not put him to that cost and charges for the cure he wrought upon him as he might have done.

*{this Syrian}* He adds this as a reason why his master might and should have taken that present which was offered him; namely, because it was offered by one that was not only a stranger but also an enemy to Israel. Thus he looks to Naaman’s former condition, and not to his present conversion; for covetousness had blinded his eyes.

*{in not receiving at his hands that which he brought}* See v. 16. This shows wherein Elisha spared Naaman.

*{but as the Lord liveth}* See 1 King. 1. 29. He binds himself to accomplish his impious purpose with a sacred oath, whereby he much aggravates his wickedness.

*{I will run after him}* This implies great speed. For the feet of wicked ones are swift to evil, Rom. 3. 15.

*{and take somewhat of him}* He runs to take what his master utterly refused, v. 16. and so, as much as in him lies, he takes away the glory of his master’s freeheartedness.

**V. 21. ***{So Gehazi followed after Naaman}* Evil men soon put in execution their evil intentions.

*{and when Naaman saw him running after him}* He discerned him to be Elisha’s servant, and supposed the speed he made implied some matter of importance.

*{he lighted down}* The Hebrew word signifies a quickness or speed in his action. So Gen. 24. 64. Josh. 15. 18.

*{from the Chariot to meet him}* Naaman here gives two evidences of the respect he bore to Elisha; One was, that at the first sight of him whom he supposed to be a messenger from Elisha, he lighted from his chariot. The other, that he turned back to meet him, and did not stay till the messenger should come to him.

*{and said, Is all well?}* Heb. *Is there peace?* See ch. 4. 23, 26. The Hebrews under this word *peace* comprise all prosperity.

**V. 22. ***{And he said, All is well}* Heb. *Peace*. Herein he answered directly to the question.

*{my master hath sent me, saying}* Both this and all the following parts of his answer are forged and false: and tendering them all in his master’s name, he does him the greater dishonor.

*{Behold}* He premises this, to make Naaman the more to heed his message.

*{even now}* Since you departed away from my master.

*{there be come to me}* He orders his speech as if the prophet himself had been speaking unto Naaman.

*{from mount Ephraim}* There was a college of prophets.

*{two young men of the sons of the Prophets}* Such were most fit to receive money and apparel, and Elisha would be most forward to relieve such, and therefore cunningly he feigns there were such come to him.

*{give them, I pray thee, a talent of silver}* It was no small sum that he asked; for a talent of silver is three hundred seventy-five pound sterling. See 1 Chron. 22. 14. Hereby he showed that he had a mind to purchase somewhat for himself and his posterity, as is intimated v. 26.

*{and two changes of garments}* See v. 5. He desired for each of the young men one garment.

**V. 23. ***{And Naaman said, Be content}* Be willing, and refuse not to take my offer.

*{take two talents}* That each of the young men might have one. This showed Naaman’s gratitude, liberality and bounty.

*{And he urged him}* See v. 16. It seems that Gehazi complementally refused to take more than one talent, which he said his master desired, cunningly pretending faithfulness.

*{and bound two talents of silver in two bags}* They must be great bags, that held three hundred seventy-five pound apiece.

*{with two changes of garments}* For so many were desired, v. 22.

*{and laid them upon two of his servants}* For one man could not well bear both the bags.

*{and they bare them before him}* i.e. before Gehazi, that he might see them go whither he would have them.

**V. 24. ***{And when he came to the tower}* Or, *secret place*. Heb. *Ophel*. It is oft used as the proper name of a place, 2 Chron. 27. 3. & 33. 14. Neh. 3. 26. & 11. 21. It signifies a high place made for safety; yea sometimes also a dark, obscure and hidden place. Here Gehazi lays what was brought, that it might be concealed from his master, and kept safely for his own use.

*{he took them from their hand}* i.e. the silver and garments which Naaman’s servants brought.

*{and bestowed them in the house}* The tower before mentioned was in that house where Elisha then abode. It may be it was a college where sons of the prophets were; or, Elisha’s own house.

*{and he let the men go}* He stayed them no longer than till they had delivered unto him the foresaid commodities.

*{and they departed}* Quietly and quickly, without staying for a reward.

**V. 25. ***{But he went in}* After he had made all sure, as he thought so as none should know it but himself.

*{and stood before his master}* With a bold impudent face, as if he had done nothing amiss. This phrase implies a readiness in a servant to do what his master shall command, 1 King. 1. 2.

*{and Elisha said unto him, Whence comest thou, Gehazi?}* He propounds his question familiarly calling him by his own name, to try if he would ingenuously tell the truth. It appears by this question, that he had been some while absent.

*{And he said, Thy servant}* This note of reverence this hypocrite complementally uses to make his master the less suspect him.

*{went no whither}* Heb. *not hither or thither*. Not to one place or other; not out of the house.

**V. 26. ***{And he said unto him, Went not my heart with thee, &c.}* Have you been so long with me, and know not that God has bestowed an extraordinary spirit upon me, whereby I can discern things done in my absence? Know that in spirit by a divine vision I saw you run after the man, and the man light out of his chariot to meet you. I saw what he gave you, and where you laid it; I tell you, I saw all, though you seek to conceal it from me.

*{Is it a time to receive money}* This implies that if the occasion, manner and time of receiving had been meet, simply to receive a gift had not been unlawful (See Matth. 18. 6. 1 Cor. 9. 12.) but, at this time it was most unlawful, because his master had refused it, and that upon good grounds. See v. 16. And his now receiving crossed all the ends his master aimed at in refusing the reward. Elisha and other prophets, living among idolaters, ought at that time to avoid all things that might any way dishonor their calling or disgrace their carriage among idolaters, so as it could not be but most unseasonable for Elisha’s servant at that time to receive gifts, which might be taken for bribes.

*{and to receive garments}* Such he had received, v. 23.

*{and oliveyards, and vineyards, and sheep, and oxen, and menservants, and maidservants?}* Elisha by a divine spirit might perceive that Gehazi had it in his thought to buy such things with the money he had of Naaman, and leaving Elisha’s service, to dwell alone, and have servants to attend him, and such enclosures and cattle as were fit for him. See v. 22. The manner of propounding the reproof interrogatively much aggravates Gehazi’s fault.

**V. 27. ***{The leprosy therefore of Naaman shall cleave unto thee}* God proportions the punishment answerably to the sin. Gehazi had a mind to be rich and glorious and in high account, but now a punishment is inflicted upon him which separates him from ordinary communion with men, and makes him seem base and abominable before all: He coveted Naaman’s goods, and gets his leprosy therewith; He had an impure mind, and answerably his body is punished.

*{and unto thy seed}* This may be taken both of the children he then had and of others that should proceed from him, in case he should have any after this. The meaning is, that the leprosy should so seize on him and his posterity as it should not be taken away. See 2 King. 2. 33. Yet as promises so threatenings had their limitations, ch. 20. 5. Jon. 3. 10. See ch. 8. 4.

*{for ever}* Generation after generation, 1 King. 8. 13.

*{And he went out from his presence a leper}* The judgment denounced was instantly executed. And because he was a leper, he could no longer abide in his master’s presence. See 2 Chron. 26. 20.

*{as white as snow}* The leprosy brings a white scurf upon the parts infected, Numb. 12. 10. Exod. 4. 6. Here we see the power of God manifested in his servant by inflicting leprosy, as well as by taking it away, ver. 14. So Numb. 12. 13. The like is often noted of Moses; he brought plagues on Egypt, and removed them. This then was Elisha’s eleventh miracle. See ch. 2. 14. Of doing contrary things by the same minister, see ch. 6. 18.

## CHAPTER 6

**Ver. 1. ***{And the sons of the Prophets said unto Elisha}* Ever since the rapture of Elijah he was the head and master of those in Israel which were called *sons of the Prophets*. See ch. 2. 15. & 1 King. 18. 4.

*{Behold now, the place where we dwell}* The college or schools where they met to hear Elisha instruct them, direct them, and resolve their doubts.

*{with thee}* Heb. *before thee*. For they which dwelt there stood as attendants before Elisha.

*{is too strait for us}* God so wrought upon many by the miracles and ministry of Elisha, as the number of his disciples daily increased more and more, so as the place where they were before was not spacious enough for them.

**V. 2. ***{Let us go, we pray thee, unto Jordan}* At Jordan there was not only conveniency of water, but also wood and other materials to build withal.

*{and take thence every man a beam}* Every one so much as he could roll down, and make fit to erect a meeting place withal. Or it may be they intended not only one spacious place to meet in, but also other rooms for habitation. This word *beam* is to be taken synecdochically for any materials fit for building.

*{and let us make us a place there}* Namely, at or by Jordan.

*{where we may dwell}* They intended not only one room for the prophet to read in, but also others to lodge in.

*{And he answered, Go ye}* He readily yielded to so just a desire.

**V. 3. ***{And one said, Be content, I pray thee}* The meaning is, that he would be willing to grant their desire. See ch. 5. 23.

*{and go with thy servants}* See ch. 2. 16. They expected some blessing from his presence, as Barak from Deborah’s, Judg. 4. 8. And they were not deceived in their expectation. See v. 6.

*{And he answered, I will go}* He was as ready to grant this desire as the former, v. 2. Willingly he let them go, and willingly he went with them.

**V. 4. ***{So he went with them: and when they came to Jordan, they cut down wood}* They were diligent to accomplish what they intended. It is probable some of these had sufficient skill to square and frame and fit timber for a building, and to cover it also, and make all fit for habitation; but here only mention is made of *cutting down wood*, because of the miracle v. 6. But surely the circumstances show that they erected no stately palace, but such buildings only as might be fit for mean persons to keep them from wind and weather.

**V. 5. ***{But as one was felling a beam}* Or, *a piece of timber*.

*{the ax-head}* Heb. *iron*. For all but the edge of it was of iron, that haply was steel.

*{fell into the water}* Iron being heavy quickly sinks to the bottom of a river: And if that be deep, it can hardly be got again by ordinary means.

*{and he cried, and said, Alas}* See ch. 3. 10.

*{Master}* As this in general is a note of reverence; so in particular it is here a note of that superiority and authority which Elisha had.

*{for it was borrowed}* Things borrowed must be restored. This made him the more desirous to recover it, and he was confident his master could get it again.

**V. 6. ***{And the man of God said, Where fell it?}* The prophet was ignorant of this circumstance. See ch. 4. 27.

*{and he shewed him the place}* His desire to have it again made him ready to do what was required.

*{and he cut down a stick and cast it in thither}* This stick was as the salt, chap. 2. 21. and the meal, chap. 4. 41. and the tree, Exod. 15. 25. Only a visible means of manifesting the power of God. The prophet using it, it became as a lodestone to draw the iron to it.

*{and the iron did swim}* Against its nature: So that this was Elisha’s twelfth miracle. See ch. 2. 14. Some Hebrew expositors say that Elisha made this piece of wood in fashion to the helve or handle of an ax, one end thereof being fit for the eye or hole in the head of the ax; and that this helve sunk to the bottom where that head was, and so both of them being fitted together rose up to the top of the water. Thus they make many miracles of one; but without warrant from the text.

**V. 7. ***{Therefore said he, Take it up to thee}* The prophet thought it not enough that they should see such an extraordinary work done, but also would have the man for whose sake it was done partake of the benefit thereof.

*{And he put out his hand, and took it}* It so floated on the top of the water and came so near to the bank, that putting out his hand he easily and readily took it.

**V. 8. ***{Then the king of Syria warred against Israel}* It seems this king bore an implacable hatred against Israel: He had written a letter to the king of Israel, and desired a great request of him, which was granted, ch. 5. 6, 14. yet he continues to war against him.

*{and took counsel with his servants}* i.e. counselors of state, or commanders in war, called *his servants* because his subjects.

*{saying, in such and such a place}* The Hebrew words are used to set out a certain but unnamed thing, whether person, as Ruth 4. 1. or place, as 1 Sam. 21. 2. and here. The first word is drawn from פלא *hid*; the other, from אלם *mute*: so as it was such a place as was secret, and they who lay therein were silent.

*{shall be my camp}* Heb. *encamping*. There he laid soldiers, as in an ambushment, having some intelligence that the king with his attendants would pass that way, and intending suddenly to surprise them.

**V. 9. ***{And the man of God sent unto the King of Israel}* Though the king of Israel were an idolater, yet the prophet had respect to the nation, which was of the seed of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, to whom a special covenant was made; and also to the true worshipers of God that in Israel were mixed among the idolaters; and withal to the insolency of the enemies, who if they had taken an advantage against the king of Israel would have blasphemed the God of Israel.

*{saying, Beware}* This admonition the prophet gave the king, to keep him out of the snare the enemy had laid for him.

*{that thou pass not such a place}* That you come not near it.

*{for thither the Syrians are come down}* This is the reason of the foresaid admonition. It seems the place was some low dale, because the Syrians are said to *descend* or *come down*, and that secretly to set upon, and to apprehend or slay such as should pass by. This manifestation of the king of Syria’s secret plots and practices was Elisha’s thirteenth miracle. See ch. 2. 14.

**V. 10. ***{And the King of Israel sent to the place which the man of God told him, and warned him of, and saved himself there}* By his scouts he discerned that to be true which the prophet had told him, and thereupon did forbear to go thither. Thus by avoiding his enemies’ snares he is said to *save himself*, and with himself such as should have accompanied him. This phrase of *saving himself* shows that it was the king’s person which the enemy especially aimed at, as 1 King. 22. 31.

*{not once nor twice}* i.e. many times: so as the enemy made many attempts to surprise the king.

**V. 11. ***{Therefore the heart of the king of Syria was sore troubled for this thing}* The word implies such a trouble in his soul as the sea uses to be troubled withal when strong storms and tempests blow upon it.

*{and he called his servants, and said unto them}* In his trouble he communicated his doubts to them. See v. 8.

*{Will you not shew me which of us}* Or, *which of them that belong to us*. It seems his ambushments were very closely carried, and none knew them but such as were of his special counsel.

*{is for the king of Israel}* Is in pay with him as an intelligencer, or so prefers him before us as to reveal our counsels to him.

**V. 12. ***{And one of his servants said, None}* Heb. *No*. You need not fear any of your own counselors; there is none so unfaithful and treacherous.

*{my lord O king}* This is a reverent and loving compellation given to his sovereign.

*{but Elisha the Prophet that is in Israel}* This servant had been in Israel with Naaman, or on some other occasion, and there heard of the extraordinary things that were done by Elisha; or else the fame of his great works was spread abroad in Syria, and this man had taken special notice thereof.

*{telleth the king of Israel the words that thou speakest in thy bedchamber}* By the report which he had heard of Elisha he knew he had the Spirit of God in him, and thereby could discern the most secret things that were done in any place.

**V. 13. ***{And he said, Go, and spy where he is}* The king’s mind was so blinded as he little thought of the divine spirit that was in Elisha, though evidence thereof was given unto him; but thinks if he might know where he was, he could deal well enough with him.

*{that I may send and fetch him}* He supposed he had to do but with a weak man, whom he could catch and fetch as it pleased him, and considered not that he who could save his king from danger could much more save himself.

*{And it was told him, saying}* Kings have those that are ready to do what they require.

*{Behold, he is in Dothan}* Dothan was a city near to Shechem and Samaria. Gen. 37. 13, 17.

**V. 14. ***{Therefore sent he thither horses, and chariots, and a great host}* Heb. *heavy host*. The king looked only to outward means, and considered not what power it was that guarded Elisha.

*{and they came by night}* Lest being espied, defense should be made both from the city and other parts of the kingdom.

*{and compassed the city about}* This shows it was a great host.

**V. 15. ***{And when the servant}* Or, *minister*, one whom Elisha had chosen to attend upon him in Gehazi’s room.

*{of the man of God was risen early and gone forth}* Upon some other occasions; not then fearing any such danger.

*{behold, an host compassed the city both with horses and chariots}* The king thought to make sure of the prophet.

*{and his servant said unto him}* As soon as he espied the enemies’ host, he ran back to his master to tell him thereof.

*{Alas, my master}* See v. 5.

*{how shall we do?}* A phrase of despair, not unlike that chap. 3. 10. He did not well consider the spirit and power of his master.

**V. 16. ***{And he answered, Fear not}* This is a phrase of encouragement, as Ex. 14. 13.

*{for they that be with us are more then they that be with them}* 2 Chron. 32. 7. This he said before his servant had seen what preparations God had made for their defense, that when he should see them, he might the rather know they came from God, because his master had made them known beforehand, Isa. 48. 5.

**V. 17. ***{And Elisha prayed, and said}* Prayer is a means not only of obtaining help in time of need, but also of enabling us well to discern and to make the best use of that help which God does afford us.

*{Lord, I pray thee, open his eyes that he may see}* It was a vision that was here represented to this servant, which without a special gift from God could not be perceived.

*{And the Lord opened the eyes of the young man}* This young man is the same that v. 15. was called *servant* or *minister*. *Minister* sets out his office; *young man* his age.

*{and he saw}* What God opens men’s eyes to see, they shall see: the eyes of the body being opened see corporal objects, Joh. 9. 6, 7. and the eyes of the mind, spiritual.

*{and behold, the mountain was full of horses and chariots of fire round about Elisha}* Dothan was at the bottom of a mountain, so as one in the city might see things on the mountain. Therefore in a vision Elisha appeared to this his servant on that mountain compassed about with fiery horses and chariots, which were no other than the angels of God, a great multitude of whom were together in manner of a host, like horses and chariots, because the enemy that besieged the city came with like preparation, v. 15. The mountain is said to be full of them by reason of their great number. They appeared to be of fire, to manifest their terror; so as with the sight of them all fear might be put out of the heart of this servant. This verifies that which is written, Ps. 34. 7. & 68. 17. & 91. 11. & Mat. 26. 53. See ch. 2. 11.

**V. 18. ***{And when they came down to him}* i.e. the host of the Syrians. They *came down*, for Dothan was in a low place. And the prophet being come out of the city, it is probable they came to him to inquire for Elisha, not knowing it was he.

*{Elisha prayed unto the Lord}* By this Elisha acknowledged that what he did or would do was from the Lord.

*{and said, Smite this people, I pray thee, with blindness}* The blindness here intended was not simply of the body, as if they could see nothing at all, but rather a blindness whereby the eyes of their mind and body both were so dazzled as they could not well distinguish things and persons in the true and proper kind thereof. Note Luk. 24. 16. Mark 8. 24. See Gen. 19. 11.

*{And he smote them with blindness, according to the word of Elisha}* See 1 Chron. 5. 20. God soon grants the desires of his servants, Jam. 5. 17, 18. Elisha by prayer procured the eyes of his servant to be opened, and the eyes of his enemies to be blinded: So as contrary works were wrought by the same person. See chap. 5. 27.

**V. 19. ***{And Elisha said unto them}* It is supposed they asked him which was the way to the city, and where they might find Elisha; and Elisha made answer as follows.

*{This is not the way, neither is this the city}* This is no proper equivocation, but a mere ambiguity of speech, which upon due heeding might have been found out. They asking the way to find Elisha, he directly tells them that was not it which they took; for Elisha was going to Samaria; nor that the city; for Elisha was come out of it. In such a sense John denied himself to be Elias, namely, as the Jews took Elias to be John, Joh. 1. 21.

*{follow me}* Heb. *come ye after me*. He was not known to them, and therefore he bid them the more boldly to follow him.

*{and I will bring you to the man whom ye seek}* He meant so to show himself to these men as they should discern and know him to be Elisha, whom they sought; herein therefore he declares a truth.

*{But}* Heb. *and*. The copulative best fits this place, for he intended to show himself to them in Samaria. If therefore we translate it, *and he led them*, it well follows upon his intent. The particle *but* implies that he pretended one place and led them to another.

*{he led them to Samaria}* Samaria was the chief city of the kingdom, where the king had his palace and court; where was a settled militia and such store of men and warlike furniture as was enough to overpower and destroy all these Syrians.

**V. 20. ***{And it came to pass when they were come into Samaria}* This proves that they were struck with such blindness as is noted, v. 18. because they followed Elisha into a place of such danger to themselves.

*{that Elisha said, Lord, open the eyes of these men that they may see}* Thus he undid what he had done before, as Jam. 5. 17, 18.

*{And the Lord opened their eyes, and they saw}* See v. 18. Besides the miracle of discovering the king of Syria’s plots and practices, v. 9. there are four other miracles mentioned in this history. 1. Angels coming down as a host for the defense of Elisha, v. 15. 2. The opening of his servant’s eyes to see them, v. 17. 3. The smiting of the Syrians with blindness, v. 18. 4. The opening of their eyes again, v. 20. And all these upon Elisha’s prayer. See ch. 2. 14.

*{and behold, they were in the midst of Samaria}* They distinctly saw and well discerned the danger wherein they were, being in the midst of their enemies; so as they could not but be much affrighted therewith.

**V. 21. ***{And the king of Israel said unto Elisha, when he saw them}* It is supposed that Elisha had beforehand sent a messenger to the king, to signify unto him that he was bringing such enemies into Samaria, and to advise him to have all his troops in readiness, that the enemy might be the more quailed. Hereupon the king thus speaks to Elisha.

*{My father}* Of this title *father* see ch. 2. 12. & 5. 13. Here the king gives it to the prophet but complementally and hypocritically; or, if he did it from his heart, he soon changed his mind, as appears, v. 31. Thus the Jews who cried unto Christ as he came into Jerusalem, *Hosanna*, Matt. 21. 15. after he had been a while in Jerusalem, cried, *Let him be crucified*, Matt. 27. 22.

*{shall I smite them?}* Elisha having brought them into the king’s power, the king would do nothing about them without his direction. He means such a *smiting* as should slay them.

*{shall I smite them?}* He doubles the phrase to testify the great joy he had in having them under his power, and the great desire he had to destroy them all.
