**V. 22. ***{And he answered, Thou shalt not smite them}* The prophet passed by that mischievous end for which the Syrians came, namely to carry him to his enemies; and the inveterate hatred that the whole nation did bear against Israel; and thought it punishment enough, that they saw themselves to be in the very pit of destruction. And now that they might live to declare the power of God, even among the enemies of God, he advises to spare them, and not to destroy them.

*{wouldest thou smite those whom thou hast taken captive}* This admits a double interpretation. 1. Men use not to kill such as in the field are taken captives, and stand not out in hostility; much less therefore ought the king to kill such as by God’s extraordinary providence are brought to him. 2. Though he might slay his own captives, yet he had no power over the Lord’s captives, but must deal with them according to that direction which he shall receive from the Lord: which direction follows afterwards.

*{with thy sword and with thy bow}* These were the two most usual weapons for war, wherewith enemies were vanquished, and therefore by name expressed. See 1 Chr. 5. 18.

*{set bread and water before them}* By *bread and water* all manner of sustenance is usually meant in Scripture, Deut. 23. 4. 1 Sam. 25. 11. Isa. 3. 1. Amos 8. 11.

*{that they may eat and drink}* The prophet here practices that which is commanded Prov. 25. 21. and overcomes evil with good, Rom. 12. 20.

*{and go to their master}* That so they might declare to him what forbearance and courtesy they had found in Israel, notwithstanding his enmity against Israel.

**V. 23. ***{And he prepared great provision for them}* The king royally feasted them, as if they had been ambassadors with an honorable retinue, and that from confederate princes; and herein he showed his respect to the Lord’s prophet.

*{and when they had eaten and drunk}* These enemies observed such entire welcome as they cast off all fear, and gladly accepted their royal entertainment, intimated under the phrase of eating and drinking.

*{he sent them away}* Neither openly nor secretly did he enterprise any mischief against them, but dismissed them in peace.

*{and they went to their master}* No evil at all was done them, but they returned in safety to him that sent them forth.

*{So the bands of Syria came no more into the land of Israel}* There is some difficulty in this place by reason of the history immediately following; but it may be taken in three distinct respects: 1. Of the bands of Syria; These very bands that were so courteously entertained never after this came as enemies against Israel. 2. Of the manner of coming; The bands of Syria made no more such inroads into Israel, nor laid such ambushments as are mentioned ver. 9. This they feared lest by the prophet they should be discovered and surprised. 3. Of the time; For a long time the bands of Syria came not into Israel.

**V. 24. ***{And it came to pass after this}* After the forementioned courtesy. See v. 14. 23. But how long it was after this is not expressed.

*{that Benhadad King of Syria}* It is very probable it was the same Benhadad that was twice overthrown by Ahab, 1 King. 20. 20, 25, 29. and by whose army Ahab was slain, 1 King. 22. 34. Then observe God’s just judgment still following the Israelites for letting Benhadad go when he had delivered him into their hands. See 1 King. 20. 35. & 1 Sam. 15. 19.

*{gathered all his host}* By experience he had found that it was in vain to send some troops or regiments against Israel, and therefore he gathers together as great a host as he could; and with it in a manner dared the God of Israel, as if that God who had caused sundry of his troops to be defeated of their purpose, could not disappoint that great army.

*{and went up and besieged Samaria}* Of Samaria see v. 20. & 1 Kin. 16. 24. Though his subjects had found extraordinary courtesy in Samaria, yet is he no whit moved therewith, but besieges even Samaria. See v. 19, 23.

**V. 25. ***{And there was a great famine in Samaria}* By reason of the long siege, against which they were not well provided with victuals. But the principal cause was their idolatry and other sins.

*{and behold, they besieged it}* They continued so to beleaguer the city as none could come out for provision, or enter in with provision.

*{until an asses head}* Ass’s flesh is no good food: by the law it was unclean. See Exod. 13. 13. Yet here by reason of the extremity of famine, it was not only eaten, but bought at a great price. Some think the ass’s head is synecdochically put for the whole ass: but it is not probable that in so great a famine so much flesh should be sold together. It is safest to take the phrase literally; and so it will better agree with the price of that which follows. Indeed there could be but little meat in an ass’s head; but in such a famine a little was made to go far.

*{was sold for fourscore pieces of silver}* The word *pieces* is not in the Hebrew. Now where a sum of silver or gold is mentioned, and no particular quantity expressed, there shekels ordinarily be understood. Now a shekel of silver was with the Jews as much as two shillings sixpence with us: By which account an ass’s head was sold for ten pound sterling. See 1 Chr. 22. 14.

*{and the fourth part of a kab}* A kab is a Hebrew measure containing about a pottle of our measure, so as the fourth part of a kab was about a pint.

*{of doves dung}* That which is here translated *doves dung* may be taken for the corn which a dove flying out of the field into the city might bring with her in her crop; Or, for that corn which came out with the dove’s dung; Or, as most take it, for the very dung itself that came from them, which the extremity of famine forced men to eat. And this may seem the more credible, because it is implied ch. 18. 27. that men by famine may be brought to eat their own dung and drink their own piss. Some to mollify this, say, that pigeon’s dung was then bought for salt to season things withal, or for fuel to dress things with. But we may well think that such as were forced to eat their own children, as v. 28. might much more be forced to eat as bad as dove’s dung.

*{for five pieces of silver}* i.e. twelve shillings and sixpence.

**V. 26. ***{And as the king of Israel was passing by upon the wall}* The king himself, in regard of his own good, could not in such an extremity be idle or careless of the state of the city. Therefore he oft passed up and down upon the wall, to see how the city remained fenced, how the soldiers kept their ward, which way they might send out for provision, how the enemy lay, what batteries might be providing against the city, and other like things which were carefully to be looked unto.

*{there cried a woman unto him, saying}* It is probable that she observed how the king day after day passed by upon the wall, and watching her opportunity made her complaint unto him.

*{Help, my Lord, O king}* See v. 12. Her case was such, and all other magistrates so for themselves, as she knew not whom to go unto but her sovereign. She propounds her case indefinitely in this word *help*, that she might the rather move him to hearken to her.

**V. 27. ***{And he said, If the Lord do not help thee}* Heb. *Let not the Lord save thee*. According to the former reading it implies a disability in him to afford her any help, in that the Lord did not help her; for he supposed she had called unto him for food: and thus it has relation to that which follows; *Whence shall I help thee?* Can I do more than God? He shows it was in vain for her or any other of his subjects to seek sustenance of him, God affording to him or to any in the city. According to the latter reading, *Let not the Lord save thee*, it is an imprecation or a curse, as if the king should wish some vengeance upon her for troubling him in that extremity.

*{out of the barn-floor, or out of the wine-press}* Out of the barn all manner of corn used to be brought for bread; and from the winepress, wine, which was an ordinary drink in those countries: *q.d.* I know not where to have either bread or drink.

**V. 28. ***{And the king said unto her, What aileth thee?}* It is probable the woman, upon the king’s hasty and wrathful answer, told him it was neither bread nor wine she came for, but rather justice: And that thereupon the king should say, *What aileth thee?* What is the cause why you complain to me?

*{and she answered, This woman}* It seems both the women betwixt whom the quarrel was, came together to the king, as 1 King. 3. 16.

*{said unto me}* There was a compact between the two women to eat their children in order, one after another.

*{Give thy son, that we may eat him to day}* Upon the agreement it fell out that the plaintiff’s son should be first eaten.

*{and we will eat my son to morrow}* This was the condition that made her yield to the agreement. Seldom has there been heard the like, that mothers should eat their own children. Here was an accomplishment of God’s threatening, Levit. 26. 29. Deut. 28. 53. And it was a great aggravation of this famine. See Lam. 4. 10. Ezek. 5. 10.

**V. 29. ***{So we boiled my son, and did eat him}* Extremity of hunger makes men and women forget natural affection. We read of some that ate their own flesh, Isa. 9. 20.

*{and I said unto her on the next day}* Heb. *other day*. The Hebrew word does not imply that they kept the child no longer than for one day’s meat.

*{Give thy son, that we may eat him}* She that had not spared her own son would not by any means have the other’s spared.

*{and she hath hid her son}* Either to keep him alive, or else to eat him alone.

**V. 30. ***{And it came to pass when the king heard the words of the woman}* Words able to move the hardest heart.

*{that he rent his clothes}* See ch. 2. 12. & 5. 7.

*{and as he passed by upon the wall}* See v. 26.

*{the people looked}* They were moved the more to heed him because of the manifestation of his passion.

*{and behold, he had sackcloth within}* His upper garment, which was a long robe being rent, the sackcloth under the same was clearly discerned. See 1 King. 21. 27.

*{upon his flesh}* It seems he wore sackcloth next his skin. This the Jews used to do both to testify their unworthiness of any soft or fine apparel, and also to make them the more watchful. See on Gen. 37. 34.

**V. 31. ***{Then he said, God do so and more also to me}* A form of an oath anciently much used. Though he had a most wicked intention, yet he binds himself by a solemn imprecation against his own soul suddenly to accomplish it. So Acts 23. 14. See ch. 5. 20. And herein as he manifested much anger and passion, so also great rashness and unadvisedness.

*{if the head of Elisha the son of Shaphat}* See 1 King. 19. 16.

*{shall stand on him this day}* No reason is rendered by him why he should thus proceed against Elisha; only upon hearing an instance of the extremity of the famine, v. 29. his spirit is thus stirred up against him. But many probable conjectures may be made. He might think 1. That Elisha was the cause of this famine, as his master Elijah had been of the drought in his father’s time, 1 King. 17. 1. Or 2. that Elisha before this by his prayers might have removed the army, v. 8. Or 3. that Elisha could have procured food in this extremity, as well as he procured rain, ch. 3. 17. Or 4. that if the Syrians that by Elisha’s direction were dismissed, v. 23. had been slain, Benhadad might have forborne to have besieged the city. Or 5. Elisha having promised the king that God would deliver the city, but he finding extremity of famine, and despairing of deliverance, and thinking himself mocked by Elisha, is thus enraged against him. Or 6. the idolatrous prophets and others having before this instigated him to take revenge on Elisha, he now took this occasion to do it. See 1 King. 18. 17.

**V. 32. ***{But Elisha sat in his house}* Either Elisha had a house of his own in Samaria, or else he was entertained in some friend’s house as in his own. Though by the Spirit he knew the king’s resolution, yet his faith in God makes him sit quietly in his house, not fearing what he could do, Psal. 118. 6, &c.

*{and the elders sat with him}* These were such as feared God, and came to the prophet for counsel and comfort in that extremity; called *elders* either in regard of age, or office, or prudence, or it may be in all these respects.

*{and the king sent a man from before him}* From his presence, or from himself; namely, to cut off Elisha’s head.

*{but ere the messenger came to him}* To make known the king’s mind.

*{he said to the Elders}* He understood by that spirit which God gave him the king’s purpose, even as he knew the king of Syria’s plots, v. 12. and Gehazi’s practice, ch. 5. 26.

*{See ye how this son of a murderer}* i.e. Joram the son of Ahab, who was an occasion of the death of Naboth and his children, 1 King. 21. 19. and had suffered the Lord’s prophets to be slain, 1 King. 18. 4. This aggravates the murderous mind of Joram; Mat. 23. 35.

*{hath sent to take away my head}* Though I have deserved no such thing at his hands. See the difference betwixt Joram’s former affection to Elisha and his affection now, v. 21.

*{Look when the messenger cometh, shut the door, and hold him fast at the door}* Saints may prevent evil as much as they can, so they do it by lawful and warrantable means. And to restrain wicked ones from executing mischief is a benefit to them as well as to those that escape the danger.

*{Is not the sound of his masters feet behind him?}* This implies that the king himself followed hard after the messenger whom he sent. See 1 King. 14. 6. The interrogative puts the matter out of question, *q.d.* Assuredly his master is near at hand. Some think the king was presently touched in conscience for that ungrateful and horrible fact, and thereupon came speedily after the messenger to hinder the performance. And certainly God can quickly change cruel men’s minds, Gen. 31. 29. & 33. 4. 1 Sam. 24. 17. & 26. 17.

**V. 33. ***{And while he yet talked with them}* While Elisha was speaking to the elders that were with him.

*{behold, the messenger came down unto him}* Either he came only to the door, and was not suffered to come in, as v. 32. or the king had overtaken him, and given him a countermand, that he should do no hurt to Elisha.

*{and he said}* Either the messenger in the king’s name uttered the king’s words, or the king himself, who had prevented the messenger, spoke as follows.

*{Behold, this evil is of the Lord}* i.e. The famine, together with the effects that followed thereupon, as mothers eating their own children, &c. That evil, as it is a punishment of sin, is from the Lord, is a sound orthodox principle, Isa. 45. 7. Amos 3. 6. but to lay it as Joram does, to the Lord’s charge, as a matter of oppression and injustice, is impious and blasphemous.

*{what should I wait for the Lord any longer?}* This argued much diffidence and despair in this impious prince. It seems Elisha had promised succor from the Lord, which made him wait some while: But when he saw the great extremity of famine, in a desperate distrust he said, *What should I wait, &c.*

## CHAPTER 7

**Ver. 1. ***{Then Elisha said, Hear ye the word of the Lord}* He spoke to the king, v. 7. and to the princes that accompanied him, and to the elders mentioned ch. 6. 32.

*{Thus saith the Lord}* See 1 King. 12. 24.

*{To morrow about this time}* Because the king manifested a wearisomeness in waiting, the prophet, to strengthen his faith, sets down a set and short time for sufficient succor. And herein God’s free grace and rich mercy was clearly manifested. For though the king was so far from being brought to repentance by that heavy judgment of famine as that he added sin to sin, and showed great impatience against God and cruelty against his prophet, yet the Lord considering their extremity promised present relief.

*{shall a measure of fine flour}* סאה *Seah* translated *measure*, held a peck and a pottle and somewhat more. Three of them make one of our bushels. He mentioned *flour* not *meal*, and *fine flour*, which was finely sifted, to amplify the miracle.

*{be sold for a shekel}* See 1 King. 10. 16. & 1 Chron. 22. 14.

*{and two measures of barley for a shekel}* Barley is a coarser grain than that of which the flour before mentioned was made; which it is supposed was wheat. Compare these commodities and prices with those mentioned ch. 6. 25. and you shall find a great difference betwixt them.

*{in the gate of Samaria}* The soldiers that went out and took those commodities for a booty, brought them to the gate of Samaria, and there sold them to the people. This circumstance of the place ratifies the truth of the prophecy, as well as the former of the time.

**V. 2. ***{Then a lord on whose hand the king leaned}* Heb. *a lord which belonged to the king leaning upon his hand*. The word translated *lord* imports *the third from the King*, or *one of the three great officers of state*, such are mentioned Dan. 6. 2. In Latin he may be called *triumvir*. He is supposed to be the man whom the king sent to kill Elisha, ch. 6. 31, 32. For great ones were wont to be so employed 1 King. 2. 29. Of the king’s leaning on an officer’s hand, see ch. 5. 18.

*{answered the man of God, and said}* See 1 King. 13. 1. Though the king and others present rested silent at the promise of the prophet, as believing the truth thereof, yet this incredulous prince opposes it.

*{Behold, if the Lord would make windows in heaven}* The word signifies *wide open places* to pour down corn in abundance, as water flowing through a sluice, Gen. 7. 11. He thought it not only impossible that such store of provision could be got on earth in so short a time, but also makes mention of heaven, as if from thence God could not send it. Either he thought not of the manna which God rained down Exod. 16. 4. Or he opposes this promise against that providence, *q.d.* Though God in the wilderness rained bread from heaven, yet now he cannot so do, Ps. 78. 20.

*{might this thing be?}* This interrogative imports an utter impossibility: so as this faithless and profane prince does not only question the prophet’s word but also God’s power.

*{And he said}* Elisha replied to that prince.

*{Behold}* This particle is premised to assure him of the certainty both of the public blessing and his particular judgment.

*{thou shalt see it with thine eyes}* You yourself shall be an eyewitness of the truth of what I have said. See v. 16, 17.

*{but shalt not eat thereof}* Infidelity deprives men of the benefit of God's promises which others enjoy. Moses himself saw Canaan, but entered not thereinto, Deut. 3. 27. See the accomplishment of the threatening against this prince, v. 20.

**V. 3. ***{And there were four leprous men}* The Jews say these were Gehazi and his three sons.

*{at the entering in of the gate}* Without the gate, according to the law, Lev. 13. 46. yet near unto it, for fear of the enemies.

*{and they said one to another, Why sit we here until we die?}* They had continued as long as they could get any food, but now having nothing to sustain them they consult among themselves as follows: This is an evidence of the hardheartedness of them that were in Samaria.

**V. 4. ***{If we say, We will enter into the city}* Thus they reason, not as if they could enter in if they would, but as putting the case, if they might enter into the city.

*{then the famine is in the city}* This would do us no good, because we should not find any food there.

*{and we shall die there; and if we sit still here, we die also}* For want of sustenance.

*{Now therefore come, and let us fall unto the host of the Syrians}* Though they be enemies, let us commit ourselves to their mercy. In that desperate case they incite one another to a desperate course.

*{if they save us alive, we shall live}* They supposed that in that miserable plight wherein they were by reason of leprosy and hunger, they might find some mercy from the enemy.

*{and if they kill us, we shall but die}* They put the worst that could fall out, and thence infer that it was better for them to be slain by the enemy than to perish with hunger.

**V. 5. ***{And they rose up in the twilight}* There are two twilights, one in the morning, at the break of day, Mat. 28. 1. the other in the evening, about an hour after sunset, Prov. 7. 9. This was the evening twilight, v. 9, 12.

*{to go unto the camp of the Syrians}* To try what succor they might there find.

*{and when they were come to the uttermost part of the camp of Syria, behold, there was no man there}* So soon as ever they came to the place where the host lay, they discerned that all were gone from thence.

**V. 6. ***{For the Lord had made the host of the Syrians to hear a noise}* This was some hideous noise that struck them all into great fear, yet it went no further than to the ears of these Syrians: for none in the city heard it, nor yet these four lepers. It was therefore supernatural, and the eighteenth miracle of Elisha. See ch. 2. 14. We read of the like 2 Sam. 5. 24. and a judgment not unlike to this is recorded ch. 19. 7, 9.

*{of chariots, and a noise of horses, even the noise of a great host}* There sounded in their ears the trampling and neighing of many horses, the running of many chariots, the shouting of soldiers, the sound of trumpets and the beating of drums.

*{and they said one to another}* Heb. *a man to his brother.* As all men in relation to their first father are brethren, so they that are of the same nation, and particularly of the same employment, use to be styled brethren. They were at their wits' end, and knew not what to think, and therefore inquire after one another's opinion; and most of them agreed in that conceit which follows.

*{Lo, the King of Israel hath hired against us}* They had no ground to think that the king of Israel had done such a thing, but only they imagined such a thing might be, because they knew not what else to think of that matter.

*{the Kings of the Hittites}* The ancient Hittites were destroyed by the Israelites according to God's charge, Gen. 15. 20. Josh. 12. 7, 8. yet some of them might remain and grow into a nation; or some other people of islands may be here meant.

*{and the Kings of the Egyptians}* The Egyptians were a mighty and warlike people, much feared of most kingdoms: Under these the Ethiopians may be comprised; and in that respect the word *Kings* in the plural number is used.

*{to come upon us}* Suddenly to surprise and destroy us.

**V. 7. ***{Wherefore they arose and fled}* That former conceit had possessed the mind of them all so as they dared not stand against them, but *arose and fled*, i.e., speedily and hastily made away as fast as they could.

*{in the twilight}* This must needs be but a little before the lepers came. Had it been in the morning twilight, certainly in that day some of the watchmen in the towers would have espied the enemy running away. This shows how seasonably the Lord orders fit occurrences to fall out together.

*{and left their tents}* For they could find no safety in them.

*{and their horses and their asses}* For they thought they had no time to saddle them, and so fit them for their flight.

*{even the camp as it was, and fled for their life}* Imagining there was no way to escape. For the Israelites were in a strong defensed city before them; And if the enemy had come upon them behind, how should they have escaped?

**V. 8. ***{And when these lepers came to the uttermost part of the camp}* See v. 5.

*{they went into one tent}* The first they came at.

*{and did eat and drink}* Refreshed themselves, being almost hunger-starved.

*{and carried thence silver and gold, and raiment}* That therewith they might provide for themselves, even in after-times.

*{and went and hid it}* Fearing lest any of the Syrians should espy them, they would take away all from them.

*{and came again, and entered into another tent}* Their former good success in one tent made them venture into another.

*{and carried thence also, and went and hid it}* Their covetous mind made them still to lay up more and more. Neither the mortal disease that was upon them made them cease hoarding up, nor yet their knowledge of the need of their brethren in the city made them as yet think upon them.

**V. 9. ***{Then they said one to another}* Now they begin something better to think of the matter.

*{We do not well}* Upon sight of their fault, they confess it one to another.

*{this day is a day of good tidings}* They now discerned that all the enemies were fled, which could not but be gladsome tidings to their brethren in the city, who had been so long besieged, and brought to that extremity which is mentioned ch. 6. 25, 28.

*{and we hold our peace}* Thus they check themselves, for not making known to the city that good news which God at first brought to their notice.

*{if we tarry till the morning-light}* So as they out of the city may discern that the enemies are fled.

*{some mischief will come upon us}* Heb. *we shall find punishment.* Or, *iniquity will find us.* The word signifies both *iniquity* and *punishment*. Their meaning is, that the truth of the matter being found out, and they known to have notice thereof, they should be accounted evildoers, and accordingly punished. See Num. 32. 23. Subjects are bound to make known such good things as concern the whole state, upon pain of disloyalty.

*{now therefore come}* They stir up themselves to communicate that good whereof themselves had been partakers.

*{that we may go and tell the Kings household}* This was the fairest way, and best becoming prudent subjects, to make known a matter of such public concernment first to the king and his counselors with him, that they might the better order the same for the common good of all.

**V. 10. ***{So they came, and called to the porter of the city}* For the city being shut up, he was the first man to whom first they could declare the matter.

*{and they told them, saying}* It appears that there were watchmen attending on the gate, together with the porter. In which respect this word *them* is used. *They*, i.e., the four lepers, to the porter and the watchmen that which follows.

*{We came to the camp of the Syrians}* This shows how they came to know what they do declare.

*{and behold, there was no man there}* We could see none at all.

*{neither voice of man}* We could not hear any man speak or make any noise.

*{but horses tied, and asses tied}* Tied to the manger, not saddled nor fitted to be ridden on.

*{and the tents as they were}* Furnished with beds, meat, drink, and other necessaries.

**V. 11. ***{And he}* The chief keeper of the city-gate, to whom the lepers first told the case.

*{called the porters}* The keepers of the gate of the king's palace.

*{and they told it to the Kings house within}* The king's porters went into the palace, and told the king's household that they might tell to the king the foresaid good tidings.

**V. 12. ***{And the King arose in the night}* Upon the hearing of so good tidings, though it were anon after he was warm in his bed, he arose out of his bed, more thoroughly to consider of the matter.

*{and said unto his servants}* Such as were of his privy council. See ch. 6. 8.

*{I will now shew you what the Syrians have done to us}* This was an apprehension of his own, which he makes known to his counselors, to try whether they were also of his mind.

*{They know that we be hungry}* Thereupon they may think that we will adventure upon anything to get food.

*{therefore are they gone out of the camp}* To lay a bait for us, and to allure us to come thereunto.

*{to hide themselves in the field}* In some secret place, where we may not know where they are.

*{saying, When they come out of the city}* Leaving their gates open.

*{we shall catch them alive}* Coming suddenly upon them, and so surprising them.

*{and get into the city}* After we have got their soldiers that come out into our tents, we shall easily make all in the city yield unto us. This shows that he was possessed with wonderful great fear, and either forgot or believed not the prophet's promise, v. 1. which was the very next day to be accomplished. Fear made him suspect more than was meet; but faith would have made him hope for some good thing from this news.

**V. 13. ***{And one of his servants}* See v. 16.

*{answered and said, Let some take, I pray thee, five of the horses that remain}* It seems all the horses in the city either died for want of provender, or were eaten up for want of other food, except five of the king's horses. These are said to remain.

*{which are left in the city}* Heb. *in it.* This particle *it* has reference indeed to the city, and therefore is here well expressed by our English.

*{Behold, they are as all the multitude of Israel that are left in it}* Under this word *multitude* are comprised men and horses. Now they were all ready to perish for want of sustenance; so as if they should be taken by the enemy, it would be no great loss, seeing they that tarried were like all to perish.

*{Behold, I say, they are even as all the multitude of the Israelites}* Because the case was too too evident and apparently true, he sticks not to press the same words again. So Gal. 1. 9.

*{that are consumed}* Are so pined away as they are ready to perish. This he says to answer a secret objection: For it might have been said, There being left but few horses in the city, even they also will be lost by sending them to the camp. But this answer shows that if so, it would be no great loss.

*{and let us send and see}* Upon the forementioned ground this counselor persuades the king to make a trial, by sending out some to espy how the camp of the enemies stood.

**V. 14. ***{They took therefore two chariot-horses}* Two that were wont to draw a chariot. It is to be supposed they set a man upon each horse.

*{and the King sent after the host of the Syrians}* The counsel seeming good to the king, he readily yields to it.

*{saying, Go and see}* Observe whether that which the lepers have said be true or no.

**V. 15. ***{And they went after them unto Jordan}* Jordan was a great and deep river; and finding no enemies betwixt Samaria and it, nor any place where they might lurk within that compass, but rather many evidences of their running away, they might well conclude they were fled.

*{and lo, all the way was full of garments}* Which they cast off that they might not hinder them in their flight.

*{and vessels}* The word is put for sundry things useful to man; as for vessels which hold liquor, Est. 1. 7. instruments of music, Isa. 22. 24. instruments of iron, Num. 35. 16. hunting instruments, Gen. 27. 3. warlike instruments, Gen. 49. 5. Here it is implied that they cast away all manner of utensils that they had. See 1 King. 10. 21.

*{which the Syrians had cast away in their haste}* Fear made them fly with all the speed they could, 2 Sam. 4. 4.

*{and the messengers returned and told the King}* After they had evidence of the enemies' flight, they did not plunder for themselves, as the lepers, v. 8. but presently brought word to the king how the case with all stood.

**V. 16. ***{And the people went out}* By *the people* are here meant the soldiers that were sent forth.

*{and spoiled the tents of the Syrians}* I.e., took all the provision they had left, and what else was fit to be carried away.

*{So a measure of fine flour was sold, &c. according to the word of the Lord}* Made known to Israel by the prophet of the Lord, v. 1. This shows that the sudden change of scarceness to plenty, was miraculous, because it was foretold. For simply to foretell a thing is a miracle. So as this was Elisha's nineteenth miracle. See ch. 2. 14.

**V. 17. ***{And the King appointed the Lord on whose hand he leaned, to have the charge of the gate}* There to stand, and to keep the people from a tumultuous running out of the city, which he feared they might do upon the news of so great plenty. This was the cause of that prince's death, and ordered by the divine providence to fulfill what was foretold v. 2.

*{and the people trod upon him}* Hunger makes men break through stone walls. All the authority and power this prince had could not restrain the people.

*{in the gate}* The soldiers bringing all manner of provision to the gate of the city, as v. 1. the people would not be kept from going out; but thrust so hastily, eagerly and disorderly, as they threw down the prince that stood in the gate, and trod upon him.

*{and he died}* His very breath was pressed out of his body by the people's trampling upon him.

*{as the man of God had said}* Though so much were not uttered in express terms, yet so much was implied when the prophet said, *Thou shalt not eat thereof:* for certainly had he lived, he would have had a good share in that provision, and have eaten thereof. This is a clear evidence of God's providence in bringing to pass what himself has foretold. And it is just that he which will not believe the truth of a promise should not partake of the benefit thereof. This also being according to Elisha's prediction may be accounted his twentieth miracle. See ch. 2. 14.

*{who spake when the King came down to him}* This plainly shows that the king himself was present when the prophet foretold this plenty, v. 1.

**V. 18. ***{And it came to pass, as the man of God had spoken to the King}* This has relation to the beginning of v. 20. that *it so fell out.*

*{saying, Two measures of barley, &c.}* Compare v. 18, 19. with v. 1, 2. and you shall find that they agree in phrases, and so give evidence of God's truth in accomplishing the words of his prophet, both of promise and of threatening. A promise is made, v. 1. the accomplishment whereof is showed v. 18. A threatening is denounced v. 2. and accomplished v. 19, 20.

**V. 20. ***{And so it fell out unto him: for the people trod upon him in the gate, and he died}* See v. 17, 18.

## CHAPTER 8

**Ver. 1. ***{Then}* Though that which follows v. 3, etc. be after the succor afforded to Samaria, ch. 7. 1, etc. yet this prediction may well be supposed to have been long before, and that the famine here mentioned was not occasioned yet much increased by the Syrians' frequent inroads into the land of Israel, where they committed much spoil, and took away or spoiled all store of food. And the famine in Samaria mentioned ch. 6. 25. was the greater because of the famine throughout the whole land. So as that great famine in Samaria might be even then when this general famine was.

*{spake Elisha unto the woman}* The Shunamite, ch. 4. 8, 9, etc.

*{whose son he had restored to life}* Chap. 4. 35. This being an extraordinary work, and a very great blessing bestowed upon her, she is described thereby, as a memorial thereof.

*{saying, Arise, and go}* See 1 King. 14. 2. She had showed great kindness to Elisha, ch. 4. 8, etc. therefore he does what kindness he can for her. See ch. 4. 13.

*{thou and thine household}* Heb. *house.* A metonymy.

*{and sojourn wheresoever thou canst sojourn}* To sojourn is to live as a stranger in a place. His meaning is, that she should inquire where sufficient provision was to be had, and there seek to be entertained for her money. This could not be in her own country.

*{for the Lord hath called for a famine}* This shows that famines, and so other judgments, are at God's command. So Psal. 105. 16. Jer. 25. 29. Hag. 1. 11. A centurion acknowledged as much of Christ's power over diseases, Mat. 8. 9.

*{and it shall also come}* The power of God's command is such as even senseless creatures yield thereto. Witness the ten Egyptian plagues, Psal. 105. 28, etc.

*{upon the land}* This famine was not only in Samaria, as ch. 6. 25. but throughout the whole kingdom of Israel.

*{seven years}* A very long time for a famine to last, as Gen. 41. 27. 2 Sam. 24. 13. In David's time there was a famine three years, 2 Sam. 21. 1. in Elijah's, three years and six months, Luk. 4. 25. in the time of the judges, ten years, Ruth 1. 2, 4. When men continue in sin impenitently, and so remove not the causes of judgments, no marvel if famine or other plagues long continue. The famine, 1 King. 17. 1. the want of water, ch. 3. 9. moved not the king and his subjects to depart from their gross sins; therefore God adds a longer famine.

**V. 2. ***{And the woman arose}* She believed the prophet's word, and thereupon quickly bestirred herself, and arose.

*{and did after the saying of the man of God}* See 1 King. 13. 1. She testified her obedience to God, and care for herself.

*{and she went with her household}* She had as great care of her family as of herself, 1 Tim. 5. 8.

*{and sojourned in the land of the Philistines}* It is very probable the famine was also in the land of Judah, or else questionless this pious matron would have sought to have sojourned there, and not among the enemies of God's people. Or it may be that at this time there was peace betwixt the Philistines and Israel. Isaac in a like case did the like, Gen. 26. 1. and David upon another occasion, 1 Sam. 17. 1, 2. and Joseph, Mat. 2. 14.

**V. 3. ***{And it came to pass at the seven years end}* Though God suffer his judgments long to lie upon a people, yet at length he is pleased to remove them.

*{that the woman returned out of the land of the Philistines}* Necessity had forced her to abide there: but now her mind was on her own country.

*{and she went forth to cry unto the King}* After she was returned into Israel. It was usual of old to make complaints to kings, 1 King. 3. 16, 17.

*{for her house and for her land}* In her absence either the king or his officers, or some other great man had seized on her house and land; or her kindred had laid claim thereto: Some way or other it was withheld from her. Sundry circumstances do evince that this woman was now a widow.

**V. 4. ***{And the King talked with Gehazi}* Gehazi was infected with leprosy, ch. 5. 27. whereupon a great doubt is made about the king's talking with him. For a leper was to be put out of the camp, Lev. 13. 46. Some think this history was acted before that of Naaman, ch. 5. 1, etc. and then there is no doubt about that point, because Gehazi was not then a leper. But there is no reason to alter the order of the histories upon that ground. For though the law commanded that the leper should dwell alone without the camp, yet it no way forbids one that is clean to talk with a leper. Besides, Gehazi upon sight of God's displeasure against him and judgment upon him for his sin might repent, and upon his repentance have his judgment removed, and be cleansed. And it was an evidence of a good disposition, that notwithstanding the plague of leprosy inflicted on him by his master, he speaks honorably of him, and faithfully declares the great works which he had done.

*{the servant of the man of God}* See ch. 4. 12. 1 King. 13. 1.

*{saying, Tell me, I pray thee, all the great things that Elisha hath done}* The histories going before do clearly show what wonderful great things that prophet had done. Twenty miracles of his have been expressly noted, ch. 7. 17. See ch. 2. 14. It appears that a great part of the time of the seven years' famine Elisha was at Damascus, v. 7. and so not in Israel to work more miracles there. But a great fame there was of those which he had done: Some this king himself had seen, ch. 3. 20. & 7. 6, etc. Others were famous everywhere almost: and the king hearing a general rumor of many of them, desired to be distinctly instructed in the particulars by this servant of the prophet, who had been an eyewitness of most of them.

**V. 5. ***{And it came to pass as he was telling the King how he had restored a dead body to life}* This was a great work indeed, and to a natural man's apprehension incredible; thereof God so disposed the falling out of matters as the king had an undoubted evidence thereof in the story following.

*{that behold}* This was so clear a demonstration of the providence of God in disposing matters most seasonably as it is most worthy to be observed.

*{the woman whose son he had restored to life}* See v. 1.

*{cried to the King}* See ch. 6. 26. It seems none could or would relieve her, and therefore she comes to the highest in authority.

*{for her house, and for her land}* See v. 3.

*{And Gehazi said, My Lord O King}* See ch. 6. 26.

*{this is the woman, and this is her son}* He was so well acquainted with them as he well knew them so soon as he cast his eye upon them.

*{whom Elisha restored to life}* Ch. 4. 35, 36.

**V. 6. ***{And when the King asked the woman}* Though that which Gehazi had told him of her son were true, yet he much desired to be fully assured of so great a matter by herself, she being then present.

*{she told him}* That it was true that her son had been dead, and that he was restored to life by Elisha.

*{So the King appointed unto her}* He was so taken with that evidence of God's power and favor to her as readily he grants her desire.

*{a certain officer}* Or, *eunuch.* See 1 King. 22. 9.

*{saying, Restore all that was hers}* Take order that all be restored to her again, and that nothing of hers be kept from her.

*{and all the fruits of the field}* I.e., all the revenues that had been made of any commodities raised out of her land.

*{since the day that she left the land}* Namely the land of Israel.

*{even till now}* The time here set down by the king was many years. For she was advised to go out of the land at the beginning of the famine, which continued seven years, v. 1. But all the fruits that in that time had grown out of the land could not have lasted so long; neither is it probable that if they could, they would have been laid up in such a time of need. The revenues therefore, as aforesaid, must here be intended. And they being the revenues of so many years must needs be great.

**V. 7. ***{And Elisha came to Damascus}* This being after the relief of Samaria, it cannot be supposed that Elisha went to Damascus for succor against the famine. The story following shows that God directed him thither to foretell the things hereafter set down. Of Damascus see 1 King. 11. 24.

*{and Benhadad the King of Syria}* See ch. 6. 24.

*{was sick}* This sickness was not in the kind of it mortal, v. 10. It is thence gathered that it was out of grief and shame upon the flight of his great army from Samaria, ch. 7. 7.

*{and it was told him}* By some that knew Elisha, and the power of God manifested by him, and that wished well to Benhadad.

*{saying, The man of God is come hither}* Elisha had done so many famous works as he was known to be a man of God not only in Israel and Judah, but also among the heathen round about, and in Syria especially, because of the healing of Naaman, ch. 5. 14. the discovering of the Syrians' counsels, ch. 6. 12. the disappointing of their troops, ch. 6. 20. and the flight of their host, ch. 7. 6, 7. See 1 King. 13. 1.

**V. 8. ***{And the King said unto Hazael}* It is probable that Naaman was at this time dead, or had given over his place of command, (whereof see ch. 5. 1.) because it required him to fight against the people of God, or was put from by the king, because he professed the true God of Israel, ch. 5. 17. and that this Hazael was put in his room as Benajah in the room of Joab, 1 King. 2. 35. So as the king sent the greatest in his kingdom next to himself, as a messenger to the prophet.

*{Take a present in thine hand}* See ch. 5. 5. & 1 King. 14. 3.

*{and go meet the man of God}* Such respect he bore to the prophet, especially by reason of this his sickness, that though he were a king, he does not send for him, but sends to him where he was.

*{and enquire of the Lord by him, saying}* The title *Lord* here is the interpretation of *Jehovah.* See 1 King. 18. 21. Here see the difference betwixt Ahaziah a king of Israel and this Benhadad king of Syria in their sickness: Ahaziah sends to a heathenish idol, ch. 1. 2. but Benhadad to Jehovah: Ahaziah sends to the prophet of the Lord to slay him; Benhadad sends a present to the prophet, to know the mind of the Lord by him.

*{Shall I recover of this disease?}* The words set out only a desire to know the issue of his sickness; but questionless they intend also a desire of the prophet's prayers for his recovery; for he had heard what he had done for Naaman, ch. 5. 14.

**V. 9. ***{So Hazael went to meet him}* Both king and subjects much respected and reverenced the prophet.

*{and took a present with him}* Heb. *in his hand.* See ch. 5. 5.

*{even of every good thing of Damascus}* This general particle *every* is to be taken of several kinds. Damascus had great store of choice commodities, and whatsoever precious and pleasant things Syria afforded (and it afforded great variety) was brought thither. The present therefore must needs be a very precious one, and also a bountiful one, as the words following show.

*{forty camels burden}* A camel uses to carry more than either ass or horse, or any other beast that is used for carriage. (See 1 King. 10. 2.) This present was far greater than Naaman's, ch. 5. 5. What will not kings and other great ones spare for their life and health?

*{and came and stood before him}* This was a testimony of much reverence. See ch. 5. 9.

*{and said, Thy son Benhadad King of Syria}* Though a king, and of another country, yet son to the prophet. The title *son* is here used in a kind of submission, implying that he was ready and desirous to be instructed by the prophet: For they who were scholars of the prophets, and came to be taught by them, were styled *sons of the prophets*, ch. 2. 3.

*{hath sent me to thee, saying}* This Hazael premises the rather to move the prophet to yield to his motion, because he came not of his own head, but by the appointment of the king his master.

*{Shall I recover of this disease?}* See v. 8. All of all sorts are very desirous beforehand to know the issue of their own or their friends' sicknesses. See 1 King. 14. 3.

**V. 10. ***{And Elisha said unto him, Go, say unto him}* The prophet was ready to gratify the king in his desire, and he does it plainly and fully.

*{Thou mayest certainly recover}* Heb. *Living thou shalt live.* See 1 King. 3. 13. In the Hebrew there is a double reading; one in the text with לא a negative particle, the other in the margin with לו a relative. The former may be thus read, *Say No, living thou shalt live.* They who thus take it, apply the negative to the word *Live*, *q.d.* *Assuredly thou shalt not live.* The latter may be thus read, *Say to him, Living thou shalt live;* Or, as our English turns it, *Thou maist certainly recover.* These may seem contradictory speeches. If the former reading stand, it does fully agree with that which follows in the next clause. But most follow the latter, *Say to him.* And thus this clause, *Thou maist certainly recover*, having reference to the nature of the disease, shows that it was not so dangerous as of itself it would deprive him of life.

*{howbeit the Lord hath shewed me}* There could be no external evidence to find out that which follows: therefore he says *the Lord shewed it him.* The Lord knows things to come as well as present.

*{that he shall surely die}* Heb. *that dying he shall die.* See 1 King. 8. 18. This is it that causes all the doubt; for it seems to contradict that former clause, *he shall live.* But distinguish betwixt the kind of disease and the event that followed, and this seeming contradiction may be easily reconciled. For (as was before noted) this clause, *Thou shalt live*, had respect to the kind of disease, which was not mortal, but recoverable; and in that regard he might have lived if no other thing had intervened. But the Lord knew what Hazael would do, v. 15. and in regard of that event it is truly said that *he should assuredly die.* To such a like purpose, but in a contrary event, another prophet said to one that in the event did not die, *Thou shalt die, and not live*, namely, in regard of the nature of the disease which was mortal and irrecoverable; yet afterwards he said unto him in the name of the Lord, *I will heal thee.* For God by his omnipotence overcame the power of that sickness, so as he recovered, and lived fifteen years after, ch. 20. 1, 5, 6.

**V. 11. ***{And he settled his countenance steadfastly}* Heb. *and set it.* I.e., removed it not from the object on which he settled it. This is spoken of Elisha, who looked wishfully (as we speak) upon Hazael's face, and turned not his eye from him.

*{until he was ashamed}* Whereunto this relative *he* has reference is not expressed: thereupon some apply it to Elisha, as if he had continued to fix his eyes till he himself was ashamed with looking so long upon a thing. But it may more fitly be applied to Hazael, who was ashamed and blushed again at the prophet's looking upon him so steadily.

*{and the man of God wept}* Being inwardly much moved with that cruelty which he foresaw Hazael would execute upon the people of God. On such an occasion was another prophet moved, Jer. 4. 19. & 9. 1. Yea and Christ also, Luk. 19. 41.

**V. 12. ***{And Hazael said, Why weepeth}* When he observed the prophet not only to settle his countenance upon him, but also to weep, he had an earnest desire to know the reason.

*{my Lord?}* This is a title of reverence. Prophets of the Lord were had in high account, and much respect was showed unto them by kings, nobles, captains, and others. See v. 9.

*{And he answered, Because I know}* The ground of this knowledge is manifested, v. 13.

*{the evil that thou wilt do unto the children of Israel}* As other kings of Syria had borne an immortal hatred against the children of Israel, so the prophet knew that this man also would be of the same mind. The evil he means is particularly expressed in the words following.

*{Their strong holds wilt thou set on fire}* You will not think it enough to enter into their towns and cities, and spoil them of their goods; but you will burn to the ground their forts, castles and other strongholds, the more to weaken them.

*{and their young men wilt thou slay with the sword}* You will give them no quarter for their lives, but kill all that you can get into your clutches.

*{and wilt dash their children}* Such as never had done him any hurt he would destroy by knocking out their brains, as Psal. 137. 9.

*{and rip up their women with child}* This he would do, to take children out of the womb, and destroy them before they were born; that if he could, he might quite cut off the remainder of Israel. These were excessive acts of cruelty.

**V. 13. ***{And Hazael said, But what}* He was so moved at the hearing of these matters as he could no longer forbear, but expostulated the case as follows.

*{is thy servant}* He continues to continue a reverent respect to the prophet (as before v. 12.) though the prophet had laid forth his cruel disposition.

*{a dog}* As a dog, fierce, cruel, merciless, that will tear in pieces all he comes at. See 2 Sam. 3. 8. Mat. 7. 6. Phil. 3. 2.

*{that he should do this great thing}* Such barbarous and inhumane cruelties as no reasonable man would do, proper only to savage, ravenous and unreasonable beasts. It may be supposed that Hazael at this time did not think he should do such cruel acts: but no man knows the depth of his own corruption. *The heart is deceitful above all things, and desperately wicked; Who can know it?* Jer. 17. 9.

*{And Elisha answered, the Lord hath shewed me}* *The Lord searcheth the heart and trieth the reins*, Jer. 17. 10. *all things are naked and open unto his eyes*, Heb. 4. 13. insomuch as he knows beforehand what men will do in after-times. Concerning this particular, the Lord showed it to Elisha by Elijah, 1 King. 19. 16. or by special revelation.

*{that thou shalt be King over Syria}* This sovereignty was it that not only gave him the occasion, but also stirred him up to execute that cruelty. The Lord was just and righteous in putting him into that place, that so he might be his scourge to punish idolatrous and rebellious Israel; but those cruel acts were effects of that evil disposition which was in him before he was king. By this does Elisha ratify that which before the Lord had done by Elijah in anointing Hazael king over Syria, 1 King. 19. 15.

**V. 14. ***{So he departed from Elisha}* Having received an answer from him.

*{and came to his master}* Who had sent him, v. 8.

*{who said unto him, What said Elisha to thee?}* The king earnestly desired to know the prophet's answer, and therefore, as soon as he saw him, demanded what it was.

*{and he answered, He told me that thou shouldst surely recover}* Heb. *Living thou shalt live.* He used the very words of the prophet, but in another sense: at least he related but a part of his answer, concerning the nature of the disease. That which the king most desired to know, the issue and event of the disease, he concealed.

**V. 15. ***{And it came to pass on the morrow}* Hazael having understood that he himself should be king, and fearing lest his master should recover, was impatient of delay, and the very next day accomplished his evil purpose for obtaining the crown.

*{that he took a thick cloth}* This is diversely expounded. Some take it for a blanket, some for haircloth, some for coarse canvas. Surely it was such a kind of cloth as was fit to stop the breath.

*{and dipped it in water}* Haply upon a pretense to cool and refresh the king; as if he aimed at his present ease and future recovery.

*{and spread it on his face}* That hereby he might stifle him. This crafty murderer of his sovereign used such a means to take away his life as might seem a remedy to cure him, and would leave no mark of violence offered unto him.

*{so that he died}* The event was according to the murderer's intent and the prophet's answer.

*{And Hazael reigned in his stead}* It appears by this that none of the Syrians suspected this murder of their king, and therefore questioned not Hazael for it, but quietly suffered him to succeed in the throne, either because the king had no children, and Hazael was of kin to him, or because he was so powerful as none dared oppose him, or so gracious with the people as they chose him.

**V. 16. ***{And in the fifth year of Joram}* This was while Jehoshaphat himself lived, 1 King. 22. 41, 42.

*{the son of Ahab King of Israel}* See on ch. 1. 17.

*{Jehoshaphat being then King of Judah}* He did not only then live, but also held his crown. Whereas Jehoram the son of Jehoshaphat is said to be king of Judah when Jehoram the son of Ahab began his reign, ch. 2. 17. it is to be taken but of the government of a viceroy. See ch. 1. 17.

*{Jehoram the son of Jehoshaphat King of Judah began to reign}* Heb. *reigned.* There is thrice mention of this Jehoram's reigning: I. As a viceroy, in the last year of Ahab, and first of Ahaziah, ch. 1. 17. II. About six years after, which was the fifth of Joram son of Ahab, here mentioned. For then Jehoshaphat made him king, even while he himself lived, and in this respect is said to give the kingdom to his eldest son Jehoram, 2 Chron. 21. 3. III. When Jehoshaphat died, 2 Chr. 21. 1.

**V. 17. ***{Thirty and two years old was he when he began to reign}* See 2 Chr. 22. 2.

*{and he reigned eight years in Jerusalem}* Four years in his father's lifetime, and other four after his death.

**V. 18. ***{And he walked in the way of the Kings of Israel}* This is especially meant of his idolatry. But other evils are also intended. See 2 Chr. 21. 4, etc.

*{as did the house of Ahab}* By *house* is here meant Ahab himself and his stock. They, besides the calves, served also Baal, ch. 16. 31. and herein the king of Judah imitated them.

*{for the daughter of Ahab was his wife}* Her name was Athalia. She drew him to follow her father's courses. See 2 Chr. 22. 2, 3. Wives use much to prevail over husbands, especially in drawing them to evil. See 1 King. 11. 4.

*{and he did evil in the sight of the Lord}* See 1 King. 21. 20. Of the evil which Jehoram did see 2 Chron. 21. 6, etc.

**V. 19. ***{Yet the Lord would not destroy Judah}* He would not utterly cast them off from being his people, though he suffered enemies much to annoy them, 2 Chr. 21. 16, 17.

*{for David his servants sake}* See 1 King. 3. 6. & 11. 12.

*{as he promised him to give him alway a light}* Heb. *candle, or lamp.* See 1 King. 11. 36.

*{and to his children}* Generation after generation, till his succession ended in Christ, Luk. 1. 31.

**V. 20. ***{In his days Edom revolted from under the hand of Judah}* See 1 King. 22. 47. & 2 King. 1. 1. & 3. 4, 5.

*{and made a king over themselves}* See 1 King. 22. 47.

**V. 21. ***{So Joram went over to Zair}* A city in Idumea.

*{and all the chariots with him}* Here is a synecdoche. For under *chariots* are meant horses, horsemen, and all manner of soldiers and preparation for war.

*{and he rose by night}* That he might come upon them suddenly and surprise them unawares; So Joshua 10. 9. and Saul, 1 Sam. 11. 11.

*{and smote the Edomites}* Had the better of them, put them to flight, and slew many of them. See 1 King. 15. 20.

*{which compassed him about}* Though Joram came upon them in the night, yet the Edomites were so numerous and so well prepared as they compassed his army round about: But the Israelites so manfully stood to it as they put the Edomites to flight.

*{and the captains of the Chariots and the people fled into their tents}* Not a wing only or a part of the army, but commanders and soldiers, even the whole host, fled, and came to their own houses.

**V. 22. ***{Yet Edom revolted from under the hand of Judah}* Though the Edomites were at that time put to the worst, yet the Israelites not pursuing their victory but returning home again, the Edomites gathered such strength as they could not be kept under, as in former times, but persisted to maintain their own right, and to uphold that king of their own nation whom they had set up. Herein was Isaac's prophecy fulfilled, Gen. 27. 40. See on 2 Sam. 8. 14.

*{unto this day}* See 1 King. 9. 13. & 11. 19.

*{Then Libnah revolted at the same time}* Libnah was one of those cities of Canaan, which of old was governed by a king of its own, Josh. 10. 29, 30. & 12. 15. It fell to the lot of Judah, and was in special manner dedicated to the use of the Levites, Josh. 21. 9, 13. It was a frontier city, and one of those which the king of Assyria set upon when he came with his huge host to have taken Jerusalem, ch. 19. 8. The revolt of this city was a matter of great consequence. After mention thereof 2 Chr. 21. 10. this clause is added, *because he had forsaken the Lord God of his fathers*, which some take to be the reason why Libnah revolted from Jehoram. But it may have a more general reference, to the revolt of Edom as well as to that of Libnah, yea, and to other judgments also that befell him. Herein we see the wise providence of God in ordering punishment according to the sin: Joram cast off God's yoke, and revolted from him, and broke his covenant; and Edom and Libnah cast off Joram's yoke, and revolted from him, and broke their covenant with him.

**V. 23. ***{And the rest of the acts of Joram, &c.}* See 1 King. 11. 41. & 14. 19, 29.

**V. 24. ***{And Joram slept with his fathers}* See 1 King. 2. 10.

*{And was buried with his fathers}* But not in their sepulchers, 2 Chron. 21. 20.

*{in the city of David}* See 1 King. 11. 43.

*{And Ahaziah his son reigned in his stead}* This was his youngest son. How he came to the throne is showed 2 Chr. 22. 1.

**V. 25. ***{In the twelfth year of Joram}* I.e., the last. For Joram reigned but twelve years, ch. 3. 1. and he and Ahaziah were both slain in a day by Jehu, ch. 9. 24, 27. This *twelfth year* must be taken current, for the very beginning of it. For in the eleventh year of Joram complete, at the end thereof, Ahaziah is said to begin his reign, ch. 9. 29. Some reconcile the difference thus, that Jehoram being unfit to govern by reason of his sore diseases, Ahaziah his son began to govern in the eleventh year of Jehoram son of Ahab; but in his twelfth year Jehoram the king of Judah died, and Ahaziah properly began to reign.

*{the son of Ahab king of Israel}* See v. 16.

*{did Ahaziah the son of Jehoram king of Judah begin to reign}* He had three names, *Ahaziah, Azariah*, 2 Chron. 22. 6. and *Jehoahaz.* So Eliakim was called also *Jehojakim*, ch. 23. 34. and *Joakim*, 1 Chron. 1. 5.

**V. 26. ***{Two and twenty years old was Ahaziah when he began to reign}* 2 Chron. 22. 2.

*{and he reigned one year in Jerusalem}* For in this first year he was slain, ch. 9. 27.

*{and his mothers name was Athaliah the daughter of Omri}* Athaliah was the daughter of Ahab, v. 18. and Omri was Ahab's father: So as by *daughter* here is meant grandchild.

*{king of Israel}* See 1 King. 16. 16.

**V. 27. ***{And he walked in the way of the house of Ahab, and did evil in the sight of the Lord, as did the house of Ahab}* The like is noted of his father, v. 18. So as like father like son.

*{for he was the son in law of the house of Ahab}* As Athalia is said to be the *daughter of Omri*, v. 26. because her father Ahab was Omri's son, so Ahaziah is said to be *the son in law of Ahab*, because his father Joram was son-in-law of Ahab. But because it is not simply said that *he was the son in law of Ahab*, but *of the house of Ahab*, some infer that he had another wife of the house or kindred of Ahab, and in that respect is said to be the *son in law of Ahabs house.*

**V. 28. ***{And he went with Joram the son of Ahab}* As his father Jehoram and his grandfather Jehoshaphat were bewitched with the house of Ahab, to associate themselves therewith, so was this Ahaziah also.

*{to the war against Hazael king of Syria in Ramothgilead}* This war begun by Ahab proved fatal to him, 1 King. 22. 3, 34. So also did it to his son.

*{and the Syrians wounded Joram}* In those days kings put forth their own persons unto great danger in battles.

**V. 29. ***{And king Joram went back}* From Ramoth-Gilead.

*{to be healed in Jezreel}* See 1 King. 18. 46.

*{of the wounds which the Syrians had given him}* Heb. *wherewith the Syrians had wounded.* They were such sore wounds as he dared not remain at Ramoth-Gilead, but came to a more secure place.

*{at Ramah}* I.e., at Ramoth-Gilead, v. 28.

*{when he fought against Hazael king of Syria}* It appears that Joram in his war had beaten the Syrians, and taken Ramoth-Gilead, in that he left Jehu the captain of his host and the rest of his soldiers there, ch. 9. 1, etc.

*{and Ahaziah the son of Jehoram king of Judah}* See v. 25.

*{went down to see Joram the son of Ahab in Jezreel}* He went with him to Ramoth-Gilead, and now he goes to visit him in Jezreel.

*{because he was sick}* He was mortally wounded: and a wounded man is sometimes said *to be sick.* See 1 King. 22. 34. Or it may be that by his wounds he was cast into a fever, and in that respect properly said *to be sick.*

## CHAPTER 9

**Ver. 1. ***{And Elisha}* The copulative particle joins this chapter to the last verses of the former, as a continuance of the same history.

*{the Prophet called one}* Seeing the Holy Ghost does not here in particular mention who this was, it is too great curiosity to determine him to be Jonah the son of Amittai, Jon. 1. 1. who they say was the widow's son of Zarephath, whom Elijah raised from the dead, 1 King. 17. 22, 23. It is probable that he was one not known to Jehu and the others that were with him, that so the matter might be carried the more privately. Had Elisha himself gone, there would have been great suspicion about the reason of his coming before the particular case had been made known. Some pretend Elisha's old age to be the reason why he went not himself: but that is not probable, because he lived about fifty years after this, ch. 13. 20.

*{of the children of the Prophets}* Styled sons of the prophets, ch. 2. 3.

*{and said unto him, Gird up thy loins}* This implies speed. See ch. 4. 29. And this case required as much speed as could be, because it was weighty and dangerous, and so fair an opportunity was offered at this time of Joram's and Ahaziah's being together at Jezreel as the like was not to be expected again.

*{and take this box of oil in thine hand}* He did not send him to the temple for holy oil, but that which was present, because the matter admitted no delay. The word translated *box* is translated *vial*, 1 Sam. 10. 1. Now whereas mention is made of a *horn of oyl* wherewith David was anointed, 1 Sam. 16. 1. and Solomon, 1 King. 1. 39. some put this difference, that the horn did intimate a perpetuity of their kingdoms, but the vial or box a deficiency or failing in Saul's and Jehu's.

*{and go to Ramothgilead}* Though Joram by reason of his wounds had gone from Ramoth-Gilead, ch. 8. 29. yet his captains and soldiers, even his whole army, there remained to keep it safe against the Syrians: By which means Jehu had the fairer opportunity, being chief captain or general over the whole host, to surprise Joram, who was absent from his army.

**V. 2. ***{And when thou comest thither, look out there Jehu}* He was the man whom God appointed to take vengeance on Ahab's house. Wherefore that this messenger might not mistake the man, he wills him to be careful in looking him out.

*{the son of Jehoshaphat the son of Nimshi}* See 1 King. 16. 1.

*{and go in}* Be not afraid, though there be many together: but go in where he is, that you may the better speak to him.

*{and make him arise up}* Not by force, but by declaring that you have a message to him.

*{from among his brethren}* Brethren in office, fellow captains, verse 8. See 1 King. 9. 13. & 20. 32. These commanders were then sitting together to consult about their warlike affairs, v. 5. and that Elisha knew by the spirit which he had.

*{and carry him to an inner chamber}* Heb. *chamber in a chamber.* This shows that he was to communicate his message unto Jehu in private, lest at the sudden hearing thereof there might be some tumult, and some speedy carrying of the news to Joram in Jezreel.

**V. 3. ***{Then take the box of oil}* See v. 1.

*{and pour it on his head}* That by this visible sign Jehu might be the more confirmed in the truth of that message which was brought him.

*{and say, Thus saith the Lord}* See 1 King. 12. 24. This divine authority alleged for warrant of the message was to work in Jehu the greater reverence and confidence.

*{I have anointed thee king over Israel}* I who have supreme and absolute power over kingdoms, to put down and to set up what kings I please, have now appointed you to this office. The word *anointed* is used in relation to the forementioned rite. See 1 King. 19. 16. Jehu is the only king of Israel from the division of the ten tribes that was anointed: for as his calling to that high dignity was expressly from God, so the task laid on him was the greatest.

*{then open the door}* It seems the door was shut on them two.

*{and flee}* Come away with the greatest speed you can: For by flying is the swiftest motion of any creature: therefore angels are said to have wings, and to fly, Isa. 6. 2.

*{and tarry not}* The expression of the speed he should make both affirmatively and negatively carries emphasis, and shows that the messenger should not on any occasion tarry any whit at all after he had done his message, lest he might incur some danger to himself, and be an occasion of interrupting the business.

**V. 4. ***{So the young man}* The messenger was young, that he might the more speedily do his message: for young men use to be nimblest.

*{even the young man}* This ingemination carries emphasis, and implies, that though the messenger were young, and the message dangerous, yet he did it punctually and boldly.

*{the Prophet}* This title is given him in regard both of his former education, and also of his present employment, which was to foretell what Jehu should be, and what he should do.

*{went to Ramothgilead}* See v. 1.

**V. 5. ***{And when he came}* To the place whither he was appointed.

*{behold, the Captains of the host were sitting}* To consult, as a council of war about their affairs.

*{And he said, I have an errand}* Some special thing to be made known.

*{to thee, O Captain}* Jehu is here meant. He was captain of the whole host of Joram; and it seems that he was well known to this messenger.

*{And Jehu said, Unto which of all us?}* As yet he knew not the mind of the Lord in that which after was made known unto him.

*{And he said}* The prophet said as follows.

*{To thee, O Captain}* This shows he was in special sent to him.

**V. 6. ***{And he}* Jehu.

*{arose}* This word implies a readiness and quickness in that which is undertaken. See 1 King. 14. 2, 4.

*{and went into the house}* The private chamber before noted v. 2. that they two might be the more private.

*{and he poured the oil on his head}* See v. 3.

*{and said unto him, Thus saith the Lord God of Israel, &c.}* The prophet holds close to the words of his charge, v. 3. wherein he showed himself a faithful prophet.

**V. 7. ***{And thou shalt smite}* Destroy. See 1 King. 15. 29.

*{the house of Ahab}* Ahab's posterity, and all that belonged to him, v. 8.

*{thy master}* Ahab was his sovereign, and had chosen him to be in special employment under himself. See v. 25.

*{that I may avenge the blood of my servants the Prophets}* Blood cries for vengeance, Gen. 4. 10. especially the blood of God's servants and prophets, Psal. 116. 15.

*{and the blood of all the servants of the Lord}* As prophets so all that profess the true God, especially in sincerity, are God's servants.

*{at the hand of Jezebel}* Jezebel was the principal author of the slaughter of the prophets, 1 King. 18. 4. and of other servants of the Lord, 1 King. 21. 10. but because Ahab was her instrument, and his sons trod in his steps, and also suffered her to continue in her regency, and to do as she listed, her sins lay upon their necks, and she and they are all judged.

**V. 8. ***{For the whole house of Ahab shall perish}* See v. 7.

*{and I will cut off, &c.}* See 1 King. 14. 10. & 21. 21.

**V. 9. ***{And I will make the house of Ahab like the house of Jeroboam, &c.}* See 1 King. 15. 19. & 16. 9, 11. & 21. 22. These judgments are mentioned to restrain Jehu from the like sins, lest the like vengeance should follow him. For Jeroboam and Baasha were set up by God to be his scourges to punish the evils of others, as well as Jehu himself.

**V. 10. ***{And the dogs shall eat Jezebel in the portion of Jezreel}* In that piece where Naboth's vineyard was, 1 King. 21. 23.

*{and there shall be none to bury her}* See v. 35, 36. It was counted a blessing to have a decent burial, and a curse to have no burial at all, Jer. 22. 19. Though the particulars from the beginning of the seventh v. are not expressed as a charge given to this messenger, yet it is to be supposed that even for these also he had his warrant from him that sent him. For the Holy Ghost uses sometimes to set down but once that which is delivered twice, both in a charge and also in the execution thereof.

*{And he opened the door and fled}* This was enjoined v. 3.

**V. 11. ***{Then Jehu came forth to the servants of his lord}* I.e., those commanders that were sitting in council, v. 5. Both he and they were subjects and special officers of king Joram's: Therefore they are called *servants*, and the king is called *Jehu's lord*, as Ahab was called his *master*, v. 7.

*{and one said unto him}* One of the aforesaid captains.

*{Is all well?}* Heb. *is it peace?* Was his message about peace?

*{wherefore came this mad fellow to thee?}* Certainly they knew him to be a prophet; and so Jehu's words following import. It may be that by reason of his sudden and hasty speaking to Jehu they so called him. But no doubt they did it the rather because they knew him to be one of the Lord's prophets. For thus have God's ministers from time to time been accounted, as Jer. 29. 26. Hos. 9. 7. Act. 26. 24. 2 Cor. 5. 13. Yea, Christ himself was so supposed to be, Mark 3. 21. Their ordinary prophecies were not pleasing to idolaters and other wicked men; therefore they accounted them mad.

*{And he said unto them}* Jehu made the answer.

*{Ye know the man, and his communication}* You cannot be ignorant that he is a prophet of the Lord; thereupon you must needs know that his message is from the Lord. By this he prepares them to take part with him in that matter which was ordered by the Lord.

**V. 12. ***{And they said, It is false}* This has relation to the latter part of Jehu's answer: They knew not his communication. Herein therefore Jehu labors to satisfy them.

*{tell us now}* They were earnest to know what the message was, for they suspected it was some weighty matter.

*{and he said, Thus and thus spake he to me, saying}* Under these general words are comprised the particulars of the prophet's message.

*{Thus saith the Lord}* See v. 3, 6. He expressly declares this to incite them the more to take part with him in so warrantable a business.

*{I have anointed thee King over Israel}* See v. 3, 6.

**V. 13. ***{Then they hasted}* God wrought upon their hearts, which made them so speedily undertake a matter of so great hazard.

*{and took every man his garment}* I.e., their upper garments.

*{and put it under him}* Not having ready such cloth of state used to be laid on thrones where kings sit, and on the ground before the throne where they tread, they upon the present sudden occasion use their own garments, to testify their acknowledgment of Jehu to be their king, and their readiness with all speed to set him up as a king. We read the like done to Christ, Mat. 21. 7, 8.

*{on the top}* The *top* signifies a *bone*, and is sometimes indefinitely put for any hard thing, Prov. 25. 15.

*{of the stairs}* Or, *of ascents and degrees.* This word signifies an *ascending*, or *a thing higher than another.* There might be in the place where they were something whereunto they ascended by steps: Thereupon they laid the garments so as they made it a kind of a throne, and they set Jehu thereon.

*{and blew with trumpets}* See 1 King. 1. 39.

*{saying, Jehu is King}* Heb. *reigneth.* They express their acclamation as a matter concluded and warranted. For as God the disposer of kings had appointed him to be so, v. 6. so they gave their consent thereto.

**V. 14. ***{So Jehu the son, &c.}* See v. 2.

*{conspired against Joram}* This is not to be taken of a treacherous conspiracy, because it had God's warrant; but indefinitely, of seeking to destroy the living king.

*{now Joram had kept Ramothgilead}* This is either to be taken of that time when Joram was in Ramoth, or else Joram is said to be there, because by his appointment the army remained there whereof he was the head.

*{he and all Israel}* The host gathered out of all Israel.

*{because of Hazael}* The Israelites had taken Ramoth-Gilead. See v. 1. Either Hazael might still hold some strong fort in that city, and the army abode to block him up; or else Hazael being thrust out they might fear he might gather more forces and come to recover the city again; and the army abode there till it should be thoroughly fortified against future invasion.

*{king of Syria}* See ch. 8. 15, 28.

**V. 15. ***{But king Joram}* Heb. *Jehoram.*

*{was returned to be healed in Jezreel}* See ch. 8. 19.

*{of the wounds which the Syrians had given him}* Heb. *smote.* This word is oft put for a mortal blow. See ch. 6. 21, 22. 1 King. 15. 29.

*{when he fought with Hazael king of Syria}* See ch. 8. 28. This is here repeated to show the opportunity that Jehu had to do what he did. See v. 2.

*{And Jehu said, If it be your minds}* Trusting to their aid, he would do nothing in this case without their consent.

*{then let none go forth, nor escape out of the city}* Heb. *let no escape go, etc.* His purpose being on a sudden to surprise Joram, he labors to prevent all means that might give any inkling of such a matter, lest he should either fence Jezreel against him, or make an escape from him.

*{to go to tell it in Jezreel}* There Joram was; and if the news of so great a matter had come to any in Jezreel, it would soon have been brought to the king's ears.

**V. 16. ***{So Jehu rode in a chariot}* They then used chariots in war for swiftness and conveniency, that in them they might the better use the bow or any other warlike instrument. He went not alone, but in the head of an army.

*{and went to Jezreel}* Towards it, and at length came to it.

*{for Joram lay there}* As he went thither, v. 15. so he abode there.

*{and Ahaziah, &c.}* See ch. 8. 29. This is here repeated to show the ground of Ahaziah's destruction. See 2 Chron. 22. 6, 7.

**V. 17. ***{And there stood a watchman on the tower in Jezreel}* See ch. 17. 9. 2 Sam. 18. 24. 1 Sam. 14. 16. Isa. 21. 5, 6, 11. & 62. 6. Israel being an inland country and surrounded with enemies, they had watchtowers in most of their cities, and watchmen upon them.

*{and spied the company of Jehu as he came}* This shows his diligence. He yet knew them not to be Jehu's troops, but because they were indeed so, they are here said to be so.

*{and said}* This shows his faithfulness.

*{I see a company}* This was all that yet he could discover.

*{And Joram said}* Here behold Joram's care and prudence.

*{Take an horseman}* Who may swiftly go and return.

*{and send to meet them}* To discern betimes who they were.

*{and let him say, Is it peace?}* He little suspected they were such enemies; but supposed they came either for succor, being overcome; or triumphing after some new exploit.

**V. 18. ***{So there went one on horseback to meet him, and said}* The scout did his message speedily and faithfully.

*{Thus saith the King, Is it peace?}* He propounds his message in the king's name, because he came from the king, and because he supposed they would give him the more direct answer.

*{And Jehu said, What hast thou to do with peace?}* Heb. *what is it to thee and peace?* See 1 King. 17. 18. The peace you inquire after concerns you nothing at all: I myself will answer Joram.

*{turn thee behind me}* Thus he kept the messenger from going back to his master, and revealing what he had seen. See v. 15.

*{And the watchman told, saying, The messenger came to them, but he cometh not again}* This was all the watchman could declare. He could tell nothing of their communication.

**V. 19. ***{Then he sent out a second on horseback}* The king wondering at his messenger's stay, and not knowing the reason thereof, sends out another single scout, to try if he could bring back any certain message. Men in doubt use to make one trial upon another.

*{which came to them and said, &c.}* See v. 18.

**V. 20. ***{And the watchman told, saying, &c.}* See v. 18.

*{and the driving}* Or, *marching.*

*{is like the driving of Jehu}* Jehu having been a chief commander, his manner of marching was well known.

*{the son of Nimshi}* Or, *the grandchild of Nimshi.* See v. 2.

*{for he driveth furiously}* Heb. *in madness.* As if he would kill his horses with galloping. Some interpret the word in a contrary sense *he marches slowly*, rendering this reason, That he might the better draw Joram out of the city, because the work intended might more easily and speedily be effected in the open field. For if the city had been shut against him, not only more delay would have been made, but the king might have escaped. This were a fair interpretation if the Hebrew would bear it.
