If these seventeen years be taken from the beginning of the three and twentieth of Joash, and the account made from the end of the two and twentieth; and if the seventeen years be accounted current, so as Jehoahaz reigned but sixteen complete; and withal if the thirty-seventh year of Joash be accounted complete, then this account may well stand with that v. 10, for from the end of the two and twentieth, or the beginning of the three and twentieth, to the end of the thirty-seventh are sixteen years complete, and seventeen current. Such accounts are frequent in Scripture. See 1 King. 15.2. Or, 2. It may be that Jehoahaz settled his son Jehoash on the throne, two or three years before his death; and if so, Jehoahaz might reign seventeen years; and yet his son begin to reign in the thirty-seventh of Joash, two or three years of Jehoahaz being accounted in and with his son’s reign. See ch. 8.16.

**V. 2. ***{And he did that which was evil in the sight of the Lord}* See 1 King. 14.22.

*{and followed}* Heb. *walked after*.

*{the sins of Jeroboam the son of Nebat, which made Israel to sin}* See 1 King. 16.19.

*{he departed not therefrom}* See ch. 3.3.

**V. 3. ***{And the anger of the Lord}* When any way God manifests his dislike of evil, and his resolution to punish evildoers, he is said to be *angry*.

*{was kindled}* Because God’s anger is terrible it is resembled to fire, and in that respect is said to be *kindled*.

*{against Israel}* i.e. The ten tribes. Though they were a church of God, yet for their idolatry he was as a fire, yea, a consuming fire, to them, Deut. 4.24,24. Heb. 12.29.

*{and he delivered them into the hand}* It is God that gives enemies that power they have over his church. See Isai. 10.5,6.

*{of Hazael king of Syria}* See ch. 8.13,15.

*{and into the hand of Benhadad}* See 1 King. 15.18.

*{the son of Hazael}* Though Hazael murdered Benhadad, ch. 8.15, yet did he give his son that name, because it was common to many kings of Syria. These kings much annoyed the people of Israel; some in one place, and some in another; some at one time, and some at another.

*{all their days}* This relative their may have reference to the days either of Jehu and his son, or of Hazael and his son.

**V. 4. ***{And Jehoahaz besought the Lord}* Idolaters and other wicked persons in their extreme necessities can call upon God, Judg. 10.10. 1 King. 21.27.

*{and the Lord hearkened unto him}* In regard of temporal mercies God oft hears wicked ones, 1 King. 21.29.

*{for he saw}* This phrase is oft attributed to God after the manner of man, to set out his pity and compassion, Gen. 31.42. Ex. 3.7.

*{the oppression of Israel}* That sore affliction wherewith the kingdom of Israel was oppressed.

*{because the King of Syria oppressed them}* By many plunderings, and taking away persons and goods, 1 King. 5.2.

**V. 5. ***{And the Lord gave Israel a Saviour}* A deliverer. For they by whom God uses to deliver people from their oppressors are called *saviors*, Isai. 19.20. Neh. 9.27. Obad. 21. The savior here meant was Jehoash, v. 25. To him also may be added Jeroboam his son, ch. 14.25. Some expositors say that immediately upon the prayer of Jehoahaz God sent an angel to restrain the rage of the Syrians.

*{so that they went out}* The inhabitants of those cities which the Syrians had taken were freed from their enemies.

*{from under the hands of the Syrians}* From their power, Numb. 31.49.

*{and the children of Israel dwelt}* Quietly and securely.

*{in their tents}* i.e. In their houses. See 1 King. 12.16.

*{as before-time}* Heb. *as yesterday, and third day*. Yesterday is put for the time past, 2 King. 9.26. Heb. 13.8. The third day being added thereunto implies continuance of time day after day. See 1 Chr. 11.2.

**V. 6. ***{Nevertheless they departed not, &c.}* See 1 King. 16.19.

*{but walked therein}* Heb. *he walked*. Continued in that idolatry.

*{and there remained}* Heb. *stood*. As it was in former times.

*{the grove}* Which Ahab made, 1 King. 16.33.

*{also in Samaria}* They did not only worship the calves in Dan and Bethel, but also Baal’s grove, and that even in the head city of the kingdom, Samaria.

**V. 7. ***{Neither did he leave}* This has relation to v. 4, where the king of Syria is said to oppress Israel; so as the fifth and sixth verses are brought within a parenthesis.

*{of the people to Jehoahaz}* By people are meant soldiers.

*{but fifty horsemen, and ten chariots, and ten thousand footmen}* These were but very few for a kingdom.

*{for the king of Syria had destroyed them}* This verifies ch. 10.32.

*{and had made them like the dust by threshing}* As overmuch threshing may bruise and break corn, and make it like dust, so by the overgreat oppressions wherewith the Syrians oppressed Israel, they were exceedingly weakened, diminished, and made contemptible. See Amos 1.3.

**V. 8. ***{Now the rest of the acts, &c.}* See 1 King. 14.19.

*{and his might}* It seems Jehoahaz was a valiant man, and manifested much valor, though for his wickedness he had no good success. If God be against men, all the might they have can do them no good, 1 Sam. 17.50.

*{are they not written, &c.}* See 1 King. 16.27.

**V. 9. ***{And Jehoahaz slept, &c.}* See 1 King. 16.28.

**V. 10. ***{In the thirty and seventh year of Joash king of Judah, &c.}* See v. 1.

*{and reigned sixteen years}* Two or three of these were in the reign of Joash king of Judah, and the rest in Amaziah’s, ch. 14.1.

**V. 11. ***{And he did that which was evil, &c.}* See v. 2.

*{he departed not from all}* Or, *from any*. Ps. 103.2.

**V. 12. ***{And the rest of the acts, &c.}* See v. 8.

**V. 13. ***{And Joash slept, &c.}* See v. 9. The life and death of Joash king of Israel are thus briefly passed over, because the most memorable things he did were by virtue of Elisha’s prophecy, v. 15, &c., and in the history of Amaziah; and therefore they are referred thither, ch. 14.8.

**V. 14. ***{Now Elisha was fallen sick}* Elisha at his death must needs be a very old man. For suppose him but twenty years old when Elijah first called him, Ahab reigned at least four years after that, 1 King. 20.26. & 22.1. Ahaziah, two, 1 King. 22.51. Jehoram, twelve, ch. 3.2. Jehu, twenty-eight, ch. 10.36. Jehoahaz, seventeen, ch. 13.1, which amount to fourscore and three years. Whereunto are to be added so many years as he was elder than twenty, when he was first called, and so many as Joash had reigned when he was sick. Some hold that he lived above an hundred years.

*{of his sickness whereof he died}* Sickness is an ordinary forerunner of death, 2 Sam. 12.15. 1 King. 14.1. & 17.17. ch. 20.1. Wise men make this benefit thereof, to be better prepared for their departure. This instance shows, that the best are subject both to sickness and death.

*{and Joash the king of Israel came down unto him}* Though the king were a wicked man, ver. 11, yet having heard of the many great good things which this prophet had done for Israel, and being now in great distress, he runs to visit him, that he might try if any further good might be done by his ministry for the land.

*{and wept}* When he saw the prophet so near his departure, he could not forbear weeping for so great a loss. Howsoever God’s ministers may be lightly esteemed in their lifetime, yet their departure uses to be bewailed.

*{over his face}* Leaning over the bed whereon Elisha lay.

*{and said O my father, &c.}* A title of reverence and love, yet given by a king, and that a wicked one, to a prophet of the Lord. See ch. 2.12. By this title he acknowledged the prophet had a fatherly care of that kingdom, and was the best defense it had; on which grounds he came to visit him, and to try if he might receive some good encouragement from him: And to move the prophet the rather to do some good, he testifies the high esteem he had of him.

**V. 15. ***{And Elisha said unto him}* The prophet was moved with the terms and profession of the king, and thereupon gives the signs following.

*{Take bow and arrows}* These instruments were here enjoined to be taken, to show that there should be yet more war, but withal that the king should destroy the enemies.

*{And he took unto him bow and arrows}* The king expecting good from the prophet readily does what he bids.

**V. 16. ***{And he said to the king of Israel, Put thine hand upon the bow}* Heb. *Make thine hand to ride*.

*{And he put his hand upon it}* See v. 15.

*{and Elisha put his hands upon the kings hands}* As a sign that the Lord would fight with him and for him; and to show that good success comes from God. For the prophet was here in God’s stead. See Gen. 49.24.

**V. 17. ***{And he said, Open the window Eastward}* For Syria lay eastward.

*{And he opened it}* See v. 15.

*{Then Elisha said, Shoot}* To show that when God calls men to war, they must fight.

*{And he shot}* He perseveres in his obedience.

*{and he said, The arrow of the Lords deliverance}* This is a sign that the Lord will deliver Israel.

*{and the arrow of deliverance from Syria}* This he adds to show that the Lord would deliver them even from that enemy that most annoyed them. And the phrase is doubled for the more assurance.

*{for thou shalt smite the Syrians in Aphek}* See 1 King. 22.26. Some expound the word, according to the meaning thereof, *powerfully*.

*{till thou have consumed them}* This certainly he should have done if he had not been too negligent in pursuing the good success which God at first gave. Or those particular armies which Joash should overcome, are here foretold to be consumed.

**V. 18. ***{And he said, Take the arrows}* He adds this further sign, to show that God would not once only help him, but also continue good success to him.

*{And he took them}* See v. 15.

*{and he said to the King of Israel, Smite upon the ground}* The prophet distinctly requires these several acts of him, both to try his faith and obedience the more, and also to make him distinctly observe the many victories that God would give him. Yea, also he would show that man’s endeavor must be used in God’s work.

*{And he smote thrice}* Herein he testified his willingness to hold out in what was required.

*{and stayed}* Here now he manifests his remissness and wearisomeness in that which he had well begun.

**V. 19. ***{And the man of God}* See 1 King. 13.1.

*{was wroth with him}* The prophet discerned by the king’s staying to strike his negligence in a matter of so great consequence, and thereat takes offense and is angry. He might well have conceived that all the forenamed signs prefigured victories against the Syrians; and that should have made him continue to smite till the prophet had bid him cease.

*{and said, Thou shouldst have smitten five or six times}* Very often.

*{then hadst thou smitten Syria till thou hadst consumed it}* To follow God’s work so long as occasion is offered is the ready way to accomplish it to the full. If enemies overthrown be still pursued, they may be utterly destroyed.

*{whereas now thou shalt smite Syria but thrice}* See v. 25. God restrained his success to punish his negligence and slothfulness.

**V. 20. ***{And Elisha died}* See v. 14.

*{and they buried him}* i.e. His surviving friends. It may be the king took some care thereabouts, and that he had an honorable burial. See 1 King. 14.13. & on Job 3.14.

*{and the bands of the Moabites}* See ch. 1.1. & 3.5. The Moabites having revolted took all opportunities to annoy Israel.

*{invaded the land}* By incursions which their troops made. See ch. 5.2.

*{at the coming in of the year}* In the spring, when plants began to sprout, and cattle to increase. By this means they did the greater spoil.

**V. 21. ***{And it came to pass as they were burying a man}* As they were carrying him to his grave. For the grave whereinto he was cast was not made for him.

*{that behold, they spied a band of men}* Enemies marching towards the place where they were.

*{and they cast the man into the sepulchre of Elisha}* Elisha was very lately buried, yet not covered over with earth, as our manner is for the most part, but laid in a sepulcher, as Christ was, Mat. 27.60. And the sepulcher being not yet covered, or the stone easy to be removed, those that carried the dead corpse being frightened upon the sight of the enemy hastily cast it in thither.

*{and when the man was let down}* Heb. *went down*.

*{and touched the bones of Elisha}* This is noted to show the means which God would have used for the effecting that miracle which follows. And because Elisha’s body (for by his bones his whole body is meant) was used, this may be accounted his one and twentieth miracle. See ch. 2.14.

*{he revived}* The miracle was wrought by the dead corpse, to show that it was God’s power, and not any virtue in Elisha, whereby such great works were done. And this was thus wrought at this time to give the king and people of Israel a sign that their dead decayed state should revive and flourish again, and to make them believe God could as easily do that as this. Besides, this was a general sign for all believers, who by faith touch Christ, that they shall rise from death to life.

*{and stood up on his feet}* This is added to manifest the truth of the miracle, and to amplify the same. For he had not only breath put into him, but also was enabled to rise, stand, and do the works of a living man.

**V. 22. ***{But Hazael King of Syria}* See ch. 8.13,15.

*{oppressed Israel all the days of Jehoahaz}* This is added to show that Joash was the savior promised v. 5.

**V. 23. ***{And the Lord was gracious unto them}* God’s free grace is the ground of that good he does to men.

*{and had compassion on them}* By reason of that misery wherein he saw them to be. See v. 4.

*{and had respect unto them}* Though most unworthy in themselves.

*{because of his covenant}* As God of his free grace is moved to enter into covenant with his people, thereby to bind himself to do them good, so by the same grace is he moved to continue that his covenant; yea many times though his people forfeit the same.

*{with Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob}* With these three patriarchs God made his covenant, and extended it to them and to their seed, Gen. 17.7. & 26.3,4. & 28.13,14. This covenant Moses pleaded, and the extent thereof, Exod. 32.13. Oft is this covenant set down as the ground of God’s doing good unto Israel, Psal. 105.8, &c., of his freeing them from affliction, Exod. 2.24, and of his patience and forbearing to take vengeance, as here, and 2 Chr. 21.7.

*{and would not destroy them}* Utterly; so as clean to cast them out of the land.

*{neither cast he them from his presence}* Heb. *face*. So as they should have no communion with him, nor be accounted his people.

*{as yet}* This intimates that afterwards he did cast them from his presence, and that because they obstinately persisted in their idolatry and other wickedness. See ch. 17.18, &c. For the present, God would further try whether at length they would turn to him, or no. God’s longsuffering is hereby set out.

**V. 24. ***{So Hazael King of Syria died}* His death was an occasion of some ease to Israel. See ch. 8.12.

*{and Benhadad his son reigned in his stead}* This was also a cruel enemy to Israel, v. 3, but not so bad as his father.

**V. 25. ***{And Jehoash the son of Jehoahaz took again}* Heb. *returned and took*. He that does a thing again, in Hebrew is said to *return to do it*, as Numb. 11.4. Now because the Israelites had formerly enjoyed these cities, Jehoash is said to *return and take* them. We say, *he recovered them*. Herein Jehoash showed himself to be the savior mentioned v. 5.

*{out of the hand of Benhadad the son of Hazael the cities which he}* Or, *his father Hazael*. For this relative he may have reference either to Benhadad or to Hazael.

*{had taken out of the hand of Jehoahaz his father by war}* The oppression mentioned v. 4 must needs be great, when the enemy took whole cities from Israel.

*{three times did Joash beat him, and recovered the cities of Israel}* This verifies the sign v. 19.

## CHAPTER 14

**Vers. 1. ***{In the second year of Joash son of Jehoahaz King of Israel}* This computation is to begin after the death of Jehoahaz. For Joash king of Israel reigned but sixteen years, ch. 13.10. Take a year or two from sixteen, and the account v. 23 will well stand.

*{reigned Amaziah the son of Joash King of Judah}* Heb. *Amazjahu*. The former name signifies *The strength of the Lord*; the latter, *The strength of his Lord*.

**V. 2. ***{He was twenty and five years old when he began to reign}* Heb. *a son of twenty and five years*.

*{and reigned twenty and five years}* Of these he spent twelve years as a banished man in Lachish, v. 19. 2 Chr. 25.27. See v. 21.

*{in Jerusalem}* See 1 King. 11.42.

*{and his mothers name was Jehoaddan of Jerusalem}* See 1 King. 15.10.

**V. 3. ***{And he did that which was right, &c.}* See ch. 12.2.

*{yet not like David his father}* i.e. not with a perfect heart, 2 Chr. 25.2. For David was a man after Gods own heart, 1 Sam. 13.14. Act. 13.22, but Amaziah did what he did to please men, as Joash his father had done before him.

*{he did according to all things as Joash his father did}* As his father was a notorious hypocrite, so was he. Both of them began well in outward show, yet both revolted and turned to idolatry, 2 Chron. 24. & 12.17,18. & 25.14.

**V. 4. ***{Howbeit the high places were not taken away}* See 1 King. 15.14.

*{as yet the people did sacrifice}* The king was loath to displease the people by crossing their custom. See 1 King. 3.2.

*{and burnt incense}* See 1 King. 9.25.

*{on the high places}* See 1 King. 3.2,4.

**V. 5. ***{And it came to pass as soon as the Kingdom was confirmed in his hand}* Settled and established in his person, as his undoubted right; as 1 King. 28.24,25. For by reason of the treason ch. 12.21 there were fears, troubles and dangers in the beginning of his reign. The murderers of his father being either great men or in high account with the people he thought it best for a time to forbear revenge.

*{that he slew}* When he saw a fit opportunity, and found his own strength, he executed justice upon them. The blood of any man unjustly slain defiles the land, Num. 35.33, and cries for vengeance, Gen. 4.10, much more of a king; and sooner or later it brings just revenge.

*{his servants}* These were his subjects, and in that respect called *his servants*. Besides, they might also have special offices, and be of the king’s household. See ch. 9.11.

*{which had slain the King his father}* Chap. 12.20. Three reasons are here couched to show the equity and necessity of Amaziah’s executing that justice which he did, 1. They had committed murder. 2. They had slain a king. 3. That king was his father.

**V. 6. ***{But the children of the murderers he slew not}* This is one evidence of his doing that which was right. The murderers’ children being left alive might seek revenge for their fathers’ death; but Amaziah preferred right before fear of danger.

*{according unto that which is written in the book of the law of Moses}* The five first books of the Bible are called *the law of Moses*; but Deuteronomy is the book here especially meant.

*{wherein the Lord commanded, saying}* All the precepts in sacred Scripture are God’s commands, and accordingly to be obeyed.

*{The fathers shall not be put to death for the children}* Deut. 24.16.

*{nor the children be put to death for the fathers}* There is the same equity for both. See on 2 Sam. 21.6.

*{but every man shall be put to death for his own sin}* By this law the Lord justifies his equal dealing with men, Ezek. 18.20.

**V. 7. ***{He slew of Edom}* These are called *children of Seir*, 2 Chr. 25.11, from their habitation, Deut. 2.4,8. The Edomites were subdued by David, but in Jehoram’s days they rebelled, ch. 8.20. And though they were then overthrown, yet they continued to rebel to Amaziah’s time. Therefore he now gathered a great army against them, 2 Chr. 25.5, and slew them with a great slaughter.

*{in the valley of salt}* There is a lake out of which rises much salt, called by Justine and others *Lacus Asphaltites*. A valley by it was called *the valley of salt*, 2 Sam. 8.13. Psal. 60.1, which is supposed to be that which is called *the valley of Shaveh*, Gen. 14.17, and that where the battle with the king of Sodom and others was fought, Gen. 14.8, and where David slew eighteen thousand.

*{ten thousand}* And he cast ten thousand more from a rock, 2 Chr. 25.11,12. These without question stood out obstinately, and would not yield, and therefore were cast down from the rock.

*{and took Selah by war}* This word Selah signifies *a rock*. Selah was also a fair city, so called because built on *a rock*, 2 Chro. 25.12. See Isa. 16.1.

*{and called the name of it Joktheel}* i.e. Obedience to God. This name Amaziah might give it in testimony of his obedience, when God sent a prophet to him to dismiss those troops he had hired out of Israel, 2 Chr. 25.9, &c.

*{unto this day}* See 1 King. 9.13.

**V. 8. ***{Then Amaziah sent messengers to Jehoash}* After his victory over the Edomites he waxed proud and insolent, and supposing he should still prevail, sought to war against others. Yea, after his detestable idolatry upon the foresaid victory God left him to himself, so as he ran into courses tending to his perdition. See 2 Chr. 25.14,15.

*{the son of Jehoahaz son of Jehu king of Israel}* See ch. 13.1,9.

*{saying, Come}* A word of challenge, as 1 Sam. 14.12.

*{let us look one another in the face}* This phrase may seem to imply only a desire of the king of Judah to consult in presence with the king of Israel about the affairs of both kingdoms; but the king of Israel’s answer and other consequences show that it was a plain challenge, *q.d.* Let us stand one against another in the open field. He meant setting their armies one against another themselves being as generals in the head of them. That this was intended, is evident v. 11. See a like phrase ch. 23.29. Amaziah haply made this challenge by reason of the wrong the men of Israel had done to the cities of Judah, 2 Chr. 25.13. Or he might do it to restrain Israel from plundering of more cities; thinking it better to decide the quarrel by main battle, hand to hand, than to suffer the enemy to forage the out coast of his dominions. Or, calling to mind Jeroboam’s defection, 1 King. 12.2, &c., and also Jehu’s slaughter of sundry princes of Judah, ch. 10.13, and being now overconfident of his own strength he resolved to take revenge, and to bring all Israel into subjection to Judah again. See on Eccl. 4.12.

**V. 9. ***{And Jehoash the King of Israel sent to Amaziah King of Judah, saying}* Jehoash was every way as proud and bold as Amaziah, and returns him a most scornful answer. They are like two proud champions, that make and return challenges in the loftiest terms they can.

*{The thistle that was in Lebanon}* A parabolical speech, as Judg. 9.8. The king of Israel in derision resembles the king of Judah to a thistle, which is prickly, but weak, growing low, not worthy to be reckoned among trees. He styles him *a thistle in Lebanon*, because Judah was within the promised land, but yet as a thistle growing under all the cedars in Lebanon.

*{sent to the cedar that was in Lebanon}* He resembles himself to a cedar, the tallest of trees, 1 King. 4.33, implying that he was the chiefest of kings.

*{saying, Give thy daughter to my son to wife}* He who seeks to match his child with another’s, supposes himself equal to that other. Hereby is intimated that Amaziah thought himself as good as Jehoash which he takes in great scorn.

*{and there passed by a wild beast that was in Lebanon}* By this wild beast the king of Israel meant his own army, by reason of the might and power of it.

*{and trod down the thistle}* Clean vanquished and trampled underfoot Amaziah and his army.

**V. 10. ***{Thou hast indeed smitten Edom}* Heb. *smiting smitten*. Jehoash confesses the success Amaziah had had over another kingdom; but so as withal he intimates that that was no sufficient cause to make him think of prevailing against Israel.

*{and thine heart hath lifted thee up}* Jehoash here strikes upon the right string, and plainly shows what it was that made Amaziah send the foresaid challenge, namely, the pride of his heart occasioned by his victory over Edom. The like is noted of Hezekiah, 2 Chr. 32.25.

*{glory of this, and tarry at home}* Heb. *at thy house*. *q.d.* If you will needs boast of that victory, do it in your own country among your own people.

*{for why shouldest thou meddle to thine hurt}* This is a dissuasive exprobation, rather taxing his folly than giving him advice.

*{that thou shouldst fall}* i.e. Perish, or be destroyed. Here Jehoash shows himself as confident of success as Amaziah.

*{even thou and Judah with thee}* Kings and commanders by their folly bring such as are under them into the same danger with themselves.

**V. 11. ***{But Amaziah would not hear}* Whom God will destroy, he first hardens. Exod. 14.4. See 2 Chr. 25.20.

*{therefore Jehoash King of Israel went up}* Into the field not staying till Amaziah should come to him.

*{and he and Amaziah King of Judah looked one another in the face}* They met with their armies in the field, face to face.

*{at Bethshemesh, which belongeth to Judah}* Josh. 15.10. & 21.16. This Bethshemesh is said to belong to Judah, because there was another Bethshemesh in Naphtali, Josh. 19.38. It is a point of prudence for a king to fight in his enemy’s land rather than his own.

**V. 12. ***{And Judah was put to the worse}* Heb. *was smitten before Israel*. The army of Judah being put to rout and followed by the Israelites, is said to be *smitten before Israel*.

*{and they fled every man to their tents}* See 1 King. 12.16.

**V. 13. ***{And Jehoash King of Israel took Amaziah}* He seized on his person, and took him captive. It seems such as were left of Amaziah’s army, forsook him, and fled clean away.

*{the son of Jehoash}* See v. 1.

*{the son of Ahaziah}* See ch. 11.2.

*{at Bethshemesh}* See v. 11.

*{and came to Jerusalem}* In triumph and in an hostile manner, with his army, having Amaziah his prisoner.

*{and brake down the wall of Jerusalem}* That he with his army might enter in. Others say the inhabitants stood out so stiffly against him as he was forced to batter down the wall.

*{from the gate of Ephraim}* See Neh. 3.32. & 12.39.

*{unto the corner gate}* So called because out of it men might look two ways, Zech. 14.10. Jer. 31.38.

*{four hundred cubits}* He broke down so much, that they might either leave their city open, or at least not quickly make up the breach again.

**V. 14. ***{And he took all the gold and silver}* Which as a treasure was laid up.

*{and all the vessels that were found in the house of the Lord}* Not those which were for daily use in the holy place, but such as were laid up in the treasury of the temple. See 1 King. 7.51.

*{and in the treasures of the Kings house}* Such as belonged to the king himself. See 1 King. 14.26.

*{and hostages}* Heb. *sons of pawnings*. These were either children of the king, or other noble persons. Jehoash having made what spoils he thought meet in Jerusalem, set Amaziah free upon certain conditions imposed upon him and his subjects. And for the surer performance of covenants on Judah’s part, and for a security of future peace, he took hostages.

*{and returned to Samaria}* Howsoever he had gotten a great victory, and made much spoil, even in the midst of Jerusalem, yet he thought not himself strong enough to hold the kingdom of Judah to himself, and to unite it to that of Israel, therefore he returned home.

**V. 15. ***{Now the rest of the acts of Jehoash, and his might}* See ch. 13.8,12. Under this word *might* his valiant deeds against Amaziah v. 12,13, and the Syrians, ch. 13.25, are comprised.

*{and how he fought, &c.}* See ch. 13.12.

**V. 16. ***{And Jehoash slept, &c.}* See 1 King. 16.28. Jehoash died in the very same year wherein he got that famous victory; so as God cast the rod into the fire anon after he had scourged his children with it. Here we see the pride of both kings punished; of Amaziah, with great loss; of Jehoash, with his life.

**V. 17. ***{And Amaziah the son of Joash King of Judah}* See v. 1.

*{lived after the death of Jehoash son of Jehoahaz King of Israel fifteen years}* He lived indeed, but it was a most miserable life, as appears v. 19, &c.

**V. 18. ***{And the rest of the acts of Amaziah, &c.}* See 1 King. 14.10,29.

**V. 19. ***{Now they made a conspiracy against him in Jerusalem}* So many of his subjects and such mighty ones plotted and wrought treason against him after his idolatry, 2 Chr. 25.14,15, and after the great mischief he had brought upon his kingdom by challenging the king of Israel v. 8, as he durst not stand out against them.

*{and he fled to Lachish}* A strong city of Judah, 2 Chro. 11.9. Isa. 36.1,2.

*{but they sent after him to Lachish}* They who conspired against him, having intelligence of the place whither he was fled, and fearing, in case he should escape, that he would take revenge of them, pursued their plot, and sent their ministers after him.

*{and slew him there}* His royal majesty and kingly authority no whit deterred them from that bloody treason. Of the time when this treason was plotted and executed, see v. 21.

**V. 20. ***{And they brought him on horses}* In a chariot drawn with horses. Thus was his grandfather Ahaziah carried, ch. 9.28.

*{and he was buried}* See ch. 9.28.

**V. 21. ***{And all the people of Judah}* It seems the common people also as well as the great ones were pleased well enough with the taking away of Amaziah, in that no mention is made of seeking any revenge of his death, but all agree to set up his son.

*{took Azariah}* Called Uzziah, ch. 15.13. Many memorable things are recorded of him, 2 Chr. 26.1, &c.

*{which was sixteen years old}* i.e. At the death of his father Amaziah. Which thus appears; Jeroboam the son of Joash began his reign in the fifteenth year of Amaziah, and reigned forty and one years, v. 23. Jeroboam’s son began his reign in the eight and thirtieth of Uzziah, ch. 15.8. Now Amaziah reigning twenty-nine years, v. 2, if fifteen be taken away from them, then remain fourteen, to which add eight and thirty of Uzziah’s, and there will arise the sum of fifty-two, the time of Uzziah’s reign, ch. 15.2. & 2 Chr. 26.3. Out of these must be taken at least eleven years to make the account of Jeroboam’s one and forty. And these eleven (or twelve, because years of kings use to be reckoned current) must needs be swallowed up in the joint reign of Amaziah and Uzziah his son together. See the like 2 King. 1.17.

*{and made him King in stead of his father Amaziah}* Namely, when his father was dead. For when they first conspired against Amaziah, he fled, and the people took his son and made him king about the fourth year of his age. Eleven or twelve years after, they who conspired might either then first hear where Amaziah was, or upon some occasion be more enraged against him, and thereupon slay him; and then the people renew the kingdom to Uzziah his son, and settle him upon the throne after his father’s death, which was in the sixteenth year of his age. See ch. 15.8.

**V. 22. ***{He built Elath}* An ancient city mentioned Deut. 2.8, but so decayed as by repairing and fortifying it might well be said to be built.

*{and restored it to Judah}* It seems the enemy bordering upon Judah had taken it, and therefore Uzziah is said to restore it to Judah. In Ahaz his time it was lost again, ch. 16.6.

*{after that the King slept with his fathers}* See 1 King. 2.10.

**V. 23. ***{In the fifteenth year of Amaziah the son of Joash King of Judah, Jeroboam the son of Joash King of Israel began to reign in Samaria}* Namely, over all Israel, as his predecessors had done. See ch. 10.30.

*{and reigned forty and one years}* This king reigned longest of all the kings of Israel, from the division to the captivity, and was the most victorious.

**V. 24. ***{And he did that which was evil in the sight of the Lord}* See 1 King. 14.22. & 21.20.

*{he departed not}* See ch. 3.3.

*{from all the sins of Jeroboam the son of Nebat, &c.}* See 1 Kings 16.19.

**V. 25. ***{He restored the coast of Israel}* Cities betwixt Judah and Israel, which were then under the power of Israel, but of right appertained to Judah. These coasts were appointed by God himself, Numb. 34.8.

*{from the entering of Hamath unto the sea of the plain}* That which is here called *the sea of the plain* is commonly called *the salt sea*. Compare Numb. 34.3 with Deut. 3.17.

*{according to the word of the Lord God of Israel}* God’s foretelling of future things shows, when they are come to pass, that they were ordered by divine providence.

*{which he spake by the hand}* See 1 King. 8.53.

*{of his servant Jonah the son of Amittai the Prophet}* Matth. 12.39,40, called Jonas; the fifth of the small prophets. We read not before of any such prophecy, but thence it is certain there was such an one: and it might be in the days of Jehoahaz, when in his trouble he prayed to the Lord, and the Lord heard him, ch. 13.3,4.

*{which was of Gathhepher}* A city of Zebulon, Josh. 19.13.

**V. 26. ***{For the Lord saw the affliction of Israel, that it was very bitter}* This certainly has relation to the time of Jehoahaz ch. 13.3,4,7.

*{for there was not any shut up, nor any left}* See 1 King. 14.10. None were safe, whether shut up in a place of defense or left abroad, whether great or mean, whether rich or poor.

*{nor any helper for Israel}* Neither could the king of Israel himself nor any of his princes help them against the enemy, neither could they get any foreign succor.

**V. 27. ***{And the Lord said not}* He had not yet determined.

*{that he would blot out the name of Israel from under heaven}* Utterly destroy them from bearing the name of a kingdom on earth. Though then he had not said, yet afterwards he said it, Hos. 1.6,9, and did it, ch. 17.18. See ch. 13.23.

*{but he saved them by the hand of Jeroboam the son of Joash}* See ch. 13.5.

**V. 28. ***{Now the rest of the acts of Jeroboam, &c.}* See v. 15.

*{and how he recovered Damascus}* This David won, 2 Sam. 8.6, and Rezon got again, but now Jeroboam recovered. See 1 King. 11.24.

*{and Hamath}* See on Isa. 10.9. & Amos 6.3.

*{which belonged to Judah, for Israel}* Of old it belonged to Judah, but now Jeroboam united it to the kingdom of Israel.

*{are they not written, &c.}* 1 King. 11.41.

**V. 29. ***{And Jeroboam slept, &c.}* See 1 King. 2.10.

*{and Zachariah his son reigned in his stead}* This Zachariah was the fourth from Jehu, and in him the promise ch. 10.30 was accomplished.

## CHAPTER 15

**Vers. 1. ***{In the twenty and seventh year of Jeroboam King of Israel}* See ch. 14.21.

*{began Azariah}* Or, *Uzziah*, v. 13,30,32,34.

*{son of Amaziah King of Judah}* See ch. 14.21.

*{to reign}* This phrase *began to reign* is in Hebrew *reigned*. So as *began* is not in the text. Yet it cannot be denied but that the word reigned is oft translated thus, *began to reign*, as 1 King. 15.25. & 22.41,51. If it be here so taken, it must be expounded of his beginning to reign alone after his father’s death; for he began to reign with his father twelve years before. See ch. 14.21. Some hold that Amaziah was slain in the sixteenth or seventeenth year of his reign, which was the fifteenth of Jeroboam, and that from thence to the seven and twentieth of Jeroboam there was no king of Judah settled, but an *inter-regnum*, or a protector only to govern the kingdom. But that opinion of an *inter-regnum* would much interrupt the computation of years by king after king. As for the other opinion of a protector, Azariah might be king in his childhood, and yet have a protector; but then he needed not have been the second time declared king, when he was sixteen years old, if he had been settled a king in his former years, and that his father being dead. Besides the time of Amaziah’s reign, ch. 14.2, cannot well stand with his cutting off by death in his seventeenth or eighteenth year. To prolong the life of Amaziah to the sixteenth year of his son Azariah, as ch. 14.21, and to give unto Azariah twelve years’ reign while his father lived a private life, will satisfy many chronological doubts. See on Isa. 7.8.

**V. 2. ***{Sixteen years old was he when he began to reign}* See ch. 14.21.

*{and he reigned two and fifty years in Jerusalem}* Of these twelve were spent in his father Amaziah’s time, ch. 14.21, and four in his son Jotham’s. See v. 30.

*{and his mothers name was Jecholiah of Jerusalem}* See 1 King. 15.10.

**V. 3. ***{And he did that which was right in the sight of the Lord}* See ch. 12.2.

*{according to all that his father Amaziah had done}* As Amaziah began his reign fairly, but ended it wickedly, so did Azariah. See 2 Chr. 26.16, &c.

**V. 4. ***{Save that the high places were not removed}* See 1 King. 15.14.

*{the people sacrificed and burnt incense}* By the priests, who willingly yielded to gratify them. See 1 King. 9.25.

*{still on the high places}* Notwithstanding that the king endeavored to have sacrifices and incense offered only in the temple.

**V. 5. ***{And the Lord smote the King}* See 2 Chr. 26.19.

*{so that he was a leper unto the day of his death}* Kings as well as others are subject to loathsome and lasting diseases.

*{and dwelt in a several house}* Heb. *a house of freedom*; free from all company. For lepers are counted unclean, and therefore separated from ordinary commerce, Lev. 13.46.

*{and Jotham the kings son was over the house}* Over the court and kingdom. For he reigned as king while his father was a leper, which was four years, v. 30.

*{judging the people of the land}* Though his father were living, yet he did the office of a king as faithfully and diligently as if he had been king alone.

**V. 6. ***{And the rest of the acts of Azariah, &c.}* See 1 King. 14.19.

**V. 7. ***{So Azariah slept, &c.}* See 1 King. 2.10. & 2 Chr. 26.23.

*{and they buried him, &c.}* In the field or place of burial where other kings were buried, but not by their sepulchers, 2 Chr. 26.23. See 2 Chr. 24.25.

**V. 8. ***{In the thirty and eighth year of Azariah king of Judah did Zachariah the son of Jeroboam reign}* Jeroboam began to reign in the fifteenth year of Amaziah, ch. 14.23. About two or three years after, to wit, in his eighteenth year, Amaziah being forced to fly from Jerusalem, the people made Azariah king; who reigned in his father’s lifetime eleven years, and after that one and forty more, which make up the two and fifty, v. 2. By this computation it appears how Jeroboam died in the thirty-eighth of Azariah, and how Zachariah his son began then to reign: Jeroboam reigned 3 years before the conspiracy against Amaziah, and thirty-eight years after, one and forty in all. See ch. 14.1,21,23. This account best stands with the chronology of the kings of Judah and Israel. See on Isa. 7.8.

*{over Israel in Samaria six months}* Some add many years hereunto; but such computations much interrupt the just chronology. Zachariah certainly was cut off in the first year of his reign. For the wickedness of the house of Jehu was so great as the Lord having accomplished his promise, ch. 10.30, would suffer that stock no longer to remain.

**V. 9. ***{And he did that which was evil, &c.}* See 1 King. 14.22.

*{as his fathers had done}* Namely Jehu, ch. 10.31. Jehoahaz, ch. 13.2. Joash, ch. 13.11, and Jeroboam, ch. 14.24. Long continuance of sin in several generations, one after another, much incenses God’s wrath. This is here rendered as a reason why God cut off Zachariah in the beginning of his reign, v. 8,10.

*{he departed not from the sins of Jeroboam, &c.}* This was that great sin wherein all his fathers before mentioned, and also other his predecessors, kings of Israel, had continued from the first division of Israel from Judah. See 1 King. 12.30.

**V. 10. ***{And Shallum the son of Jabesh}* This was some great commander in the kingdom, and probably in the army also; such an one as Omri, 1 King. 16.16,17, and Jehu, ch. 9.5.

*{conspired against him}* As Zimri against Elah, 1 King. 16.9.

*{and smote him before the people}* Openly, publicly. First he might plot against the king secretly, which is implied in this word *conspired*; and then having got the people to favor his cause and to be on his side, he might smite him, as is here said.

*{and slew him}* This sets out the kind of smiting the king, even unto death, as ch. 9.7,27.

*{and reigned in his stead}* It was an ambitious aspiring to the crown which made Shallum traitorously conspire against the king.

**V. 11. ***{And the rest of the acts, &c.}* See 1 King. 14.19.

**V. 12. ***{This was the word of the Lord which he spake unto Jehu}* See on ch. 14.27.

*{saying, Thy sons shall sit on the throne of Israel to the fourth generation}* See ch. 10.30.

*{And so it came to pass}* God’s word has been, and ever shall be accomplished, and that every way as it is revealed. In the continuance of Jehu’s stock thus long we may observe God’s wisdom in intermingling justice and mercy; justice in cutting Israel short, even in Jehu’s time, ch. 10.32, and in delivering them into the hands of the kings of Syria in the days of Jehoahaz, ch. 13.3. Mercy in making Jehoash and Jeroboam saviors, ch. 13.15. & 14.25, and also in continuing Jehu’s stock to the fourth generation.

**V. 13. ***{Shallum the son of Jabesh began to reign in the nine and thirtieth year of Uzziah king of Judah}* In the eight and thirtieth of Uzziah Zachariah began to reign, after the greater part of that year was spent: For Zachariah reigned but half a year, and is here implied to be slain in the thirty-ninth of Uzziah. This Uzziah is called Ozias Mat. 1.9.

*{and he reigned a full month in Samaria}* Heb. *a month of days*. Having all the days whereof it consisted, fulfilled. See Gen. 29.14.

**V. 14. ***{For Menahem the son of Gadi}* Josephus says he was captain of Zachariah’s army, as Omri was of Elah’s. He hearing that Shallum had slain his master, came with the army, and destroyed him, as Omri destroyed Zimri, 1 King. 16.17,18.

*{went up from Tirzah}* Of Tirzah see 1 King. 14.17.

*{and came to Samaria}* See 1 King. 16.29.

*{and smote Shallum the son of Jabesh in Samaria, and slew him, and reigned in his stead}* See v. 10. Though Shallum were God’s instrument in destroying the last of Jehu’s stock, yet because he did it with a traitorous and ambitious mind, God soon destroyed him.

**V. 15. ***{And the rest of the acts, &c.}* See 1 King. 14.19.

**V. 16. ***{Then Menahem smote Tiphsah}* A city situate in the way to Tirzah. See 1 King. 4.24.

*{and all that were therein}* He spared neither great nor mean, old nor young, male nor female. Wrath mixed with scorn and disdain makes men spare none.

*{and the coasts thereof from Tirzah}* Some conceive the distance betwixt these two cities was about six miles.

*{because they opened not to him, therefore he smote them}* Because they would afford him no refreshing from their city, nor entrance into it, much less passing through it, he destroyed them. Thus dealt Gideon with the men of Succoth, and Penuel, Judg. 8.5, &c.

*{and all the women therein that were with child he ripped up}* This is a great aggravation of his rage, that women great with child should not be spared, but their wombs opened, because the gates of the city were not opened to him. Some take this metaphorically for forts and towers wherein men were, that he did even rip them up by forcing an entrance into them. See Amos 1.13.

**V. 17. ***{In the nine and thirtieth year of Azariah king of Judah}* In the space of one year, namely from the thirty-eighth to the thirty-ninth of Azariah, there were four kings in Israel, succeeding one another; Jeroboam, Zachariah, Shallum and Menahem. Now was the time of Israel’s calamity: for judgment after judgment followed them till they were utterly destroyed.

*{began Menahem the son of Gadi to reign, &c.}* See 1 Kings 16.29.

**V. 18. ***{And he did that which was evil, &c.}* See 1 Kings 15.26.

**V. 19. ***{And Pul the king of Assyria}* 1 Chron. 5.26. By other authors he is Phul-Belosus, and Belochus. Assyria and Babylon are accounted one monarchy. After the Syrians had been long deadly enemies to Israel, the Assyrians annoyed them much more, and that from time to time, till they had utterly ruined them, 2 King. 17.6.

*{came against the land}* That part especially which was on the other side of Jordan, 1 Chr. 5.26.

*{and Menahem gave Pul a thousand talents of silver}* See 1 Chro. 22.14.

*{that his hand might be with him}* One end questionless of giving that money was to move Pul to depart from him; but withal it is probable that he made the sum the greater, to procure aid from him against other invasions.

*{to confirm the kingdom in his hand}* Menahem came to the crown by treason, murder and usurpation, and thereupon might justly fear intestine insurrections and rebellions against himself. For usurpers and tyrants cannot but live in continual fear. He sought therefore by might and force, and that of a foreign enemy, to secure his kingdom to himself.

**V. 20. ***{And Menahem exacted}* Heb. *caused to come forth*. Forced to bring forth.

*{the money of Israel}* i.e. Of his subjects, inhabitants of Israel.

*{even of all the mighty men of wealth}* Such as were best able to pay the great sums he exacted.

*{of each man fifty shekels of silver}* See 1 King. 10.16.

*{to give to the king of Assyria}* For the reasons rendered v. 19.

*{so the King of Assyria turned back, and stayed not there in the land}* There being so fair a silver bridge to pass over, he soon takes the opportunity and departs. Some say the distinction of persons before noted was in relation to Pul’s soldiers, that each of them should have fifty shekels. But then why should it be said that the money was exacted *to give to the king of Assyria*? Besides they say this money was given as a yearly tribute, to move the king of Assyria to continue his aid. But a thousand talents was too vast a sum to be year after year given to an enemy.

**V. 21. ***{And the rest of the acts of Menahem, &c.}* See 1 King. 14.19.

**V. 22. ***{And Menahem slept, &c.}* See 1 King. 2.10.

*{and Pekahiah his son reigned in his stead}* Though Menahem were a wicked and cruel king, yet God suffered him ten years, that he might be a scourge to rebellious Israel. His son succeeded him, but continued not long.

**V. 23. ***{In the fiftieth year of Azariah king of Judah}* Menahem reigned ten years, v. 17, which passed betwixt the nine and thirtieth and fiftieth of Azariah.

*{Pekahiah the son of Menahem began to reign over Israel in Samaria, and reigned two years}* God punished Menahem in his son, as he punished Baasha in his, 1 Kings 16.8.

**V. 24. ***{And he did that which was evil, &c.}* See 1 King. 14.22.

**V. 25. ***{But Pekah the son of Remaliah a captain of his conspired against him}* Great commanders have oft proved ambitious of regal dignity, and pernicious to their sovereigns, v. 10,14. 1 King. 16.9,16. ch. 9.2.

*{and smote him in Samaria, in the palace of the kings house}* Where may a man think himself more secure than in his own house? Yet so bold was this captain as to set upon his sovereign in the royal palace, where he was attended with a guard night and day.

*{with Argob and Arieh}* These might be two assassins which conspired with Pekah and aided him in his treason and murder. Some think they were on the king’s side, and slain with him. There be that take them to be names of places, and translate this preposition *with* as if it were *by*, or *near unto*, or *in*. Indeed Argob is the name of a region, Deut. 3.4,13,14. 1 Kings 4.13. The word Arieh signifies *a lion*; and they would have the house intended by it to be so called, because the image of a lion stood at the door thereof. But I take the former acception for conspirators with Pekah, to be most agreeable to the letter and sense intended.

*{and with him fifty men of the Gileadites}* That these also were conspirators with Pekah is most probable: But some hold they were on the king’s side, and slain with him.

*{and he killed him}* Pekah killed Pekahiah his sovereign.

*{and reigned in his room}* See v. 14.

**V. 26. ***{And the rest of the acts of Pekahiah, &c.}* See 1 Kings 14.19.

**V. 27. ***{In the two and fiftieth year of Azariah King of Judah}* This was the last year of that king, v. 2. Six kings reigned successively in Israel while he reigned in Judah.

*{Pekah the son of Remaliah began to reign, &c.}* See 1 King. 16.29.

*{and reigned twenty years}* Though he reigned so long, yet was his reign very troublesome; and in the end, he was violently cut off, v. 30. So was his treason and murder punished.

**V. 28. ***{And he did that which was evil, &c.}* See 1 King. 14.22.

**V. 29. ***{In the days of Pekah King of Israel came Tiglathpileser King of Assyria}* This was the son of Pul mentioned v. 19. He was hired by Ahaz to annoy Pekah, because he and Rezin king of Syria joined together to invade Judah, Isa. 7.1. See ch. 16.7. The signification of Tiglath-Pileser is remarkable, i.e. *the removing of captivity*. See ch. 17.6.

*{and took Ijon, and Abelbethmaachah}* See 1 King. 15.20.

*{and Janoah}* Situate on the borders of Ephraim, Josh. 16.6.

*{and Kedesh, and Hazor}* Judg. 4.6. 1 Kings 9.15. Josh. 19.32,36,37.

*{and Gilead}* See ch. 10.33.

*{and Galilee}* See 1 King. 9.11.

*{all the land of Naphtali, and carried them captive to Assyria}* The inhabitants of the places before named were carried away captive. The places whither they were carried are set down 1 Chr. 5.26, namely Halah, and Habor, and Hara, &c. This was the beginning of Israel’s captivity. The two tribes and half beyond Jordan, and Naphtali, yea and Zebulon too, on this side, were now carried away. To this miserable condition of the Israelites has the prophet relation, Isa. 9.1.

**V. 30. ***{And Hoshea the son of Elah made a conspiracy against Pekah, &c.}* See v. 25. As Pekah served his sovereign, so he himself was dealt withal by his subjects.

*{in the twentieth year of Jotham the son of Uzziah}* This twentieth year must be accounted from the time that Jotham began to reign in his father’s lifetime. See v. 5. For after his father’s death he reigned but sixteen years, v. 33. Some say he lived four years after those sixteen, having given up the kingdom to his son Ahaz. But no mention being as yet made of Ahaz, the account is to be taken from the beginning of Jotham’s reign.

**V. 31. ***{And the rest of the acts of Pekah, &c.}* See 1 King. 14.19.

**V. 32. ***{In the second year of Pekah the son of Remaliah king of Israel}* This year is to be taken current. For after sixteen years of Jotham’s reign Ahaz began to reign, in the seventeenth of Pekah, ch. 16.1. Now betwixt the second and the seventeenth there are not sixteen complete. See 1 King. 15.2.

*{began Jotham the son of Uzziah, &c.}* See ch. 15.1.

**V. 33. ***{Five and twenty years old was he when he began to reign}* Solely, by himself, his father being dead.

*{and he reigned sixteen years in Jerusalem}* See v. 30.

*{and his mothers name was Jerusha}* See 1 King. 15.10.

*{the daughter of Zadok}* It may be this Zadok was a priest: for priests married kings’ daughters, 2 Chr. 22.11, and kings might marry priests’ daughters.

**V. 34. ***{And he did that which was right}* Right is here to be taken both for the matter of what he did; it was agreeable to God’s word: and also for the manner, i.e. uprightly, sincerely. For nothing is recorded of Jotham which may argue him to have been an hypocrite or an apostate; as there is of Joash, 2 Chr. 24.17, of Amaziah, 2 Chr. 25.14, and of Uzziah, 2 Chr. 26.16. So as that which is here spoken may be taken in the largest extent, as ch. 18.3. and 22.2.

*{in the sight of the Lord}* See ch. 12.2.

*{he did according to all that his father Uzziah had done}* This is to be restrained to the good things Uzziah did, before he presumed to offer incense, 2 Chr. 26.4, &c.

**V. 35. ***{Howbeit, the high places were not removed, the people sacrificed and burnt incense still in the high places}* See v. 4.

*{He built the higher gate of the house of the Lord}* See ch. 11.6. This is noted as a part of his piety, and is set down in some opposition to the people’s practice: They doted upon high places, but his care was to build and beautify the house of the Lord, and the things pertaining thereunto.

**V. 36. ***{Now the rest of the acts of Jotham}* See 1 King. 14.29.

**V. 37. ***{In those days the Lord began to send against Judah}* In the latter end of Jotham’s reign enemies might consult about invading Judah; the Lord having a quarrel against the people’s doting on high places. But that which they began to plot in Jotham’s time, they put in execution in the beginning of Ahaz’s reign, ch. 16.5. Isa. 7.1. For Ahaz began his reign but three years before Pekah’s death, ch. 16.1. & 15.27.

*{Rezin the king of Syria}* He and his predecessors were deadly enemies against Israel; yet he with the king of Israel could join in confederacy against Judah.

*{and Pekah the son of Remaliah}* See v. 25. This Pekah in Ahaz’s time slew in Judah an hundred and twenty thousand in one day, 2 Chr. 28.6.

**V. 38. ***{And Jotham slept with his fathers, &c.}* See 1 King. 2.10.

## CHAPTER 16

**Vers. 1. ***{In the seventeenth year of Pekah the son of Remaliah}* This is to be taken of the year current. See ch. 15.32.

*{Ahaz the son of Jotham king of Judah began to reign}* This was a woeful change. An idolatrous son succeeded a pious father.

**V. 2. ***{Twenty years old was Ahaz when he began to reign}* By this account Ahaz was but eleven years old when his son Hezekiah was born: for he reigned but sixteen years, and lived but six and thirty; and Hezekiah was five and twenty years old when he began to reign, ch. 18.2. Some refer the relative he to Jotham, mentioned in the clause immediately before; *q.d.* *Twenty years old was Ahaz when Jotham began to reign*. We have a like reference 2 Chron. 36.9.

*{and reigned sixteen years in Jerusalem}* He sat on his throne as long as his father, ch. 15.33, but his father’s reign was a blessed time, his most wretched.

*{and did not that which was right}* This is expressly contrary to that which is recorded of David, 1 King. 14.8.

*{in the sight of the Lord}* See ch. 12.2.

*{his God, like David his father}* The Lord was Ahaz his God, in respect not only of creation but also of profession, and by his being king of God’s people; and David was his father, in regard of his lineal descent from him: But this wicked prince by his idolatry and other heinous sins violated the covenant of his God, and basely degenerated from the piety and virtues of that his famous ancestor, and so rendered himself unworthy of holding relation to either. See Isa. 7.11. John 8.39, &c. The Lord was his God, but he would not own him; David his father, but he would not imitate him.

**V. 3. ***{But he walked in the way of the kings of Israel}* Their way or course of life was idolatrous, impious, and every way wicked.

*{yea, and made his sons to pass through the fire}* This was expressly forbidden Lev. 18.21, but the law of God he cast behind his back, as a thing not to be regarded.

*{according to the abominations of the heathen}* The most abominable and detestable things that the heathen committed. The word *abomination* in the abstract is very emphatical.

*{whom the Lord cast out from before the children of Israel}* These heathen were the Canaanites, the worst of nations, accursed of God, Gen. 9.27. Jer. 18.25,27. The heathen burnt their children for a sacrifice to their idols: and this the Jews learned of them. This kind of sacrifice is sometimes called *offering up their children for a sacrifice*, Psal. 106.37,38. Jer. 7.31. & 19.5. Sometimes *causing them to pass through the fire*, 2 Chr. 33.6. Jer. 32.35. The idol to which they offered them was Molech: whereof see 1 Kin. 11.5,7. The place where the Israelites used to do was called *the valley of the son of Hinnom*, Josh. 15.8. & 18.16, and *the valley of Hinnom*, Neh. 11.30. In this valley was a place called Topheth from the Hebrew word תוף *toph*, *tympanum*, *a timbrel*, or *drum*. See on Exod. 15.20. At these sacrifices they used to beat a drum and sound other instruments, that the cry of the child might not be heard. Therefore they called the place Topheth. Of sacrificing children in this valley see 2 Chro. 28.3. & 33.6. Of Topheth see Jer. 7.31,32. Of their cruelty therein see on Lev. 18.21. From that cruelty used in *the valley of Hinnom*, which in Hebrew is גיהנם *Gehinnom*, the Greek word Γέεννα *Gehenna*, which signifies *hell*, arises, Matth. 5.22,29. Yea Topheth also is used in a like signification, Isa. 30.33. Of *Gehinnom* see on Josh. 15.8.

**V. 4. ***{And he sacrificed}* See 1 Kings 3.2,15.

*{and burnt incense}* See 1 Kings 9.25.

*{in the high places}* See 1 Kings 3.4.

*{and on the hills, and under every green tree}* See 1 King. 14.23.

**V. 5. ***{Then Rezin king of Syria, and Pekah, &c.}* See ch. 15.37. & 1 Kings 11.23.

*{came up to Jerusalem to war}* It seems they came with a formidable army, in that they came so far as to the walls of the chief city. They intended utterly to subdue the kingdom of Judah: for they meant to set another king therein, Isa. 7.6.

*{And they besieged Ahaz}* He was at this time in Jerusalem, begirt with enemies.

*{but could not overcome him}* The Lord sent Isaiah to tell him that the enemies’ counsel should not stand, Isa. 7.7. God determined not as yet to destroy the house of Judah, but his purpose was still to continue a light for David in Jerusalem, according to his promise 1 King. 11.36.

**V. 6. ***{At that time}* Either coming to Jerusalem, or departing from the same. The former is the more probable.

*{Rezin king of Syria recovered Elath to Syria}* This phrase implies that formerly the Syrians had possession of Elath, having gotten it from Israel, to whom of old it belonged, Deut. 2. 1 King. 9.26. Uzziah restored it to Judah, ch. 14.22. And now Rezin recovers it again to Syria.

*{and drave the Jews from Elath}* Forced them to depart thence.

*{and the Syrians came to Elath and dwelt there}* The Syrians took full possession thereof to themselves. Besides, they killed many, and took others captives, 2 Chron. 28.5. Pekah also slew a great number in Judah, and took many captives, 2 Chron. 28.6,8, which slaughter was questionless before their joining together to besiege Jerusalem. See on Isa. 7.1. Ahaz thinking himself strong enough to grapple with Pekah, ventured a fight, wherein God left him, and so he was shamefully overcome; God using one idolater to scourge another. But soon after this Pekah himself was destroyed. See ch. 15.30.

*{unto this day}* See 1 King. 9.13.

**V. 7. ***{So Ahaz sent messengers}* Though the Lord had expressly promised that Pekah and Rezin should not bring their wicked plot to pass, Isa. 7.5,7, yet faithless Ahaz durst not rest upon God’s promise, but seeks human help.

*{to Tiglathpileser King of Assyria}* See ch. 14.2. This was an ambitious man, and sought to get all nations under his dominion; yet does Ahaz most slavishly seek to him.

*{saying, I am thy servant}* I am content to be your tributary, in subjection under you, and to serve you:

*{and thy son}* Under your protection to be preserved by you, as a son by his father: promising to obey you, as becomes a servant; and to rely upon you, as becomes a son. Some take these words as a desire, thus, *Let me be thy servant and thy son*.

*{come up}* For Jerusalem was upon an hill.

*{and save me}* Deliver me and my people.

*{out of the hand}* From the power.

*{of the king of Syria, and out of the hand, &c.}* See v. 5.

**V. 8. ***{And Ahaz took the silver and the gold, &c.}* See ch. 12.18. & 18.16.

*{and sent it for a present to the king of Assyria}* It must needs be a great present that wrought so much as this did with such a king as the Assyrian was.

**V. 9. ***{And the king of Assyria hearkened unto him}* Not to come up, but to save him, which was the principal thing that Ahaz desired:

*{for the king of Assyria went up against Damascus}* Heb. *Dammasek*. See 1 King. 11.24. Amos 1.3. This city was in the confines of Syria, but sometimes in the Israelites’ power. See ch. 14.28. The king of Assyria went to Damascus purposely to divert Rezin from Jerusalem, which was moved thereby to forsake the siege, and to defend his country. Upon a like ground Saul turned from following David, 1 Sam. 23.28.

*{and took it}* Clean vanquished it, and took the possession thereof to himself.

*{and carried the people of it captive to Kir}* This Kir was a city in Media, as may be gathered from Isa. 22.6, where Elam and Kir are joined together. This was expressly foretold Amos 1.5. There was another Kir in Moab, Isa. 15.1, but that is not here meant.

*{and slew Rezin}* By this it appears that Rezin went in person to Damascus. He dearly bought his conspiracy with Pekah against Judah.

**V. 10. ***{And King Ahaz went to Damascus to meet Tiglathpileser King of Assyria}* He went to congratulate his victory, and obtain further aid against the Syrians; but all in vain: For the King of Assyria came afterwards unto him, and distressed him, but strengthened him not, nor helped him, 2 Chr. 28.20,21. By the chargeable present which he took of Ahaz he impoverished him, but sent him no aid: only for his own turn he vanquished Damascus, but suffered others to spoil Ahaz, 2 Chr. 28.11. Yea and he himself afterwards spoiled Judah, Isa. 7.17.

*{and saw an altar that was at Damascus}* Idolaters are much taken with outward shows. They have no certain rule to be guided by; their rule is their own and other men’s inventions. But pious and religious persons have God’s word to be their rule, to which they hold close: And this makes them constant in their serving of God, whereas the other are very changeable.

*{and King Ahaz sent to Urijah the Priest}* This priest was a temporizer, and one that Ahaz knew to be ready to do what he would have him; else he would not have committed such a matter to him.

*{the fashion of the altar, and the pattern of it}* Such a pattern as was every way fashioned according to that altar.

*{according to all the workmanship thereof}* Both in the several measures thereof; and also in the engravings and deckings.

**V. 11. ***{And Urijah the Priest built an altar according to all that King Ahaz had sent from Damascus}* The priest was or should have been better instructed in the law, Mal. 2.7. But they who set themselves to please princes, regard not God’s law, Gal. 1.10. Though there be one letter difference betwixt this Urijah and Uriah mentioned Isa. 8.2, yet they may well be supposed to be one and the same person. And if so, then he which was accounted not long before a faithful one, proves now most unfaithful to the Lord.

*{so Urijah the Priest made it against King Ahaz came from Damascus}* That the king might see how forward he was to conform himself to his will, and to please him, though it were in that which he could not but know was much displeasing to God.

**V. 12. ***{And when the King was come from Damascus, the King saw the altar}* As the priest had been ready to make it, so the king was very desirous to see it.

*{and the king approached to the altar}* He himself in his own person.

*{and offered thereon}* With his own hands. Herein Ahaz committed two great sins: 1. To usurp the priest’s office, as Uzziah his grandfather had done, 2 Chr. 26.16. 2. To bring in a strange altar into the house of God. In this he is said to *sacrifice unto the gods of Damascus*, 2 Chr. 28.23. He knew the Syrians offered sacrifices to their idols on the altar whereof this was a pattern, so as herein he preferred the idols of the heathen before Jehovah the God of Israel. This is pleaded against Israel, Jer. 2.9,11. Of the reason which Ahaz rendered for this, see 2 Chron. 28.23.

**V. 13. ***{And he burnt his burnt offering, &c.}* Of the several kinds of offerings here mentioned, see 1 King. 3.15.

*{and sprinkled the blood of his peace-offerings}* Heb. *which were his in special for himself*. This rite of sprinkling blood was revealed by the law, Exod. 24.6, and was a type of the application of Christ’s blood, Heb. 9.13,14.

*{upon the altar}* He did this to dedicate his new altar. Idolaters seek many outward rites to imitate God’s true worshipers.

**V. 14. ***{And he brought also the brazen altar}* That great altar which Solomon made, 2 Chr. 4.1.

*{which was before the Lord}* Which was so placed in the priests’ court as one standing by it might see through the porch and holy place into the most holy place, where the ark stood. In this respect it is said to be *before the Lord*, Exod. 40.29. 1 Kings 8.56.

*{from the fore-front of the house}* That part of the court which was just before the entrance into the temple.

*{from between the altar and the house of the Lord}* That the Lord’s altar might not stand betwixt his altar and the temple.

*{and put it on the north-side of the altar}* He put the Lord’s altar on the side of his altar, so as it could not well be seen by such as came to offer sacrifices, and set his own altar in the midst, directly before the entrance in the temple, where the Lord’s altar before stood.

**V. 15. ***{And king Ahaz commanded Urijah the Priest, saying}* As he commanded the priest to make this new altar, so he commands him to use it according to his pleasure.

*{Upon the great altar}* i.e. That new strange altar. Which was not in quantity, yet in his account greater than the Lord’s altar.

*{burn the morning burnt offering, &c.}* See v. 13.

*{and the brazen altar}* Which was the Lord’s, v. 14. Some from this title brazen altar infer that the new altar was of gold. And certainly idolaters will spare no cost for their idolatry.

*{shall be for me to enquire by}* He would have his own altar continually used for all holy services enjoined to the people of God; and the Lord’s altar only serve for what he pleased.

**V. 16. ***{Thus did Urijah the Priest according to all that king Ahaz commanded}* Like an impious and idolatrous hypocrite, basely and slavishly he conforms himself to the king’s will, and that in the matter of God’s worship.

**V. 17. ***{And king Ahaz cut off the borders of the bases}* See 1 Kings 7.27,28, &c. The meaning is that Ahaz defaced those holy vessels which Solomon had made.

*{and removed the laver from off them}* There were ten lavers which all stood upon bases, 1 Kings 7.38. He took them all off.

*{and took down the Sea from off the brazen oxen that were under it}* See 1 Kings 7.24,25. He did not clean take away these things; for we read they remained in the temple at the time of the captivity, Jer. 52.20, but his impiety in this appeared, that he defaced and disordered the holy things of the Lord.

*{and put it upon a pavement of stones}* If Solomon’s setting the sea and lavers upon supporters were an ornament, surely this setting them upon the pavement was a disparagement.

**V. 18. ***{And the covert for the Sabbath that they had built in the house}* Hereby is meant a place in the temple for the priests, who on Sabbaths or weeks kept their courses (See ch. 11.5) to rest therein. This covert is otherwise taken by others. But in this all agree, that it was a retiring place covered over to keep men from rain and weather. This Ahaz took away.

*{and the kings entry without}* See 1 King. 10.5.

*{turned he from the house of the Lord}* Because he had no mind to go to God’s house; The doors thereof he shut up, 2 Chr. 28.24.

*{for the king of Assyria}* Partly to collogue with him in showing his detestation of the worship and holy things of God, and partly to cut off all by-passages to his palace, through fear of the king of Assyria.

**V. 19. ***{Now the rest of the acts of Ahaz, &c.}* See 1 Kings 14.29.

**V. 20. ***{And Ahaz slept, &c.}* See 1 King. 2.12.

*{and Hezekiah his son reigned in his stead}* A most pious son succeeded an idolatrous father.

## CHAPTER 17

**V. 1. ***{IN the twelfth year of Ahaz King of Judah began Hoshea, &c.}* It is said ch. 15.30. that *Hoshea slew Pekah, and reigned in his stead*, *in the twentieth year of Jotham*, which was his last year, in which Ahaz Jotham’s son began to reign. Hence arises a doubt, how Hoshea should begin to reign in the twelfth of Ahaz: for by this computation there are twelve years over-reckoned. But the Hebrew clean takes away the doubt, in that it only says, *Hoshea reigned*; the word *began* being added by the translators. I will not deny but that the Hebrew word here used is oft put to set out the beginning of a king’s reign, as ch. 8.16. 1 Kings 14.21. Take it so, and then all the years betwixt the death of Pekah and the twelfth of Ahaz are swallowed up by reason both of the great tumults that were in Israel, and also of that power which the king of Assyria had over Israel in those days. See ch. 15.29. Though therefore it be said that *Hoshea slew Pekah, and reigned in his stead*, yet that may be taken of Hoshea’s setting himself upon the throne so soon as he had killed Pekah; but because the people pulled him down again, and suffered him not to reign quietly till this twelfth of Ahaz, he may be here said to begin his reign. Or though he did continue to reign from Pekah’s death, yet the king of Assyria making him his vassal, he reigned merely as a tributary king, and that reign was accounted no reign: But in the twelfth of Ahaz he rebelled against the Assyrian, and refused to be tributary any longer, and therefore now he is said to begin to reign, because he now reigned as an absolute king.

*{nine years}* Of these he reigned six before the Assyrian came against him, and in the other three Samaria was besieged, and at last taken, ch. 18.9,10. See v. 4,5.

**V. 2. ***{And he did that which was evil in the sight of the Lord}* See 1 King. 14.22.

*{but not as the Kings of Israel that were before him}* Hoshea did evil in that he continued in Jeroboam’s idolatry: But other kings of Israel served Baal also as 1 King. 16.31. Yea, and all the host of heaven v. 16. and committed sundry other abominations, v. 17. Hoshea invented no new idolatry. It is also probable that he suffered his people to worship the Lord as they themselves thought meet, and made no laws absolutely to tie them to any one kind of idolatry, as Jeroboam had done, 1 King. 12.28. and Omri, Mich. 6.16. but such of his subjects as were willing he permitted to worship the Lord in his holy temple at Jerusalem, which was forbidden 1 King. 12.28. If that which is noted 2 Chr. 30.11. of the inhabitants of Asher, Manasseh and Zebulon coming to Jerusalem to the Passover were before the carrying away of the Israelites captive, then there is an evidence of Hoshea’s suffering his people to go to Jerusalem, there to observe the ordinances of the Lord. God sometimes does pour out the full vials of his wrath upon kings and people that are not altogether so wicked as their predecessors, to show that he executes judgment not for the sins of the present time only, but of former times also. And smaller sins added unto greater fill up the measure, and provoke wrath.

**V. 3. ***{Against him came up Shalmaneser king of Assyria}* This king is taken to be the son of Tiglath-Pileser, that great oppressor of Israel, ch. 15.29.

*{and Hoshea became his servant}* After Tiglath-Pileser was dead, Hoshea thought he might free himself from the yoke of Assyria, and pay no more tribute: hence Shalmaneser took occasion to come against him, and so far prevailed as he made him tributary to him also. Hence he is styled *his servant*.

*{and gave him}* Heb. *rendered to him*. Because Hoshea enjoyed the liberty he had upon condition of the tribute he paid, he is well said to render what he gave.

*{Presents}* Or, *tribute*. Not only one great present at once, but a tribute year after year. See v. 4.

**V. 4. ***{And the king of Assyria found conspiracy in Hoshea}* The conspiracy here meant was breach of covenant, as ch. 24.20. Ezek. 17.13,14,15. Hoshea about this matter consulted with his counselors of state; and in that respect it may be called a conspiracy; but another reason is rendered in the words following.

*{for he had sent messengers to So king of Egypt}* Egypt was an ancient and potent kingdom: And there had been continual emulation betwixt it and Assyria; Hoshea therefore sends to So for aid against the Assyrian; and upon promise of his aid refuses to keep covenant with Shalmaneser.

*{and brought no Present to the king of Assyria}* See v. 3.

*{as he had done year by year}* This proves the present to be an annual tribute.

*{therefore the king of Assyria shut him up}* This is spoken by anticipation. For questionless he had taken Samaria before he shut up Hoshea.

*{and bound him in prison}* Heb. *in an house of shutting up*. A fit description of a prison. Therein men are so shut up as they cannot go abroad. The king of Assyria thus dealt with the king of Israel before he carried him away captive into another land, that he might be the more sure of him when he should demand him. Kings sinning against God are subject to the like restraints and punishments that meaner persons are, 2 Chr. 33.11. Psal. 149.8.

**V. 5. ***{Then the king of Assyria came up}* After he had found conspiracy in Hoshea, v. 4.

*{throughout all the land}* He brought a great army, and with it passed from city to city, conquering and subduing all places throughout all the tribes to himself.

*{and went up to Samaria}* This was as the fairest and largest, so the strongest and best defensed city of the kingdom. See 1 Kin. 16.24.

*{and besieged it three years}* By this it appears the city was well fenced, fortified, furnished and every way prepared to hold out, or else such a potent enemy would sooner have entered into it.

**V. 6. ***{In the ninth year of Hoshea}* In the seventh year Samaria began to be besieged, and was taken in the ninth, ch. 18.10. The seventh and ninth year being accounted current, the siege may be said to continue three years.

*{the king of Assyria took Samaria}* Having either battered down the walls and made an entry, or forced the inhabitants by a long siege to yield up the city.

*{and carried Israel away into Assyria}* This was the utter rejecting of those ten tribes, whereby they came to be in God’s account *Lo-Ammi* Hos. 1.9. *no people of God’s*. We do not read that ever after this they returned to their own habitations; but to this day they remain as a people cast off. From the beginning of the division of the tribes from Judah to this their last captivity there are reckoned about two hundred and sixty years.

*{and placed them in Halah and in Habor}* Cities of Assyria, whereunto Hara is added 1 Chr. 5.26.

*{by the river of Gozan}* Or, *Nehar-Gozan*, so as both words together make the name of one city. Some of the Jews hold this to be the Sabbatical river.

*{and in the cities of the Medes}* These were other cities than they which are before mentioned to be in Assyria. For Media was then under the king of Assyria. As the Assyrian carried the Israelites into other countries, so he brought people from other countries to inhabit the land of Israel. See v. 24.

**V. 7. ***{For so it was}* Here is showed how long Israel had continued to provoke the Lord, and how much, before he executed the foresaid judgment on them.

*{that the children of Israel had sinned against the Lord their God}* Sins pull down judgments, especially if long continued in.

*{which had brought them up out of the land of Egypt}* See 1 Kings 8.9.

*{from under the hand of Pharaoh king of Egypt}* This is added to set forth the great mercy of God towards them in their beginning to be a free people, in that he delivered them from a miserable bondage. And this mercy much aggravates their sin.

*{and had feared other gods}* They soon began to be idolatrous, Ezek. 20.8, &c.

**V. 8. ***{And walked in the statutes of the heathen}* They followed their inventions, customs, orders and ordinances, though God long before had threatened that their land should spew them out for such abominations, Lev. 18.26,28.

*{whom the Lord cast out from before the children of Israel}* This sets out their obstinacy and impudency in sinning against God, in that they committed those very things for which the inhabitants of that land were cast out before them.

*{and of the kings of Israel which they had made}* The Israelites themselves, without any command from the Lord, made Jeroboam king, 1 Kings 12.20. and Omri, 1 Kings 16.16. both which made statutes for idolatry, 1 Kings 12.18,19. Mic. 6.16. So also did other kings: and in their statutes did the Israelites walk.

**V. 9. ***{And the children of Israel did secretly}* Heb. *they hid*. Or, *covered, or cloaked over what they did*.

*{those things which are not right}* Not agreeable to the right rule, which is God’s word, but rather contrary thereunto. These things they covertly and deceitfully did upon pretense of serving God. Yea, beside their open idolatry, they did many secret abominations, Ezek. 8.12.

*{against the Lord their God}* Against his will, and to the dishonor of his name.

*{and they built them high places in all their cities}* See 1 Kings 3.4. These were places for idolatry.

*{from the tower of the watch-men to the fenced city}* Watchtowers used to be up and down in the country, to descry danger afar off, ch. 9.17. yea, they had watchtowers in many places to look to their cattle and to their orchards and vineyards. Their great and populous cities were wont to be fenced. This then is a proverbial speech, whereby the extent of their idolatry is set out; and that, to be everywhere, in country and city, in meaner and greater places, from one end of the land to the other.

**V. 10. ***{And they set them up images}* Heb. *statues*. See ch. 10.26.

*{and groves}* See 1 King. 16.33.

*{in every high hill, and under every green tree}* See 1 Kings 14.23.

**V. 11. ***{And there they burnt incense in all the high places}* Of incense see 1 King. 9.25.

*{as did the heathen}* Who were aliens from the commonwealth of Israel, and had not the light of the word to direct them in that which was acceptable to God.

*{whom the Lord carried away before them}* See ch. 16.3.

*{and wrought wicked things}* This is a general phrase comprising under it not only idolatry and other branches of impiety against God, but also wrongs, oppressions and other like sins against man.

*{to provoke the Lord to anger}* Though that was not the end they aimed at, yet it followed as an effect of their sins. See 1 King. 14.9.

**V. 12. ***{For they served idols}* Heb. *dirty gods*, 1 King. 15.12.

*{whereof the Lord had said unto them, Ye shall not do this thing}* Deut. 4.19. God’s express charge nothing at all prevailed with them. Yea, the corrupt heart of man is oft stirred up the rather to do sundry evil things because God forbids them, Rom. 7.8.

**V. 13. ***{Yet the Lord testified against Israel}* God did ordinarily declare to his people their sins, and used means to turn them from them, before he executed his judgments.

*{and against Judah}* Though the reason of God’s judgment on Israel be here principally intended, yet mention is made of Judah also, that they might by the judgment on Israel be warned beforehand, and also that afterwards the equity of God’s proceeding against them might be the better discerned.

*{by all}* Heb. *by the hand of all*. See 1 King. 8.53.
