*{the Prophets, and by all the Seers}* Prophets properly were such as had the will of God made known unto them beforehand, and thereupon foretold things to come. *Seers* had the will of God made known by visions or representations of things which they saw, and in that respect were called Seers. Both titles imply such as were immediately stirred up by God, and extraordinarily assisted by his Spirit, in such weighty matters as could not otherwise be effected. The same person has both titles many times given him, 1 Sam. 9.9. Compare 1 Sam. 3.20. with 1 Sam. 9.19. Here is intended, that God afforded unto his people not only ordinary ministers, priests and Levites, but also extraordinary, prophets and seers.

*{saying, Turn you from your evil ways}* Jer. 18.11. & 25.5. & 35.15. As God’s mercy is wonderfully amplified in accepting sinners if they turn to him, Ezek. 18.21. so man’s obstinacy is exceedingly aggravated if he hearken not to his gracious invitations.

*{and keep my commandments and my statutes}* See 1 King. 2.3.

*{according to all the law which I commanded your fathers}* He means their fathers in the wilderness, to whom God by Moses gave his law, as a rule for them and their posterity, Psal. 78.5,6.

*{and which I sent to you by my servants the Prophets}* Prophets were in special manner called *God’s servants*, and distinguished from others, ch. 9.7. because they were extraordinarily instructed in the will of the Lord, and so guided by his Spirit as they were most faithful in doing his will. God contented not himself, once to make known his will to their fathers, but time after time inculcated it by his prophets to succeeding generations.

**V. 14. ***{Notwithstanding they would not hear, but hardened their necks}* Deut. 31.27. This metaphor imports a stubborn disposition, which will not bow nor submit to God’s will, 2 Chr. 29.6. & 36.13. Prov. 29.1. Isa. 48.4. Jer. 7.26. It is taken from beasts which will not bear the yoke.

*{like to the neck of their fathers}* For children to imitate their fathers in wickedness is a great aggravation of the evil.

*{that did not believe in the Lord their God}* Deut. 1.32. Ps. 78.22,32. & 106.24. This is here noted as the cause of all other their wickedness. Unbelief is a mother-sin.

**V. 15. ***{And they rejected his statutes}* Here is impudency added to their obstinacy.

*{and his covenant that he made with their fathers}* See 1 King. 11. & ch. 13.23.

*{and his testimonies which he testified against them}* See 1 King. 2.3. God’s testimonies are said to be *against them*, because they transgressed them, and so pulled upon their own pates the penalties annexed to them. In this respect God’s statutes are said not to be good, Ezek. 20.25.

*{and they followed vanity}* Idolatry. See 1 King. 16.13.

*{and became vain}* Foolish, without understanding, Rom. 1.21. Ps. 115.8. See 1 Kings 16.13.

*{and went after the heathen that were round about them}* See v. 11.

*{concerning whom the Lord had charged, &c.}* See v. 12.

**V. 16. ***{And they left all the Commandments}* They who take liberty to transgress one commandment, will soon transgress all, and make conscience of none.

*{of the Lord their God}* See 1 Kings 8.28.

*{and made them molten images}* Exod. 32.8. These images are called *molten*, because they were made of such metal as might be molten, and so cast into any shape.

*{even two calves}* See 1 Kings 12.28.

*{and made a grove}* See 1 Kings 16.33.

*{and worshipped all the host of heaven}* The stars placed in heaven, which as an host are many, set in good order, keep their rank, and are ready to do what the Lord will, Judg. 5.20. See Deut. 41.9. Jer. 8.2.

*{and served Baal}* See 1 Kings 16.31.

**V. 17. ***{And they caused their sons, &c.}* See ch. 16.3.

*{and used divination}* Heb. *divined divination*. This they did by intercourse with the devil. See Deut. 18.10. Jer. 14.14. Ezek. 26.21. Acts 16.16. 1 Sam. 28.7. Divination is a conjecturing of things future, or things concealed and secret.

*{and enchantments}* Heb. *They inchanted*. To enchant or use enchantment is by words or signs to make pretense of doing some rare thing. See Ps. 58.5. Eccles. 10.11. Jer. 8.17. The word does also signify *to descry or know what is done in secret*, *to divine*, Gen. 44.5,15.

*{and sold themselves to do evil}* See 1 King. 16.33. & 21.20.

*{in the sight of the Lord}* See 1 Kings 14.22.

*{to provoke him to anger}* See v. 11.

**V. 18. ***{Therefore the Lord was very angry with Israel}* See ch. 13.3. 1 Kings 11.9.

*{and removed them out of his sight}* Out of that land whereupon the eyes of the Lord were continually, after an especial manner, set, Deut. 11.12. where the evidences of his gracious presence were, and where his church was, v. 20,23. ch. 23.27. This shows the accomplishment of what the Lord threatened before, Hos. 1.6.

*{there was none left but the tribe of Judah only}* Under Judah are comprised so many as associated themselves to Judah, together with the priests and Levites. See 1 Kings 11.13. 2 Chr. 11.16.

**V. 19. ***{Also Judah kept not the Commandments of the Lord their God}* This is mentioned as another cause of Israel’s ruin. They were an ill example to Judah, and infected that nation. It is also an aggravation of Judah’s sins, in that she would not be warned by Israel’s fall, Jer. 3.8. but neglected the good advice, Hos. 4.15.

*{but walked in the statutes of Israel which they made}* See v. 8.

**V. 20. ***{And the Lord rejected all the seed of Israel}* If this be taken of the judgment mentioned v. 6. only the ten tribes are meant by all the seed of Israel. And to them it does the more properly belong, because their captivity was never yet returned, and so they were indeed rejected. Judah was not so; yet because mention is made of Judah’s walking in the statutes of Israel, v. 19. and they also were afterwards carried away captive, it may be extended even to them also.

*{and afflicted them}* By sundry judgments before and in their captivity.

*{and delivered them into the hands of spoilers}* Kings of Syria, ch. 13.3,7. and kings of Assyria, ch. 15.19,29. & 18.9. Judah also was delivered into the hands of spoilers in the time of Ahaz, 2 Chr. 28.5, &c. and of Manasseh, 2 Chr. 33.11.

*{until he had cast them out of his sight}* See v. 18.

**V. 21. ***{For he rent Israel from the house of David}* The Lord rent them, 1 Kings 11.11,31. & 12.29. Or Israel rent itself, 1 Kings 12.16.

*{and they made Jeroboam the son of Nebat King}* 1 King. 12.20.

*{and Jeroboam drove Israel from following the Lord}* By using means to keep them from going to the house of the Lord, 1 King. 12.23. as also by causing them to follow other gods, 1 King. 12.28,29.

*{and made them sin a great sin}* See 1 King. 12.30. Idolatry is a great sin in the kind of it; but the Israelites' idolatry was the greater, in that it spread throughout all the ten tribes, and continued till they were carried away captive.

**V. 22. ***{For the children of Israel walked in the sins of Jeroboam which he did}* See 1 King. 14.15. From this Jeroboam to the captivity were twenty kings of ten several stocks, (See the argument) yet all agreed in the sin of Jeroboam. See the reason 1 King. 12.26.

*{they departed not from them}* See ch. 3.3.

**V. 23. ***{Until the Lord removed Israel out of his sight}* See v. 18.

*{as he had said by all his servants the Prophets}* Heb. *by the hand of all*. See 1 Kin. 8.53. He means all that God had stirred up in those to foretell this judgment, as ch. 8.12. 1 Kings 13.22. & 14.16. Hos. 14.6,9. Amos 5.27. Mic. 1.6.

*{so was Israel carried away out of their own land to Assyria}* To those several countries mentioned v. 6.

*{unto this day}* See 1 King. 9.13. Of this judgment it may be said *even still unto this day*, for yet Israel remains cut off.

**V. 24. ***{And the King of Assyria brought men}* Shalmaneser brought some, as here. See v. 3. Afterward Eser-Haddon, Sennacherib’s son, brought others, Ezr. 4.2.

*{from Babylon}* Heb. *Babel*. This was the chief city in Assyria.

*{and from Cuthah}* No mention is elsewhere in scripture of this city. It is supposed it was in the confines of Syria.

*{and from Ava, &c.}* Or, *Iva*, Deut. 2.23. This Iva and Hamath and Sepharvaim were cities which the Assyrian had conquered, ch. 18.34.

*{and placed them in the cities of Samaria}* i.e. those cities which appertained to the kingdom of Israel, which was called Samaria from the chief city. See 1 King. 16.24.

*{in stead of the children of Israel}* The king of Assyria having removed the Israelites to other countries v. 6. placed strangers in the cities of Israel; that in all places they might hold what they had, not as inheritors of their own, but as his vassals. Joseph used the like policy, Gen. 47.21. These were they which in the N.T. are called Samaritans, Mat. 10.5. Luke 9.52. They had learned of the priests that were sent to them, v. 27. that the great patriarchs were the fathers of those that dwelt in the ten tribes, and thereupon they dwelling in that great city they laid claim to that privilege, Joh. 4.20.

*{and they possessed Samaria, and dwelt in the cities thereof}* For the Israelites were forced to leave it, v. 6.

**V. 25. ***{And so it was at the beginning of their dwelling there}* This has relation to the time before a priest was sent to them, v. 27.

*{that they feared not the Lord}* They neither inwardly did know or believe in Jehovah, nor outwardly yield any worship to him, not so much service as the idolatrous Israelites did, who worshiped the true God, though after a wrong manner.

*{therefore the Lord sent lions among them}* All creatures, even the most ravenous, are at God’s command. See 1 King. 13.24. ch. 2.24. 1 Kings 13.24.

*{which slew some of them}* The creatures do that for which God sends them. God the rather inflicted this heavy judgment on this people, because being in his land, the land which he had chosen for a special habitation for himself, they did not so much as own him to be the true God.

**V. 26. ***{Wherefore they spake to the king of Assyria, saying}* By letters or messengers they made known their mind to him.

*{The nations which thou hast removed and placed, &c.}* See v. 24.

*{know not the manner of the God of the land}* They know not how he will be worshiped. It was a foolish conceit of the heathen, that every nation, country and city had its proper God: Thereupon these here imagined, the rather by the judgment inflicted on them, that the land of Israel had another God than the lands had from whence they came.

*{therefore he hath sent lions, &c.}* See v. 25.

**V. 27. ***{Then the king of Assyria commanded, saying}* He was unwilling his subjects should be utterly destroyed, therefore he quickly hearkens to their complaint.

*{Carry thither one of the Priests whom ye brought from thence}* Not one of the true priests descended from Aaron; for they attended at the temple; but one of Jeroboam’s order, 1 king. 12.31. for such were they that were carried captive from Samaria.

*{and let them go and dwell there}* He mentioned before but one of the priests, but here he uses the plural number, let them; therefore either there by a synecdoche one is put for many, or the plural number here is put for the singular; or here is implied the priest and his company, wherein other priests also haply were. Or, here may be meant the priest and the messengers that came to the king. Some conceive other inhabitants were sent in the room of those that were slain by the lions, and so a new plantation made.

*{and let him teach them the manner of the God of the land}* Two things are here intended, that the priest should instruct the people; 1. in the knowledge of the God of Israel, who was the God of that land; 2. how that God should be worshiped.

**V. 28. ***{Then one of the Priests, &c.}* See v. 27.

*{came and dwelt in Bethel}* Here one of the golden calves were set, 1 king. 12.30. but it is probable is was now taken away, because it was of gold.

*{and taught them how they should fear the Lord}* The priest made known unto them that Jehovah was the God of that land, and also in general declared what kind of sacraments, sacrifices, feasts, meats, and other like rites were to be observed in honor of him. It may be he instructed them in some superstitious courses whereunto the Israelites before their captivity were addicted, yet because the true Lord was made known to them, this phrase is used, *how they should fear the Lord*. The judgment of lions questionless ceased because the true Lord was acknowledged, though he were not altogether worshiped after a right manner. When in Ahab’s time the Israelites acknowledged the Lord to be God, though the calves were continued, yet the drought was removed, 1 king. 18.39,41, &c. Temporal judgments may be taken away upon a turning from some gross sins, though there be not a through reformation.

**V. 29. ***{Howbeit every nation}* Heb. *nation nation*. Gen. 7.2.

*{made gods of their own}* Idols of their own native country. They are styled *gods* in regard of the people’s esteem of them. See 1 Cor. 8.5. People are much addicted to the things of their own country. See 1 King. 11.8.

*{and put them in the houses of the high places}* See 1 king. 12.31.

*{which the Samaritans had made}* The Israelites which before their captivity had dwelt in Samaria and other cities of that kingdom are here called Samaritans.

*{every nation in their cities wherein they dwelt}* Thus one city had one God; and another, another.

**V. 30. ***{And the men of Babylon made Succothbenoth}* This and the titles following are proper names which the heathen gave their several idols. Succoth-Benoth according to the Hebrew notation signifies *coverings of daughters*. This idol was fashioned like a hen.

*{and the men of Cuth made Nergal}* This Cuth is the same that was called Cuthah, v. 24. Nergal had the shape of a woodcock.

*{and the men of Hamath made Ashimah}* Hamath is before mentioned v. 24. Their idol Ashimah had the shape of a goat.

**V. 31. ***{And the Avites}* The inhabitants of Avah, v. 24.

*{made Nibhaz and Tartak}* The Avites had two sorts of gods; others had more. Nibhaz was like a dog; Tartak like an ass.

*{and the Sepharvites}* Inhabitants of Sepharvaim, v. 24.

*{burnt their children in fire}* See ch. 16.3.

*{to Adrammelech and Anammelech}* These were the same with Molech. See 1 King. 11.5. Adrammelech signifies *a glorious king*; Anammelech, *an afflicting king*. Both names not unfitly applied to Satan, to whom they burned their children. For in their esteem Satan was glorious, yet he afflicted them by bereaving them of their children. The former name was also given to one of the sons of Sennacherib, ch. 19.37. The idol Adrammelech was made in the shape of a horse; Anammelech, of a mule. Hereby is verified that of the apostle, Rom. 1.23.

*{the gods of Sepharvaim}* These people had also many gods.

**V. 32. ***{So they feared the Lord}* They had an high esteem of the Lord, and bore a reverent respect to him, Zeph. 1.5.

*{and made unto themselves of the lowest of them Priests of the high places}* They had no priests of the Lord’s ordaining, and the greater sort cared not for that office, so as they were willing to take any that would offer himself. Thus had Jeroboam done before, 1 King. 12.31. & 13.33.

*{which sacrificed for them in the houses of the high places}* Light of nature taught men both to have a distinct function for divine services, and also by some of that function to offer up their sacrifices. Of the houses of the high places see 1 King. 12.31.

**V. 33. ***{They feared the Lord}* See v. 32.

*{and served their own gods}* Though they acknowledged the God of Israel to be the true God, yet they did service to idols.

*{after the manner of the nations}* Whence they came, and where the gods which now they worshiped formerly were, Zeph. 1.5.

*{whom they carried away from thence}* Or, *who carried them away from thence*. This divers reading shows that by nations may be understood either the idolatrous Israelites which were before in Samaria, or those heathen whom the Assyrians brought thither.

**V. 34. ***{Unto this day}* See 1 King. 9.13.

*{they do after the former manners}* This is meant of the Israelites carried captive into Assyria. The former manner has relation to the time wherein they lived in their own land. From this verse to the last their obstinate disposition in their captivity and in the places wherein they then lived, is set down.

*{they fear not the Lord}* They testify no respect to God neither inwardly nor outwardly.

*{neither do they after their statutes}* i.e. such statutes as the Lord ordained for the Israelites, as proper and peculiar to them.

*{or after their ordinances}* i.e. such directions for divine worship as God had ordained for his people.

*{or after the law and commandment}* See 1 Kings 3.2.

*{which the Lord commanded the children of Jacob}* This shows the reason why they were called *theirs*, They were enjoined to them.

*{whom he named Israel}* Here they are put in mind of the great mercy which God showed to their ancestor when he first gave him that name Israel, Gen. 32.28. and of the gracious promises made to him and his posterity when that name was again confirmed to him, Gen. 35.10, &c. This is mentioned also as an aggravation of their obstinacy. See the like 1 King. 18.31.

**V. 35. ***{With whom the Lord had made a Covenant}* This has relation to Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, and also to their posterity, Gen. 17.7. & 26.4. & 28.14. Exo. 19.5. & 24.7.

*{and charged them, saying}* This was a condition to be observed on their parts, that thereby they might have the surer evidence of God’s keeping covenant with them.

*{ye shall not fear other gods}* Judg. 6.10. This forbids an inward esteem of any thing as of a God, save the Lord.

*{nor bow your selves to them}* This forbids an outward manifestation of any such esteem, Exod. 20.5.

*{nor serve them}* This forbids all duties of piety to be performed to them, Deut. 5.9.

*{nor sacrifice to them}* This was a particular kind of service proper to him that was accounted a God. Herein Ahaz failed, 2 Chr. 28.23.

**V. 36. ***{But the Lord who brought you up out of the land of Egypt}* See 1 Kings 8.9. This mercy is here mentioned as a motive to the duties following.

*{with great power}* This has relation to the ten plagues of Egypt, and to the destruction of Pharaoh and all his host in the Red Sea, together with the Israelites passing through as on dry land, Exod. 9.14. & 11.1. & 14.4,28,29.

*{and a stretched-out arm}* See 1 King. 8.42. So extraordinary was the power manifested in rescuing Israel out of Egypt, as God, by way of resemblance to man, is said to *stretch out his arm*, i.e. to extend his strength, to effect it. Or it was such as it plainly appeared to be *the arm of the Lord*, even a divine power. See Exo. 6.6. Deut. 5.15. Jer. 32.21. Ezek. 20.33,34.

*{Him shall ye fear}* Deut. 6.13. Fear imports such an inward reverent esteem of God as makes us careful in avoiding all things that offend him, and in doing what may please him.

*{and him shall ye worship}* Worship when it is distinguished from fear of God signifies an external manifestation of an high esteem of God, and that by some warrantable gesture, as kneeling, bowing, prostrating the body, Ps. 95.6. Job 1.20.

*{and to him shall ye do sacrifice}* As inward fear and outward worship were due unto God alone, so sacrifice also, which was an especial evidence both of the one and the other. See 1 Kings 13.4,15.

**V. 37. ***{And the statutes, &c.}* See 1 Kings 2.2,3.

*{which he wrote for you}* If this be properly taken it sets forth the Decalogue, or ten commandments, Exod. 31.18. & 34.1. but metonymically it may be taken for such commandments as Moses wrote from the mouth of God, and delivered to the people, Exo. 34.27.

*{ye shall observe to do for evermore}* This applied to the ceremonial and judicial laws implies a continuance of them all the while that the policy of the Jews remained; yea it implies a further perpetuity in the truth and substance of them, which was Jesus Christ.

*{and ye shall not fear other gods}* See v. 35.

**V. 31. ***{And the Covenant that I have made with you}* See v. 35.

*{and ye shall not forget}* That which is forgotten is as not known; and therefore it cannot be well observed. This negative is much pressed by Moses, Deut. 6.12. & 8.11.

*{neither shall ye fear other gods}* This is here the third time pressed, as v. 35,37. Fear of other gods will either keep out or drive out a right fear of the true God.

**V. 39. ***{But the Lord your God ye shall fear}* It is not sufficient to withdraw our hearts from false gods, unless we set them upon the true God.

*{and he shall deliver you out of the hands of all your enemies}* God takes care of such as fear him, both to provide for them, and also to protect them, yea, and to deliver them from their enemies. The covenant before mentioned on God’s part, together with the conditions annexed for God’s people to observe, is here set down to aggravate the rebellion of the Israelites, and to justify God’s proceedings against them.

**V. 40. ***{Howbeit they did not hearken}* No mercies work upon obstinate hearts.

*{but they did after their former manner}* See v. 34.

**V. 41. ***{So these nations}* Here he returns to set forth the disposition and carriage of those men whom the king of Assyria had brought out of other countries and placed in Samaria; of whom see v. 29, &c. These somewhat altered their former practice, but the Israelites in captivity remained unalterable in their evil courses.

*{feared the Lord}* Acknowledged Jehovah to be a God, and did some services to him. See v. 32,33.

*{and served their graven images}* This shows that their fear of the Lord was unsound and servile. See v. 33. Zeph. 1.5.

*{both their children and their childrens children}* Their posterity, generation after generation, till the last destruction of them by the Romans. The Samaritans mentioned in the New Testament were of this posterity.

*{as did their fathers, so do they}* They continued a mongrel generation, halting betwixt two religions. See 1 King. 18.21.

*{unto this day}* See 1 King. 9.13.

## CHAPTER 18

**V. 1. ***{Now it came to pass in the third year of Hoshea}* This third year is to be reckoned from the beginning of the nine years mentioned ch. 17.1. Ahaz reigned sixteen years current, ch. 16.2. Hoshea beginning to reign in the twelfth of Ahaz, there remain four to make up the sixteenth. But in reckoning years current the last uses to be swallowed up; and in this respect Hezekiah’s reign takes beginning from the third of Hoshea, which being complete may be brought to the end of Ahaz his reign and beginning of Hezekiah’s.

*{the son of Elah king of Israel}* See ch. 17.1.

*{that Hezekiah the son of Ahaz king of Judah began to reign}* 2 Chron. 28.27. & 29.1. He is called Ezekias, Mat. 1.9. See ch. 16.20.

**V. 2. ***{Twenty and five years old was he, &c.}* See ch. 16.2.

*{and he reigned twenty and five years in Jerusalem}* By this account he was fifty years old when he died.

*{his mothers name also was Abi}* Or, *Abijah*, 2 Chron. 29.1. Abi signifies *My father*. Abijah, *the father of the Lord*. Why the mothers of kings are mentioned, see 1 King. 15.10.

*{the daughter of Zachariah}* Some take this Zachariah to be the king of Israel mentioned ch. 14.29. Josephus says he was of Jerusalem.

**V. 3. ***{And he did that which was right, &c.}* See 1 King. 14.8. & 15.5.

*{according to all that David his father did}* Hezekiah in sincerity of heart, and integrity of life was like unto David, who was his father both in respect of natural descent and royal succession. See 1 King. 3.14. & 9.4.

**V. 4. ***{He removed the high places}* 2 Chron. 31.4. according to the commandment Deut. 7.5. See 1 King. 3.4.

*{and brake the images}* Heb. *statues*. See ch. 10.26. & 13.13.

*{and cut down the groves}* See 1 King. 16.33.

*{and brake in pieces the brazen serpent that Moses had made}* Num. 21.8,9. This brazen serpent was first erected to cure such as were stung by fiery serpents, and afterwards was kept as a monument of God’s great mercy towards the children of Israel. It was also an especial type of Christ, Joh. 3.14,15.

*{for unto those days the children of Israel did burn incense to it}* Because God had used it as an especial means of curing their ancestors, they so much doted thereon as to yield divine honor thereunto. Therefore did this good king break it in pieces, that God might be no longer dishonored thereby. When things lawful and useful are perverted to idolatry, they may lawfully be destroyed.

*{and he called it Nehushtan}* The word signifies *brass* or *brazen*. By this name he gave the people to understand that it was but a piece of brass, and that there was no deity in it, and therefore no worship to be done unto it, no sacrifice or incense to be offered unto it. Some read, *they called it*, namely, the people, when they saw it so broken to pieces.

**V. 5. ***{He trusted}* This is laid down as the ground of all that piety, integrity, zeal, courage, and other graces hereafter mentioned. For faith and confidence in God is a mother-grace.

*{in the Lord God of Israel}* See ch. 8.15.

*{so that after him was none like him of all the kings of Judah}* This may literally and properly be taken. For in reforming religion he went beyond all that succeeded him. In good Josiah’s time there was more idolatry in the land than in Hezekiah’s, Jer. 3.6,11. Besides, Hezekiah began his reformation in the very first month of his reign, but Josiah in his eighth year, 2 Chron. 34.3. But above all, there were such miraculous effects of Hezekiah’s prayer as of no king’s else, chap. 19.15. & 20.2, &c.

*{nor any that were before him}* Apply this to such as were properly called kings of Judah, namely such as reigned in Judah after the revolt of the ten tribes, and we shall find none of them like Hezekiah. As for David, Hezekiah is said to be like him, v. 3. and Hezekiah is the first that is noted to have removed the high places. But to remove all doubts, know that this phrase, *none like him either after or before him*, is a proverbial speech, which highly commends one. And proverbs carry a general sense, wherein they hold true, though one or two particulars may seem to make against them. The like is said of Josiah, ch. 22.25. So in discommendation, sundry wicked ones are said to *do worse then any before them*, as 1 King. 16.25,30.

**V. 6. ***{For he clave to the Lord}* Cleaving implies a close and fast adhering to a thing so as not to be parted from it. Thus a man is said to *cleave to his wife*, Gen. 2.24. Ruth, to her mother, Ruth 1.14. the men of Judah, unto their king, 2 Sam. 20.2. Jehoram, to the sins of Jeroboam, 2 King. 3.3. The apostle exhorts to cleave to that which is good, Rom. 12.9. And we are oft exhorted to cleave to the Lord, Deut. 4.4. & 10.20. Josh. 23.8.

*{and departed not from following him}* Heb. *from after him*. See 1 King. 9.6. This negative added to the affirmative shows the force of that word *cleaving*.

*{but kept his commandments}* This is a proof of following the Lord.

*{which the Lord commanded Moses}* Moses was that minister by whom God made known all those commandments which should from time to time be a rule to his people.

**V. 7. ***{And the Lord was with him}* As he was with Abraham, Gen. 21.22. See 1 Chro. 22.11.

*{and he prospered whithersoever he went forth}* God blessed all his enterprises, whether in war or in peace, in fortifying his defensed cities, towers and other places, in purging the temple, and other public works that he undertook.

*{and he rebelled against the king of Assyria}* Not that he broke any covenant which himself had made, but in that he refused to be tributary to that king, as his father was forced to be, chap. 16.7.

*{And served him not}* This shows in what respect he is said to rebel. They who do not serve such as they are made subject unto, are said to rebel. Now indeed the kingdom was left unto him by his father in a subjection to the king of Assyria; but he being no way bound to serve as his father did, because he used his liberty, and cast that yoke from him, he is said to rebel. Or the phrase may be used in regard of the king of Assyria’s opinion; he thought Hezekiah rebelled, though he did not.

**V. 8. ***{He smote the Philistims}* The Philistims had in the reign of his father taken many cities from Judah, 2 Chron. 28.18. He had therefore just cause to smite them, i.e. to destroy them. See 1 King. 15.29.

*{even unto Gaza}* Heb. *Azza*, one of the five principal cities of the Philistims, 1 Sam. 6.17. See 1 King. 4.24.

*{and the borders thereof}* Such parts as coasted on the land of Israel.

*{from the tower of the watch-men to the fenced city}* See ch. 17.9.

**V. 9. ***{And it came to pass in the fourth year of king Hezekiah}* In the very beginning of it. See v. 1.

*{which was the seventh year of Hoshea, &c.}* The third of Hoshea being the first of Hezekiah, the sixth of Hoshea should be the fourth of Hezekiah: But in comparing one king’s reign with another the first and last years are oft swallowed up, because the years are reckoned current. See v. 1.

*{that Shalmaneser king of Assyria}* See ch. 17.3. The history of Israel’s captivity is here repeated, to show how God can preserve the godly when he destroys the wicked, 2 Pet. 2.9.

*{came up against Samaria and besieged it}* See ch. 17.5.

**V. 10. ***{And at the end of three years they took it}* See chap. 17.6.

*{even in the sixth year of Hezekiah}* He being three years in besieging it, the first of those three was the fourth of Hezekiah, the second his fifth, the third his sixth.

*{that is the ninth year of Hoshea, &c.}* ch. 17.6.

**V. 11. ***{And the king of Assyria did carry, &c.}* ch. 17.6.

**V. 12. ***{Because they obeyed not the voice of the Lord, &c.}* This reason of the Lord’s giving his people over to their enemies is largely amplified, ch. 17.7, &c. Of this style, servant of the Lord, see 1 King. 3.6.

**V. 13. ***{Now in the fourteenth year of king Hezekiah}* See on 2 Chr. 32.1, &c. and on Isa. 36.1, &c. This was eight years after Israel’s captivity. Certainly the Assyrian in those years was occupied about subduing other countries, or else he would not have suffered Hezekiah to have enjoyed quiet so long. But there was a greater cause than that, namely God’s providence, which kept Hezekiah from being interrupted in his work of reformation till he had finished the same. See 2 Chron. 32.1.

*{did Sennacherib, &c.}* Heb. *Sanherib*. This was the son of Shalmaneser. See ch. 19.12. It appears that Shalmaneser had work enough to do with other nations, in that he suffered the king of Judah, who cast off his yoke, to remain quiet so long as he did: Or it may be he conceived the kingdom of Judah too strong for them to get it. But after his death Sennacherib his son waxed more bold, and attempts to do what his father did not.

*{come up against all the fenced cities of Judah}* i.e. Such as were well furnished and prepared against an enemy, and were placed on the frontiers of the land for a defense thereof.

*{and took them}* His army was very formidable: for there were slain thereof an hundred fourscore and five thousand, ch. 19.25. no marvel therefore though he took the cities of Judah. God herein might punish the sins of Judah, discover the weakness of Hezekiah, and bring more glory to his name by the enemies' after-confusion, ch. 19.32, &c. He took many of those cities, not all; he thought to do more than he did, 2 Chr. 32.1. In his conceit of conquest he was conquered, ch. 19.28,35.

**V. 14. ***{And Hezekiah king of Judah sent to the king of Assyria}* Though before the danger his heart failed him, yet when he was in the danger, he was courageous himself, and encouraged others, 2 Chron. 32.7, &c.

*{to Lachish}* See ch. 14.19. The king of Assyria was before this city with his army.

*{saying, I have offended}* By refusing to be tributary and by casting off that yoke which was laid upon my father’s neck. See v. 7. This was a condemning of himself unjustly, and argued too much pusillanimity. At first he manifested more courage, 2 Chron. 32.2, &c. but continuance and increase of danger quailed his spirit.

*{return from me}* What he dared not attempt by force he seeks by treaty.

*{that which thou puttest on me I will bear}* I will willingly pay by way of ransom what you shall require. Thus he seeks to buy his peace.

*{And the king of Assyria appointed unto Hezekiah king of Judah, three hundred talents of silver, and thirty talents of gold}* The value of each metal was the same, even an hundred and twelve thousand and five hundred pounds sterling, so as the total of all is two hundred twenty five thousand pounds. See 1 Chr. 22.14.

**V. 15. ***{And Hezekiah gave him all the silver that was found in the house of the Lord, &c.}* See 2 King. 16.8. Thus had Ahaz his father done to this king of Assyria’s father; but such a father as Ahaz was no good precedent for such a son as Hezekiah. The best have their failings.

**V. 16. ***{At that time did Hezekiah cut off the gold from the doors of the Temple of the Lord}* This aggravates Hezekiah’s fault, in that he did not only spoil the temple of its treasure, v. 15. but also pulled the gold from the doors thereof. Such plates of gold are here meant as were laid upon the doors, 1 King. 6.35.

*{and from the pillars which Hezekiah King of Judah had overlaid}* Piety moved Hezekiah in the beginning of his reign to repair with gold what his father had pulled off, 2 Chr. 29.3. but now fear makes him pull off the same again.

*{and gave it}* Heb. *them*. Many plates of gold being pulled off from many places, it is said in the plural number *he gave them*.

*{to the king of Assyria}* This king was a mortal enemy to the Jews, yet to prevent danger Hezekiah presents a bountiful present to him.

**V. 17. ***{And the king of Assyria sent}* He received the present, but stood not to the condition, nor kept his word. For having received the money, he would have Hezekiah give over himself wholly to the will of his enemy, and deliver Jerusalem into his hands, v. 31,32.

*{Tartan, and Rabsaris, and Rabshakeh}* These were three great commanders in the king of Assyria’s army. But Rabshakeh was the chief of them all, and their spokesman. v. 19.

*{from Lachish to King Hezekiah}* At this time the king of Assyria lay in siege before Lachish, or else having taken it abode therein.

*{with a great host against Jerusalem}* Heb. *heavy host*. This implies that he had taken Lachish, or else he would not have raised his army from before it, and sent them to Jerusalem. He sent this huge host to terrify Hezekiah, and to make him yield to his demands.

*{and they went up and came to Jerusalem}* They were ready to put their charge of their sovereign and general in execution.

*{And when they were come up, they came and stood by the conduit of the upper pool}* This conduit was without Jerusalem, into which water was let out of a lake in a field, and from it was conveyed into the city. See on Isa. 7.3. & 22.9.

*{which is in the high way of the fullers field}* The word translated *highway* signifies also *a causeway*. Some take it for *a way paved with stones*; others, *a beaten way*. See on Isa. 7.3.

**V. 18. ***{And when they had called to the king}* To speak with him: for they were not admitted into the city. But the king and his counselors not thinking it meet that the king himself should go to him in his own person, messengers are sent.

*{there came out to them Eliakim the son of Hilkiah, which was over the household}* See 1 King. 4.6. By this title it appears that Shebna’s office was transferred upon this Eliakim, according to the prophecy, Isa. 22.20,21.

*{and Shebna the Scribe}* Or, *Secretary*. See 1 King. 4.3. Because another office is here ascribed to Shebna than is Isa. 22.15. it is supposed by some that this Shebna was another man than he that Isaiah speaks of. For that was so proud a statesman that if so be he had been put out of his stewardship, the king would not have made him his secretary. Others are of opinion that Shebna here mentioned was an old man, and though he were put from being over the household for his disability well to manage that great place, yet the office of secretary being an honorable place, and not requiring so much pains and care as the other, he was made scribe.

*{and Joah the son of Asaph the Recorder}* See 1 King. 4.3.

**V. 19. ***{And Rabshakeh said unto them, &c.}* Because the king himself came not forth to them, v. 18. they require that their message be delivered to him.

*{Thus saith the great king}* This is a proud and presumptuous preface. They press the name of their king as prophets were wont to press the name of the Lord of hosts, Jer. 6.9. Zach. 1.3. See v. 29.

*{What confidence is this wherein thou trustest?}* He slights and scorns confidence in all others but in himself.

**V. 20. ***{Thou sayest}* Or, *talkest*. He reckons up those grounds of confidence which he supposes Hezekiah and his people might have to stand against the king of Assyria, and takes upon him to manifest the vanity of them all.

*{(but they are but vain words)}* Heb. *word of the lips*. Such words as are only uttered with the mouth, but come not from any understanding or prudence; such as can never be ratified by a good issue: They are fruitless, worth nothing.

*{I have counsel and strength for the war}* Or, *but counsel and strength are for the war*. This he pretends was the first ground of the Jews' confidence. And indeed these two, counsel and strength, are both necessary for defense against an enemy. But this by way of derision he objects to Hezekiah, as if he had neither; and therefore says they were *vain words*.

*{now on whom dost thou trust that thou rebellest against me?}* This is a second supposed ground of Hezekiah’s confidence. He implies that if he had not some foreign aid to trust to he would not dare to rebel against the king of Assyria.

**V. 21. ***{Now behold}* This phrase shows that what follows is uttered in way of derision.

*{thou trustest}* Heb. *trustest thee*. Repose yourself and your confidence.

*{upon the staff of this bruised reed}* He uses this metaphor, because Egypt lying low among rivers had much reedy ground. Egypt is here resembled to a staff, by reason of that pretense it made of help and support; to a staff of reed, by reason of its impotency; to a bruised, or broken reed, by reason of the damage like to accrue from thence. For a reed broken or bruised is sharp and full of teeth, and pierces into a man’s flesh, Isai. 36.6. Ezek. 29.6,7.

*{even upon Egypt, on which if a man lean, it will go into his hand, and pierce it}* Here he expresses the meaning of his metaphor.

*{So is Pharaoh king of Egypt unto all that trust on him}* He implies that Pharaoh had failed and still would fail all that place confidence in him.

**V. 22. ***{But if ye say unto me, We trust in the Lord our God}* This is a third ground of confidence, and indeed a sure ground; And he here alleges it in its full extent, expressing the title *Jehovah* under this word *LORD*, and also adding that special relation which was betwixt God and the Jews, in this phrase, *our God*. God can demonstrate even to enemies the ground of his saints' confidence; but they slight and pervert it.

*{is not that he whose high places}* Here he labors to prove even this ground of confidence but vain, accounting such acts of piety and holy zeal as Hezekiah had done to be matters of impiety and sacrilege. See 1 King. 3.4.

*{and whose altars Hezekiah hath taken away}* Among other signs, means and instruments of idolatry, altars whereon sacrifices to idols were offered, were principal ones. These indeed, together with high places, did Hezekiah take away, 2 Chro. 31.1. but therein he much pleased God, and no way offended him, as this blasphemer avouches.

*{and hath said to Judah and Jerusalem}* To the inhabitants of these places, which were in his dominions.

*{Ye shall worship before this altar in Jerusalem}* With emphasis he expresses it by this particle one, 2 Chron. 32.12. yet herein was he deceived, in that he said incense was to be burnt upon it. For there was one great altar of brass for sacrifices, 2 Chron. 4.1. and another of gold for incense, 2 Chron. 4.19. 1 Chron. 28.18.

**V. 23. ***{Now therefore}* This is his inference from the supposed vanity of Hezekiah’s confidence.

*{I pray thee}* This in the Hebrew is but one little word of two letters נא, which is oft taken for a mere complement or supplement, and translated *now*. As Psal. 111.1,2,3,4. and we can take it no otherwise here. Our English phrase, *I pray thee*, is oft so used.

*{give pledges}* Or, *hostages*. His meaning is, that they should willingly submit themselves, and give assurance of their fidelity.

*{to my Lord the king of Assyria}* For in his name he agitated this cause.

*{and I will deliver thee two thousand horses}* Here he speaks in the king’s name, as he began v. 19. Or it may be the king had given him power to perform what here he proffers.

*{if thou be able on thy part to set riders upon them}* This is a plain scoff, whereby he implies that Judah had neither two thousand horses, nor yet two thousand men to be troopers. And thus he may in scorn use the former phrase in his own name, *q.d.* I myself am able to afford you two thousand horses, but you have not men enough to use them. Thus he prefers not only his master but even himself also before Hezekiah, as is more manifest v. 24.

**V. 24. ***{How then wilt thou turn away the face}* i.e. Deny what is required. See 1 King. 2.16,20. Or it may be taken for putting to flight.

*{of one Captain of the least of my Masters servants}* He does not only prefer his sovereign and himself, but also every captain, even the lowest and meanest, of the king of Assyria, before the king of Judah.

*{and put thy trust on Egypt}* He thought that all Hezekiah’s trust was on Egypt (to whom he sought not at all in this case;) and therefore as he pressed it, v. 21. so here he returns to it again.

*{for chariots and for horsemen}* Of all nations Egypt most abounded with these. See 1 King. 10.28.

**V. 25. ***{Am I now come up without the Lord}* This blasphemer having, as he thought, sufficiently discovered the vain confidence of Hezekiah, now lays down the ground of his master’s confidence; and that is, God’s sending him. For the interrogative implies a negative; and this phrase, *without the Lord*, implies a commission, direction and assistance from the Lord. Indeed it was by God’s secret counsel that he came, Isai. 10.5, &c. but he knew it not.

*{against this place to destroy it}* This is the end he aimed at in coming against Jerusalem, and he pretends God sent him for that end. This he says to affright them the more.

*{The Lord said unto me, Go up against this land and destroy it}* He never had any such message from the Lord; but either willingly he suggests a falsehood, to accomplish his ends the sooner; or he might have such an imagination by reason of the wonderful success he had, not only in conquering other nations, v. 34. but also in taking sundry cities in Judah; and by reason of that sacrilege which he supposed Hezekiah had committed against the Lord. See v. 22.

**V. 26. ***{Then said Eliakim the son of Hilkiah, and Shebna, and Joah, &c.}* Because these three were sent, v. 18. they are all named; but Eliakim only spoke, and the rest assented to what he said. See v. 17.

*{speak, I pray thee}* See v. 23.

*{to thy servants}* He uses this phrase of subjection complementally, that he might gain audience, and not provoke wrath.

*{in the Syrian language}* Though the Syrian were a distinct language from the Hebrew, yet in many words, phrases, and kind of pronunciation it came near it.

*{(for we understand it)}* They being courtiers were skilled in foreign languages, as courtiers, nobles and gentry use to be with us.

*{and talk not with us in the Jews language}* Some hence gather that Rabshakeh was a Jew trained up among the Assyrians: for though the Jews might be skillful in other languages, yet very few in other nations learned the Jews' language.

*{in the ears of the people}* So as they may hear and understand. See v. 27.

*{that are on the wall}* By this phrase he shows what kind of people he meant, namely such soldiers as were placed on the wall of the city to maintain it.

**V. 27. ***{But Rabshakeh said unto them}* Rabshakeh was so far from being moved with Eliakim’s complemental speech, as he is thereby the more stirred up to acquaint the people with his mind and meaning, and to affright them. Had Eliakim well considered to whom he spoke, namely, to an implacable enemy, he would certainly have forborne to utter what he did. It was a rash and unadvised motion.

*{Hath my master sent me to thy master}* Both of them were sent from their own king.

*{and to thee, to speak these words}* These phrases *thy* and *thee* are intended exclusively, *q.d.* to him and thee alone.

*{hath he not sent me to the men that sit on the wall}* See v. 26. To the soldiers that stand out against us, as well as to you.

*{that they may eat their own dung, and drink their own piss with you}* Heb. *the water of their feet*. For men use to make water betwixt their feet. He implies, that if they yielded not, they were likely to endure all the miseries of a siege, and be brought to extreme hunger and thirst.

**V. 28. ***{Then Rabshakeh stood and cried with a loud voice}* Heb. *great voice*. The greater a sound is the farther it is heard. He lift up his voice the higher, that more might hear him, the more to affright them.

*{in the Jews language}* This Eliakim got by his unadvised motion to his enemy. He stirred him up the more to affright the soldiers.

*{and spake, saying, Hear the word of the great King, the King of Assyria}* See v. 19.

**V. 29. ***{Thus saith the King}* See v. 19.

*{Let not Hezekiah deceive you}* He would make them believe that all the encouragements which Hezekiah could give them would be to no purpose.

*{for he shall not be able to deliver you}* He was not well acquainted with God’s almighty power, whereupon Hezekiah trusted, and by which he was delivered.

*{out of his hand}* From the power of the king of Assyria.

**V. 30. ***{Neither let Hezekiah make you trust in the Lord}* Proud men seek to draw others from their confidence in the Lord. Rabshakeh was persuaded that Hezekiah did move them to trust in the Lord.

*{saying, the Lord will surely deliver us}* Heb. *delivering will deliver us*. See 1 King. 8.13.

*{and this city shall not be delivered into the hand of the King of Assyria}* He was overconfident of getting Jerusalem, and too peremptory in thinking the Lord would not or could not deliver them.

**V. 31. ***{Hearken not to Hezekiah}* He thought it was Hezekiah’s persuasion that kept them from yielding, and therefore he so much strikes upon this string. See v. 29,30.

*{make an agreement with me by a present}* Or, *seek my favour*. Heb. *make with me a blessing*. His meaning is, they should make peace with him, or tender a present to him; or by a present procure their peace.

*{and come out to me}* Yield to me; and deliver the city to my disposal.

*{and then eat ye every man of his own vine, and every one of his fig-tree, and drink ye every one of the waters of his cistern}* Or, *pit*. As Jer. 2.13. If they would yield to him, they should by his permission and under his protection enjoy their own inheritances and the fruit of their labors. Vines and fig trees were very plentiful in Judah. And where they had not rivers, they had ponds, and wells, and conduits, and other like means for water; which are here expressed synecdochically.

**V. 32. ***{Until I come}* This is a limitation of his promise: upon their yielding to him he would depart, and suffer them quietly to enjoy what they had, and after a time come again.

*{and take you away to a land like your own land}* He would deal with them as his predecessors had done with Israel, ch. 17.6. He plainly tells it, because he could not but know that they would fear he would so deal with them.

*{a land of corn and wine, a land of bread and vineyards, a land of oil-olives and of honey}* These were plentiful in Judea; and he would settle them in places that should abound with the like commodities: Deut. 32.13,14. See Exod. 3.8. Job 20.17.

*{that you may live and not die}* This he adds to show the difference betwixt their standing out against him, and yielding to him. If they yielded, they should live; if they persisted to stand out, they could expect nothing but death.

*{and hearken not unto Hezekiah when he persuadeth you}* Or, *deceiveth you*. See v. 31.

*{saying, The Lord will deliver us}* See v. 30.

**V. 33. ***{Hath any gods of the nations delivered at all}* Heb. *delivering delivered*. See 1 King. 8.13.

*{his land out of the hand of the King of Assyria}* He equals the idols of the nations to the only true God. See v. 35. See also v. 30.

**V. 34. ***{Where are the gods}* This is a most insolent insultation of a mortal man.

*{of Hamath and of Arpad}* See on Isa. 10.9. Jer. 49.23.

*{where are the gods of Sepharvaim, Hena, and Ivah?}* These were other cities vanquished by the Assyrian, ch. 17.24. Sundry of their gods' names are expressed ch. 17.30,31.

*{have they delivered Samaria out of mine hand?}* Either some of these gods were worshipped in Samaria before it was overcome by the Assyrians: or the gods of Samaria are understood under this relative they.

**V. 35. ***{Who are they among all the gods of the Countries that have delivered their country out of mine hand}* See v. 33. His pride makes him oft inculcate the same things, and that against those whom he accounted gods. He advanced himself above them all.

*{that the Lord should deliver Jerusalem out of mine hand}* Here again he equals Jehovah with plain idols.

**V. 36. ***{But the people held their peace, and answered him not a word}* The people seeing what issue followed upon Eliakim’s answer, prudently held their peace. By silence no quarrel could be picked against them.

*{for the kings commandment was, saying, Answer him not}* This was one special reason of their silence. The king might suppose that fear might make them give some rash answer; whereby the enemy might take some advantage and be the more exasperated. Besides, silence is oft a contempt of an insolent speech.

**V. 37. ***{Then came Eliakim, &c.}* See v. 18.

*{with their clothes rent}* Heb. *rents of their clothes*. This rite of rending clothes was of old used upon occasion of grief, as an expression of much passion, Gen. 37.34. Judg. 11.35. and upon apprehension of God’s displeasure, as a sign of great contrition, 2 King. 22.11,19. and also upon hearing of blasphemy, as an evidence of great indignation, Mat. 26.65. All these may be here intended.

*{and told him the words of Rabshakeh}* That the king himself might as deeply apprehend the common danger as they did, and might seriously advise about preventing it.

## CHAPTER 19

**V. 1. ***{And it came to pass when king Hezekiah heard it}* See Isai. 37.1. This has relation to all Rabshakeh’s railings ch. 18. It is added 2 Chron. 32.16. that Sennacherib’s servants spake yet more against the Lord God, and against his servant Hezekiah.

*{that he rent his clothes}* Even as Eliakim and others ch. 18.37.

*{and covered himself with sackcloth}* See 1 King. 21.27.

*{and went into the house of the Lord}* There to make solemn prayer and to offer sacrifice. See 1 King. 8.33,62.

**V. 2. ***{And he sent Eliakim, &c.}* See ch. 18.18.

*{and the Elders of the Priests}* Instead of Joah the recorder these elders are now sent. As those were elder in age than others, so had they a government over other priests and Levites.

*{covered with sackcloth}* King, priests and nobles were all covered with sackcloth, because it was a common and public danger that hung over all their heads. So Jon. 3.5.

*{to Isaiah the Prophet the son of Amoz}* This was that prophet whose prophecies are set before all the rest, Isai. 1.1. He sent to this prophet to know the issue of this danger, to be directed by him what to do, and to have the benefit of his prayers: for it was one part of a prophet’s function to pray for people, Gen. 20.7.

**V. 3. ***{And they said unto him, Thus saith Hezekiah}* Hezekiah put their message into their mouth, and they answerably deliver it as from him:

*{This day is a day of trouble, and of rebuke}* At this time we are much molested by the enemy, who does not only besiege our city, but also exceedingly reproach us.

*{and blasphemy}* Or, *provocation*. By blaspheming the great name of God they do much provoke God himself.

*{for the children are come to the birth, and there is not strength to bring forth}* A parabolical speech. Jerusalem besieged is resembled to a woman in travail; the inhabitants, to infants in the womb; the straitness of the siege, and threatening of the potent enemy, to pains of travail; the weakness of the Jews, to the disability of the woman to help herself. The sense is this, We are brought to the utmost extremity of danger, and are utterly unable to help ourselves: Now therefore is the time for the Lord to show himself; if he do not now help us, we perish.

**V. 4. ***{It may be}* This is a phrase taken from men, when they conceal their mind in such things as there is strong hope, they will do. In this place it is not a phrase of doubt and diffidence, but of good hope and confidence, as Josh. 14.12. 1 Sam. 14.6. & 2 Sam. 16.12.

*{the Lord thy God}* See 1 King. 1.17. He uses this phrase *thy God* to stir him up the more to call upon the Lord, who was in special his God.

*{will hear all the words of Rabshakeh}* This being spoken in the future tense concerning words before uttered, implies that God would show that he had heard those words, and that by confounding the devices of so proud an enemy.

*{whom the King of Assyria his master hath sent to reproach}* To blaspheme and dishonor God’s name.

*{the living God}* That God who has life of himself, and in himself, and ever so remains; who also has the power of others' life, to continue it or take it away as it pleases him. To put this God in the number and rank of dumb and senseless idols, is to reproach him. So did Rabshakeh, ch. 18.35.

*{and will reprove the words which the Lord thy God hath heard}* To reprove such words is to show how vain and false they are, by crossing that which they set forth, and by bringing confusion upon the utterers of them.

*{wherefore lift up thy prayer}* This phrase implies as a raising up of the heart on high to God that is in heaven, so also earnestness and fervency in prayer.

*{for the remnant that are left}* Heb. *found*. i.e. Those that remained in Judah who were comparatively, in regard of former times, but a remnant. For first, a great spoil was made of the men of Judah in Ahaz his time, 2 Chron. 28.6. Secondly, Shalmaneser had taken away the ten tribes, ch. 17.3,6. Thirdly, this Sennacherib had destroyed many, ch. 18.13. And it may be the fear of this enemy had forced many to fly into other places.

**V. 5. ***{So the servants of king Hezekiah came to Isaiah}* These servants were those venerable persons mentioned v. 2. who were of the king’s court, and in that respect as his household servants. Besides all sorts of subjects are servants to their sovereign. These readily do the king’s message.

**V. 6. ***{And Isaiah said unto them, Thus shall you say to your master}* The Lord whose eyes are in every place beholding the evil and the good, well knowing the enemy’s mischievous mind and Hezekiah’s faith, soon revealed his will to his prophet.

*{Thus saith the Lord, Be not afraid of the words which thou hast heard}* The Lord encourages his humble servant against the proud threats of the enemy.

*{with which the servants of the king of Assyria}* The word properly signifies *young men*. See ch. 4.24. The plural number is here used, though Rabshakeh only uttered the words, because many assisted him and consented to his blasphemy, ch. 18.17,18.

*{have blasphemed me}* By making me no better than an idol, ch. 18.35. God here appropriates the quarrel to himself, the more to strengthen Hezekiah’s faith concerning his just revenge on their proud enemy.

**V. 7. ***{Behold, I will send a blast upon him}* Heb. *a wind*. Here it signifies that which like the blast of a terrible tempest should overthrow his formidable army; or drive it as chaff clean away.

*{and he shall hear a rumour}* This he did when Tirhakah came, v. 9. and when destruction fell upon his army, v. 35.

*{and shall return to his own land}* Which he was forced to do upon the loss of his army, v. 36.

*{and I will cause him to fall}* God wisely and righteously orders the plots of the wicked.

*{by the sword in his own land}* See v. 37.

**V. 8. ***{So Rabshakeh returned}* Without the army, that was left still before Jerusalem. He might return to the king of Assyria to inform him of the Jews' unalterable resolution, and having heard of Tirhakah, to advise with the king, whether it were meet to remove the army or no.

*{and found the king of Assyria warring against Libnah}* See ch. 8.22.

*{for he had heard that he was departed from Lachish}* Rabshakeh heard that Sennacherib was departed. Some hold that Sennacherib found the taking of Lachish too difficult a task at that time, and therefore left it; for it was a very strong city, ch. 14.19. Others, that he had taken Lachish: And it is not probable that he would remove his army from city to city before he had done his work. See v. 24. He had two great armies at this time, one before Jerusalem, and another before Libnah.

**V. 9. ***{And when he heard say}* i.e. Sennacherib who heard this rumor either from Rabshakeh or some other way.

*{of Tirhakah king of Ethiopia}* Heb. *Cush*. The Ethiopians used to raise huge hosts. See 2 Chron. 14.9. therefore this news must needs affright the king of Assyria.

*{Behold, he is come out to fight against thee}* Whether he went to invade Sennacherib’s own land, or to meet with his army in the field, is not expressed: Either the one or the other was cause enough to draw Sennacherib from his enterprise against Judah.

*{he sent}* Heb. *he turned and sent*. He desisted from his enterprise at Libnah, and sent.

*{messengers again unto Hezekiah, saying}* He was very eager to have Jerusalem, both because it was a large city, well fenced and every way fit to retreat unto upon all occasions in his war, and also to avoid the infamy of raising so great an army as he had brought against it, without doing anything.

**V. 10. ***{Thus shall ye speak to Hezekiah king of Judah, saying}* It appears v. 14. that he sent letters; but withal, he might enjoin his messengers to deliver the sum of what he had written by word of mouth, lest Hezekiah should lay the letter aside and refuse to read it; or that both by word and writing he might the more terrify him.

*{Let not thy God in whom thou trustest deceive thee, &c.}* This in effect was delivered before, ch. 18.29,30. But he might think that what Rabshakeh had spoken to the soldiers on the wall, ch. 18.27. was not reported to the king; or else, that by inculcating the same thing again and again he might the more terrify Hezekiah, 2 Chr. 32.18.

**V. 11. ***{Behold thou hast heard what the Kings of Assyria have done}* This also was in effect delivered, ch. 18.33, &c.

*{to all lands}* This particle all is to be restrained to the nations thereabouts; or be taken of all that he attempted to take; or synecdochically for many.

*{by destroying them utterly}* Not that he slew all, but dispeopled the countries of their inhabitants, ch. 17.6,24.

*{and shalt thou be delivered?}* This interrogation imports a strong negation, Gen. 18.17.

**V. 12. ***{Have the gods of the nations delivered them}* See ch. 18.33.

*{which my fathers}* Predecessors; as his father Shalmaneser, ch. 17.3,6. his grandfather Tiglath-Pileser, ch. 15.29. his great-grandfather Pul, ch. 15.19.

*{have destroyed}* See v. 11.

*{as Gozan}* See ch. 17.6.

*{and Haran}* See Gen. 11.31.

*{and Rezeph}* This is said to be in Mesopotamia.

*{and the children of Eden}* Some take Eden for the proper name of a man; others for that country wherein the garden of God was, Gen. 2.8. situate in Chaldea, Ezek. 27.23. Thus children are put for inhabitants, as Ezr. 2.21,26. children of Bethlehem, children of Ramah.

*{which were in Thelasar}* This is taken for a country by some; by others for a fortress by Euphrates.

**V. 13. ***{Where is the King of Hamath, &c.}* Of these places Rabshakeh made mention ch. 18.34. Only instead of *gods* the word *King* is here used. By these two words it is implied that neither their God nor their king could deliver them.

**V. 14. ***{And Hezekiah received the letter of the hand of the messengers and read it}* His faith made him ready to hear and read the worst that his enemy could say or write.

*{and Hezekiah went up into the house of the Lord}* See v. 1.

*{and spread it}* He laid the letter wide open, that by the sight of those blasphemies which were in it he might quicken his own spirit and strengthen his faith the more. When men read in letters things grievous to themselves and dishonorable to others, they will open and show them to those others, and say, Behold, see, read what he has written against you.

*{before the Lord}* Before the altar, which was a representation of God’s presence. So Solomon, 1 King. 8.22,28. Or it might be before the door of the temple, through which he might see the most holy place, where the ark was, the most special evidence of God’s presence, Levit. 1.3. 1 Chron. 16.1. 1 King. 8.64. See ch. 16.14.

**V. 15. ***{And Hezekiah prayed before the Lord}* See v. 14. He did not make a dumb show when he spread the letter, but withal made a fervent and effectual prayer unto God.

*{and said, O Lord God of Israel}* See 1 King. 8.15.

*{which dwellest between the Cherubims}* The cherubins were placed near the ark, 1 King. 8.7. Where did God use to declare his mind, Numb. 7.89. and thereupon he is said to sit or dwell there, Psal. 80.1. The king to strengthen his faith in a gracious answer, thus sets out God; the rather, because the golden cherubins were the most glorious types under the law, 1 King. 6.23, &c. and they set out the glory of God, as angels do now, Mark 8.38. or as heaven does, Mat. 6.9. See 1 Sam. 4.4. These cherubins did also put the king in mind of the mercy seat, which represented the throne of grace, Heb. 4.16. & 9.5. See 1 Chron. 28.2.

*{thou art the God}* This he acknowledges in opposition to their making of God an idol. See ch. 18.35.

*{even thou alone, of all the Kingdoms of the earth}* Though in a peculiar respect he was the Lord God of Israel, yet in his general sovereignty he was the God of all nations. Every kingdom had its own idol; but none of those idols was the true God.

*{thou hast made heaven and earth}* This effect proves him to be the only true God, Jer. 10.11,12.

**V. 16. ***{Lord, bow down thine ear, and hear}* These phrases are applied to God after the manner of man, to make men better conceive how God hears their prayers.

*{open, Lord, thine eyes, and see}* These phrases are used in the same sense, to assure men that God seeing their misery will pity them.

*{and hear the words of Sennacherib}* His meaning is, that God would give some evidence that he heard them: for the words of Sennacherib were uttered before.

*{which hath sent him}* i.e. Rabshakeh, ch. 18.19. They who enjoin others to do evil are accounted the doers of it.

*{to reproach the living God}* See v. 4.

**V. 17. ***{Of a truth, Lord}* This is a phrase whereby a truth, but such a truth as makes nothing against the point in hand, is granted.

*{the Kings of Assyria have destroyed the nations and their lands}* This the king of Assyria said of himself, v. 11. and Hezekiah denies it not. A truth is to be granted to an enemy.

**V. 18. ***{And have cast}* Heb. *given*. Fire is as a devouring beast. As therefore we are said to give ravenous beasts that which we cast before them, so this phrase is used of such things as are cast into the fire.

*{their gods into the fire}* Such statues and images as they imagined to be gods.

*{for they were no gods}* None in deed and truth. This is rendered as a reason why men had such power over them.

*{but the work of mens hands}* Men and all other creatures are the work of God’s hands: It is therefore strange blindness to account the work of men’s hands to be gods.

*{wood and stone}* These were the most common materials of which they framed and formed their gods.

*{therefore they have destroyed them}* This they could not have done, if they had been true gods.

**V. 19. ***{Now therefore, O Lord our God}* See 1 King. 8.28. This is spoken in opposition to what he had said of the gods of the nations. *q.d.* Because you are not as they, but a true God, the only true God, and in special manner a God unto us.

*{I beseech thee save thou us out of his hand}* From the power of Sennacherib. That special relation of the Lord’s being their God makes him the more earnest and steadfast in desiring and expecting preservation from him.

*{that all the Kingdoms of the earth may know}* Hezekiah in his prayer had respect to the glory of God’s name as well as to his own preservation. For the power and providence of God being known far and near throughout all kingdoms makes much to his glory.

*{that thou art the Lord God, even thou only}* This Hezekiah knew and acknowledged before, v. 15. but here he desires that all others might know as much.

**V. 20. ***{Then Isaiah the son of Amoz}* See v. 2.

*{sent to Hezekiah, saying}* So soon as Hezekiah had ended his prayer, God testifies his hearing and accepting it by a speedy gracious return thereof. See Dan. 9.23.

*{Thus saith the Lord God of Israel}* See 1 King. 12.24. & 8.15.

*{That which thou hast prayed unto me against, &c.}* This word *against* shows that Hezekiah prayed not only for his and his people’s preservation, but also for some judgment to fall on the enemies. Imprecations against obstinate enemies may be made.

*{I have heard}* I have taken such notice thereof as I am resolved to grant it.

**V. 21. ***{This is the word that the Lord hath spoken concerning him}* This is the warrant the prophet had to declare his message. On such a warrant may Christ’s ministers be emboldened and encouraged.

*{the Virgin}* This title is given to the state of the Jews in sundry peculiar respects: 1. In relation to their king, and the government under which it was. It had in that respect remained faithful. 2. In relation to God. At this time she was incorrupt in religion, and as a virgin, not deflowered. 3. In regard of the first constitution thereof. When that state was first settled in Moses his time, and afterwards confirmed in David’s and Solomon’s, it was a virgin; and being as yet not cast off, it was still accounted a virgin. 4. In regard of those many which in the corruptest times remained faithful. 5. Because that state continued still in that land which God the Father thereof gave her, and wherein he settled her. For it is the part of a virgin to abide in her father’s house. 6. Because she was fair, beautiful, and comely, as a virgin uses to be, in regard of the temple, divine ordinances, and other excellencies conferred upon her. 7. This resemblance may also have respect to her present weak condition. For a virgin in regard of her sex and youth, uses to be weak. True it is that Israel after she fell to idolatry is called *Virgin*, Amos 5.2. and Judah also, Jer. 18.13. & 31.21. But this was in relation to their former times, before they were idolaters; and also in relation to their duty, what they should have been. It is also true that Judah after her destruction, Jer. 14.17. Lam. 1.15. is called *Virgin*; but it is to aggravate the judgment inflicted on her: She who should have remained safely and delicately in her father’s house, was pulled out and sorely oppressed. Yea further it is true, that Zidon, Isa. 23.12. Babylon, Isa. 47.1. Egypt, Jer. 46.11. and other heathenish nations are styled *Virgins*; but that is in regard of their temporal estate, they were outwardly glorious and unconquered; or else in regard of their priding and setting forth themselves as virgins, Isa. 62.5. This hinders not but that God’s people may in other respects be thus resembled, Jer. 31.4.

*{the daughter of Zion}* That which was called *Virgin* is also called *daughter*, because that people was as a *daughter* brought forth and brought up, nourished and cherished, in Zion and Jerusalem.

*{hath despised thee}* The proud king of Assyria despised, derided and scorned the Jews: Therefore the more to manifest the Jews' contempt of the proud king, it is here said that that *Virgin and Daughter* despised him. Thus these two metaphors may have relation to the present weak estate of the Jews.

*{and laughed thee to scorn}* Laughter is an external sign of scorn.

*{the daughter of Jerusalem hath shaken her head at thee}* Shaking of the head is another sign and gesture of contempt and scorn. There is an elegancy in doubling those phrases, daughter of Zion, daughter of Jerusalem; and there is also great emphasis in multiplying the phrases and signs of derision and scorn. These are further amplified by the apostasy, wherein the virgin and daughter, though female, and in that respect weak, does in a manner dare the proud king to his face; but all this is upon confidence in God’s succor afforded to the Jews: and of the vain attempts of the king of Assyria. And it is observable that it is God himself who thus sets forth his people outfacing their enemy.

**V. 22. ***{Whom hast thou reproached and blasphemed?}* See v. 3,4. All manner of blasphemy is a reproaching of God. His blasphemy and reproach was his making the Lord to be like the gods of the nations, and able to do no more than they, ch. 18.35.

*{and against whom hast thou exalted thy voice}* ch. 18.28.

*{and lift up thine eyes on high}* Both this and the former are gestures of insolent arrogancy. This reproof is set down interrogatively, to make him and others judges of that folly, and to convince them the more thereof.

*{even against the holy One of Israel}* This answer to the former question does to the life set forth their folly; and that by a description of him whom they blasphemed: He was the Lord God, who is holy in regard of his essential property: the holy One, by an excellency and property; none like him therein: the holy One of Israel, in that he dedicated and consecrated himself to be in a special manner Israel’s God. See Psal. 71.22. Isai. 1.4. Ezek. 39.7.

**V. 23. ***{By thy messengers}* Heb. *by the hand of thy messengers*. i.e. Those mentioned ch. 18.17.

*{thou hast reproached the Lord}* See v. 3,4,16,22.

*{and hast said}* Though the words following were not in so many syllables uttered, either in Sennacherib’s letter, or by his messengers, yet the sense was thus. Or, though these words are not expressed in the story, yet they might be uttered, or written in the letter.

*{With the multitude of my chariots I am come up to the height of the mountains}* The proud and arrogant speech of that enemy is expressed in elegant and pertinent comparisons. By coming with the multitude of his chariots is meant a conquering and triumphing manner of showing himself: By mountains are meant cities of Judah; by the height of them, the midst of them, or the chief places or strongest forts and towers in them.

*{to the sides of Lebanon}* He resembles Jerusalem to Lebanon, which was goodly and fair to behold.

*{and will cut down the tall cedar-trees thereof}* Heb. *the tallness, &c.* This Hebraism carries emphasis, and implies very high and tall cedars.

*{and the choice fir-trees thereof}* Lebanon was full of cedars and fir trees. See 1 King. 5.6,8. By these are set out the king, nobles, and other eminent persons of Judah.

*{and I will enter into the lodgings of his borders}* i.e. His frontier towns where soldiers lodge.

*{and into the forest of his Carmel}* Or, *the forrest and his fruitful field*. Of Carmel see 1 King. 18.19. By these titles are meant the fields, vineyards, orchards, gardens, and other like places in Judea and Jerusalem. Some take these words literally. *q.d.* Though Lebanon be a high mountain, so as it is thought impossible to come up with an army to the top of it, and though Carmel be another mountain far remote from the same, yet I can pass over the one and the other, and cut down all the cedars and fir trees of Lebanon, and pass through all the frontier towns of Judah. Whether we take it metaphorically or properly, it sets out the proud spirit of Sennacherib, who accounted nothing unpassable or impregnable to him.

**V. 24. ***{I have digged and drunk strange waters}* Waters in places where none were before, as Numb. 21.18. He means hereby, that though there were no rivers, nor springs, nor ponds, nor any other places for water, whither he should come, yet he could provide water enough for himself and his whole army; could either dig up springs of water, or cause strangers to bring water enough to him.

*{and with the sole of my feet have I dried up all the rivers of besieged places}* Or, *fenced places*. i.e. With the treading of his numerous army he stopped all the streams that ran into the places that stood out against him. A very proud brag, both of providing for himself, though his enemies did what they could to distress him and bring him to want water or food, as appears v. 23. and also of annoying his enemies, and bringing them to distress and want, notwithstanding the great pains they took to provide for themselves, as in this verse. Herein he derides Hezekiah’s care and diligence both in stopping the water of the fountains, and the river that ran through the midst of the country, and also in bringing water into the city, 2 Chr. 32.3,4,30.

**V. 25. ***{Hast thou not heard long ago, how I have done it, and of ancient times that I have formed it? Now have I brought it to pass that thou shouldst be to lay waste fenced Cities into ruinous heaps}* Or, *Hast thou not heard how I have made it long ago, and formed it of ancient times? Should I now bring it to be laid waste, and fenced cities to be ruinous heaps?*

*{Hast thou not heard}* The prophet changes the person, and brings in God answering the proud brags of Sennacherib, who tells him he had no cause to boast of his victories.

*{how I have done it}* God professes that he did that which Sennacherib arrogated to himself. See Isa. 10.5,7,13,15.

*{and of ancient times that I have formed it?}* God here also shows, that he of old purposed and determined that to be done which Sennacherib did.

*{now have I brought it to pass}* My counsel is now put in execution.

*{that thou shouldst be}* This God speaks to Sennacherib, who should be his scourge and sword.

*{to lay waste fenced cities}* To vanquish and overthrow strong and well prepared cities, forts and castles.

*{into ruinous heaps}* Being battered down even to the ground. The other reading that is in the margin of our Bibles implies thus much, that God had aforetime of old chose the Jews to be a nation peculiar to himself, and settled them in that land wherein they were; and thereupon Sennacherib might well think that God would not give them over into his hand to be utterly spoiled and ruined. And where he says, *Hast thou not heard*, his meaning is, that it was so well known throughout all ages and in all places as it could not but come to the hearing of Sennacherib.

**V. 26. ***{Therefore their inhabitants were of small power}* Heb. *short of hand*. By a man’s hand his power is most manifested: and he is said to be *short of hand* who cannot reach that which he puts out his hand unto. This has relation to that which is noted v. 25. of God’s giving those over to Sennacherib whom he destroyed. See Isa. 50.2.

*{they were dismayed and confounded}* They who are of short hand, i.e. They who cannot maintain themselves against an enemy, cannot be but much dismayed and thereupon with shame much confounded.

*{they were as the grass of the field}* Which uses soon to be cut down with the scythe, or to be eaten up with beasts, Psal. 90. Isa. 40.6.

*{and as the green herb}* Green herbs in the garden, if they be not plucked up will soon wither.

*{as the grass on the house-tops}* Which having no depth of earth cannot long continue, Ps. 129.6.

*{and as corn blasted before it be grown up}* Such corn never comes to maturity, but withers away. All these are fit comparisons to set out the impotency and instability of those who are left of God: they cannot long continue.

**V. 27. ***{But I know thy abode}* Or, *sitting*. What you plot and contrive by yourself or with others, Ps. 139.2,3.

*{and thy going out, and thy coming in}* What you do attempt and practice within doors and without, Psal. 121.8. Deut. 28.6,19.

*{and thy rage against me}* Hereby his blasphemy against God may be intended, but especially his furious attempts against God’s people. See Act. 9.4.

**V. 28. ***{Because thy rage against me and thy tumult}* Your preparing arms, men, horses, and other things fit for war, your marching up and down with them, your assaults and your threatenings.

*{is come up into my ears}* Is known to me. This phrase is attributed to God after the manner of men. They hear such things as come to their ears, and what they hear they know and take notice of.

*{therefore I will put my hook into thy nose}* I will deal with you as men use to do with bears, bulls, and other wild beasts, or with great fishes, into whose nose or mouth if an hook be put, they may be drawn or led up and down whither a man pleases.

*{and my bridle in thy lips}* This has relation to men’s dealing with horses, especially with sturdy steeds: By a bridle they turn them whither they list, Jam. 3.3. Psal. 32.9.

*{and I will turn thee back by the way by which thou camest}* i.e. To your own country. See this accomplished v. 36.

**V. 29. ***{And this shall be a sign unto thee}* The prophet here in the name of the Lord turns his speech from the enemy to Hezekiah and his people; and to strengthen their faith the more in that deliverance which is here promised, he gives them the sign following.

*{Ye shall eat this year}* This was the year of rest, wherein they sowed no corn at all. See Lev. 25.4,5.

*{such things as grow of themselves}* That crop which arises from the shedding of the last harvest, and grows up of itself without any plowing or sowing the ground by man. Though the enemy came that year into the land, and so might have been an occasion of spoiling the Jews of all that increase which the earth of itself brought forth, yet through God’s providence they did partake even of that fruit of the earth, and lived thereon.

*{and in the second year}* i.e. The year after that year of rest.

*{that which springeth of the same}* In this second year the enemy was in their land, and so annoyed them as in seedtime they could not sow their ground; yet the Lord so blessed their land as of itself it brought forth an harvest even this year also. Herein especially lies the miracle, that the enemy coming upon them in their year of rest, and keeping them from sowing their land the year following, the ground should yield of itself sufficient food two years together.
