*{and in the third year sow ye, and reap}* Here is both an exhortation and a promise; an exhortation to use their accustomed means for obtaining a good crop, *sow ye*; a promise that they should partake of the benefit of their pains, *and reap*.

*{and plant vineyards}* He encourages them not only to use means for that which was most necessary, as corn, but also for that which was for their delight, as vines, and the wine that came from thence.

*{and eat the fruits thereof}* This also is a promise: and it shows that they should not only have store of provision in their barns and vessels, but should also themselves eat and drink thereof for their nourishment and refreshing.

**V. 30. ***{And the remnant that is escaped of the house of Judah}* Heb. *The escaping of the house of Judah that remaineth*. See v. 4.

*{shall yet again take root downward, and bear fruit upward}* This similitude is taken from plants, whose roots first grow downward and gather sap from the earth, and are made steady, before they shoot branches upward. Thus by God’s blessing his people were established in their land, and had means of thriving, as they had done in former times, though the enemy had much unsettled them, and hindered them in their callings. Therefore it is promised they should add root; or again took root. See Isa.27.6.

**V. 31. ***{For out of Jerusalem shall go forth a remnant}* This has relation to the present condition of the Jews, who were now besieged and shut up in Jerusalem. The promise is, that they should freely, without fear, go out of the city, and go abroad whither they pleased: For the enemy should raise his siege, and be forced away. This may typically be applied to the gospel, which first went out of Jerusalem, Isa.2.3.

*{and they that escape}* Heb. *the escaping*. This implies the same with remnant. Many had perished by the enemy, but some escaped through God’s providence; and of these he speaks.

*{out of mount Zion}* This is added either for elegancy’s sake, being in effect the same that was implied by this phrase, *out of Jerusalem*; or because Zion was a city situated within the walls of Jerusalem; so as they that remained in the one, or the other, should have the same liberty.

*{the zeal of the Lord of hosts}* The fervent love of God to his people, and his fiery indignation against his enemies, but especially his earnest respect to his own glory, are here comprised under this word *zeal*.

*{shall do this}* This shall be done by the Lord himself upon the forenamed reasons, and not by man, nor for anything in man.

**V. 32. ***{Therefore thus saith the Lord concerning the King of Assyria}* Here is added as the reason of the former promise, the removing of that which might otherwise have hindered it.

*{He shall not come into this City}* The enemy was now going against the king of Ethiopia, v.9. and thought to have returned and taken Jerusalem; but here is showed how far he should be from accomplishing that his purpose.

*{nor shoot an arrow there}* He should be so far from entering the city as he should not come so near as to shoot an arrow into it. This is especially to be taken of his intended return with that army that marched with him against the Ethiopians.

*{nor come before it with shield}* By a synecdoche this one weapon is put for all warlike preparations both defensive and offensive.

*{nor cast a bank against it}* Nor raise any batteries. See 2 Sam.20.15.

**V. 33. ***{By the way that he came, by the same shall he return}* He came out of his country with great pomp, but returned with much loss and great shame, v.36. See v.28.

*{and shall not come into this City}* See v.32.

*{saith the Lord}* This is added to strengthen Hezekiah’s faith.

**V. 34. ***{For I will defend this City to save it}* What God undertakes shall assuredly be performed. Mortal men thought to destroy Jerusalem, but the mighty Lord undertook to preserve it.

*{for mine own sake}* To evidence my power, providence, mercy, goodness and care over my church, that my name may be acknowledged, feared and praised. See Isa.48.9,11. Ezek.36.22.

*{and for my servant Davids sake}* See ch.20.6. 1 King.11.12.

**V. 35. ***{And it came to pass that night}* Isa.37.36. There are two opinions concerning the time when the destruction following fell out. One is, that it was the night after this king of Assyria returned from the Ethiopians, and came again towards Jerusalem: The other, that it was the very night after this prophecy was made, wherein the enemies were fitting themselves to assault Jerusalem: for they had not yet assaulted it, v.32.

*{that the Angel of the Lord}* Such an angel as destroyed the Israelites 1 Sam.24.16. God is here pleased to use an angel, that the judgment might more evidently appear to be of that Lord whom this proud enemy had before vilified, v.10. ch.18.35.

*{went out}* From the Lord.

*{and smote}* Utterly destroyed, and slew: Some think, by a plague; others, by fire; others, by an earthquake. Whatsoever the particular means were, surely the destruction was extraordinary. See the last note on this verse.

*{in the camp of the Assyrians}* Either that which Rabshakeh brought, ch.18.17. or that which returned from the Ethiopians, before they had done anything against Jerusalem, v.32.

*{an hundred fourscore and five thousand}* The number of soldiers slain imports that this army was a very great one. Indeed we read of greater 2 Chron.14.9. But it may be that all this army was not destroyed. This is certain, that *all the mighty men of valor and the leaders and captains were cut off*, 2 Chron.32.21. and without these what could the common soldiers do?

*{and when they arose}* Such as were spared. This must be taken of the meaner sort who carried the news to Sennacherib.

*{early in the morning}* It may be to receive order from their commanders what to do about assaulting Jerusalem.

*{behold, they were all}* The universal particle may be taken of the greater part, or of the better part, namely the commanders and officers. They were such as were accounted in a manner all.

*{dead corpses}* They were destroyed. But this word *corpses* implies they were not consumed with fire, nor swallowed up with an earthquake.

**V. 36. ***{So Sennacherib King of Assyria departed}* With sorrow and shame, 2 Chr.32.21. his proud peacock-feathers were now pulled off from him. Thus God abases the proud.

*{and went and returned}* Here was verified the prophecy v.33.

*{and dwelt at Nineveh}* The head-city of Assyria. See Gen.10.11,12. This implies that he had no great mind to renew his wars.

**V. 37. ***{And it came to pass, as he was worshipping}* Howsoever this were an idolatrous act, yet the heathen accounting this kind of worshiping piety, it much aggravates the parricide of his sons, that were not any whit restrained thereby from slaying him.

*{in the house of Nisroch his god}* This phrase *his god* implies that this was some special god of Sennacherib: For there was another general god of the Assyrians, which the heathen call *Jupiter Belus*. Though by the great destruction of his army the Lord had showed himself to be the true God, yet was not he thereby moved to acknowledge him to be so: therefore he caused him and others to see that his false god could not defend him; no not when he was worshiping him.

*{that Adrammelech}* See ch.17.31.

*{and Sharezer}* This word signifies *a prince of treasure*.

*{his sons}* Even they that came forth of his own bowels, 2 Chr.32.21. These are supposed to be his elder sons, and to be exasperated against their father, because he had manifested that he would settle Esar-haddon his younger son on the throne, or else because he had already settled him thereon.

*{smote him with the sword}* God’s judgment on this blasphemer was in sundry respects very remarkable: 1. That he should see his great and well prepared army so suddenly destroyed. 2. That he should be forced to fly with shame into his own country. 3. That himself should be slain, in the temple of his idol, in the act of his idolatry, by his own sons, and that by two of them conspiring together. Let proud men and blasphemers lay this example before their eyes.

*{and they escaped into the land of Armenia}* Heb. *Ararat*. See Gen.8.4.

*{and Esarhaddon his son reigned in his stead}* This man was the last of the Assyrian monarchs. See Ezra 4.2.

## CHAPTER 20

**V. 1. ***{IN those days}* 2 Chr.32.24. Isa.38.1. This has reference to the history immediately going before; and it implies that anon after God had preserved Hezekiah and his people from the attempts of the king of Assyria, he visited him with the sickness following. For Sennacherib came against Hezekiah in the fourteenth year of his reign, ch.18.13. and in the same year his sickness fell out; which thus appears. He reigned but nine and twenty years, chap.18.2. and he lived after this fifteen years. The enemies coming might be in the beginning of the year, and his sickness in the latter end thereof.

*{was Hezekiah sick unto death}* His sickness was unto death, in regard both of its kind, see v.7. and also of its strength. Probably the plague had seized on his vital parts, and so was become incurable; as they who have the tokens are in regard of ordinary means; For Hezekiah’s recovery was extraordinary. He was thus visited for his good, 1. to try his faith, 2. to give him occasion of considering his disposition to God-wards; 3. to quicken him up to pray more fervently, 4. to strengthen his faith by more miracles.

*{and the Prophet Isaiah the son Amoz came to him}* Some take this as a voluntary and friendly visit of the prophet, the rather because Hezekiah was so good a king; but the words following show rather that he was sent of God.

*{and said unto him, Thus saith the Lord}* See 1 King.12.24.

*{Set thine house in order}* Heb. *Give charge concerning thine house*. Make your will, and give order about ruling your family and kingdom after your decease, 2 Sam.17.23. This was the more needful because he then had no son. See v.3. This is a good ground of men’s making their wills, seeing this direction is sent from God himself.

*{for thou shalt die and not live}* This is not to be taken of God’s absolute determination, but of the nature of the disease, which was such as could not be recovered by any creature. See ch.8.10. Jon.3.4,10.

**V. 2. ***{Then he turned his face to the wall}* Either that he might be more free from distracting objects, and so the more fervently call upon God; or because the temple was that way. Now because God’s presence was manifested in the temple, Solomon prayed that God would hear such as made their prayer towards it, 1 King.8.44. and answerably saints used to pray, Dan.6.10.

*{and prayed unto the Lord, saying}* His prayer was for recovery. It appears therefore that he took the sentence of death, v.1. not to be absolute; for then certainly he would not have prayed against it. And though in the ordinary course of nature his sickness were incurable, yet he believed God was able to cure it. See 2 Sam.12.22.

**V. 3. ***{I beseech thee, O Lord, remember now}* This phrase does not imply that God can forget his servants' goodness; but Hezekiah uses it to strengthen his own faith, by calling to mind that God did not forget but remember the same.

*{how I have walked before thee in truth}* See 1 King.2.4.

*{and with a perfect heart}* See 1 King.8.61.

*{and have done that which is good}* As the former branches had respect to his inward disposition and the manner of what he did, so this has respect to his outward acts and the matter of what he did, being according to the rule of righteousness, 1 King.3.9. Psal.14.1. Jer.5.29. This in other places is called *right*, 1 Sam.12.23. See 1 King.15.5.

*{in thy sight}* See 1 King.14.8. These things Hezekiah pleads, not as meritorious causes, but only as a condition annexed to the covenant. See 1 King.2.4. So as herein he only pleads God’s truth in keeping covenant. So Exod.32.13. Neh.13.22,31.

*{and Hezekiah wept sore}* Heb. *with a great weeping*. Though the approach of death be in itself terrible to nature, yet we are not to think that it was simply fear of death that wrung these tears from Hezekiah, he being so pious a man, and having received so many evidences of God’s favor; but rather the glory of God, and the good of the church. For God had promised to David that his house and kingdom should be established for ever, 2 Sam.7.16. i.e. that one coming by lineal descent from him should generation after generation sit on his throne: But at this time Hezekiah had no son to succeed him: for Manasseh, his eldest son, was born three years after this; which thus appears. Fifteen years after this Hezekiah died v.6. and Manasseh was then but twelve years old, ch.21.1. Now he might think that by want of a son to succeed him in the kingdom God’s promise might seem to fail, and so his honor be impeached. And for God’s church, he might fear there might be many divisions and contentions about the crown, because he had no son by whom the just right might be determined. He might also fear lest idolatry should again creep in after his departure. I will not deny but that some grief might possess him upon so sudden a taking, after so miraculous a deliverance from so potent an enemy.

**V. 4. ***{And it came to pass before Isaiah was gone out into the middle Court}* Or, *city*. If the word be taken for the city, it implies the midst thereof: for the king’s palace was on that side of Jerusalem where the temple was: over against it was Zion, which is oft called *the city of David*, 1 King.8.1. The space betwixt these two might be called *the middle city*, or *the midst of the city*. If it be taken for *the middle court*, it means the court of the king’s palace mentioned 1 King.7.8. Both may intend one and the same thing, namely God’s speedy return of a gracious answer to Hezekiah’s prayer, as is implied in the words following.

*{that the word of the Lord came to him, saying}* God declared to Isaiah his purpose of much mercy to Hezekiah. See v.5,6.

**V. 5. ***{Turn again}* Hereby God implies that he had another message to send to the king.

*{and tell Hezekiah the Captain of my people}* It may be Hezekiah had gone forth as general of his army against the enemy. Or captain may be taken for governor and chief commander in time of peace as well as of war.

*{Thus saith the Lord the God of David}* The Lord is in special manner styled *the God of David* in regard of his special favor to David manifested by many promises made to him, 2 Sam.7.12, &c. and by many blessings conferred upon him; and also in regard of David’s strong confidence in God, and entire respect to him. Thus also is the Lord styled *the God of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob*, Exod.3.6. *the God of Elijah*, 2 King.2.14. *the God of Daniel*, Dan.6.26. *the God of Shadrach, Meshech, and Abednego*, Dan.3.28.

*{thy father}* Though there were twelve generations betwixt, yet by lineal descent, and that in a direct line, Hezekiah came from David.

*{I have heard thy prayer, I have seen thy tears}* Of God’s hearing and seeing see ch.19.16. God has this title, *O thou that hearest prayer*, ascribed to him Psal.65.2. and he is said to have a bottle to put his saints' tears into, Psal.56.8.

*{behold I will heal thee on the third day}* This implies a miraculous recovery, in that it was so soon. Christ’s cures are proved to be miraculous by this circumstance, Mark 1.31. & 2.12.

*{thou shalt go up}* An evidence of health and strength, as Mark 2.12.

*{unto the house of the Lord}* There to give public thanks for your recovery. This sets out the duty of those that are recovered, what they ought to do, and the disposition of Hezekiah, what he would be forward to do.

**V. 6. ***{And I will add unto thy days}* The time of man’s life is set out by days by reason of its brevity, Gen.47.9. Now God is said to add, in regard of the nature of Hezekiah’s disease, which would have put a period to his days.

*{fifteen years}* Hezekiah is the only man we read of who had the set term of his life made known unto him, especially so long before. Indeed God has certainly determined the time of every man’s life, Job 7.1. but he does not use expressly to make it known: for then would man be too secure, and not so watchful against death as he should be. This good king did not render again according to the benefit done unto him; for his heart was lifted up, 2 Chron.32.25.

*{and I will deliver thee}* God did not only hear his prayer in that particular he desired, but also superadded more mercies. See 1 King.3.13.

*{and this city}* Places fare the better for their good governors' sake.

*{out of the hand of the king of Assyria}* Some hence infer that Hezekiah was sick before the destruction of the Assyrian army. But the series of the history imports the contrary. And this phrase does not necessarily intend so much: for this promise might be made in regard of further attempts of the kings of Assyria, which Hezekiah might fear.

*{and I will defend this city}* Not only from the assaults of the Assyrians, but also from all other dangers. This is an amplification of the mercy promised.

*{for mine own sake, and for my servant Davids sake}* See chap.19.34.

**V. 7. ***{And Isaiah said, Take a lump of figs}* Figs have a mollifying and ripening virtue, and so are a fit remedy for a hard bile. God oft in his extraordinary works uses means as visible evidences of his power; yet the kind of disease here and suddenness of the cure showed that it was a miraculous cure. Christ also oft used means, Mark 7.33. Joh.9.6.

*{And they took and laid it on the boil}* Boils usually arising from the plague, it may probably be collected that it was a plague-sore.

*{and he recovered}* Means appointed by God shall be effectual. The promise made v.5. is here accomplished.

**V. 8. ***{And Hezekiah said unto Isaiah}* This is to be taken as spoken before he was recovered.

*{What shall be the sign that the Lord will heal me}* He desires a sign, not as doubting of the truth of God’s promise, but as sensible of the weakness of his own faith. This he did the rather, because the promise was of so difficult a matter, and because it seemed to thwart the former word of God, that he should die, v.1. See Judg.6.17,37. Besides, he did not simply ask a sign, but taking it for granted that God would give him one, he asked what it should be. Thus the virgin Mary, though she believed she should bring forth a son, yet asks How shall this be? Luk.1.34. His father Ahaz was rebuked for refusing to ask a sign, Isa.7.12, &c.

*{and that I shall go up into the house of the Lord the third day}* He prudently makes use of every branch of the promise, and seeks to have every particular made good unto him.

**V. 9. ***{And Isaiah said, This sign shalt thou have of the Lord}* God is ready to grant the desires of his servants that are rightly made, and on good grounds.

*{that the Lord will do the thing that he hath spoken}* Miracles are to assure us that God’s word shall be accomplished.

*{shall the shadow}* i.e. The shadow of the gnomon of a sundial.

*{go forward ten degrees, or go back ten degrees}* It was all one with the Lord to do the one or the other: The prophet therefore refers it to Hezekiah’s choice, lest he should after the first grant desire the other, as Gideon did, Judg.6.39. and also that his faith might be the more strengthened upon his choosing that which he thought the most difficult. Concerning the ten degrees, it is to be supposed that this message was brought to the king at high noon, when the shadow might equally go as many degrees backward as forward.

**V. 10. ***{And Hezekiah answered, It is a light thing for the shadow to go down ten degrees}* This is spoken in regard of man’s apprehension, and in comparison to the shadow’s going backward. For it being the proper course of the sun to go forward, the miracle would only have been in the speedy and more than ordinary hastening of the shadow forward; which notwithstanding simply in itself is a very great matter, extraordinary and miraculous. For the sun that causes this shadow is constant in his course, and never runs more swiftly or speedily at one time than at another. But to go backward would have been miraculous in the very thing itself; for the sun never does so of itself; and also in the suddenness of it, which this other intended.

*{nay, but let the shadow return backward ten degrees}* The choice being referred to Hezekiah, he prudently chooses that which to himself and others appeared the most strange, and did most confirm his faith.

**V. 11. ***{And Isaiah the Prophet cried unto the Lord}* i.e. Earnestly prayed. See 1 King.17.20. Warranted means may and must be used for obtaining things promised, 1 King.18.42. Exod.14.15. Ezek.36.37.

*{and he brought the shadow}* This God did by causing the sun to run backward. They certainly are deceived who imagine the miracle was only in the shadow, as if the sun had gone his course, but the shadow had gone backwards, otherwise than the sun did. In Isa.38.8. it is expressly said, *the sun returned ten degrees*. Besides it is said 2 Chron.32.31. that *the ambassadors of the Princes of Babylon came to him to enquire of the wonder that was done*. How could they in Babylon see the shadow on a dial in Jerusalem go backward contrary to the course of the sun? but they might easily observe the running back of the sun.

*{ten degrees backward}* i.e. from twelve at noon to seven in the morning. In this distance there being five hours, they make ten degrees of half-hours.

*{by which it had gone down in the dial of Ahaz}* Heb. *degrees*. Ahaz had made a fair, lasting dial, which was common for all of all sorts to look upon. By this the course of the sun was openly and distinctly discerned. Questionless the shadow went backward in all sundials. But this is here mentioned because it was a great dial, and stood in a place whither many of all sorts resorted, and which the king himself also out of the window of his bedchamber might easily discern.

**V. 12. ***{At that time Berodachbaladan}* He is also called Merodach, Isa.39.1. which is supposed to be a name common to the kings of Babylon, as Pharaoh to the kings of Egypt, Jer.50.2. See 1 King.2.39. Evil-Merodach, ch.25.27. had his name thence.

*{the son of Baladan}* This shows the reason why he was called Berodach-Baladan, even for descent sake, because his father was called Baladan; so as this was his surname: And it implies that he came from his father by natural descent.

*{king of Babylon}* This has relation especially to Berodach-Baladan, who is taken to be the first that raised the Babylonian monarchy upon the ruin of the Assyrians. He is thought to have been a viceroy or deputy in Babylon under the king of Assyria, and that he took advantage from the destruction of the army, ch.19.35. and from the murder of Sennacherib, ch.19.37. and from the youth of Esar-haddon, to usurp the kingdom.

*{sent letters}* By the hand of ambassadors, 2 Chron.32.31.

*{and a present unto Hezekiah}* Kings were wont to send presents with their ambassadors, whom they sent to other kings, 1 Kings 4.21. especially when they sent in a fair and friendly way.

*{for he had heard that Hezekiah had been sick}* This was one reason of sending his ambassadors and presents unto Hezekiah, namely to congratulate his victory and recovery, as 2 Sam.8.10. & 10.20. Another was, to inquire of the wonder about the sun’s going backward, 2 Chr.32.31. For questionless all the world, especially astronomers, astrologers, and others, that took notice of the course of the heavens, could not but observe such a remarkable alteration. True it is that many in all places of the world might observe it, and yet not know the ground and reason thereof: But it is probable that they in Babylon had heard that the Lord of heaven and earth had caused that alteration, and that upon Hezekiah’s recovery; therefore Berodach-Baladan sends both to congratulate the king’s recovery, and also to be more fully instructed in the cause of the sun’s going backward; and it may be withal to ingratiate himself into the favor of Hezekiah, that he might have assistance from him in case of need against the king of Assyria, whom he knew to be Hezekiah’s mortal enemy.

**V. 13. ***{And Hezekiah hearkened unto them}* He gratefully accepted their kind visit, informed them in the ground of the miracle, and entered into league with them according to their desire: Isa.39.2. it is, And Hezekiah was glad of them.

*{and shewed them the house of his precious things}* Or, *spicery*. Spices of old were counted very precious commodities, 1 King.10.2,10,15,25. Kings therefore used to lay them up in their treasuries.

*{the silver and the gold, and the spices, and the precious ointment}* Ointments also were precious commodities, and of much use in those times.

*{and all the house of his armour}* Or, *Jewels*. Heb. *vessels*. Kings of the Jews used to have houses full of armor: See Isa.22.8. Cant.4.4. All kings also in all ages have had their treasures of jewels, 2 Chron.32.27. Under the word vessels may be comprised silver or golden plate, or costly cabinets to hold jewels and other treasures.

*{and all that was found in his treasures}* Such as belonged to himself.

*{there was nothing in his house}* In his royal palace.

*{nor in all his dominion}* In other houses that belonged to him in any part of his kingdom. Some extend this general phrase to the temple, as if he had brought these ambassadors thither and showed them all the treasures thereof. But it is not probable that so pious a king would so far exceed the bounds of piety, and so apparently transgress the law as to bring heathen into the house of God, especially into those parts where the treasures lay.

*{that Hezekiah shewed them not}* It is apparent that he was too much puffed up with God’s blessings, as the great deliverance he had from the king of Assyria, his recovery from a mortal disease, the great miracle wrought thereupon, with the costly and precious presents that all the nations round about brought him by reason of these blessings of God bestowed on him, 2 Chron.32.23; For certainly it was one special means of his great treasures, that he was magnified in the sight of all nations, and had many gifts brought unto him. It is said *his heart was lifted up*, 2 Chron.32.25. and in this pride of heart he showed to the ambassadors all his treasures.

**V. 14. ***{Then came Isaiah the Prophet unto king Hezekiah, and said unto him}* The Lord was not willing that festering corruption of pride should continue, and puff him up more and more, and therefore sends Isaiah to prick that bladder, and to make him more humble-minded.

*{What said these men? and from whence came they unto thee?}* These interrogations are propounded, not so much for information, as for the more evident conviction of his folly.

*{And Hezekiah said, They are come from a far country}* This he says to amplify the great honor that was done him, in that not only the neighboring nations, but also such as were far off, congratulated his welfare, and sought friendship with him.

*{even from Babylon}* This shows that Babylon was a country far remote from Jerusalem. It is said to be betwixt six and seven hundred miles from it. So far the Jews were carried captive from their own country.

**V. 15. ***{And he said, what have they seen in thine house?}* By this third question the prophet brings the king more thoroughly to discern his egregious folly.

*{And Hezekiah answered, All the things that are in my house have they seen, &c.}* See v.13. Hezekiah deals fairly with the prophet, not mincing the matter, but making known the whole truth.

**V. 16. ***{And Isaiah said unto Hezekiah, Hear the word of the Lord}* This is premised to make him the better mark the message.

**V. 17. ***{Behold, the days come}* This phrase implies a time future, but yet such a time as should assuredly fall out.

*{that all that is in thine house}* Which you yourself have laid up.

*{and that which thy fathers have laid up in store to this day}* Kings did use to leave treasures to their children, and that generation after generation.

*{shall be carried into Babylon}* This began to be accomplished in his son’s time, 2 Chron.33.11. but was further accomplished in Jehojachin’s and Zedekiah’s time, ch.24.13. & 25.13. Jer.27.22.

*{nothing shall be left}* This was accomplished to the full at Zedekiah’s captivity, 2 Chron.36.18. Jer.38.19, &c.

*{saith the Lord}* This has the same force that the usual preface of prophets has, *Thus saith the Lord*, 1 King.12.24.

**V. 18. ***{And of thy sons that shall issue from thee, which thou shalt beget}* Either immediately, as Manasseh and his brethren, 2 Chr.33.11. Or successively, as Jehojachin, and other sons of Josiah, ch.24.12.

*{shall they take away}* Captives into another country.

*{and they shall be Eunuchs}* See 1 Kings 22.9.

*{in the palace of the King of Babylon}* See Dan.1.3.

**V. 19. ***{Then said Hezekiah unto Isaiah}* The good king was so touched at the hearing of that heavy but just judgment, as he was deeply humbled, and returned a meek answer.

*{Good is the word of the Lord which thou hast spoken}* By good he here means just and equal. He acknowledges that it was no other than what he and his people had deserved.

*{And he said, Is it not good, if peace and truth be}* Or, *shall there not be peace and truth*? Here good implies as much as merciful; so as he acknowledges God’s justice mixed with mercy. This interrogation, Is it not good? is a steadfast acknowledgment of God’s mercy to him. Under peace he comprises all manner of prosperity; Under truth, sound and sincere religion. And his manner of inferring peace and truth thus, If peace and truth be, shows that God’s mercy was manifested in the mixture of both those together.

*{in my days}* While I live. This is added not simply as a desire of peace and truth only in his time, but in relation to the threatening applied to his son’s time; so as he acknowledges forbearance of judgment in his time, who had deserved it, a great mercy.

**V. 20. ***{And the rest of the acts of Hezekiah}* See 1 King.15.23. Many other acts of Hezekiah, especially concerning his reformation of religion, are registered 2 Chron.29, & 30, & 31. And no question but many more were recorded of him in their civil chronicles.

*{and all his might}* See 1 King.15.23. Great might was showed against the Philistines, ch.18.8. and likewise other countries, which distinctly was set down in their civil chronicles.

*{and how he made a pool, and a conduit}* Of these see ch.18.17. Neh.3.14. 2 Chr.32.4,30.

*{and brought water into the City}* This he did both for the benefit of the people in the city, and also to withdraw water from the enemy, 2 Chr.32.3,4,30. He might well do both those by bringing water in pipes underground.

*{are they not written, &c.}* See 1 King.14.29.

**V. 21. ***{And Hezekiah slept with his fathers}* See 1 King.2.10. Of his honorable burial see 2 Chr.32.33.

*{and Manasseh his son reigned in his stead}* This is one of the most woeful changes that ever was in Judah; Hezekiah being one of the best kings, and Manasseh one of the worst. Such a change is noted 1 Kings 16.30.

## CHAPTER 21

**V. 1. ***{Manasseh was twelve years old when he began to reign}* 2 Chr.33.1. He was born three years after his father’s great recovery. See ch.20.5.

*{and reigned fifty and five years}* This was the longest time that ever any judge or king of Judah or Israel ruled. His great grandfather Uzziah reigned two and fifty years; but in many of those years he was shut up and dwelt as a leper in a house apart, 2 Chr.26.3,21. Of these fifty and five years many were passed over in the youth of Manasseh, others in great misery and captivity, others in a time of repentance; all which being taken away there will not be so many left for the time of his wickedness as many imagine. Many years, and that with a crown on one’s head, is no sure evidence of God’s favor.

*{in Jerusalem}* In the beginning and in the end, and in many years of the middle of his reign he was in Jerusalem; but in some part thereof he was a prisoner in Babylon.

*{and his mothers name was Hephzibah}* See 1 King.15.10. Hephzibah was that title whereby God testified his delight in his church, Isa.62.4. Some from this very name gather that her parents, who gave it, and she herself, were pious persons. And then the impiety of her son is much aggravated, because he degenerated so far from such a father and from such a mother.

**V. 2. ***{And he did that which was evil in the sight of the Lord}* See 1 King.14.22. The evil he did is comprised under three heads, I. Idolatry. II. Sorcery, v.6. III. Cruelty, v.6,16.

*{after the abomination of the heathen whom the Lord cast, &c.}* See 1 King.14.24. If a due and thorough view be taken of the particular abominations which Manasseh did, the truth of this aggravation of his wickedness will most evidently be manifested.

**V. 3. ***{For he built up again}* Heb. *he returned and built*. See ch.13.25. & 2 Chr.33.3.

*{the high places}* See 1 King.3.4.

*{which Hezekiah his father had destroyed}* ch.18.4. This is a great aggravation, that a son should set up again that idolatry which his own father had put down.

*{and he reared up altars for Baal}* Herein he imitated his grandfather Ahaz, 2 Chr.28.2. and impiously supposed him to be wiser than his father Hezekiah. Of Baal see 1 King.16.31,32.

*{and made a grove}* See 1 King.16.33. Manasseh coming by lineal descent from Athaliah the daughter of Ahab, 2 Chr.22.10. 2 Kings 8.18. was more inclinable to follow his idolatrous kindred on the mother’s side than his pious predecessors on the father’s side.

*{and worshipped all the host of heaven, and served them}* See ch.17.16. 1 King.16.31.

**V. 4. ***{And he built altars in the house of the Lord}* Jer.32.34. Here are two further aggravations. I. He multiplied altars, which was unlawful, Josh.22.19. II. He brought altars of his own invention into the house of the Lord, herein also imitating his idolatrous grandfather, ch.16.19.

*{of which the Lord said, In Jerusalem will I put my name}* 2 Sam.7.13. Jerusalem is here mentioned, because the temple was in that city. See 1 King.8.13,16,17.

**V. 5. ***{And he built altars for all the host of heaven}* See ch.17.16.

*{in the two courts of the house of the Lord}* See 1 King.6.36. One of these courts was that where the altar of the Lord was set, and where the priests and Levites executed their ordinary functions: Manasseh placing his altars here showed that he equalized them to God’s altar. The other was the court of the Israelites where the people met together to worship God: And there he set altars, that he might draw all the people to worship his idols.

**V. 6. ***{And he made his son pass through the fire}* See ch.16.3. It seems he continued long in his idolatry. For he could not have a son in the beginning of his reign, being but twelve years old, v.1. And it is probable that his son was grown to some years before he made him thus pass through the fire. In 2 Chr.33.6. it is said that *he caused his children to pass thorow the fire*; which aggravates his idolatry, in that he exercised that inhumane cruelty upon more sons than one.

*{and observed times}* The Hebrew word properly signifies *to overcloud*, Gen.4.19. and is frequently applied to such as overcloud or overshadow what they do, as jugglers use to do; and to such as observe the clouds, planets, stars, flying of fowls, and other like things, and thereby judge of matters present or future. Such are called *soothsayers*, Isa.2.6. Mic.5.12. *enchanters*, Jer.27.9. *sorcerers*, Isa.57.3. and *observers of times* here, and 2 Chr.33.6. Under this title they are all forbidden, Lev.19.26. Deut.18.10,14. They were counted *observers of times* who by the stars pretended to foretell future contingents, or by the flying of birds judging things at such and such times to be fit or unfit to be done, or on other signs or occasions days to be lucky or unlucky. Sometimes they would judge by the birds singing as well as flying, and sometimes by looking into the entrails of beasts.

*{and used enchantments}* See ch.17.17.

*{and dealt with}* Heb. *made*. By the devil’s subtle yielding to them they made evil spirits familiar with them.

*{familiar spirits}* The word אוב *Ob* signifies *a bottle*, Job 32.19. and they who use evil spirits oft swell in the belly as a bottle, and the spirit seems to speak out of their belly with a low hollow voice. Such a one is called in Greek ἐγγαστρίμυθος, *one that has a spirit speaking in the belly*, Isa.8.19. and 29.4. By such answers were oftentimes given to questions and cases propounded. And this idolatrous king would always have some possessed with such spirits to resolve his doubts; And their answers he reputed oracles. Such were by the law to be stoned, Lev.20.27. The law also forbade to deal with such, Lev.19.31. Deut.18.11. and they who dealt with such are threatened to be cut off, Lev.20.6. This was Saul’s sin, 1 Sam.28.7. for which he died, 1 Chr.10.13.

*{and wizards}* ידאנים *Id-onim* signifies *such as are thought to know much*, called in Greek γνῶσται. Our English word *wizards* imports such as would thought to be wise and full of knowledge. They undertake to know all things, even secret and future. We also call them cunning men. They are in all the forenamed places joined with such as deal with familiar spirits.

*{he wrought much wickedness in the sight of the Lord}* See 1 Kings 14.22.

*{to provoke him to anger}* See 1 Kings 14.9.

**V. 7. ***{And he set a graven image of the grove which he had made}* Appertaining to that grove v.3. Some conceive that on this image the grove was curiously carved or engraven or cast; so as he would have a memorial and representation of his idolatrous grove on the idol thereof, though it were removed from the grove.

*{in the house of which the Lord said to David and to Solomon his son}* The high account which God would put upon the temple is amplified by the charge he gives about it, first to David, then to Solomon, 2 Sam.7.10. 1 Kings 9.3. See ch.23.27.

*{In this house and in Jerusalem}* Jerusalem was a holy city, Neh.11.1,18. Isa.52.1. Dan.9.24. Mat.4.5. and 27.53. and the temple a holy place, 1 King.8.8. Ezr.9.8. Act.9.13. Heb.9.12. therefore they are both joined together, to aggravate Manasseh’s wickedness.

*{which I have chosen out of all the tribes of Israel}* See 1 Kings 8.44.

*{will I put my name for ever}* See 1 King.8.13,29.

**V. 8. ***{Neither will I make the feet of Israel move any more out of the land}* I will not suffer them to be carried away captives to another land, as ch.17.6.

*{which I gave their fathers}* See 1 King.8.34.

*{only if they will observe to do}* This being the condition of the promise, their observation thereof gave them assurance of God’s performing his part: But their failing therein made the promise void.

*{according to all that I have commanded them}* Hereby is meant especially the moral law, Exod.20.3.

*{and according to all the law that my servant Moses commanded them}* This phrase points at the ceremonial and judicial law, Exo.21.1, &c. There are three words 2 Chr.33.8. whereby those three kinds of *laws* are distinguished; I. laws, set out in the two tables, Exod.34.38. II. *statutes*, for governing the state, Exod.21.18. III. *ordinances*, concerning the worship of God, Lev.1.1, &c. Of this style *servant of the Lord* see 1 Kings 3.6.

**V. 9. ***{But they hearkened not}* People settled in idolatry regard no admonitions.

*{and Manasseh seduced them}* By example and encouragement, v.11.

*{to do more evil then did the nations}* They set up more idols, and sinned against more light and more means. See Jer.2.10,11.

*{whom the Lord destroyed before the children of Israel}* See v.2.

**V. 10. ***{And the Lord spake by his servants}* Heb. *by the hand of his servants*. See 1 Kings 8.53.

*{the Prophets, saying}* God afforded prophets to Judah and Israel in their worst times, to draw them from their impieties.

*{Because Manasseh King of Judah hath done these abominations}* By abominations are meant abominable things which the Lord abhorred. The abstract adds much emphasis.

*{and hath done wickedly above all that the Amorites did}* See v.9. Under Amorites all the heathen were comprised, Gen.15.16.

*{which were before him}* Which lived in the land before the Jews possessed it.

*{and hath made Judah also to sin with his idols}* Heb. *dungy gods*. See 1 King.15.12.

**V. 12. ***{Therefore thus saith the Lord God of Israel}* God threatens judgment, before he inflicts it, to draw men to repentance.

*{Behold, I am bringing such evil}* This phrase implies a near approach of judgment.

*{upon Jerusalem and Judah}* Upon city and country. See ch.23.27.

*{that whosoever heareth of it}* Whether he be hardhearted or softhearted, great or mean, prince or people.

*{both his ears shall tingle}* He shall have as it were a ringing sound in his ears affrighting and astonishing him. Jer.19.3.

**V. 13. ***{And I will stretch over Jerusalem}* This metaphor is taken from such as intending to dig up ground, measure out how much they intend to dig up.

*{the line of Samaria, and the plummet of the house of Ahab}* The measure and weight of their judgments, i.e. the like judgments. Workmen use by rule, measure and weight to make up and break down their works, Isa.34.11. Amos 7.7,8. By the line of Samaria is meant the utter desolation thereof, ch.17.6. and by the plummet of the house of Ahab the utter destruction thereof, ch.10.11. See Mic.6.16.

*{and I will wipe Jerusalem as a man wipeth a dish, wiping it and turning it upside down}* Heb. *he wipeth and turneth it upon the face thereof*. The state of Jerusalem is resembled to a dish; the inhabitants thereof to the filth thereon. Now as maids use to wash and wipe clean away the filth on the dish, so should the inhabitants of Jerusalem be clean taken away, and the state of the Jews utterly overthrown and turned upside down.

**V. 14. ***{And I will forsake the remnant}* See ch.19.4.

*{of mine inheritance}* See 1 King.8.51. The Jews were a people whom God chose, protected, provided for and delighted in, as men in their inheritance. This is added to show that no outward privileges could exempt them from judgment.

*{and deliver them into the hand of their enemies}* When God once forsakes a people, they soon fall into their enemies' power. For God does not only withhold his protection, but also stirs up enemies to be the executioners of his justice.

*{and they shall become a prey and a spoil}* This is the advantage that enemies quickly take of such as fall into their hands.

*{to all their enemies}* When one creditor has cast a debtor into prison, all other the creditors will quickly bring their actions against him; so all sorts of enemies.

**V. 15. ***{Because they have done that which is evil, &c.}* See 1 Kings 11.6.

*{and have provoked me to anger}* See 1 King.4.9.

*{since the day their fathers came forth out of Egypt}* See 1 Kings 8.9,16. The prophet Ezekiel ch.20. distinctly shows how the people of Israel from their first coming out of Egypt, generation after generation, did provoke the Lord.

*{even unto this day}* See 1 King.9.13. and 12.19.

**V. 16. ***{Moreover Manasseh shed innocent blood very much}* Of such as were no malefactors, but upright and righteous. It is thought he caused the prophet Isaiah to be sawn asunder.

*{till he had filled Jerusalem from one end to another}* Heb. *from mouth to mouth*. By mouth he may mean a gate or entrance or corner of the city; and so it imports from one side to another. This is a hyperbolical expression of his unsatiable cruelty; and it implies that not only eminent prophets, who to his face denounced God’s judgments against him, but also all sorts of people who would not conform to his idolatry and wicked courses, were slain by him.

*{beside his sin wherewith he made Judah to sin}* See v.11. & 1 King.12.30. Besides Manasseh’s seducing his people, v.9. he also compelled them to sin by slaying such as refused. The special sin here intended was idolatry.

*{in doing that which was evil in the sight of the Lord}* See v.15. By his example he drew his people to sin.

**V. 17. ***{Now the rest of the acts of Manasseh, and all that he did}* Both evil and good, 2 Chr.33.3,12, &c.

*{and his sin that he sinned}* Sin may well be taken more largely here than v.16. and by synecdoche comprise under it not only idolatry, but also oppression, murder, sorcery, sacrilege.

*{are they not written, &c.}* See ch.20.20.

**V. 18. ***{And Manasseh slept with his fathers}* 2 Chron.33.20. See 1 King.2.16.

*{and was buried in the garden of his own house}* It is probable he had built a sepulcher for himself in this garden while he lived. Whether he did this upon a mean account of himself, judging himself unworthy to lie among other kings, or whether the people would not suffer him to be buried among them, is not expressed: I leave it as uncertain.

*{in the garden of Uzzah}* Uzzah might be a former owner of it, and thereupon the garden still carry his name. Some conceive that under this name Uzzah king Uzziah is meant, and that the field where he was buried, 2 Chr.26.23. was made a garden, and that this is the garden here intended.

*{and Amon his son reigned in his stead}* We read how Amon imitated his father’s sins, but not that he repented as his father did.

**V. 19. ***{Amon was twenty and two years old when he began to reign}* So as he was born in the forty-fifth of his father’s age, which is supposed to be after his repentance.

*{and he reigned two years in Jerusalem}* God saw it high time to cut off so wicked a king within a year or two.

*{and his mothers name was Meshullemeth the daughter of Haruz}* See 1 King.15.10.

*{of Jotbah}* Or, *Jotbath*. A town of Judah. It was of old one of the stations where the children of Israel rested in the wilderness, Deut.10.7. It is called Jotbathah, Numb.33.34. This place shows this woman was no alien, but an Israelitess.

**V. 20. ***{And he did that which was evil in the sight of the Lord}* See 1 King.11.6.

*{as his father Manasseh did}* In the former part of his reign, before his repentance.

**V. 21. ***{And he walked in all the way that his father walked in}* In idolatry, oppression, murder, sorcery, sacrilege, and the like. See v.3,4, &c.

*{and served the idols that his father served, and worshipped them}* See ver.3.

**V. 22. ***{And he forsook the Lord God of his fathers}* See 1 King.9.9. All idolaters forsake the Lord.

*{and walked not in the way of the Lord}* See 1 King.11.33.

**V. 23. ***{And the servants of Amon conspired against him}* See ch.12.20.

*{and slew the King in his own house}* It seems they were such servants as attended the king in his house.

**V. 24. ***{And the people of the land slew all them that had conspired against king Amon}* Amon’s eldest son being now very young was unable to revenge his father’s death; therefore the people undertake it, and effect it; for it was an especial part of justice.

*{and the people of the land made Josiah his son king in his stead}* A blessed change, as when Hezekiah succeeded Ahaz, ch.16.20.

**V. 25. ***{Now the rest of the acts, &c.}* See v.17.

**V. 26. ***{And he was buried in his sepulchre}* This may have reference to Manasseh his father, *q.d.* in his father’s sepulcher, whereof see v.18. Or it may be Amon had made a sepulcher for himself, as his father Manasseh had done.

*{in the garden of Uzzah}* See v.18. So as this garden now became a place of burial for kings.

*{and Josiah}* He is called Josias Matt.1.10.

*{his son reigned in his stead}* See v.24.

## CHAPTER 22

**V. 1. ***{Josiah was eight years old when he began to reign}* 2 Chr.34.1. Though he were but young, and succeeded an idolatrous father, yet he proved very pious, and a great blessing to that kingdom. His name signifies *the salvation of God*, and he well answered that name, in that he made God his salvation by trusting in him, and also through God’s help saved his people all his days from idolatry and from foreign enemies. This king was by name prophesied of above two hundred and thirty years before this. See 1 King.13.2.

*{and he reigned thirty and one years in Jerusalem}* He was taken away in the prime and strength of his days, being thirty-nine years old.

*{and his mothers name was Jedidah}* See 1 King.15.10. This name signifies *beloved of the Lord*. It is probable she was a pious woman, and seasoned her son in his younger years with piety: and this was an evidence that she was *beloved of the Lord*.

*{the daughter of Adaiah of Boscath}* A city of Judah, Josh.15.39. She was not an alien, but born and brought up in the church of God.

**V. 2. ***{And he did that which was right in the sight of the Lord}* See 1 King.14.8. and 15.5.

*{and walked in all the way of David his father}* i.e. those righteous courses wherein David walked, especially those that manifested his zeal of God’s glory, as duties of piety. See ch.18.3.

*{and turned not aside to the right hand or to the left}* God’s law is the only way wherein men ought to walk: All sins are aberrations from it either to the right hand, by excess, or to the left hand, by defect. Josiah did carefully avoid all aberrations on both hands. The metaphor is taken from travelers, who are careful to walk on in that way which leads them to the end of their journey, and will not turn out of it to the one side or the other, Numb.20.17. 1 Sam.6.12. A strict circumspection over the course of a man’s life, comprised under this phrase, is oft commanded, Deut.5.32. Josh.1.7.

**V. 3. ***{And it came to pass in the eighteenth year of King Josiah}* i.e. the eighteenth year of his reign, 2 Chr.34.8. Many great evidences of this good king’s integrity, piety and zeal are here omitted; For in the eighth year of his reign, while he was yet young, he began to seek the Lord; and in the twelfth year he began to purge Judah and Jerusalem, 2 Chr.34.3. Hence many infer that the great reformation noted ch.23.1, &c. was done before that which is here noted.

*{that the King sent Shaphan the son of Azaliah, the son of Meshullam, the Scribe}* Of scribe see 1 King.4.3. In this place it has relation to Shaphan, not Meshullam. In 2 Chron.34.8. there were others sent with this messenger, namely Maaseiah and Joah, both great officers; which manifested the great respect the king had to the work in hand.

*{to the house of the Lord, saying}* Where Hilkiah the priest was. The main end of this message was to take order for repairing the temple, 2 Chr.34.8. We do not read of any solemn repairing thereof since the days of Jehoash, 2 King.12.2,5. though there were many reformations and purgings of it, especially in Hezekiah’s time, 2 Chron.29.3, &c. Now many years had passed betwixt Jehoash and Josiah, namely above two hundred, so as the temple must needs stand at this time in great need of repair.

**V. 4. ***{Go up to Hilkiah the high Priest}* This was a good high priest, and careful to put on all things that might make to the stability and beauty of the house of God, and to his pure worship, It may be that he was the father of Jeremiah the prophet, Jer. 1.1.

*{that he may sum the silver which is brought into the house of the Lord}* Questionless order had been taken in the former years of this good king to collect money for this purpose; some such order as we read of 2 King. 12.4,9. and now care is taken for disposing that money to the end for which it was collected. By *summing the silver* he means telling it, to see how much there was.

*{which the keepers of the door}* Heb. *threshold*. See ch. 12.9.

*{have gathered of the people}* By receiving it of such as came to the house of the Lord, there to offer their offering.

**V. 5. ***{And let them deliver it into the hand of the doers of the work}* There were two sorts of men styled *doers of the work*; 1. such as had charge to see it well done; 2. such as labored in the work itself, called *workmen*. I take the former to be here meant.

*{that have the oversight of the house of the Lord}* These were they that before are styled *doers of the work*. They are expressly noted to be Levites, and set down by name, 2 Chron. 34.12.

*{and let them give it to the doers of the work}* Here the phrase is used for workmen, such are mentioned v. 6.

*{which is in the house of the Lord}* Such work as appertains to the temple, for the strengthening and beautifying of the same.

*{to repair the breaches of the house}* Caused by the violence of idolaters, or by continuance of time. See ch. 12.5.

**V. 6. ***{Unto Carpenters, and builders, and Masons}* Under these three heads are comprised all manner of artificers requisite for repairing of the temple. See ch. 12.11,12.

*{and to buy timber and hewn stone to repair the house}* For the very stone walls, besides rafters, beams and other things made of timber, were decayed. See ch. 12.11,12.

**V. 7. ***{Howbeit there was no reckoning made, &c.}* See ch. 12.15. The persons thus trusted were Levites.

*{because they dealt faithfully}* Heb. *in truth, or faithfulness*. See ch. 12.15.

**V. 8. ***{And Hilkiah the high Priest said unto Shaphan the Scribe}* See v. 3,4.

*{I have found the book of the law in the house of the Lord}* He means the original copy, 2 Chr. 34.14. This Moses caused to be put by the side of the ark, Deut. 31.26. It might by the disorder in Manasseh’s and Amon’s time be laid aside, or by some pious priest be laid up in some secret place of the temple, lest it should be taken away by the idolaters, and burnt, or otherwise destroyed. Great question is here made about the strangeness of the law at that time. For by the high account of this book it may seem there were but few copies of it, in that Hilkiah the high priest sent this as a choice present to the king, and in that both king and princes were so astonished at the hearing of the law read out of it. It may well be granted hereupon that indeed there were but few copies, and that the idolaters had destroyed as many as they could come by. It is also probable that such copies as they had remaining were not thoroughly read; but when that very original which Moses wrote was found, the very rarity of it caused it to be thoroughly read and seriously weighed.

*{And Hilkiah gave the book to Shaphan}* See v. 3.

*{and he read it}* He himself was desirous to know at least the contents thereof, that so he might see the greater reason for delivering it to the king.

**V. 9. ***{And Shaphan the Scribe came to the King, and brought the King word again, and said}* This word *again* has reference to the king’s first sending of Shaphan, v. 3. and to a second coming of his to the king. We also in this sense do say, Such a messenger came again.

*{Thy servants}* He means Hilkiah, himself, and other messengers whom the king sent.

*{have gathered}* Heb. *melted*. For things of the same kind being melted use to gather together.

*{the money that was found in the house}* Being contributed by such as were well devoted to the service of God.

*{and have delivered it into the hand of them that do the work, that have the oversight of the house of the Lord}* See v. 5.

**V. 10. ***{And Shaphan the Scribe shewed the King, saying}* He showed the book to the king, and declared how he came by it.

*{Hilkiah the Priest hath delivered me a book}* See v. 8.

*{and Shaphan read it before the King}* Questionless he read it by the king’s appointment and command, as Jer. 36.21.

**V. 11. ***{And it came to pass when the King had heard the words of the book of the law}* Surely he had not heard them to the full before this time; which implies a neglect some way or other. For there was a charge given that the king should *read therein all the dayes of his life*, Deut. 17.19.

*{that he rent his clothes}* See ch. 18.37. He was deeply affected with that he heard, as is more fully expressed, v. 19.

**V. 12. ***{And the king commanded Hilkiah the Priest}* See v. 4.

*{and Ahikam the son of Shaphan}* This was another Shaphan than the scribe.

*{And Achbor the son of Michaiah}* He is called Abdon the son of Micah 2 Chr. 34.20. Either he had two names, or there were different pronunciations of the same name.

*{and Shaphan the Scribe}* See v. 3.

*{and Asahiah a servant of the Kings, saying}* This is a description of a courtier that is always at the king’s hand.

**V. 13. ***{Go ye, enquire of the Lord for me}* The good king hearing the curses denounced in the law, and knowing how much his predecessors had transgressed it, supposed the judgments threatened therein were even then hanging over their heads; and therefore sent to inquire whether there might be any means to pacify God’s wrath, and prevent those judgments.

*{and for the people, and for all Judah}* By the people he may mean such as used to meet at public assemblies; and by all Judah, all other of that kingdom, whether men, women or children. His care was as great for his subjects as for himself.

*{concerning the words of this book which is found}* He means in particular the judgments denounced in that book.

*{for great is the wrath of the Lord that is kindled against us}* Though in his days there were no apparent manifestations of God’s wrath, yet by that which he had heard of the sins of the land in former times, he might well conclude that God’s wrath was kindled. By a metaphor he resembles God’s wrath to a fire. See Deut. 32.22.

*{because our fathers have not hearkened unto the words of this Book}* Under fathers he comprises Amon his immediate father, Manasseh his grandfather, Ahaz and other his predecessors before them. By not hearkening he means their rebellion, especially in worshipping idols.

*{to do according to all that which is written concerning us}* That which is registered in God’s Word. For general directions or prohibitions concern all ages. See Rom. 15.4. 1 Cor. 10.6,11.

**V. 14. ***{So Hilkiah the Priest, and Ahikam, &c.}* See v. 12.

*{went unto Huldah the Prophetess}* It pleased God to endow women with an extraordinary spirit, to show that he is tied to no sex. Though he forbade women ordinarily to exercise a ministerial function in the church, 1 Tim. 2.12. 1 Cor. 14.34. yet he would leave liberty to himself to bestow his gifts as pleased him. Miriam was a prophetess, Exod. 15.20. and Deborah, Judg. 4.4. and Anna, Luk. 2.36. There were then extraordinary prophets in Judah, as Jer. 1.1,2. Zeph. 1.1. and it may be Urijah, Jer. 26.20. Yet this woman was now sent unto, because she was near at hand; even in that city, and known to be endued with an extraordinary spirit; so as they had respect to the gift of God rather than the person on whom the gift was bestowed.

*{the wife of Shallum the son of Tikvah}* Hereby this Shallum is distinguished from the king’s son, Jer. 22.11. and from Jeremiah’s kinsman, Jer. 32.7. And he is here set down for honor’s sake; for it was a great honor to have such a wife.

*{the son of Harhas}* Or, *Hasrah* 2 Chr. 34.22. The difference is only in transposing of the letters.

*{keeper of the wardrobe}* Heb. *garments*. Holy vestments are here meant appertaining to the temple. See Neh. 7.72.

*{now she dwelt in Jerusalem in the College}* Or, *in the second part*; by which is meant a part of the city divided from that part where the king’s palace was. Some take it to be the middle court mentioned ch. 20.4. Being taken for a college (as well it may be taken) it implies the place where prophets and children of the prophets had their usual abode. See 1 Kings 6.1. and 18.4.

*{and they communed with her}* About the king’s message and those things which were written in the law.

**V. 15. ***{And she said unto them, Thus saith the Lord}* See 1 Kings 12.24.

*{God of Israel}* See 1 King. 8.15.

*{Tell the man that sent you to me}* She styles the king *man* to show that though he were a king, yet he was but a mortal man. But she does it not in any contempt of his royalty: for she adds this phrase, *that sent you unto me*, which implies authority; and she afterward expressly calls him *King of Judah*, v. 16.18.

**V. 16. ***{Thus saith the Lord}* See v. 15.

*{Behold, I will bring evil}* Sore and heavy judgments.

*{upon this place}* Upon Jerusalem, and all Judah.

*{and upon the inhabitants thereof}* For not only the houses and walls of cities were demolished, but also the dwellers therein were either destroyed by plague, famine or sword, or else carried away captive.

*{even all the words of the book which the king of Judah hath read}* She means all the curses written in the law.

**V. 17. ***{Because they have forsaken me}* See 1 King. 11.33.

*{and have burnt incense unto other gods}* See 1 King. 9.25.

*{that they might provoke me to anger}* See 1 King. 16.26.

*{with all the works of their hands}* He means especially the idols, together with their altars, groves and other things that were made by men for idolatrous uses.

*{therefore my wrath shall be kindled against this place}* See v. 13.16.

*{and shall not be quenched}* The meaning is that the time was come for judgment to be executed to the utter extirpation of the Jews out of their land: and though the good king Josiah found mercy himself, yet his people retaining their rebellious and idolatrous disposition should be utterly destroyed; as when a fire has taken such hold of a house as it cannot be quenched till the house be clean burnt down.

**V. 18. ***{But to the King of Judah, which sent you to enquire of the Lord}* Hereby she shows that she was a prophetess of the Lord, and that the king did so account of her.

*{thus shall ye say to him, Thus saith the Lord, &c.}* See v. 15. She shows she spoke not of any private motion, but from the Lord.

*{As touching the words which thou hast heard}* See v. 11.

**V. 19. ***{Because thine heart was tender}* Soon moved at the hearing of judgment. A tender heart is a soft heart, and is opposed to a hard and obstinate heart.

*{and thou hast humbled thy self before the Lord}* Josiah humbled his soul for his own and others' sins. So to do, is a matter acceptable unto God, Micah 6.8.

*{when thou heardest what I spake}* God’s judgments made known unto us give just occasion of humbling our souls before him.

*{against this place, and against the inhabitants thereof}* See v. 16.

*{that they should become a desolation}* Be emptied of inhabitants.

*{and a curse}* That the land should lie under the vengeance of God, and have all the curses written in the law executed upon it.

*{and hast rent thy clothes}* See v. 11.

*{and wept before me}* See ch. 20.3.

*{I also have heard thee}* Observed what you have done, and accepted what you desired.

*{saith the Lord}* She oft inculcates her warrant, to make her word the better heeded.

**V. 20. ***{Behold therefore, I will gather thee unto thy Fathers}* You shall die as well as your fathers, and be in eternal glory with them.

*{and thou shalt be gathered into thy grave}* Or, *thy graves*. The word is plural, to set out a fair and sumptuous grave, or else one of many graves. So 2 Chron. 16.14. & 35.24.

*{in peace}* Two things are intended hereby, 1. That he should end his days while his kingdom was in peace, before the desolation and curse mentioned v. 19. should fall out. 2. That he should die in peace of conscience, and in peace with God. True it is that he died in war, and was slain by an enemy, ch. 23.29. but not an enemy that invaded the land, or had any quarrel against Israel before that time. Though Josiah failed much in provoking the king of Egypt, yet was there not then any trouble in Israel: Neither did his sudden and violent death break off his peace with God; but thereby he was taken from the great evils to come, Isa. 57.1,2.

*{and thine eyes shall not see all the evil}* This is rendered as one reason of his dying in peace. After his death the evil here intended fell out.

*{which I will bring upon this place}* God has his hand in all judgments, especially such as befall his church, Isa. 1.21. Amos 3.6. Job 45.7. See v. 16.

*{And they brought the King word again}* See v. 9. It was in part a doleful message, yet they faithfully related it.

## CHAPTER 23

**V. 1. ***{And the King sent}* This was done before the book of the law was found, 2 Chr. 34.3. even in the twelfth year of his reign.

*{and they gathered unto him}* The messengers sent for that purpose.

*{all the Elders}* See 1 King. 8.1.

*{of Judah and of Jerusalem}* That were dispersed abroad in the country, or abode in Jerusalem.

**V. 2. ***{And the King went up into the house of the Lord}* To offer up sacrifices to the Lord, to call upon him for pardon, and to enter into a solemn covenant with him, v. 3.

*{and all the men of Judah, and all the inhabitants of Jerusalem with him}* Because all sorts had sinned, and the judgments were like to fall upon them all, the king brought them all to join with him in those duties which he undertook.

*{and the Priests}* They who are teachers must themselves observe those ordinances wherein they instruct others.

*{and the Prophets}* Those extraordinary prophets which were then among them, as Jeremiah, Zephaniah, and also they who are called *children of the prophets*, ch. 2.3. are here meant.

*{and all the people both small and great}* Heb. *from small even unto great*. Under this phrase the common sort of people, women and children, and strangers also are comprised. See Deut. 31.11. Josh. 8.35. Neh. 8.3.

*{and he read}* He caused one of the priests or Levites to read.

*{in their ears}* Audibly and distinctly, so as all might hear and understand.

*{all the words of the book of the Covenant}* It is so called because that covenant which God made with Israel was contained therein. See 1 King. 8.9.

*{which was found in the house of the Lord}* See ch. 22.8.

**V. 3. ***{And the King stood by a pillar}* See ch. 11.14.

*{and made a Covenant before the Lord}* They promised and vowed to be faithful servants to the Lord. See 2 Chr. 34.32.

*{to walk after the Lord, &c.}* This and the other branches following are parts of the covenant. To walk after the Lord is to observe what he prescribes unto us. Of this and the other phrases following see 1 King. 2.3,4. & 11.11.

*{to perform the words of this Covenant that were written in this book}* To do that which by virtue of God’s promise was prescribed to them, and expressly set down in that book. Of every covenant between God and man there are two parts; one, a promise of grace and blessing, on God’s part; the other, of duty and obedience on man’s, Gen. 17.8,9,10.

*{and all the people stood to the Covenant}* They gave their consent to it, and bound themselves to perform it: But it appears by the prophets' complaints even in Josiah’s time, Jer. 3.6,10,11. and by the people’s running to idolatry openly and impudently after Josiah’s death, that this pretense of standing to the covenant was but feigned. The king did entirely with his whole heart what the people did dissemblingly with their mouth only. But Josiah conceiving and hoping the best of them, brought them to this covenant, because ordinarily a covenant, as well as an oath, is a forcible means to bind men to good behavior.

**V. 4. ***{And the king commanded Hilkiah the high Priest}* See ch. 22.4.

*{and the Priests of the second order}* i.e. They which were next unto them that lineally descended from the high priest. Aaron left two sons, Eleazar and Ithamar, Numb. 3.4. They who descended from Eleazar, were of the first order; they who descended from Ithamar, of the second. The meaning is, All sorts of priests were called to employ their service in purging the temple.

*{and the keepers of the door}* Heb. *threshold, as* ch. 22.4. & 12.9. These were Levites.

*{to bring forth out of the Temple of the Lord}* Such things as are not fit for the house of the Lord, are not to be kept therein, but cast out.

*{all the vessels that were made for Baal}* All manner of instruments and utensils, as altars, basins, cups, and whatsoever belonged to the worship of their gods. Idolaters imitated the Lord’s servants, in making such vessels for their gods as were made for the service of the Lord. Under these also may be comprised images and their appurtenances. Such things had Manasseh, ch. 21.4,7. and Amon, ch. 21.4,7,21. and other idolatrous kings before them, brought into the house of the Lord; which though in the time of other good kings and in the former years of Josiah had not been used, but set aside in some by-places, yet still they remained in the temple: But now the zeal of Josiah would not suffer them to abide there any longer.

*{and for the grove}* See 1 King. 16.33. This grove was near the temple, that idolaters might with the greater delight worship their idols there.

*{and for all the host of heaven}* Many idolaters accounted the sun, moon, and other stars to be gods, and yielded divine worship to them. See 2 King. 17.16.

*{and he burnt them}* Many of the vessels before mentioned were of precious matter and curious workmanship, yet the pious king would not suffer them to continue as they were, but utterly destroyed them.

*{without Jerusalem}* Detestable and contemptible things were wont to be carried out of the city when they were to be burnt or otherwise spoiled.

*{in the fields of Kidron}* In those fields by which the river Kidron did run along. See 1 King. 2.37.

*{and carried the ashes of them unto Bethel}* Bethel was the city wherein Jeroboam set one of his golden calves, 1 King. 12.29. It was about eight miles from Jerusalem. For its iniquity it was called also Beth-aven, Hos. 10.5. To stain that conceit which many had of the holiness of this idolatrous city, the zealous king lays the ashes of those idolatrous vessels which he had burnt, in Bethel.

**V. 5. ***{And he put down}* Heb. *caused to cease*. Suffered them not to enjoy their idolatrous functions or use their idolatrous courses any longer.

*{the idolatrous Priests}* Heb. *Chemarim*. The root כמר *Camar*, whence this word comes, is used to set forth the heat and ardency of the bowels when one is moved with compassion, as Gen. 43.30. 1 King. 3.26. Hos. 11.8. It is also used, 1 Sam. 5.10. for the blackness or shrinking of the skin with famine. This title was given to idolatrous priests either by reason of their preposterous zeal and fervency about their idolatry, or because they used to wear black clothes; or because they were much exercised in burning sacrifices and incense. The Jewish rabbins give this title to monks. Many Israelites did entertain these *Chemarims* in Josiah’s time Jer. 3.6, &c. Zeph. 1.1,4, &c. as many Papists do yet still entertain Mass-priests in England.

*{whom the Kings of Judah had ordained}* i.e. Such kings as regarded not the priests of the Lord, but had priests of their own making, 1 King. 12.31,32. & 13.33.

*{to burn incense, &c.}* See 1 King. 9.25. This shows the reason of their name *Chemarim*.

*{unto Baal}* See 1 King. 16.31.

*{to the Sun, and to the Moon}* The moon was accounted by idolaters *the queen of heaven*, Jer. 44.17. and the sun, a great god: This they called *Phœbus*, her *Phœbe*.

*{and to the planets}* Or, *twelve signs, or constellations*. The planets are those which in English are called the seven wandering stars, because they are not so set in their orbs as the multitude of stars are which we commonly call fixed stars. The sun and moon are two of these; Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Mercury, Venus, the other five. These were accounted prime deities by the heathen. The twelve signs are those which are placed in the zodiac, and each of them appropriated to a distinct month, and to distinct and several parts of the body. Every one of these signs is counted a constellation, or gathering together of many stars into one figure or shape. All the rest of the fixed stars are brought under one constellation or other. The Hebrew word imports such things as send down an influence; and this the seven planets are supposed to do most effectually.

*{and to all the host of heaven}* All the other stars besides those comprised under the former names. See 2 King. 17.16.

**V. 6. ***{And he brought out the grove from the house of the Lord}* We cannot think that a grove could be planted and made to grow in any part of the temple properly so called: So that either the house of the Lord is taken for the courts about the temple, whereof see 1 King. 6.36. or else he means that image whereon a grove was engraven, ch. 21.7.

*{without Jerusalem, &c.}* See v. 4.

*{and stamped it small to powder}* To testify the greater detestation.

*{and cast the powder thereof upon the graves of the children of the people}* Of such as had worshipped idols, 2 Chr. 34.4. This also he did in detestation of such as had been idolaters, and for a forewarning to such as then were or afterwards might be idolaters. In particular he showed that they deserved to be beaten to powder. By children of the people are set forth the common sort of people.

**V. 7. ***{And he brake down}* Both to manifest an utter detestation of that filthiness, and also to keep them from being so used again. See the like zeal ch. 18.4.

*{the houses of the Sodomites}* See 1 King. 14.24.

*{that were by the house of the Lord}* Idolaters so disdained the Lord’s holy house as they joined the most abominable things thereto.

*{where the women wove hangings}* Heb. *houses, or, curtains*. Whereby they might keep themselves from the sight of others. Idolatry and all manner of filthiness used to go together.

*{for the grove}* They had their little houses, or tents, or booths, in their grove, which was a delightsome place: and they thought to make it to their impure souls the more delightsome by their abominable filthiness.

**V. 8. ***{And he brought all the Priests}* Here sons of Aaron, who had their calling to be priests, are meant; yet such as had defiled themselves with idolatry.

*{out of the cities of Judah}* Where they had exercised their idolatry. For though the Lord had only one house, and that in Jerusalem, yet idolaters had their idolatrous places in every city.

*{and defiled the high places}* See 1 King. 3.4. These he defiled by destroying the altars and other religious ornaments in them, and by causing dung and other filthy things to be laid in them.

*{where the Priests had burnt incense}* See 1 King. 9.25.

*{from Geba to Beersheba}* From the north to the south coast. See 1 King. 15.22. & 19.3.

*{and brake down the high places of the gates}* Such as were publicly erected at the gates of cities, whither the greatest sort of people did resort.

*{that were in the entering-in of the gate of Joshua the governor of the City}* Not Joshua the son of Nun, but another of the same name, a man of great place in Josiah’s days, and who had caused high places to be erected by his own house. Josiah in his reformation spared none, no nor the greatest.

*{which were on a mans left hand at the gate of the City}* They might be without the city, but near the entrance thereinto.

**V. 9. ***{Nevertheless the Priests of the high places}* Such as were mentioned v. 8. but had renounced their idolatrous places and services.

*{came not up to the altar of the Lord in Jerusalem}* Were not permitted to offer sacrifices on that altar, but they and their posterity were removed from the priestly function. See Ezek. 44.10, &c.

*{but they did eat of the unleavened bread among their brethren}* Under unleavened bread are meant the showbread Lev. 24.5, &c. and that part of the meat offerings which was reserved for Aaron and his sons, Levit. 2.1, &c. By this one kind of provision for the priests all their provision is meant; and thereof might the priests here mentioned be made partakers. Though they might not do the proper offices of priests, yet they were to be maintained by that allowance which was due to the priests. See Ezek. 44.29, &c.

**V. 10. ***{And he defiled}* As v. 8.

*{Topheth which is in the valley of the children of Hinnom}* See ch. 16.3. Josh. 15.18.

*{that no man might make his son or his daughter to pass through the fire to Molech}* See ch. 16.3. 1 King. 11.5.

**V. 11. ***{And he took away the horses that the Kings of Judah had given to the Sun}* These were living and light horses, which were from time to time nourished and kept, to ride with speed, and to carry men to those places where they might see the first rising of the sun, that so they might worship it at the first peeping of it. This was an idolatrous practice of the Persians, and the Israelites had learned it of them. This phrase *he took away*, implies that they were living horses. Had they been only images of horses, he would have burnt them, as he did other images and superstitious monuments, v. 4. By giving these to the sun is meant a religious or rather superstitious dedicating of them to the sun as to a god.

*{at the entering-in of the house of the Lord}* Which was the east end. That way they might the soonest espy the sun rising.

*{by the chamber of Nathanmelech the chamberlain}* Or, *eunuch, or, officer*. See 1 King. 22.9.

*{which was in the suburbs}* Namely of the city; which city some take to be Zion, the city of David.

*{and burnt the Chariots of the Sun with fire}* They are called *chariots of the sun*, because they were made and dedicated to the honor of the sun. By the help of the forementioned horses they and many in them might quickly be drawn to some place where the rising of the sun might sooner be seen. Or as the heathen had, so the idolatrous Israelites might have a glorious image of the sun set in one of these chariots, which being fastened to the forementioned horses might be drawn up and down for all sorts of people to see, and admire, and adore it. It is here said that *the chariots were burnt*, but *the horses taken away*; which further implies that the horses were living creatures, but the chariots made of wood.

**V. 12. ***{And the altars that were on the top of the upper chamber of Ahaz}* Fair great houses were then wont to have flat roofs leaded over, Deut. 22.8. Mar. 2.4. On these idolaters used to erect altars, especially to the host of heaven, Jer. 19.13. Zeph. 1.5. Ahaziah had such a roof over his upper chamber, 2 King. 1.2.

*{which the Kings of Judah had made}* As Ahaziah in his time, so other kings in theirs had made altars in such places: which was a great aggravation of their idolatry. They contented not themselves with altars in temples, in groves, on hills, and other like places on the ground, but must needs erect some also on their housetops.

*{and the altars which Manasseh had made in the two courts of the house of the Lord}* See ch. 21.5.

*{did the King beat down}* According to the law. Deut. 7.5.

*{and brake them down from thence}* Heb. *ran from thence*. He caused them to be tumbled down, broken to pieces, and beaten to powder. It may seem strange how these should stand till Josiah’s time, seeing it is said that Manasseh after his repentance *cast out of the city all the altars that he had built*, 2 Chr. 33.15. But they might remain and be still though they were cast out of the city: Yea, Amon might bring them again to the places where Manasseh had first set them.

*{and cast the dust of them into the brook Kidron}* See v. 4,6.

**V. 13. ***{And the high places that were before Jerusalem}* See 1 King. 3.4. These might be without the city, but before the chief entrance thereinto, so as out of them men might look into the temple, and out of the temple into them.
