*{which were on the right hand of the mount of corruption}* i.e. the mount of Olives. It is called *the mount of corruption*, because they had there corrupted themselves by their idolatry.

*{which Solomon the King of Israel had built}* This circumstance much commends Josiah’s courage and zeal. Though these high places were first erected by one that was accounted a very wise king, and also had continued many generations, yet he was not afraid to demolish them; God’s word being to him more than the practice of a wise man, or antiquity. Herein he excelled all the good kings that were betwixt Solomon & himself: It may be these high places were not frequented or much used in the days of Asa, Jehoshaphat, and Hezekiah, and thereupon suffered to abide: but Amon might again put them to those idolatrous uses for which they were first erected; and thereupon Josiah might take occasion utterly to demolish them, that they might not again be so used.

*{for Ashtoreth, &c.}* Of all these idols see 1 King. 11.5,7.

*{did the King defile}* See v. 8.

**V. 14. ***{And he brake in pieces the images}* Heb. *statues*. See 2 King. 10.26.

*{and cut down the groves}* See 1 King. 16.33.

*{and filled their places with bones of men}* This was one way whereby he defiled places, as v. 8,10. And thus he dealt with them, to keep men from using them any more so superstitiously as they had done formerly. Those bones of men he took out of the graves of idolaters, v. 16.

**V. 15. ***{Moreover the altar that was at Bethel}* 1 King. 11.33. & 13.1. Though Jeroboam the first had Bethel in his possession when he erected altars there, 1 King. 12.33. yet was it afterwards won by Abijah king of Judah, 2 Chr. 13.19. Indeed that part of Bethel where the idol was, the kings of Israel retained till their captivity. But Israel at this time was carried captive out of the land, and they that inhabited the land did not much care for the idols and the idolatrous places which the Israelites had set up: therefore it is said that *Josiah did to all the houses of the high places that were in the cities of Samaria as he had done to them in Bethel*, v. 19.

*{and the high place which Jeroboam the son of Nebat}* Here may be meant that house of high places which Jeroboam built, 1 King. 12.31.

*{who made Israel to sin, had made}* See 1 King. 12.30.

*{both that altar and the high place he brake down}* He let nothing remain.

*{and burnt the high place}* After he had broken it down.

*{and stamped it small to powder}* See v. 6.

*{and burnt the grove}* Where his house of high places was. See 1 King. 3.4. & 16.33. That Jeroboam had groves is evident 1 King. 14.15.

**V. 16. ***{And as Josiah turned himself}* This is a special evidence of God’s providence, bringing to pass what was foretold above three hundred years before, 1 King. 13.2.

*{he spied the sepulchres that were there in the mount}* That was by the idolatrous Israelites accounted a holy place, and thereupon their priests were there buried, and had some monuments over their graves to continue a memorial of their names. Those monuments continued till Josiah’s time; and he being there present soon espied them.

*{and sent and took the bones out of the sepulchres}* Certainly he was moved thereunto by some divine instinct.

*{and burnt them upon the altar}* A like thing was done chap. 11.18.

*{and polluted it}* Burning of priests' bones upon an altar could not but be accounted a great pollution, at least by idolaters.

*{according to the word of the Lord which the man of God proclaimed}* See 1 King. 13.2, &c. God’s word shall assuredly in due time be accomplished, though the date for the accomplishment thereof be long.

*{who proclaimed these words}* This is added to show the faithfulness of the man of God in doing his message.

**V. 17. ***{And he said, What title is that that I see?}* There was some gravestone, and a superscription upon it, or else a pillar by the sepulcher of the man of God, such a one as Jacob set upon Rachel’s grave, Gen. 35.20. wherein the story of that man of God was engraven. See 1 King. 13.30,31.

*{And the men of the city told him}* They which dwelt in Bethel (which city is here meant) were oft put in mind by this monument of the story of the man of God; thereupon they well remembered it, and could readily tell it the king.

*{It is the sepulchre of the man of God, &c.}* See 1 King. 13.1, &c.

*{and proclaimed these things that thou hast done, &c.}* See v. 16.

**V. 18. ***{And he said, Let him alone}* The king was moved hereunto either by special instinct, or else by the story that he heard of the man of God.

*{Let no man move his bones}* God has care even of the dead dry bones of his saints, that they may quietly rest.

*{So they let his bones alone}* Heb. *to escape*. They did not take his bones out of the grave, as they had done the bones of others, v. 16.

*{with the bones of the Prophet that came out of Samaria}* Which with a lie deceived the man of God. Yet that old prophet believing the truth of that which the man of God had said, received this recompense of his faith, and had that which he desired and expected accomplished. See 1 King. 13.31,32.

**V. 19. ***{And all the houses also of the high places, &c.}* See v. 15. & 1 King. 13.32. The whole country and kingdom of Israel was oft called Samaria. See 1 King. 17.24. In this extent is the word Samaria here used.

*{which the Kings of Israel had made to provoke the Lord to anger}* See 1 King. 14.9.

*{Josiah took away}* See v. 11.

*{and did to them according to all the acts that he had done in Bethel}* See v. 15. It is probable that the cities of the country of Samaria in sundry tribes, mentioned 2 Chron. 34.6. were under the jurisdiction of the king of Judah at that time, and that many in those tribes yielded themselves to him, 2 Chron. 30.1,10,11. As for the royal city Samaria, it is not said that the king entered into it; for the inhabitants thereof cleaved close to their idols, 2 King. 17.41.

**V. 20. ***{And he slew}* Heb. *sacrificed*. After he had slain them, he burnt them upon their altars, whereby he defiled those altars, as v. 16.

*{all the Priests of the high places that were there, upon the altars}* It may be these idolatrous priests resisted Josiah, and did what they could to hinder him, and thereupon he slew them.

*{and burnt mens bones upon them}* Such as are mentioned v. 16.

*{and returned to Jerusalem}* Though for reformation’s sake he went into the idolatrous tribes, yet he meant not there to abide; therefore when his work was finished, he went to his own city, where his royal palace and the house of God were.

**V. 21. ***{And the King commanded all the people, saying}* That which follows was immediately upon the covenant which Josiah and his people made with the Lord, and an especial fruit and effect of it. See 2 Chr. 34.31, &c. & 35.1.

*{Keep the Passover}* Of the Passover see Exod. 12.1, &c.

*{unto the Lord your God}* See 1 King. 8.61. The Passover being a sacred ordinance was to be observed to the honor of God.

*{as it is written}* Exod. 12.3. Deut. 16.2.

*{in this book of the Covenant}* See v. 2. Exod. 24.7. The particle this has reference to that book which Hilkiah found, ch. 22.8. and the king then had with him.

**V. 22. ***{Surely there was not holden such a Passover}* With such a preparation, such humiliation, such detestation of idolatry, such reformation of religion, such devotion and solemn renovation of the covenant. See 2 Chron. 35.1, &c.

*{from the days of the Judges that judged Israel}* The judges living near the time of the institution of this ordinance might be better directed in the observation thereof. The last of the judges may be here meant, because mention is made of Samuel, who was the last, 2 Chron. 35.18. And so this phrase implies the beginning of the kingdom of Israel.

*{nor in all the days of the Kings of Israel}* Hereby may be meant the reigns of David and Solomon, who reigned over all the twelve tribes, and in that respect might have a greater multitude of people and more store of sacrifices; and yet this ordinance not be kept after such a spiritual manner then as now.

*{nor of the Kings of Judah}* From the beginning of Rehoboam’s reign, who first lost the ten tribes, 1 King. 12.20,21. unto this very time of Josiah.

**V. 23. ***{But}* This particle has reference to the former verse.

*{in the eighteenth year of King Josiah}* Of his reign, but the six and twentieth of his age.

*{wherein this passover was holden to the Lord}* See v. 21.

*{in Jerusalem}* There was the house of God, and thither they were commanded to go to keep the Passover, Deut. 12.5. & 16.2.

**V. 24. ***{Moreover}* This questionless has relation to the former reformation, v. 4, &c. and the Passover was held after this.

*{the workers with familiar Spirits, and the wizards}* See ch. 21.6.

*{and the images,}* Or, *Teraphim*. These were household gods, fashioned in the shape of men. Such a one Michol put into David’s bed, 1 Sam. 19.13. The heathen consulted with such, Ezek. 21.21. and answers have been returned by them, Zach. 10.2. either by the subtlety of the devil, or by the forgery of their priests. Teraphim (translated *idolatry*, 1 Sam. 15.23.) is reckoned among things most detestable to God. See on Judg. 17.5.

*{and the idols}* See ch. 17.12. & 1 King. 15.12.

*{and all the abominations}* All abominable idolatry and other filthy wickedness. See 1 King. 14.24.

*{that were spied in the land of Judah, and in Jerusalem, did Josiah put away}* He that was careful to reform other places would not neglect his own most proper dominion. See v. 15,19.

*{that he might perform the words of the Law}* Lev. 20.27. Deut. 18.11. His eye was especially on God’s Word for a warrant of all he did.

*{which were written in the book that Hilkiah, &c.}* See ch. 22.8.

**V. 25. ***{And like unto him was there no King before him}* In a holy zeal and thorough reformation, and also in the integrity of his life, ordered according to all God’s law. See ch. 18.5. As some particulars were there noted wherein Hezekiah went beyond Josiah, so others may be noted wherein Josiah excelled Hezekiah; as his contrition for the wickedness before his days, ch. 22.19. the solemnity of the Passover, 2 Chr. 35.1, &c. his purging other places besides Judah and Benjamin, verse 19. his conforming himself to the law of God, as in this verse. Besides, he was never puffed up, as Hezekiah was, 2 Chr. 32.25.

*{that turned to the Lord}* This phrase is not to be taken as if he had ever departed from the Lord; for when he was a child he sought after him, 2 Chron. 34.3. but it has relation to his father Amon and other idolatrous kings, who turned from the Lord: He did not so; he turned to no other, but to the Lord.

*{with all his heart and with all his soul}* See 1 King. 2.4.

*{and with all his might}* With the uttermost extent of his soul, with all the faculties thereof; and of his body, with all the parts thereof. He put forth his utmost endeavor to serve the Lord, 2 Sam. 6.14. 1 Chr. 13.8.

*{according to all the law of Moses}* See v. 24. Because Moses was the penman of all the laws that God prescribed to the Jews, God’s law is styled *the law of Moses*.

*{neither after him arose there any like him}* After him there was not a good king. And none of those governors that were over the Jews after the captivity was like him in holy zeal.

**V. 26. ***{Notwithstanding the Lord turned not from}* God’s wrath may be so far provoked as unalterably to be settled with a resolution to take vengeance, ch. 24.4. 1 Sam. 3.14.

*{the fierceness of his great wrath}* This is attributed unto God after the manner of man, to aggravate the heinousness of sin, and to imply the heavy judgments that should follow.

*{wherewith his anger was kindled}* See ch. 22.13,17.

*{against Judah}* They who profess themselves to be God’s people may exceedingly provoke his wrath.

*{because of all the provocations}* Heb. *angers*. For sins provoke God’s anger.

*{that Manasseh had provoked him withal}* Indeed Manasseh repented, and questionless obtained pardon, 2 Chr. 33.12,13. but that pardon was for his own person only. For the people in his time, and after, even in this good king’s days, and after also, continued in his sins, and therefore his sins were theirs, and justly punished in them. Besides, though God pardoned Manasseh himself, yet for Manasseh’s sins he might justly punish Manasseh’s posterity, who continued in those sins. Thus were all the Israelites, generation after generation, that continued in Jeroboam’s sin, punished for the same. Yea heinous sins, though pardoned, may leave a perpetual scar in the name of him who is pardoned.

**V. 27. ***{And the Lord said, I will remove Judah also}* God had given a long time of repentance to Judah after he had cast off Israel, yet Judah waxed worse than Israel, Jer. 3.11. therefore the Lord justly denounced this judgment against them.

*{out of my sight}* See ch. 17.18,20,23. & 24.3.

*{as I have removed Israel}* See ch. 17.6, &c.

*{and will cast off this City Jerusalem}* In Jerusalem was the temple. This therefore was a sore aggravation of the judgment.

*{which I have chosen}* See 1 King. 8.44.

*{and the house of which I said, My Name shall be there}* See 1 King. 8.16,29. & 9.3. & ch. 21.7.

**V. 28. ***{Now the rest of the acts, &c.}* See 1 King. 15.23.

**V. 29. ***{In his days Pharaoh Nechoh King of Egypt}* 2 Chr. 35.20. Pharaoh was a common name to kings of Egypt, as Abimelech to kings of the Philistines, and Caesar to Roman emperors. See 1 King. 11.40. Nechoh is added to distinguish this Pharaoh from others; as Hophra, Jer. 44.30.

*{went up against the King of Assyria}* Pharaoh had no quarrel against Josiah or his people, but only against the king of Assyria. The kingdom of Assyria was extremely weakened, if not in the power of the Babylonians. Some take the Babylonian kingdom to be here meant, and called Assyria, because that had been a title of great fame. See 2 King. 20.12.

*{to the river Euphrates}* See ch. 24.7. The city Carchemish bordering upon this river, 2 Chron. 35.20. which being now in the power of the Assyrian, Pharaoh went to recover it. Either the inhabitants thereof had some way or other annoyed the Egyptians, or in former times it had belonged to the king of Egypt, and therefore he came to fight against it.

*{and King Josiah went against him}* Part of Judea lay betwixt Egypt and Carchemish, so as Pharaoh could not well go with his army thither but through part of Josiah’s kingdom: In this respect Josiah might have cause to go against Pharaoh, that he might hinder him from passing through his country. Or it may be Josiah was willing to do a kindness for the king of Assyria, and in that respect would endeavor to hinder the king of Egypt from invading the land of the king of Assyria. But whatsoever the cause were, herein Josiah failed, that notwithstanding the king of Egypt sent him word that he intended no hurt to him nor to his people, and withal informed him that he went upon God’s appointment (and it is probable that Pharaoh had some prophet to inform him in the will of God about that point, 2 Chr. 35.21.) yet Josiah would persist in going out against him.

*{and he slew him}* The archers in the king of Egypt’s army slew Josiah, 2 Chr. 35.23. but what is done by any in an army is attributed to the general. Josiah was not immediately slain in the battle, but so wounded as he died of the wound, and thereupon is said to be slain. So 2 Chr. 35.23,24.

*{at Megiddo}* So 1 King. 9.15. See Zech. 12.11.

*{when he had seen him}* So soon as army was set against army, and had begun to fight. See ch. 14.8.

**V. 30. ***{And his servants carried him in a Chariot dead}* Deadly wounded. By servants are here meant in general such subjects as were soldiers, in particular his lifeguard, or courtiers, and such as were of his household.

*{from Megiddo}* It is said 2 Chr. 35.24. that he died at Jerusalem: he could not therefore be dead at Megiddo; for from thence he was carried to Jerusalem.

*{and brought him to Jerusalem}* There was his palace and court; there might he have best means for cure; and there, if he died, he would choose to die.

*{and buried him in his own sepulchre}* Some special sepulcher that he had made for himself in his lifetime, among the sepulchers of his fathers, 2 Chron. 35.24. even in the city of David. See 1 King. 2.10.

*{and the people of the land}* 2 Chron. 36.1. All of all sorts are here understood, who did what they did, with an unanimous consent.

*{took Jehoahaz the son of Josiah}* Called Johanan, 1 Chr. 3.15. and Shallum, Jer. 22.11. We read 1 Chr. 3.15. that Josiah had four sons, Johanan, Jehoiakim, Zedekiah, Shallum; and seeing Jehoahaz is not there mentioned, he is by many taken to be the same with Johanan. But because Jehoiakim is supposed to be elder than Jehoahaz, others take this Jehoahaz to be the same with Shallum. The reason why the people made him king see v. 36.

*{and anointed him}* See ch. 11.12. This they did to settle him more surely in the kingdom. See v. 36.

*{and made him King in his fathers stead}* A woeful change.

**V. 31. ***{Jehoahaz was twenty and three years old when he began to reign}* This implies he was younger than Jehoiakim, v. 36.

*{and he reigned three months in Jerusalem}* He lived longer, but the king of Egypt put him from his throne, v. 33.

*{and his mothers name was Hamutal}* See 1 King. 15.10.

*{the daughter of Jeremiah of Libnah}* Of Libnah see ch. 8.22. The mention of this place distinguishes this Jeremiah from the prophet, who was of Anathoth, Jer. 1.1. See ch. 24.18.

**V. 32. ***{And he did that which was evil in the sight of the Lord}* See 1 King. 11.6. He walked not in the steps of his good father Josiah, but in the short time he reigned he returned to idolatry: and he endeavored to bring in again those evils which his father had cast out. This is he of whom Ezekiel, 19.2, &c. speaks.

*{according to all that his fathers had done}* Josiah cannot be here meant, but former predecessors, as Amon, Manasseh, Ahaz, and such like.

**V. 33. ***{And Pharaoh Nechoh}* See v. 29.

*{put him in bands}* Laid him in prison, and so kept him fast, till he should come again and carry him into Egypt. See v. 34.

*{at Riblah, in the land of Hamath}* Both these places were in the circuit of the land of Canaan, Numb. 34.8,11. See ch. 15.6.

*{that he might not reign in Jerusalem}* Or, *because he reigned*. Pharaoh discerned that the people had set him up in a faction, and that in opposition against himself; and therefore he made speed to quash that faction, and within three months put him down.

*{and put the land to a tribute}* Heb. *set a mulct upon the land*. In revenge of that opposition Josiah had made against him, v. 29.

*{of an hundred talents of silver, and a talent of gold}* See 1 Chron. 22.14.

**V. 34. ***{And Pharaoh Nechoh}* See v. 29.

*{made Eliakim}* This was the first name of this man, signifying *God rising*; and it showed the piety of his father Josiah, and the hope he had of him. But he himself did not answer his name. This name was given to that good governor mentioned ch. 18.18. & Isa. 22.20. There was also a priest of that name, Neh. 12.41. And two of Christ’s predecessors, after the days of Eliakim here mentioned, had this name; one in the legal descent, Mat. 1.13. the other in the natural, Luk. 3.30.

*{the son of Josiah}* This distinguishes the Eliakim here mentioned from all the others. By many he is taken to be Josiah’s eldest son, and heir to the crown.

*{King in the room of Josiah his father}* He says not in the room of Jehoahaz, because he was set up tumultuously by the people, and in regard of his short and troublesome time was reputed as no king.

*{and turned his name}* As an evidence of his power over him. For conquerors oft used to change the names of such kings as they vanquished, to show they accounted them as their substitutes and vassals. He that had the power over Daniel and his three companions, hereby manifested it, Dan. 1.7.

*{to Jehoiakim}* Called Jakim Matth. 1.13. Jehoiakim has the same notation and signification with Eliakim: For the first syllable in each is a name of God, and both signify *God’s rising*. So some think Joakim the father of the B. Virgin was called Eliakim, and for shortness Eli, Luk. 3.23.

*{and took Jehoahaz away}* From the place where he had bound him, v. 33. yea, and from the land where he had a while reigned as king.

*{and he came to Egypt}* He did not go thither of his own accord, but was carried thither as prisoner by the king of Egypt.

*{and died there}* This was foretold Jer. 22.12. where he is called Shallum. And that prophecy plainly shows that Jehoahaz here and Shallum there were both names of the same person.

**V. 35. ***{And Jehoiakim gave the silver and the gold to Pharaoh}* i.e. The hundred talents of silver and talent of gold mentioned v. 33. Upon this condition he held his crown, and therefore would not fail to pay it.

*{but he taxed the land to give the money}* He laid a rate upon all his subjects throughout Judah. The land is here put by a metonymy for the inhabitants.

*{according to the commandment of Pharaoh}* Heb. *mouth of Pharaoh*. For commandments use to be given by the mouth. This charge of Pharaoh had relation to the sum of money to be paid, not to the particular manner of raising it. Jehoiakim raised it by a levy upon all his subjects, because he had no treasury of his own: For it is probable the king of Egypt had spoiled the king of Judah’s treasures, as Shishak had done in Rehoboam’s time, 1 King. 14.26. Other kings of Judah used to pay great sums to their enemies out of the treasures of their own house, and out of the treasures of the house of God, as Asa, 1 King. 15.18. Jehoash, ch. 12.18. Hezekiah, ch. 18.15.

*{he exacted the silver and the gold of the people of the land}* The people were not very willing, but for fear of him, and of Pharaoh that set him up, they paid it; and thereupon he is said to exact it.

*{of every one according to his taxation}* According to that rate which the king’s officers had laid on every one.

*{to give it unto Pharaoh Nechoh}* It was not for any positive public good, but only to satisfy an enemy.

**V. 36. ***{Jehoiakim was twenty and five years old when he began to reign}* Hence it appears that he was elder than his brother Jehoahaz, who but three months before this is said to be but twenty and three years old. Others say this time of Jehoiakim’s age must be reckoned from the death of Jehoahaz, who died about two years after he was carried into Egypt, v. 34. Now while Jehoahaz lived, though he were a captive in another land, Jehoiakim might be accounted a chief governor in his brother’s absence, but not a full and absolute king. They who hold Jehoiakim to be the elder brother, say Jehoahaz was a more stern and stout man, better able and more forward to stand against Pharaoh, than Jehoiakim. He is taken to be the first lion mentioned Ezek. 19.3. The people through a strong faction chose him, and anointed him, thinking to establish him the more firmly in the kingdom, as 1 King. 1.39. See v. 30.

*{and he reigned eleven years in Jerusalem}* From the time that Pharaoh set him on the throne, v. 34.

*{and his mothers name was Zebudah}* See 1 King. 15.10. Jehoiakim and Jehoahaz had one father, but not one mother. See v. 31.

*{the daughter of Pedaiah of Rumah}* This shows she was an Israelite.

**V. 37. ***{And he did that which was evil &c.}* See v. 32. He took no warning by the judgment on his brother, v. 33. Much evil of this king is set down, Jer. 22.13,17. & 26.20, &c. & 36.21,22,23. & 52.23. Ezek. 17.5,6,7.

## CHAPTER 24

**V. 1. ***{IN his days}* This has reference to ch. 24.36. where mention is made of the beginning of Jehoiakim’s reign. It is said, *in his days*, not, *in the beginning*, because there were about three years of his reign passed over before this time. It is said Dan. 1.1. that what is here mentioned fell out *in the third year of the reign of Jehoiakim*, namely in the latter end thereof; but Jer. 25.1. *in the fourth year of his reign*, namely *in the beginning* thereof. In the third year Nebuchadnezzar began his expedition, in the fourth he laid his siege against Jerusalem.

*{Nebuchadnezzar King of Babylon}* This was Nebuchadnezzar the second, or Nebuchadnezzar the son. His father’s name also was Nebuchadnezzar, who subdued the Assyrians, and began to erect the Babylonian monarchy. He also subdued many other nations. His days being ended, his son, that before was a kind of a lieutenant general under his father, had now the sole jurisdiction. The first year of his reign was at the end of Jehoiakim’s third year, and went on in his fourth year, Jer. 25.1. This is further evident, in that the year wherein Jehoiakim was last taken captive, which was his eleventh, ch. 23.36. was the seventh of Nebuchadnezzar, Jer. 32.28.

*{came up}* Namely against Jerusalem. The occasion may be this; The king of Egypt went to surprise Carchemish, which belonged to the king of Babylon, ch. 23.29. and it is probable that he then got it, and returning to Jerusalem made this Jehoiakim king of Judah. Jehoiakim thereupon became tributary to the Egyptian, and haply assisted him in his wars: But Nebuchadnezzar recovered Carchemish again, and smote the King of Egypts army there, in the fourth year of Jehoiakim, Jer. 46.2. and to be revenged on the king of Judah for assisting him, and also to get thereby a fair and free passage into Egypt, came against Jehoiakim and his land.

*{and Jehoiakim became his servant}* Yielded to be tributary to him, as ch. 17.3. 2 Sam. 8.2,6. He saw he was not able to stand out against the forces of Nebuchadnezzar, and therefore entered into a composition, and made a covenant with him.

*{three years}* These were the fifth, sixth and seventh years of Jehoiakim: In these he performed his covenant with the king of Babylon.

*{then he turned}* From the king of Babylon to the king of Egypt. It is probable that Babylon being far from Judea, and Egypt near unto it, Jehoiakim confided in the king of Egypt more than in the king of Babylon: Yea it may be also that the king of Egypt solicited him with fair promises to be on his side.

*{and rebelled against him}* i.e. Nebuchadnezzar. See ch. 1.1. & 17.4.

**V. 2. ***{And the Lord sent against him}* See 1 King. 12.15.

*{bands of the Chaldees}* Chaldees and Babylonians were all one; the former name was from the country Chaldea, the latter from the city Babylon.

*{and bands of the Syrians}* These Syrians were always enemies to Israel. Now they were brought under the power of the king of Babylon, and served him.

*{and bands of the Moabites}* The Moabites came from Lot, Gen. 19.37. and were mortal enemies to the Israelites. See ch. 1.1.

*{and bands of the children of Ammon}* The Ammonites came also from Lot, Gen. 19.38. and were as great enemies to the Israelites as the Moabites. All the bands here mentioned served under Nebuchadnezzar, and made up an army. Some take it as if these several bands by themselves made inroads into Judea.

*{and sent them against Judah}* This inculcating again God’s sending these enemies against his people, shows it is a point not slightly to be passed over.

*{to destroy it}* This is spoken in relation to the intent of the enemies, who had a purpose utterly to destroy Judah. To make way to that purpose, God sent them forth. For they so far prevailed, as the king of Babylon took the king of Judah’s wife and children, many of the princes of the land, and part of the vessels of the house of God, together with the king’s treasures, and carried them away, 2 Chr. 36.6,7.

*{according to the word of the Lord}* Ch. 20.17. & 23.26.

*{which he spake by}* Heb. *by the hand*. See ch. 8.53.

*{his servants the Prophets}* See ch. 21.10. Prophets are in special manner God’s servants, being by him sent upon his special messages. Isaiah was one of the prophets here meant, ch. 20.17. Jeremiah another, Jer. 25.9. Urijah another, Jer. 26.20. Huldah also may be reckoned amongst them, ch. 22.16.

**V. 3. ***{Surely at the Commandment}* Heb. *mouth*. See ch. 23.35.

*{of the Lord came this upon Judah}* God does not only permit, but also appoint and command enemies to afflict his people when they sin against him, Isa. 10.6.

*{to remove them out of his sight}* See ch. 23.27.

*{for the sins of Manasseh}* See ch. 23.26.

*{according to all that he did}* To wit, before his conversion.

**V. 4. ***{And also for the innocent blood which he shed}* This is in particular mentioned, because it is a crying sin, Gen. 4.10. and defiles a land, Numb. 35.33. See 2 King. 21.16.

*{for he filled Jerusalem with innocent blood}* See ch. 21.16.

*{which the Lord would not pardon}* Though this sin, as well as others, might be pardoned to Manasseh upon his repentance, so as he was not eternally damned for it; yet, in regard of temporal punishments the Lord would not pardon it to the land so far as to free it from all such kind of judgments. Besides, if there were not many then living in the land that had a hand in shedding that innocent blood, yet the present king and his people might shed blood in their days, and thereupon God in revenging that blood which they shed might also call to mind the blood shed in Manasseh’s time; and all joined together made the greater cry, and moved God to execute the sorer vengeance. See Jer. 22.17. & Ezek. 16.6.

**V. 5. ***{Now the rest of the acts, &c.}* See 1 King. 15.23.

**V. 6. ***{So Jehoiakim slept with his fathers}* This is to be taken in a general sense. As death is a sleep, and all his fathers or predecessors died, so also Jehoiakim died. But concerning the particular kind of his death, it was different from the death of most of his predecessors, if not of them all. For he was taken by Nebuchadnezzar, bound with chains 2 Chron. 36.6. and as he was going captive to Babylon he died; and without any burial or mourning for him, he was as an ass cast forth beyond the gates of Jerusalem, Jer. 22.18,19.

*{and Jehoiachin his son reigned in his stead}* Jehoiachin was also called Jechoniah, 1 Chr. 3.16. which signifies *the Lord’s strength*; and in contempt he was called Coniah, Jer. 22.24,28. leaving out the syllable that has relation to the Lord; for he had forsaken the Lord, Jer. 22.21,28. See on the title of Psal. 9.

**V. 7. ***{And the king of Egypt came not again}* By this particle again some gather that this king of Egypt had come out of his land to help Jehoiachin, when Nebuchadnezzar besieged Jerusalem, and that was one cause why the siege lay so long against it. But Pharaoh could not deliver the king of Judah out of the hand of the king of Babylon.

*{any more out of his land}* Till the last year of Zedekiah, whom he came to help, but with no success; for he quickly returned home again, Jer. 37.7.

*{for the King of Babylon had taken from the river of Egypt}* Called Sihor Josh. 13.3. It is taken by some to be Nilus. See 1 King. 8.65.

*{unto the river Euphrates}* See Gen. 2.14. & 15.18. The river of Egypt was on the south of the land of Israel, and Euphrates on the north.

*{all that pertained to the King of Egypt}* And lay betwixt the two aforesaid rivers. This is here noted, as to show the reason why the king of Egypt durst not again come out of his land against the king of Babylon, so also to show how little cause the Jews had to trust to Egypt for help. See Jer. 2.36.

**V. 8. ***{Jehoiachin was eighteen years old when he began to reign}* See 2 Chron. 36.9. So soon as his father was dead, they set up his son on the throne, in hope to be defended by him against the Babylonians.

*{and he reigned in Jerusalem three months}* Ten days are added 2 Chron. 36.9. Odd days and months use in Scripture frequently to be left out to make the number run the more roundly. The reason why he continued no longer is rendered v. 10,11,12.

*{and his mothers name was Nehushta}* There is but a letter difference betwixt this name and that given to the brazen serpent, being broken in pieces, ch. 18.4. See 1 King. 15.2,10.

*{the daughter of Elnathan of Jerusalem}* This is added to show she was no alien. See ch. 14.2. & 15.2.

**V. 9. ***{And he did that which was evil, &c.}* See 1 King. 11.16.

*{according to all that his father had done}* This is added as a reason why he was cut off so soon, Jer. 22.21,30.

**V. 10. ***{At that time}* This time is said to be *when the year was expired*, or *at the return of the year*, which was about the spring: Then kings used to go out to battle. See 1 King. 20.22.

*{the servants of Nebuchadnezzar King of Babylon came up against Jerusalem}* The army of Nebuchadnezzar is comprised under this phrase, *the servants, &c.* They were such as were under his command. Nebuchadnezzar might fear that Jehoiakim would rebel against him, as his father had done; therefore he thought it not meet to suffer him to retain the crown; the rather, because he was set on the throne without his consent.

*{and the city was besieged}* Heb. *came into siege*. This Hebraism is elegant. Because the city was compassed about with the siege, it is said to *come into the siege*.

**V. 11. ***{And Nebuchadnezzar King of Babylon came against the city}* In his own person, having sent his army before, v. 10.

*{and his servants did besiege it}* They continued the siege till their king came.

**V. 12. ***{And Jehoiakim the King of Judah went out to the King of Babylon}* Jeremiah had told the king of Judah, that if he yielded himself to the king of Babylon, his life should be preserved, and the city and temple kept from destruction: Therefore so soon as the king of Babylon came in his own person, Jehoiachim submitted himself unto him, herein preferring the safety of his kingdom before his own.

*{he, and his mother}* The king of Babylon, who hated Jehoiachim, understanding that his widow was living, would not suffer her to remain in Jerusalem, lest she should plot against him. Jehoiachim’s wives also were taken, v. 15.

*{and his servants}* His courtiers and such as were of his household.

*{and his princes}* Such as were of the royal blood, and such noble persons as were of his council and acquaintance. For many of the princes of the land were left in Zedekiah’s time, Jer. 24.8.

*{and his officers}* Or, *Eunuchs*.

*{and the king of Babylon took him}* To be his prisoner: For he carried him captive to Babylon, v. 15.

*{in the eighth year of his reign}* i.e. Nebuchadnezzar’s reign. For this fell out after Jehoiakim’s reign, which continued eleven years, ch. 23.36. and also after his son Jehoiachin’s reign, which continued three months and ten days, 2 Chr. 36.9. Now Nebuchadnezzar began his reign in the fourth year of Jehoiakim, Jer. 25.1. from whence this is the eighth.

**V. 13. ***{And he carried out thence all the treasures of the house of the Lord}* The temple had at the first building great treasures therein, 1 King. 7.51. And there was from time to time, in several kings’ reigns much added thereunto, ch. 12.18. Yet as kings of Judah were wont to take out of those treasures, when they were to pay great sums to their enemies, 1 King. 15.18. 2 King. 12.18. & 16.8. & 18.15. so enemies that entered into the land of Judah by conquest used to take away those treasures, at least so much thereof as they could meet withal, 1 King. 14.26. & 2 King. 14.14.

*{and the treasures of the kings house}* The kings of Judah had also great treasures of their own, 1 King. 14.26. & 15.18. Herein the threatening mentioned ch. 20.17. Isai. 39.6. was accomplished.

*{and cut in pieces}* The enemy of God’s people had no respect to the sacred things of God, but cut them in pieces, that he might the more conveniently barrel them up together, and carry away the greater quantity. See ch. 29.13.

*{all the vessels of gold}* The general particle used in this and the next verse must have some limitation; as all that he could find; Or, all that he thought meet. For there were left vessels in the house of the Lord, and treasures in the king’s house, Jer. 27.18,20,21. Or it may imply that it was in Nebuchadnezzar’s power to have taken away all. After this were many more vessels carried away, ch. 25.13, &c.

*{which Solomon king of Israel had made in the Temple of the Lord}* See 1 King. 7.48, &c.

*{as the Lord had said}* This has reference to the threatening mentioned ch. 20.17. & Isa. 39.6.

**V. 14. ***{And he carried away all Jerusalem}* Jerusalem is here put for the inhabitants thereof; and under this particle all all sorts of people in Jerusalem are comprised, as noble, strong, skillful in workmanship, and other the like. This the enemy did to weaken the city, and keep it from rebelling again. The like policy did other enemies use in former times, Judg. 5.8. 1 Sam. 13.19.

*{and all the princes}* See v. 12.

*{and all the mighty men of valour}* Such as were trained up to the war, and well experienced therein, and had manifested good courage. See 2 Chr. 17.13.

*{even ten thousand captives}* There is mention v. 16. of seven thousand, and also a thousand more; so as by this account the whole number of captives carried away with Jehoiachin was eighteen thousand. See more v. 16.

*{and all the craftsmen and Smiths}* Some by this general word *craftsmen* mean carpenters in particular. And they are joined with smiths, because these two sorts of workmen were of greatest use to make fortifications against an enemy. The king of Babylon therefore, to prevent future oppositions, takes away such workmen, as might be most serviceable to that purpose. See the first note on this verse. If craftsmen be taken in a larger sense, for bricklayers, masons and other artificers, the reason before rendered will be the more forcible.

*{none remained}* This is not simply to be taken. For Zedekiah, and sundry princes, officers, and other sorts of people remained in Judea after Jehoiachin was carried away. The phrase is to be taken of that power which Nebuchadnezzar had over all, and that none remained but such as he appointed and permitted.

*{save the poorest sort of the people of the land}* These the conqueror did not much seek after, nor cared much for. He might purposely leave all them to manure and till the land, ch. 25.12.

**V. 15. ***{And he carried away Jehoiachin to Babylon}* 2 Chr. 36.10. Esth. 2.6. Jehoiachin having yielded himself to the king of Babylon, v. 12. had his life preserved, but his enemy would not suffer him to abide in his native country, lest he should rebel, as his father had done, v. 1. But God so provided for him, that though he were thirty-seven years in prison, yet at length he was restored, and had great favors done him, ch. 25.27. It is expressly said Jer. 24.4, &c. that the condition of Jehoiachin and those that were carried away captive with him into Babylon was far better than the condition of those that remained in Jerusalem and Judea.

*{and the kings mother}* See v. 12.

*{and the kings wives}* Both kings and others used to have many wives in those days.

*{and his officers}* Or, *Eunuchs*. See 1 King. 22.9.

*{and the mighty of the land}* This word *mighty* may comprise under it princes, nobles, captains, valiant men, wealthy men, men excellent in any science.

*{those carried he into captivity from Jerusalem into Babylon}* These, or some of these at least, were those that continued seventy years among their enemies, and there had habitations, Jer. 29.4, &c. and after the seventy years returned to lay the foundation of a new temple, Ezek. 3.12.

**V. 16. ***{And all the men of might}* In strength of body, Psal. 76.5. valor of mind, ch. 5.1. and estate, Ruth 2.1.

*{even seven thousand}* See v. 14.

*{and craftsmen and smiths a thousand}* These two numbers being added to the ten thousand v. 14. make up eighteen thousand.

*{all that were strong and apt for war}* This description has special relation to soldiers that followed the war.

*{even them the king of Babylon brought captive to Babylon}* See v. 18.

**V. 17. ***{And the king of Babylon made Mattaniah his fathers brother king in his stead}* Jer. 37.1. & 52.1. This relative his twice used has relation to Jehoiachin. The king of Babylon having now an absolute power over all the land of Judah, as an evidence thereof, he took the scepter from one, and gave it to another. This other was first by his parents called Mattaniah, which signifies *the gift of God*; the Jews of old being wont to give significant names to their children. He was one of Josiah’s sons, and Jehoiachin’s uncle, and in that respect said to be *his father’s brother*.

*{and changed his name to Zedekiah}* As the king of Egypt had dealt with Eliakim, so did the king of Babylon do with Mattaniah. See the reason ch. 23.34. Nebuchadnezzar had bound this Mattaniah by solemn oath to be tributary to him. See v. 20. & 2 Chr. 36.13. Therefore he gave him this name Zedekiah, which signifies *the justice of God*, implying that if he broke his oath he should find and feel the fearful effects of God’s justice.

**V. 18. ***{Zedekiah was twenty and one years old when he began to reign}* He was then fifteen years younger than his brother Jehoiakim, who was five and twenty years old when he began to reign, and reigned eleven years, ch. 23.36.

*{and he reigned eleven years in Jerusalem}* All full of troubles.

*{and his mothers name was Hamutal, &c.}* This is the same that was mother to Jehoahaz: so as these two were own brothers by father and mother. But Jehoiakim was their brother only by the father, for his mother’s name was Zebudah, ch. 23.36.

**V. 19. ***{And he did that which was evil, &c.}* See v. 9. & 2 Chr. 36.12. Much evil is recorded of him in the prophecy of Jeremiah. He and his people are resembled to a basket of very naughty figs, which could not be eaten, they were so evil, Jer. 24.8. He broke covenant and oath with him that had seated him on his throne, Ezek. 17.18,19. He also with his princes broke the Lord’s covenant made with his subjects, Jer. 34.8,11,17, &c. He consented to the casting of Jeremiah into a loathsome dungeon, Jer. 38.5. He hearkened not to the word of the Lord, Jer. 37.2.

**V. 20. ***{For through the anger of the Lord}* See 1 King. 12.15. God’s anger had long and much been provoked in the days of many kings of Judah, so as he was resolved to forbear no longer; and thereupon in his justice suffered Zedekiah to go on in those courses which would bring utter destruction. See ch. 23.26.

*{it came to pass in Jerusalem and Judah}* The Jews in city and country, every where followed the evil courses of their king.

*{until he had cast them out from his presence}* This implies an utter forcing them out of the land of Judah and city of Jerusalem, where God in special manner manifested his presence. See ch. 23.27.

*{that Zedekiah rebelled against the king of Babylon}* This has reference to that former phrase, *it came to pass*. *It came to pass that Zedekiah rebelled, &c.* As Jehoiakim had rebelled v. 1. so did Zedekiah here. The king of Babylon sought to be monarch of the whole world, and thereupon went from kingdom to kingdom to bring them under subjection. Herein God made him his scourge to punish the world, that every where had long and much provoked him. The prophet Jeremiah plainly made known as much to all the nations thereabouts, Jer. 29.5, &c. but they not fearing God, nor regarding his word, sought to combine themselves against Nebuchadnezzar; and understanding that Zedekiah had sworn fealty to the king of Babylon, sent their ambassadors, Jer. 27.3. to persuade him to stand against Nebuchadnezzar, and to be no longer his vassal. The like did the king of Egypt: And thus all of them prevailed with Zedekiah to break his oath: And this was the cause of his rebellion and ruin.

## CHAPTER 25

**V. 1. ***{And it came to pass in the ninth year of his reign}* Jer. 39.1. & 52.4. This relative his has reference to Zedekiah mentioned ch. 24.20.

*{in the tenth month, in the tenth day of the month}* Hereby it appears that this fell out toward the latter end of Zedekiah’s ninth year. Because woeful consequences followed upon that which is here mentioned to have fallen out in this tenth month, there was an annual fast kept by the Jews all the time of their captivity in the tenth month, Zech. 8.19.

*{that Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon came}* This was he that first set upon Jehoiakim, ch. 24.1. and took him, 2 Chr. 36.6. who also carried his son Jehoiachin captive into Babylon, ch. 24.15. and and being provoked by Zedekiah attempts the like against him, and effects it, v. 7.

*{he and all his host, against Jerusalem}* Zedekiah’s rebelling against Nebuchadnezzar fortified that great and strong city against him.

*{and pitched against it}* There set his army, and with it besieged the city.

*{and they built forts against it}* The enemy discerned that this city was well fortified, and that they could not easily and speedily enter it; therefore he prepared for a long siege, and built strong places without the wall for engines to be raised on to batter the city, and to make passages thereinto. Some take the word *forts* for a warlike engine like a turret, whereby they might throw great stones into the city, and against the walls. This word is used Jer. 51.4. Ezek. 4.2. & 17.17.

*{round about}* It must needs be a very great army that could begirt so great a city. By this means neither king nor subject could escape. When at a secret place and in the nighttime the king and some with him got out, the enemy that compassed the city round about had quickly notice thereof, and pursued and overtook them, v. 4,5.

**V. 2. ***{And the city was besieged}* See ch. 24.10.

*{unto the eleventh year of king Zedekiah}* Compare v. 1. & 3. and we shall find that the siege continued about a year and a half. It was for a while interrupted, because the Chaldeans heard the king of Egypt was coming against them, Jer. 37.5,11. but the Egyptians saw themselves too weak to fight with the Chaldeans, and thereupon returned to their own land, and the Chaldeans returned to the siege.

**V. 3. ***{And on the ninth day of the fourth month}* The word *fourth* is well supplied out of Jer. 52.6. Because in this fourth month the city was broken up, an annual fast was kept therein all the time of the captivity, Zech. 8.19.

*{the famine prevailed in the city}* Though they had laid in store of provision, yet being so close blocked up for a year and a half as they could neither themselves go out to fetch in new provision, nor any other bring in any to them, the famine must needs be very great. And though by stinting them that were in the city unto a set quantity of bread and meat, and that but a small one, they did long strive against famine, yet at length it so prevailed against them, as many perished therewith.

*{and there was no bread for the people of the land}* This is especially meant of the common and meaner sort. For there was some bread left for the king and the chief sort, Jer. 37.21. This famine so prevailed against many as they were forced to eat one another. It is said Ezek. 5.10. *the fathers shall eat their sons, and the sons their fathers*. See Lam. 4.10. Besides this famine, the pestilence also was very sore in the city, Jer. 21.6,7,9. & 24.10.

**V. 4. ***{And the city was broken up}* A breach was made, insomuch as the enemies entered thereinto, and the princes and other commanders in the king of Babel’s army sat in the middle gate, as in a place of judgment, Jer. 39.3.

*{and all the men of war fled}* This word *fled* is taken out of Jer. 39.4. The king also fled with them. When they saw the enemy entered into their city, and all hope of standing out against them any longer taken away, they fled to save their lives.

*{by night}* That the enemy might not discern them.

*{by the way of the gate}* Through a postern, or backdoor.

*{between two walls, which is by the kings garden}* It is supposed these walls were within the great wall of the city; and that in one of them there being a backdoor, they dug a passage through the other in the nighttime, and by a vault underground went under the great wall of the city, and under that place where the besiegers lay, into the wilderness. These walls are said to be *by the king’s garden*, to intimate that they were so near the palace as ordinary people did not use to come thither; so as their flight might not be discerned by the inhabitants within, or the enemies without.

*{now the Chaldees were against the city round about}* By this means the king and they that fled with him were the sooner discovered. See on v. 1.

*{and the king}* With the men of war before mentioned, Jer. 52.7.

*{went the way toward the Plain}* Or, *wilderness*. This wilderness reached to Jericho. See v. 5. Jer. 39.5. & 52.8.

**V. 5. ***{And the army of the Chaldees pursued after the king}* Either some of them that lay round about the city espied them, or else some scout, or other person gave them notice thereof.

*{and overtook him in the plains of Jericho}* In the tribe of Benjamin, Josh. 18.21. They were not far fled before they were overtaken.

*{and all his army were scattered from him}* In this common danger every one shifted for himself, and most of them neglected the king. These that were thus scattered are supposed to be the men that came to Gedaliah, v. 23. Jer. 40.7.

**V. 6. ***{So they took the king}* They who pursued him, v. 5.

*{and brought him up to the king of Babylon}* They would not themselves slay him, nor let him escape, but tendered him, as a great present, to their sovereign.

*{to Riblah}* See ch. 23.33. Here the king of Babylon himself remained till he might see the issue of the war. This place being betwixt Judea and Babylon, he might here the better receive intelligence both about the affairs of his own kingdom, and the success of his army.

*{and they gave judgment upon him}* Heb. *spake judgment with him*. The king of Babylon and his attendants expostulated the case with him, about his breach of covenant, which he had confirmed by solemn oath. See Ezek. 17.18. Some refer this relative him to the king of Babylon, as if they had conferred with him about the judgment they should execute upon Zedekiah. Others so take this clause as if Nebuchadnezzar having expostulated with Zedekiah about his breach of covenant, should refer him to a council of war, for them to determine what judgment was fit to execute upon him.

**V. 7. ***{And they slew the sons of Zedekiah}* It is probable that Zedekiah took his sons with him when he fled: They therefore being taken in the flight, fell into the enemies’ power. It may be also they consented to his rebellion; and therefore are slain.

*{before his eyes}* He being present, beholding this bloody spectacle, the judgment on Zedekiah was herein much aggravated.

*{and put out the eyes of Zedekiah}* Heb. *made blind*. This was done at Riblah; so as though he were carried to Babylon, yet he could not see Babylon. And herein the prophecy of Ezekiel was accomplished, *He shall not see Babylon though he shall die there*, Ezek. 11.13.

*{and bound him with fetters of brass}* They little respected his royal dignity, but dealt with him as a notorious malefactor.

*{and carried him to Babylon}* There he died as a miserable prisoner. But his misery moved pity and compassion in many: For they afforded him burial, and mourned for him, Jer. 34.5.

**V. 8. ***{And in the fifth month}* Because the house of the Lord and other houses were burnt, and the walls of Jerusalem broken down in the fifth month, an annual fast was kept by the Jews all the time of their captivity in that month, Ezek. 7.3.

*{and on the seventh day of the month}* On this day he began to make spoil, and on the tenth day he clean burnt down all, Jer. 52.12.

*{which is the nineteenth year of Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon}* The fourth year of Jehoiakim was the first of Nebuchadnezzar, Jer. 25.1. Now Jehoiakim reigned seven years after that, and Zedekiah eleven. This being somewhat after Zedekiah’s eleventh year, might be betwixt the eighteenth and nineteenth of Nebuchadnezzar: Some things here set down being done in his eighteenth year, Jer. 52.29. Others in the nineteenth, as here.

*{came Nebuzaradan Captain of the guard}* Or, *chief Marshall*. Heb. *master of those that slay*. It was an high and honorable office, as general of the army, provost-marshal, captain of the guard, or, as some say, chief jailer. See Gen. 37.36. Dan. 2.14.

*{a servant of the king of Babylon}* One of his subjects, one of his army, yea a principal commander therein, put in trust by his king.

*{unto Jerusalem}* The king of Babylon having taken away the king, princes, and other inhabitants of Jerusalem, and executed judgment on them, his wrath was so incensed as he would not spare an house or wall belonging to that city: therefore about a month after the city was taken he sends a cruel commander to make havoc of all.

**V. 9. ***{And he burnt the house of the Lord}* This was an evidence both of the enemies’ profane and impious disposition, and withal of God’s righteous judgment. When external means of God’s worship are profaned by his people, he cares not to have those external means utterly spoiled.

*{and the Kings house, and all the houses of Jerusalem}* God’s house and king’s house and other houses were all alike to the impious enemy.

*{and every great mans house}* This comprises all the fair houses within and without Jerusalem. In the city he spared no house at all, Jer. 39.8. & 52.13. Without the city he burnt only great men’s houses.

*{he burnt with fire}* Thus he spoiled the houses, all the materials, and all things in them that the enemy cared not to carry away.

**V. 10. ***{And all the army of the Chaldees}* This army consisted of sundry sorts of people, and those of several nations, ch. 24.2. and all of them mortal enemies to the Jews.

*{that were with the Captain of the guard}* i.e. That cruel Nebuzaradan whom the king of Babylon sent to Jerusalem, v. 8.

*{brake down the walls of Jerusalem round about}* Some in one place, others in another; so as that great work was the sooner dispatched.

**V. 11. ***{Now the rest of the people that were left in the city}* The Jews that fled not with the king and the men of war, v. 4.

*{and the fugitives that fell away}* Heb. *fallen away*.

*{to the king of Babylon}* Such as in the time of the siege yielded to the king of Babylon, and came into his camp, Jer. 52.15.

*{with the remnant of the multitude}* Such as remained in any place within or without the city, except such as are mentioned v. 12.

*{did Nebuzaradan the Captain of the guard carry away}* It appears Jer. 40.1. that these were carried in chains, lest they should get away. The express number is set down Jer. 52.29.

**V. 12. ***{But the Captain of the guard}* See v. 8.

*{left of the poor of the land}* Such as were most accustomed to labor and best exercised in husbandry.

*{to be vine-dressers}* That they might take care of vineyards, orchards, and other places where trees of all sorts of fruit were planted, that they might not fall to decay, but continue to yield their fruit.

*{and husband-men}* Who might plow, sow, and till the land, and reap the crop. This the king of Babylon did prudently, that he himself might receive profit from this plentiful land which he had subdued.

**V. 13. ***{And the pillars of brass that were in the house of the Lord}* See 1 King. 7.15.

*{and the bases}* See 1 King. 7.27,28,37, &c.

*{and the brazen Sea that was in the house of the Lord}* See 1 King. 7.23, &c.

*{did the Chaldeans break in pieces}* There could be no use for the things before mentioned beside the use for which they were at first made; therefore the Chaldees, that had no mind to put them to that use, broke them to pieces. See ch. 24.13.

*{and carried the brass of them to Babylon}* The brass whereof these things were made was very fine brass, 1 King. 7.25. The Chaldees therefore might well think it worth the carrying to Babylon, ch. 20.17. Jer. 27.22.

**V. 14. ***{And the pots, and the shovels, and the snuffers, and the spoons}* Some of these particulars are mentioned 1 King. 7.45. but shovels, snuffers, and spoons of brass are not. Yet being here set down they were without question then all made, being useful for the altar of brass and the fire thereon, for the flesh that was sod, and for the lights.

*{and all the vessels of brass wherewith they ministered}* There were other vessels than those here mentioned needful about the great brazen altar; therefore this general is added to the particulars.

*{took they away}* These being small, so as they might well be laid together, and being fit also for sundry uses in other places, they did not break in pieces, but carried them whole to Babylon.

**V. 15. ***{And the fire-pans}* These were to take fire from the great brazen altar, and to carry it to the altar of gold, to burn incense thereon; for they were of gold.

*{and the bowls}* See 1 King. 7.50.

*{and such things as were of gold}* There being sundry other things of gold besides the fire-pans and bowls, 1 King. 7.48, &c. they are all comprised under this phrase.

*{in gold, and of silver, in silver}* This phrase implies that he carried away the golden and silver vessels in their kind, as he found them, and that distinctly by themselves.

*{the Captain of the guard took away}* He broke them not, but carried them whole as they were. See v. 14. With those which they took in the time of Jehoiachin they dealt otherwise, chap. 24.13.

**V. 16. ***{The two pillars}* See v. 13.

*{one Sea}* Heb. *the one Sea*. That Hebraism has an emphasis. It was so great as there needed no more than one, 1 King. 7.23. yet there were ten lavers made, 1 King. 7.38.

*{and the bases which Solomon had made for the house of the Lord}* See 1 King. 7.27, &c.

*{the brass of all the vessels was without weight}* See 1 King. 7.47.

**V. 17. ***{The height of one pillar was eighteen cubits}* See 1 King. 7.15. & Jer. 52.21.

*{and the chapiter upon it was brass}* Of the very same metal whereof the body of the pillar was.

*{and the height of the chapiter three cubits}* See 1 King. 7.16.

*{and the wreathen work, and pomegranates upon the chapiter round about}* See 1 King. 7.17.

*{all of brass}* So fine and excellent was the brass as they might work it into any curious work.

*{and like unto these had the second pillar with wreathen work}* The two pillars were every way uniform.

**V. 18. ***{And the Captain of the guard}* See v. 8.

*{took Seraiah the chief Priest}* He is commonly called the high priest. Of this man’s pedigree see 1 Chron. 6.14,15. It is there said that Jehozadak, who was Seraiah’s son, was carried into captivity by Nebuchadnezzar. Hence we may infer that both father and son were taken; but the father was slain at Riblah, ch. 6.21. and the son carried into Babylon.

*{and Zephaniah}* This Zephaniah was the son of Maaseiah, Jer. 37.3. Hereby he is distinguished from another Zephaniah, 1 Chr. 6.36. This priest was one of those whom Zedekiah sent to Jeremiah to pray for them, Jer. 21.1. & 37.3. It seems he was a man of authority among the people, in that Shemaiah wrote to him from Babylon to reprove Jeremiah for writing to the captives at Babylon a comfortable letter, Jer. 29.24,25,27. There was a faithful prophet of this name but before this man’s time, Zeph. 1.1. There is mention made of two good men, Josiah and Hen, sons of Zephaniah, after the captivity, Zach. 6.10,14. But whether they were the sons of this man, or no, is uncertain.

*{the second Priest}* See ch. 2.34. Zephaniah was next in office to the high priest, and chief over the Levites, Num. 3.32. Seraiah and Zephaniah were now, as of old Aaron and Eleazar, Eleazar and Phinehas, Zadok and Ahimelech, Zadok and Abiathar, 2 Sam. 8.17. & 20.25.

*{and the three keepers of the door}* Heb. *threshold*. See ch. 12.9. & 22.4.

**V. 19. ***{And out of the City he took an Officer}* Or, *Eunuch*. See 1 King. 22.9. It seems this officer and the two priests before mentioned and the others following lurked in the city after the enemy had taken it; But they were found out, and taken away.

*{that was set over the men of war}* He was at least a colonel, if not a lieutenant general; for *Sopher* was the general. See the sixth note on this verse.

*{and five men}* It is said Jer. 52.25. there were seven men. Add therefore to these five the officer going before and the scribe following, and there will be a just agreement, and the number of seven be made up.

*{of them that were in the kings presence}* Heb. *saw the kings face*. These were great men and of high place, either of the king’s bedchamber or of his privy council. They were of such account with the king, and in such office, as they might upon all occasions have free access to him, and speak unto him face to face. See Esth. 1.14.

*{which were found in the city}* See the first note on this verse.

*{and the principal Scribe of the host}* Or, *Scribe of the captain of the host*. The word translated *scribe* is by some taken for a proper name, as if it were thus translated, *Sopher captain of the host*. Thus may he well be reputed general of Zedekiah’s army, as Naaman was of the king of Syriah’s ch. 5.1. The same phrase translated *captain of the host* is attributed both to Naaman and him.

*{which mustered the people of the land}* This act is more proper to a captain or general than to a scribe; and it confirms that which is noted of taking ספר *Sopher* for a proper name.

*{and threescore men of the people of the land}* Though they were not in any special office, and therefore said to be *of the people*, yet they were not of the poor and meaner sort (for such he left behind, v. 12.) but some wealthy men, such as feared to be surprised.

*{that were found in the city}* Where they had hid themselves.

**V. 20. ***{And Nebuzaradan Captain of the guard}* See v. 8.

*{took these, and brought them to the king of Babylon to Riblah}* See v. 6.

**V. 21. ***{And the King of Babylon smote them}* See 1 King. 15.29.

*{and slew them}* All those before mentioned v. 18,19. having taken part with Zedekiah, and counseled him to break his covenant, the king of Babylon having got some intelligence thereof, slew them. At least they stood out to the uttermost, and did not go out, (as Jehoiachin and others did, ch. 24.12.) and so put themselves into the power of the enemy.

*{at Riblah in the land of Hamath}* See v. 6. & ch. 23.33.

*{so Judah was carried away out of their land}* Such as remained alive, and were not escaped into other countries, as v. 23. nor left by the king of Babylon in the land, as v. 12.

**V. 22. ***{And as for the people that remained in the land of Judah}* Jer. 40.5,9. These were such as had not obstinately stood out, but yielded to the king of Babylon according to Jeremiah’s frequent and earnest exhortations, Jer. 21.9. & 27.12,17. & 38.2,17,20.

*{whom Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon had left}* See v. 12.

*{even over them he made Gedaliah}* See the story of this Gedaliah at large set down, Jer. 39.14. & 40.6, &c. & 41.1, &c.

*{the son of Ahikam the son of Shaphan}* This is added to distinguish him from the grandfather of the prophet Zephaniah, who was Gedaliah the son of Amariah, Zeph. 1.1.

*{ruler}* As the king of Babylon left many of the meaner sort in the land of Judah, so also he left sundry of the Chaldees, v. 25. Now surmising there might be differences betwixt people of different nations, and that the people left in Judea might raise tumults, if they had not a governor to keep them in order: therefore he substitutes Gedaliah who was a Jew, but, as all the circumstances of his history show, a pious, prudent, and meek-spirited man, who hearkened to the counsel of Jeremiah, and submitted himself with the first to the king of Babylon. Jeremiah’s willingness to abide with Gedaliah, Jer. 40.5,6. gives proof of his pious and meek spirit.

**V. 23. ***{And when all the Captains of the army, they}* Jer. 40.7. It is probable these captains and their soldiers were some of those that were scattered from Zedekiah when he fled, v. 5.

*{and their men}* i.e. The soldiers under their command.

*{heard that the King of Babylon had made Gedaliah governor}* Such matters are soon divulged and spread abroad.

*{there came to Gedaliah to Mizpah}* Mizpah was a frontier city in Benjamin, Josh. 18.26. betwixt the two kingdoms of Judah and Israel, 1 King. 15.22. The great city Jerusalem being destroyed, this was the fittest place for Gedaliah to abide in, whither the people from all parts might come to him.

*{even Ishmael the son of Nethaniah, &c.}* See v. 25.

*{and Jaazaniah the son of a Maachathite, they and their men}* The Maachathites dwelt beyond Jordan, Josh. 12.5.

**V. 24. ***{And Gedaliah sware to them and to their men}* That which he swore to them, was, that he would not betray them to the Chaldees, but rather protect them, and suffer them to live quietly in the land. Of this he assured them by oath, that he might make them the more confidently rest upon him. See 1 King. 2.8.

*{and said unto them, Fear not to be the servants of the Chaldees}* The Chaldees had destroyed their city and carried away most of their brethren into Babylon; the Jews therefore had cause to fear them. But Gedaliah having received assurance from Nebuchadnezzar that they should not be molested by any from him, if they would remain faithful to him, labors to assure them also that the king would keep his word; so as they need not fear him. He puts in this, *to be his servants*, to show that fidelity was required of them; and on that condition they need not fear.

*{dwell in the land}* Make choice of places and houses where you think you may be most secure.

*{and serve the King of Babylon}* Acknowledge him to be your sovereign, and apply yourselves to no other.

*{and it shall be well with you}* You shall live quietly and securely in the land, and enjoy the commodities thereof.

**V. 25. ***{And it came to pass in the seventh month}* In this month by the destruction of Gedaliah the land of Judah being brought into utter desolation, the Jews kept an annual fast therein all the time they were in captivity, Zech. 7.5.

*{that Ishmael the son of Nethaniah, the son of Elishama}* This Ishmael by his parentage is distinguished from Ishmael the son of Jehohanan, 2 Chron. 23.1. and sundry of other stocks.

*{of the seed royal}* Heb. *of the kingdom*. One that descended from David, and was (as we use to say) of the blood royal. This is added to intimate the cause of his cruelty upon Gedaliah, namely, envy and disdain that one whom he thought inferior to himself should have the government committed to him.

*{came, and ten men with him}* These came with him from the king of Ammon, who put him on the more to destroy Gedaliah, because the Ammonites did bear an inveterate hatred against the Israelites. See Jer. 40.14.

*{and smote Gedaliah, that he died}* They first came in appearance peaceably, and Gedaliah entertained them as friends; but while Gedaliah and others of his friends were feasting with them, Ishmael treacherously slew him. Gedaliah was forewarned hereof beforehand, but would not believe the report brought to him against Ishmael: so through credulity and confidence in a notorious dissembler he perished. See Jer. 40.13, &c. and 41.1, &c.

*{and the Jews and the Chaldees that were with him at Mizpah}* It was not only envy against Gedaliah, but also a cruel mind against all that subjected themselves to him, whether Jews or Chaldees, that made him execute this cruelty: He therefore destroys all he could meet withal.

**V. 26. ***{And all the people both small and great}* They were struck with a great fear, that the king of Babylon, to revenge the blood that was thus treacherously shed, would destroy all that remained in the land of Judah.

*{and the Captains of the armies arose}* In this case the men of war had no more courage than the common sort of people. They arose from their habitations to go out of that land.

*{and came to Egypt: for they were afraid of the Chaldees}* They did not presently go to Egypt. There are many passages of these captains and people, noted by Jeremiah, before they entered into Egypt. For first, they rescued from Ishmael such as he was carrying away captives to the Ammonites, Jer. 41.11, &c. After that, they went to Jeremiah with a pretense to know the mind of God, but dissemblingly, Jer. 42.1, &c. Yea further, when Jeremiah had told them the mind of the Lord, they accuse him of falsifying God’s word, Jer. 43.1, &c. After these and other like passages directly against the word of God, made known unto them by Jeremiah, they went into Egypt, Jer. 43.4, &c. When they are in Egypt, Jeremiah denounces from the Lord utter destruction against them in Egypt, Jer. 43.8, &c. and 44.1, &c. Hereupon this obstinate people lay the blame of all their misery upon the prophet, and upon the Lord who sent him; and justify themselves in their former idolatrous courses, and protest to continue therein, Jer. 44.15, &c. Thus they miserably perish.

**V. 27. ***{And it came to pass in the seven and thirtieth year of the captivity of Jehoiachin king of Judah}* This was the fifty-fifth year of his age. See ch. 24.8. So long had Nebuchadnezzar kept him in prison, though he had voluntarily yielded himself unto him, ch. 24.12. But howsoever he had so many years suffered imprisonment, yet because upon the advice of a prophet he had yielded to his enemy, God preserved his life, and at length brought him out of prison.

*{in the twelfth month, on the seven and twentieth day of the month}* This is said Jer. 52.31. to be on the five and twentieth day. The work might be a day or two in effecting. The king of Babylon might consult about delivering Jehoiachin, and decree to do it, on the five and twentieth day, but the publication and execution of his decree not be till the seven and twentieth. Or he might be let out of prison on the five and twentieth day, and lift up above other kings and brought to the king of Babylon’s table on the seven and twentieth.

*{that Evilmerodach king of Babylon}* Historians write that Nebuchadnezzar was so offended with his son as he cast him into prison, and that Jehoiachin there becoming acquainted with him, found the favor here mentioned. But whether that be true or no, sure it is that God inclined the heart of Evilmerodach to favor Jehoiachin.

*{in the year that he began to reign}* So soon as he had the power in his own hands, he showed mercy to that poor captive king. By this account Nebuchadnezzar reigned forty-four years: For he began his reign in the fourth year of Jehoiachin, Jer. 25.1. who reigned seven years after that; to which seven add the seven and thirty of Jehoiachin’s imprisonment, and they make up forty-four.

*{did lift up the head of Jehoiachin king of Judah out of prison}* Heb. *house of inclosing*. For men in prison are enclosed. By lifting up the head he means an alteration of the condition, or a delivering of the person of Jehoiachin. Prisoners use to hang down the head, Gen. 40.13. but when they are freed they cheerfully lift up the head.

**V. 28. ***{And he spake kindly to him}* Heb. *good things with him*. 1 King. 12.7. Jer. 2.6. Under this phrase is comprised a friendly countenance, fair dealing, and bestowing many good things upon him.

*{and set his throne above the throne of the kings}* By throne is meant a civil estate. The king of Babylon afforded some evidences of royal dignity to such kings as he held in captivity: but he gave evidences of more dignity to this king.

*{that were with him in Babylon}* Detained out of their dominions, in the city where his own royal throne was. And this much tended to the amplification of his own royalty.

**V. 29. ***{And changed his prison-garments}* Prisoners in prison use to wear mean apparel: but the king of Babylon ordered that the king of Judah being freed out of prison should wear such apparel as became a king. This implies an alteration of his former miserable condition, as Psal. 30.11.

*{and he did eat bread}* *He did meal*, or, *he did dine and sup*. Bread is put for all manner of sustenance, Deut. 8.3.

*{continually before him}* In his court, or at his table, 2 Sam. 9.7.

*{all the days of his life}* So long as they two lived together. If Jehoiachin died before Evilmerodach, then indeed this holds properly, so long as Jehoiachin lived.

**V. 30. ***{And his allowance was a continual allowance}* For himself and for his retinue.

*{given him of the king}* At the king’s cost and charges.

*{a daily rate}* A set allowance provided for him every day.

*{for every day}* Heb. *The things of a day in his day*. See 1 King. 8.59.

*{all the days of his life}* See v. 29.
